Redesign: v1 is email-only chase from the LO's domain: one ask, upload link, reference code, independently sourced contact pinned at file open, document check on arrival. When no reply comes, it hands a human a call card with the number and a script. Revisit AI calling only if data shows many conditions stall on phone-only counterparties.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.1, 2.2, 4.6), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D4 (Twilio Lookup line type, signature-verified webhooks, opt-out list), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work- (ARIVE Zapier triggers and actions, from the market survey), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000319289-arive-ai-beta-doc-classification-extraction-update (doc-AI beta turned off), https://www.twilio.com/en-us/changelog/conversationrelay-is-now-available-in-ga (from the market survey), https://www.goodwinlaw.com/en/insights/publications/2025/02/alerts-practices-dpc-ftec-ai-voice-products-subject-to-california-invasion-of-privacy-claims (Ambriz v. Google, CIPA), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/47/227 (not opened this session), https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/codes_displaySection.xhtml?lawCode=BPC&sectionNum=17941 (not opened this session)


## Live Doc Session: a booked call where M8 helps the borrower gather documents and checks each upload while they're still on the line (round 1, medium impact, ~5 weeks)

**Instead of five rounds of 'page 3 is missing', the borrower books a 15-minute call. M8 texts one upload link, walks them through each item on their checklist, and checks every upload in seconds while they're on the phone (pages, dates, names, legibility), so the file arrives complete in one sitting.**

- **Problem:** Document collection is asynchronous and lossy: missing pages, statements that are too old, screenshots instead of PDFs, the wrong account, an unsigned page. Every miss costs a day and a borrower's patience, and broker POS stacks give little feedback at the moment of upload (ARIVE's AI document beta was switched off on 2026-08-30). Async condition chasers, proposed in the market survey, still work by nagging after the fact.
- **How it works:** DATA: the file's needs list (from the LOS conditions, or the loan officer's checklist), per-lender document rules in config (freshness windows, number of statements, whether all pages are needed, signature needs), and the borrower's application-time consent for service texts and calls.

TRIGGER: the borrower books from a link in a service text or email, taps 'Help me upload' in M8 chat, or calls the loan officer's line.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES:
(1) It gives the AI disclosure, the recording notice, a licensing check and a light identity check (a passkey or a one-time code to the number on file, never SSN or DOB).
(2) It texts a one-time upload link the borrower asked for on the call. A single text the consumer requests at that moment is covered by their request [COUNSEL to confirm against the plan's consent tiers].
(3) For each item it says, for example, 'Next is your last two Chase statements. In the app, tap Statements, then Download PDF.' This is generic guidance with no screen sharing and no credential capture.
(4) Each upload is checked within seconds: document type, page count ('page 3 of 5' detection), statement dates against the lender's freshness rule, the name on the document against the borrower, legibility, and signatures where required. M8 replies by voice: 'Got it, all 5 pages. The May statement is older than this lender accepts, so let's grab June.'
(5) For bank statements it may say only 'lenders often ask where deposits that aren't payroll came from', and lets the borrower add a note in their own words. It never asks about alimony, child support or public assistance unless the borrower is relying on that income (Reg B 1002.5(d)(2)), and never asks about protected traits.
(6) Text inside documents is data. Any instruction-like text in an upload is ignored and logged.
(7) Files are encrypted and delivered to the POS or LOS where an API allows (Floify's Custom CRM integration API key, ARIVE through the loan officer's own process), then deleted from Command Center after delivery under the retention policy.

WHAT THE LOAN OFFICER SEES: 'Live Doc Session with Dana, 14 min: 9 of 10 items complete; the gift letter needs the donor's signature.' The Application Clock is updated.

WHAT THE BORROWER EXPERIENCES: one call, done. M8 says 'looks complete for the checklist', never 'approved'.
- **Technologies:** Twilio ConversationRelay voice with barge-in, one-time signed upload links (Vercel Blob or S3 with KMS encryption), vision-model page, date and name checks with zod schemas, per-lender document rules table in config, lib/m8/guard.ts, redact.ts and moderation.ts, Floify Custom CRM Integration API (approved by Floify Support) and ARIVE Zapier for status, passkeys or a one-time code for identity, QStash for post-session follow-up
- **Why it's new:** Lender POS platforms (Blend, nCino) classify uploads asynchronously, and the survey's condition-chaser idea follows up after the fact. ARIVE, the broker market's largest LOS, has no document AI running after 2026-08-30. AI receptionists book appointments, but they don't do the work during the call. A synchronous session that combines voice guidance and upload checking, ending with a complete file and a per-lender rule check, isn't offered by any vendor found in this research. That's a search limitation, labelled as such: the web search budget was exhausted in this session.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: the session is booked or placed by the borrower. The call and the one requested link-text are service only, and the guard blocks any marketing content, so nothing converts into a marketing call. Revocation by any means ends the session and the channel. The AI disclosure, then the recording notice (all-party states, and given everywhere), come before any substance. ECOA and Reg B: a fixed question set with protected-income and protected-trait questions excluded, and an automated check on the prompt and transcripts. GLBA: the upload link is encrypted, short-lived and single use; reads are audited; account numbers are redacted from transcripts; nothing goes into logs or model training (zero-retention vendor terms). No underwriting conclusions and no rates. SAFE Act: the AI collects documents for a licensed loan officer's file and doesn't take the application or offer terms [COUNSEL]. Licensing: only in the loan officer's licensed states.
- **Moat:** The per-lender document rules table and the 'what goes wrong' patterns (which banks' PDFs drop pages, which payroll exports fail) improve with every member's sessions, as playbook text only with no borrower data. A larger member network means fewer document cycles per file for everyone.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: It is compliant: borrower-requested service calls, a single requested text (consistent with the FCC's 2012 SoundBite one-time confirmation reasoning, from training knowledge), recording notice, and protected-income questions excluded. SAFE Act risk is low because collecting documents is support work, provided M8 never interprets eligibility. Bank-app guidance must never ask for or capture credentials. Redesign: Keep a fixed phrase guard so M8 never says 'qualifies', 'enough' or 'approved'. Log a check that no credential-like strings were collected. Delete files from the platform after delivery to the POS, per policy. / keep 5/10: Blend, Floify and Encompass Consumer Connect already classify uploads, some of them fast. The new part is a live voice-guided session, which is a modest step over async checking plus a chat prompt. Redesign: Build it as a mode of M8 chat or voice that reuses the per-lender document rules table. Share that table with the Chase Agent. / keep 6/10: Document rework is a real time sink, and per-page vision checks are cheap (cents per page). Risks: (1) Command Center turns into a shadow POS holding uploads the POS (Floify or ARIVE) should own, which doubles storage and GLBA scope. (2) A booked voice call is costlier and harder than a chat session, and many borrowers upload at night without a call. (3) The per-lender document rules table needs ongoing upkeep. Redesign: Start with a chat or text session (/chat upload with instant checks), with voice as an option later. Check uploads in transit, deliver to the POS, keep only check results, and delete after delivery. Start the rules table with agency defaults and add lender overlays only when a member reports one.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/lib/prep/machine.ts (containsSensitive; the prep flow never asks for SSN, DOB or account numbers), /home/user/RATEM8/lib/m8/redact.ts, guard.ts, moderation.ts, /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.1, 2.2, 2.5), https://help.floify.com/en/articles/16300950-how-to-create-a-custom-integration (Floify Custom CRM integration, from the market survey), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000319289-arive-ai-beta-doc-classification-extraction-update (doc-AI beta turned off), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1002/section-1002.5 (not opened: host blocked)


## Licensed Exception Desk: AI agents do the routine work, and a shared pool of licensed processors on the platform takes what AI can't finish (round 1, high impact, ~8 weeks)

**Every stalled chase, document mismatch or 'I want a person' from the agents goes to a shared desk of state-licensed contract processors. Each gets scoped access to that one item only, for a limited time, and every action passes the same send gate. A solo loan officer gets a processing department without hiring one.**

- **Problem:** AI agents will always leave exceptions: a counterparty who won't talk to a machine, a document that doesn't fit the rules, a borrower who wants a human. Solo and small-broker loan officers can't afford a full-time processor. Outsourced processing firms are separate, per-file vendors with no link to the CRM or the AI. The SAFE Act also requires independent-contractor loan processors to hold an MLO license (12 U.S.C. 5103(a)(2), from model knowledge, not re-opened). So an informal gig marketplace of processors would be unlawful.
- **How it works:** DATA: exception items emitted by the chase agent, Live Doc Session, Application Clock and the inbox. Each holds the minimal slice of the file needed (the condition, the counterparty, the redacted transcript and the deadline). Plus a processor registry: each processor's NMLS ID and licensed states, entered by them and checked through NMLS Consumer Access for the permitted purpose of deciding whether to do business with them, never for recruiting outreach.

TRIGGER: an agent hits a stop condition (a refusal, a mismatch, a deadline at risk, a retry cap).

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES:
(1) It routes the item to an available processor licensed in the property state, or to a W-2 processor of the loan officer's sponsor.
(2) It grants just-in-time access to that item only, expiring in 72 hours or at resolution, under Postgres row-level security. Every read is audited.
(3) The processor works the item: calls, emails, follows up. Anything sent to a borrower uses the loan officer's number and the D3 gate, with the loan officer's consent records.
(4) The resolution and a redacted 'how I fixed it' note return to the file. The playbook note (with no borrower data) feeds the training loop, so agents handle that pattern next time.

WHAT THE LOAN OFFICER SEES: 'Exception: HOA manager refuses automated calls. Assigned to a licensed processor (WA), resolved in 3 h.' With capacity shown as hours included in the subscription.

WHAT THE BORROWER EXPERIENCES: a person steps in exactly when needed, identified by name and by role as 'a licensed processor working with [loan officer]'.

ECONOMICS: the platform pays processors hourly or a flat amount per item type. It never pays by loan amount or terms, and never pays the loan officer anything. Members get a set number of exception hours in the flat subscription, plus metered hours at published cost.
- **Technologies:** Neon row-level security with time-boxed grants (just-in-time access), append-only audit log (D1 cc_audit), the D3 send gate and D4 messaging for any processor touch, a work queue with SLAs (QStash), NMLS Consumer Access checks, used only to verify the processor's own license, the training loop (plan section 5.10) for redacted playbook notes, Stripe metering for exception hours
- **Why it's new:** AI processing vendors (Friday Harbor, Candor, Vesta, from the survey) aim to replace people inside lenders. Contract processing firms sell per-file human work with no AI or CRM integration (general industry knowledge; not verified this session). No vendor found pairs autonomous agents with a licensed human pool shared across independent loan officers, with access scoped to a single item. It also fits the founder's ownership thesis: a capacity pool that only exists because many small members share it.
- **Compliance design:** SAFE Act: contract processors must be licensed MLOs in the relevant state, or W-2 employees under a licensed loan officer's supervision; the registry blocks routing otherwise. Reg Z 1026.36(d): processors are paid hourly or per item type, never on loan terms. Loan officers receive nothing, and the subscription stays flat, never per loan. If licensed processors join the ownership plan as members, their units follow the same subscription-and-tenure rule [COUNSEL: mortgage and securities]. RESPA: no borrower is charged a platform processing fee. Any processing fee on a Loan Estimate is the sponsor's, bona fide, for services actually performed (Reg X 1024.14) [COUNSEL]. GLBA: processors are service providers under the sponsor's Safeguards program, with item-scoped access, audited reads and a signed confidentiality agreement. TCPA: processor outreach uses the same gate and consent tiers, and human dialing is still blocked for contacts who said stop. NMLS data is used only for the permitted 'whether to do business' purpose.
- **Moat:** A larger membership gives coverage across time zones and more licensed states, faster resolution and a richer playbook library that makes the agents need humans less often. A single broker or a closed enterprise can't pool capacity across independent loan officers. An MLO-owned platform can, and the network can be governed so processors themselves can be members.
- **Reviews:** keep 5/10: The structure as stated has a licensing problem. Under the SAFE Act and Reg H, an independent-contractor processor must hold an MLO license, and state MLO licenses must be sponsored by a licensed company. A tech platform that pays a pool of processors is itself a third-party processing company. Several states require a company license for that, and the platform has none. W-2 processors employed by the platform are not 'at the direction and supervision of' the borrower's licensed MLO's company. Including processing hours in the MLO's subscription also blurs who pays for processing and whether a processing fee on the LE is bona fide for the sponsor (Reg X 1024.14). Redesign: Have the platform route items, never employ or pay processors. Processing is performed either by the sponsor's own W-2 staff, or by independently licensed processing companies that hold company licenses where required, contracted by the sponsor under its Safeguards vendor program. The platform invoices only software. Hold-capacity marketplace fees go from sponsor to processing company, hourly or per item and never per loan amount. / keep 6/10: Contract processing firms and outsourcers already sell per-file human work. What is new is single-item scoped access paired with AI escalation, pooled across independent LOs. It is an operations business as much as software. Redesign: Start with the sponsor's own W-2 processors and a small pilot of vetted contract processors before promising pooled hours in the subscription. / drop 3/10: This is a labor marketplace, not software: recruiting, vetting, scheduling and quality-managing contract processors across states, carrying their errors, and paying them. Contract processing is a low-margin service already sold per file. Two-sided supply and demand at small scale means idle processors or unmet SLAs. Including hours in a flat subscription exposes the platform to unbounded cost. It distracts a small team. Redesign: Replace it with 'bring your own processor': a sponsor or MLO attaches their own W-2 or licensed contract processor with item-scoped, expiring access through Delegation Desk. A partner directory of contract-processing firms can come later, with no fee taken.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.4, 4.9, 5.10), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D1 (roles, audit log) and D3 (send gate), https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/policies/NMLS%20Document%20Library/Consumer%20Access%20Terms%20of%20Use.pdf (permitted use; ban on solicitation, from the market survey), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5103 (SAFE Act licensing of independent-contractor processors; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (loan originator compensation, from the market survey)


## Closing Week Conductor with pinned contacts and a wire-instruction callback check (round 1, medium impact, ~5 weeks)

**For the final 10 days, an agent coordinates borrower, agents, title and the lender's closer. It confirms Closing Disclosure receipt, signing logistics and ID needs by consented text or email, never transmits wire instructions, and confirms the last four digits of the borrower's wire instructions with title by calling title's independently sourced number, which was pinned at file open.**

- **Problem:** The last week is where files slip and money disappears. CD receipt timing (the 3-business-day rule, 1026.19(f)(1)(ii)), signing appointments, ID and funds-to-close all get coordinated by hand, and business-email compromise targets exactly this week with forged wire instructions. The FBI's IC3 reports track real-estate wire-fraud losses each year; I didn't verify the figures because search was exhausted. Title-side wire-verification tools exist (CertifID, for example), but the loan officer, who is the borrower's main contact, has no control that checks independently.
- **How it works:** DATA: closing date, CD issued and received dates, the title or escrow company and the signing method (in person or RON) from the LOS, and the parties' contacts pinned at file open from independent sources (title's state license record or verified website, the agents' brokerage pages). Any later change to a contact that arrives by email is treated as suspect.

TRIGGER: CTC or a scheduled closing date 10 days out.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES:
(1) A countdown on the file, plus service texts and emails to each party who consented (agents get identical status, which protects RESPA neutrality): CD sent, CD acknowledged, signing time and place, which ID to bring, 'funds-to-close comes only from your CD and title'.
(2) Every borrower message in closing week ends with: 'We will never send wire instructions. Never wire based on an email. Call title at [pinned number].'
(3) Wire check: the borrower uploads a photo or PDF of the instructions they received. The system extracts only the bank name, routing number and the last four digits of the account, and redacts the full number immediately. Then the agent, or the loan officer if title refuses automated calls, calls title's pinned business number, gives the AI disclosure and recording notice, and asks title to confirm the bank name, routing number and last four. If they match, the borrower is told: 'title confirmed these at [time] by phone at their number on file'. If they don't match, the system tells the borrower not to wire, phones the loan officer and title right away, and adds FBI IC3 reporting guidance. If title uses a verification service, the agent reads that service's status instead.
(4) Any request to change a pinned contact (a 'new escrow officer email') blocks automation until a human verifies it by callback.

WHAT THE LOAN OFFICER SEES: one strip per closing (CD received ✓, signing booked ✓, wire verified ✓) and red alerts.

WHAT THE BORROWER EXPERIENCES: a calm, checklist-driven final week, and a fraud check they can see.
- **Technologies:** LOS key dates (ARIVE Zapier and webhooks; Encompass milestones later), business-day calendar shared with Application Clock, Twilio voice for business-line calls (landline check through Lookup) and 10DLC service texts through the D3 gate, vision extraction limited to bank name, routing number and last four, with immediate redaction, Neon pinned-contacts table with change-control, optional integration with title-side wire-verification services (vendor TBD), RON platform scheduling links where the eClose rules allow
- **Why it's new:** Milestone tools (Floify, plan section 5.5) push status. Agent Line (repo idea 8) answers status calls. Wire-verification products serve title companies. None found ties the loan officer's CRM to pinned, independently sourced counterparty contacts with change-control, or to a borrower-visible check of the last four digits with title. The combination of closing-week coordination, business-email-compromise defense and TRID CD-receipt tracking in one broker-side agent is new as far as this research could tell. That is a search limitation: the shared web-search budget was exhausted.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: borrower and agent messages are service only, consented at application or engagement, and pass the gate. Calls to title go to its business line, with the AI disclosure and the recording notice. GLBA and FTC Safeguards: the full account number is never stored, only routing number and last four, redacted immediately, with audited reads. The breach and incident runbook covers suspected business-email compromise. TRID: the system tracks CD receipt for the creditor's file and never sets or promises a closing date, keeping the plan's 'never promise a closing date' guard. RESPA: every agent on a file gets identical status, with nothing of value. Liability: the copy says 'confirmed with title by phone', never 'guaranteed safe'. Counsel reviews the wording and the platform's role [COUNSEL]. No rates are ever discussed.
- **Moat:** A pinned, verified directory of title and escrow offices (business facts only) grows with every member closing, and a member network can flag a title office whose contact details changed suspiciously across many files. A single loan officer couldn't see that pattern. Fraud signals are shared as business facts, never borrower data.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: No statutory violation. Liability and UDAAP risk sit in the 'title confirmed these' assurance. If the AI misreads, or a compromised title office confirms, the borrower relied on the platform's statement. Many title offices refuse to confirm wire details to third parties or bots. Handling the routing number and last four is low-sensitivity, but it still needs Safeguards treatment. Redesign: Make the borrower the one who verifies. Offer a warm transfer: the platform dials title's pinned number and bridges the borrower in, and the borrower confirms. Use AI callbacks only as a fallback, worded 'title's office stated X by phone at [time]', with no assurance of safety. Prefer reading title's own verification-service status. / drop 3/10: Wire verification is a crowded, shipped category. CertifID (title, buyer and payoff verification), Closinglock (buyer-facing verified instructions) and FundingShield (lender-side wire and title-agent verification) all ship it. AI calls to title to confirm the last four digits add liability with little new value. Redesign: Integrate the title company's existing verification service status. Keep the 'we never send wire instructions' footer and the pinned-contact change control as Deal Clock features. / keep 5/10: Closing-week status and the 'we never send wire instructions' banner are cheap and valuable. The AI callback to title asking them to confirm routing and last four is the weak part. Title and escrow offices often refuse to confirm instructions to third parties, many already use wire-verification services (CertifID and similar; recalled, not verified), and the broker takes on liability if a 'confirmed' wire is still diverted. Building outbound AI calls for this step costs more than it saves. Redesign: Keep the countdown, the CD-received tracking from the LOS, and the pinned independently sourced title number with change control. Swap the AI call for 'call title at this pinned number' prompts to the borrower, plus showing the title company's own verification-service status where it offers one. No platform-made confirmation claims.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 4.6, 5.5; 'never promise a closing date' guard), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 8 Agent Line, for contrast), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (FTC Safeguards Rule, cited in the market survey; not opened), https://www.ic3.gov/ (FBI IC3 annual reports; not opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1026/subpart-E/section-1026.19 (CD timing; not opened: host blocked)


## Delegation Envelopes: signed, machine-readable limits on what each AI agent may do, widened only with evidence (round 1, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**Every autonomous agent (chase, doc session, hold-for-you, closing conductor, M8 check-ins) runs inside a signed envelope that lists who it may contact, on which channels and for which purposes, which tools and data fields it may use, plus hours, retries, spend, model version and expiry. An envelope widens only on measured evidence and counsel approval, and narrows automatically on any complaint or guard failure.**

- **Problem:** The plan says texts stay approve-to-send until counsel approves a play (section 2.5), and the first 50 calls of a play get a human sample (section 4.4). Once agents do real work, though (calling businesses, holding queues, chasing documents), 'approved or not' is too coarse. There's no record of exactly what an agent was allowed to do at the moment it acted, no evidence-based path to more autonomy, and nothing to show an examiner or a plaintiff's lawyer. Model churn makes this worse: the survey reports gemini-3.1-flash-live-preview reached its earliest shutdown date on 2026-11-17.
- **How it works:** DATA: an `agent_envelopes` table. Each envelope is a versioned JSON policy signed with a KMS key. Fields: agent type; allowed counterparties (borrower, business landline, agent); channel and purpose pairs mapped to D2 consent tiers; tools allowlist; readable data fields; max attempts and hours; per-day spend in minutes and dollars; pinned model IDs plus a fallback; counsel_status; MLO approval; expiry. Also `envelope_evidence`: per envelope version, actions taken, how much the loan officer edited drafts before approving, guard hits, opt-outs, complaints, human-review scores.

TRIGGERS: every agent action checks the envelope and then the D3 send gate (both must pass, fail closed). Nightly evaluation. Any complaint, revocation-handling miss or guard failure.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES:
(1) Each action is stamped with the envelope version and the send-gate decision.
(2) Levels: L0 drafts only; L1 the loan officer approves batches; L2 acts with sampled review; L3 acts within the envelope. Promotion needs, for example, 200 actions at the current level, approval edits under a threshold, zero guard failures, opt-outs under the play's bound, the new model version passing the red-team suite (tests/m8-redteam*) and recorded-call replays, and counsel_status = approved for that purpose.
(3) Automatic demotion to L0, for every member running that envelope template, on any complaint, a missed revocation, or a model version change that hasn't passed.
(4) A one-click export of envelope history for counsel.

WHAT THE LOAN OFFICER SEES: a 'What M8 is allowed to do' page in plain English, with toggles that only narrow scope. Widening shows the evidence and needs counsel's flag.

WHAT THE BORROWER OR COUNTERPARTY SEES: nothing directly. The effect is that AI never acts outside what was approved for that purpose.
- **Technologies:** Neon versioned policy tables, KMS signing (Vercel KMS or a cloud KMS), zod policy schema evaluated in the agent runtime, the D3 send gate as the second check, tests/m8-redteam.spec.ts and m8-redteam-remote.spec.ts as promotion gates, recorded-call replay harness, Sentry over plain HTTP (lib/monitoring.ts) for demotion alerts
- **Why it's new:** The plan's Play Exchange (repo ideas doc) pools play results and pauses on opt-out spikes, and the survey's compliance receipt stamps each call. Neither defines what an agent is authorized to do per action, nor ties more autonomy to evidence, counsel approval and model version. Enterprise AI-voice vendors (Sela, Flair, Total Expert) publish no such authorization model (survey; vendor pages). This makes 'what was the AI allowed to do on 11/18 at 2:10 PM' answerable for every action.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA and state law: envelopes can only reference consent tiers and purposes that exist in D2, so an envelope can't grant consent, only use it. A revocation instantly overrides every envelope through the gate. Counsel_status is a hard field for widening, never inferred. Reg Z, RESPA and fair lending: envelopes can't include loan-term, pricing-ranking or demographic fields, enforced by a schema allowlist and a CI test like idea 12's comp firewall. GLBA: field-level read allowlists, audited. AI disclosure and 'no rate outside a priced result' are invariants no envelope can turn off. Records are kept 5 years to match the TSR recordkeeping period cited in the survey [COUNSEL].
- **Moat:** Promotion evidence pools across members for shared envelope templates. A template proven over 50,000 actions network-wide reaches L2 sooner for a new member, while demotions protect everyone immediately. That network evidence, and a governance process the MLO members own, are things a single vendor's opaque autonomy settings can't offer.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: This is governance infrastructure with no inherent violation, and it strengthens TCPA, GLBA and fair-lending controls. Two risks: auto-promotion of autonomy on metrics alone could outrun counsel's purpose approvals (the design requires counsel_status, which is good); and the 5-year retention follows the 2024 TSR amendment (from training knowledge) but must yield to deletion rights and holds. Redesign: Keep counsel_status as a non-inferable, human-set field. Tie record retention to Complaint Radar's legal-hold and deletion logic, with the longer of TSR or state periods. / keep 6/10: Generic agent-governance and policy engines exist (OPA-style policy as code, enterprise AI governance tools). In mortgage, no vendor publishes per-action authorization tied to consent tiers and model version. It overlaps Guard Commons and Licensed Hands. Redesign: Merge with Guard Commons into one 'Agent Governance' layer: envelopes plus the shared test corpus as the promotion gate. / keep 6/10: MLOs never use it directly, but any autonomous agent needs it. As a versioned JSON policy checked before the D3 gate it is cheap, and it gives a clean counsel export. Over-engineered parts: KMS signing per envelope, four autonomy levels with network-pooled promotion evidence (needs many tenants), and automatic network-wide demotion logic. Those add build time before there are enough agents to govern. Redesign: v1: a policy table (allowed counterparties, channel-purpose pairs, tools, fields, caps, pinned model and fallback, counsel_status) checked on every action and stamped on the log, with two levels (draft-only and act-within-envelope). Add evidence-based promotion and network pooling once there are 3 or more agents and 20 or more tenants.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.5, 4.4, 4.9), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (Play Exchange; idea 12 comp firewall pattern), /home/user/RATEM8/tests/m8-redteam.spec.ts (existing red-team suite, per CLAUDE.md), https://aiengineerguide.com/tools/model-retirements/gemini-3-1-flash-live-preview/ (model churn, from the market survey), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-310 (TSR recordkeeping, cited in the market survey; not opened)


## Fair Lending Commons: pooled, privacy-preserving disparate-impact testing of every ranking model and play (round 1, high impact, ~7 weeks)

**A solo MLO's book is far too small to test for disparate impact. Each tenant computes proxy-based fairness counts locally and shares only differentially private aggregates, so the network gets real statistical power and blocks any model or play version that fails, for everyone.**

- **Problem:** The plan requires an automated fair-lending check on every scoring model (section 2.2 and 4.9). But Today's ranking and each play reach a few hundred people per MLO, too few for any adverse-impact test to mean anything. Federal disparate-impact enforcement is paused (EO 14281). State AGs still act (the Massachusetts AG's Earnest settlement, July 2025) and so do private plaintiffs, and limitation periods outlast any administration. Black-box propensity scores such as Aidium's 5,000+ third-party data points can't be audited by an LO at all.
- **How it works:** DATA, computed inside each tenant only, in a separate testing schema:
- A BISG race and ethnicity proxy, built from the Census surname list and the geocoded census tract of the contact's address.
- Tract LMI and MMCT flags from public HMDA and FFIEC census data.
- The firewall: the ranking engine's database role cannot read this schema. It's the same role-grant and CI privilege test pattern as idea 12's Comp Firewall. A lint rule bars the ranker from importing it.

TRIGGER: every new version of the Today ranking model, play copy or ladder, owner training-notes bundle, or Fraud Signal Exchange rule, plus a monthly rerun.

SYSTEM:
- Each tenant computes counts at each funnel stage, by proxy group and by tract type: surfaced on Today, contacted, offered a priced result, booked, applied.
- Only noisy aggregates leave the tenant: differential privacy via OpenDP, and minimum cell sizes.
- The platform sums across tenants and computes adverse impact ratios and confidence intervals for each model or play version.
- A version that fails is blocked platform-wide, with the failing stage named. Every run is stored immutably with the rule and model version, so a 2026 model can be defended in 2029.
- LMI and MMCT may only ever add outreach, such as down-payment-assistance education. The test also confirms that this additive use happened, and logs it.

MLO: sees 'This play version passed the network fair-lending test (n = 41,000 contacts across 900 MLOs)', plus their own tenant's result marked 'small sample'.

SPONSOR AND COUNSEL: an exportable evidence file for exams.

BORROWER: never sees a proxy. No proxy is ever stored on the contact record or used for any decision about them.
- **Technologies:** BISG proxy method (Census surname list plus geocoding), HMDA and FFIEC census tract data, Differential privacy (OpenDP) with minimum cell sizes, Postgres role grants plus a CI privilege test (idea 12 pattern), Append-only results ledger with model-version hashes, Statistical testing (adverse impact ratio, Fisher's exact test, bootstrapped confidence intervals)
- **Why it's new:** Fair-lending testing vendors exist for lenders' underwriting and pricing models. From my knowledge, not verified this session: FairPlay AI and Zest AI, plus in-house BISG testing at large banks. CFPB has used BISG in fair-lending analysis since its 2014 methodology paper. None tests outreach and marketing ranking for broker MLOs, where ECOA discouragement risk sits. None solves the small-sample problem by pooling many tiny firms without pooling their data. The survey found that no CRM publishes explainable rankings or a per-model-version fairness check, and that Aidium's scores can't be audited. This session couldn't open ecfr.gov, so the Reg B self-test details below come from my knowledge.
- **Compliance design:** ECOA and Reg B:
- No proxy is ever an input. Proxies are computed only for testing and are never asked of the consumer. Reg B 1002.5 bars inquiring about race except in narrow cases, including self-tests under 1002.15.
- Whether the 1002.15 self-test privilege covers a broker, a sponsor or the platform as their service provider is a counsel question. Results should be structured so the privilege can apply where it does.
- [COUNSEL: ECOA, Reg B, state fair-lending law, and special-purpose credit program rules for any additive targeting]

GLBA: only differentially private aggregates leave a tenant, never person-level rows.

Antitrust: no prices, comp or pipeline values are exchanged, only fairness rates by model version.

No lead is sold: aggregates can't be reversed into people.

The same test covers Fraud Signal Exchange Verify cards and Guard Commons opt-out detection.
- **Moat:** Statistical power grows with the number of members, so every new MLO inherits models that have already been tested on large samples. A competitor with a few hundred users can't produce a meaningful test. The immutable, versioned evidence history builds up over years and can't be backfilled.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: BISG is not an 'inquiry' under Reg B 1002.5(b), so testing is lawful. But storing per-contact race and ethnicity inferences in a testing schema is processing sensitive data under state privacy laws without full GLBA exemptions. Oregon's OCPA has a data-level GLBA exemption, and prospects who never applied are not GLBA consumers. OCPA and Colorado require opt-in consent to process data revealing racial or ethnic origin. Colorado's rules exempt inferences only if they are deleted within about 24 hours and not transferred (from training knowledge). The CCPA also treats such inferences as sensitive personal information. Whether the 1002.15 self-test privilege covers a broker is uncertain, and the idea flags it. Redesign: Compute BISG ephemerally inside the job, aggregate immediately to DP counts with minimum cells, and never persist person-level proxies (deleted within the run, well under 24 hours). Document the purpose in privacy notices. Run it under counsel's direction to preserve privilege. / keep 6/10: FairPlay, Zest and SolasAI test underwriting and pricing models for lenders. Pooling differentially private outreach-funnel counts across many tiny firms is new. It is complex (BISG pipeline, DP) and only useful at scale. Redesign: Ship Paired-Tester Lab first, since it needs no real data. Add the pooled funnel test once there are 100+ members, and share the firewall pattern. / drop 3/10: With tens of MLOs, differentially private aggregates over small funnels are mostly noise, so the statistical-power promise doesn't hold until there are many members. BISG needs geocoded addresses and the surname list per contact, plus a separate privileged schema. OpenDP integration and cross-tenant aggregation are specialist work. MLOs never use it weekly. Paired-Tester Lab gives most of the near-term fair-lending assurance at a fraction of the cost. Redesign: Defer. Now: a per-sponsor outreach funnel report by tract LMI/MMCT flag (public FFIEC data, no person-level proxy), run by the sponsor under counsel. Add pooled DP testing once there are 300 or more MLOs.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.2 and 4.9), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (ideas 3, 7 and 12), Survey: Aidium opaque scores, https://www.g2.com/products/aidium-mortgage-crm/reviews, Survey (regulatory horizon): EO 14281, https://www.whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/2025/04/restoring-equality-of-opportunity-and-meritocracy/ ; Massachusetts AG and Earnest (from memory, medium confidence), Reg B self-test, 12 CFR 1002.15: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1002/section-1002.15 (fetch failed: host not allowed), CFPB BISG methodology (2014): https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/201409_cfpb_report_proxy-methodology.pdf (not opened), HMDA public data: https://ffiec.cfpb.gov/documentation/publications/modified-lar/resources/using-mlar-data, OpenDP: https://opendp.org


## Guard Commons: a shared immune system for M8 across every tenant (round 1, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Every guard block, reviewer correction or missed opt-out on any MLO's M8 becomes a de-identified test case, once a person approves it. Every prompt, model, voice pipeline and MLO training-notes change must then pass the whole network's test suite before it can talk to a borrower.**

- **Problem:** M8 runs under thousands of MLOs' names, each with its own owner instructions (/mlo/training). One tenant sees few prompt injections, rate-baiting attempts or indirect revocations ('I'm good, take me off your thing'). The 2026 Texas AI-voice suit alleged the agent kept selling after repeated 'no's. A single MLO's custom training note can quietly break the guard. Today the repo's red-team suite is 47 offline cases written by hand.
- **How it works:** DATA: guard blocks from lib/m8/guard.ts, call-review corrections (plan 4.9), borrower complaints, 'stop' signals the AI missed but a human caught, and abuse blocks.

TRIGGER: any of those events in any tenant.

SYSTEM, inside the tenant:
- The existing redaction runs.
- A server-side model rewrites the exchange into a synthetic case with fake names, numbers, places and lender names.
- A leakage check (n-gram and entity overlap against the source) rejects any case that keeps original tokens.
- The case is queued for platform compliance review. Approved cases join the shared suite (the same format as tests/m8-redteam-cases.ts).
- Approved indirect-revocation phrasings join the opt-out lexicon used by D4's inbound STOP detection and by voice, for every tenant.

NIGHTLY AND BEFORE ANY CHANGE: the full suite runs against the production prompt, each MLO's training-notes bundle, each candidate model ID and the voice path. Any regression blocks that change, with the failing case shown:
- a new owner note
- a model swap
- a play's copy

MLO: sees 'Your new training note made M8 state a rate without APR in 3 network cases. Edit it, or keep the old note.'

BORROWER: an M8 that has already been tested against every trick seen anywhere on the network.
- **Technologies:** lib/m8/guard.ts and the redaction in lib/m8, LLM paraphrasing with zod-validated output and an entity and n-gram leakage checker, Vitest red-team harness (tests/m8-redteam*.spec.ts) with a remote runner, QStash nightly jobs and gating in CI and deploy, Versioned prompt, training-notes and model registry, D4 opt-out lexicon (lib/messaging)
- **Why it's new:** Per-vendor safety testing exists inside AI-voice companies, but it's private and generic. The survey shows Total Expert discloses AI only 'when required or if prompted', UWM's Mia has no published consent script, and Retell, Vapi and Bland have no mortgage logic. No mortgage CRM tests each customer's own custom instructions against a shared, growing corpus of real-world failures before they go live. This also differs from the survey's 'deprecation shield', which pins and fails over models, and from 'compliance receipts', which check each call afterwards. Both of those would consume this corpus. Guard Commons is how the corpus gets built from network events.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA:
- No nonpublic personal information crosses tenants. Cases are synthetic, checked for leakage and approved by a person.
- Raw transcripts stay in the tenant under the existing retention and 'Delete my chat' rules.
- Model vendors run under no-training, zero-retention terms. The corpus is test data and rules, not training data for a vendor's model.

TCPA: a growing revocation lexicon puts 'any reasonable means' into practice, and a missed revocation becomes a platform-wide fix within a day.

AI disclosure and no rate outside a priced result: both are standing assertions in every case.

CIPA: no audio leaves the tenant. Voice cases are re-synthesized from text.

No model identifiers appear in pushed artifacts (CLAUDE.md rule 8). Case IDs are neutral.
- **Moat:** The corpus of real attack and failure patterns grows with every call on the network and compounds over years. A new entrant starts with an empty suite, and every member gets safer as others join.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: It is sound if cases are truly de-identified. The risk is GLBA reuse limits (Reg P 1016.11): using sponsor A's borrower conversations, even rewritten, to improve the service for sponsor B is beyond service-provider purposes unless the sponsor agreement permits it and the output is de-identified. Model rewriting of NPI must run under zero-retention vendor terms. Redesign: Add an express clause in sponsor agreements permitting creation of de-identified test cases. Keep human approval plus the leakage check as mandatory. Let a sponsor opt out of contributing while still receiving the suite. / keep 6/10: Generic red-team tooling and corpora exist (Promptfoo, Lakera and others), and the repo's Play Exchange already pools play results. A cross-tenant, de-identified mortgage regression corpus that gates each MLO's training notes is a modest but real step. Redesign: Merge into the Agent Governance layer with Delegation Envelopes. Extend tests/m8-redteam-cases.ts rather than building a new system. / keep 8/10: Cheap and directly reuses tests/m8-redteam-cases.ts and the guard. Every new owner training note or model swap already needs regression tests, and running a growing suite costs little. The one costly piece is the human compliance review queue for each candidate case. The synthetic-rewrite leakage check needs care but is simple n-gram and entity overlap. Redesign: Build now, single-tenant first: guard blocks and reviewer corrections become candidate cases, a person approves them, and the suite runs before any prompt, training-note or model change. Share across tenants once there is more than one.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (C4 hardening, C19 owner instructions, red-team specs), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D4 opt-out handling), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.5, 4.4, 4.9, 5.10), Texas AI-voice suit (survey): https://www.mondaq.com/unitedstates/consumer-law/1821332/ai-robocalls-tcpa-and-texas-converge-in-class-action, Total Expert disclosure practice: https://www.totalexpert.com/features/ai-sales-assistant, UWM Mia: https://www.mpamag.com/us/specialty/wholesale/mortgage-giant-launches-ai-powered-loan-officer-assistant-this-has-never-been-done-ever/536038


## Agent Passport: one consent, preference and file-status center for every real estate agent across the network (round 1, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**An agent verifies once and decides which platform MLOs may contact them, about what, and when. A 'no new lenders' setting is honored by every MLO instantly. The agent also sees every borrower-authorized shared file from every platform MLO in one place.**

- **Problem:** Agent prospecting is the plan's growth engine (sections 4.5 and 5.1), but MLOs text agents' cell phones with no provable consent. The TCPA and state mini-TCPAs (Florida, Texas SB 140 and Virginia, per the survey) can apply to business-used cells, and many agents' cells are on the DNC list. Agents are buried in lender spam, so cold outreach performs poorly. RETR's terms bar its data from AI, rules engines and automation, so the plan's automated agent cards rest on data that can't legally drive them.
- **How it works:** DATA: entered by the agent and licensed to the platform for these uses:
- identity
- real estate license, checked by a person against the state commission's public lookup where its terms allow
- markets
- per-MLO, per-purpose consent: file status, listing co-marketing, market notes, intro calls
- quiet hours
- preferred channel
- 'no new lenders'

TRIGGER: the agent claims a passport from the Agent Line (idea 8), from a status text on a shared file, or from a link an MLO sends.

SYSTEM:
- Consent is recorded per named seller (MLO and sponsor), so it stays specific to that seller.
- 'Do not contact' or 'no new lenders' is written to the network-wide hashed stop list (One Stop, extended to agents) and blocks every MLO's send gate.
- Shared-file status from every platform MLO reaches the agent in their preferred format and cadence. Each file is shared only under that borrower's authorization (idea 8).
- Passport fields are first-party data, so they can feed Today cards and plays lawfully. RETR rows stay display-only.

MLO: an agent card shows 'Consented: file status and listing notes, texts after 10 AM', or 'No new lenders: hand-written note only'. An MLO who isn't yet consented gets one request-to-connect, which the agent approves or ignores, and it's never resent.

AGENT: one control panel instead of 40 lenders' spam, and one view of their files across lenders on the platform.
- **Technologies:** Consent ledger (D2 consents, extended to agents) with a seller-specific grant model, D3 send gate and a network-wide hashed stop list, Twilio 10DLC texting and the Agent Line voice, Passkeys for agent sign-in, State real-estate license lookup (manual or terms-permitted), Milestone guardian (plan 5.5) feeding per-agent preferences
- **Why it's new:** Lender CRMs treat agents as prospects to be messaged. RETR, Model Match and MMI sell agent data, and RETR's 2026-09-17 terms bar AI and automated use. Homebot sells lender-sponsored agent seats, which the survey flags as a RESPA 8 risk. The survey found no agent-controlled consent or preference registry across lenders. The closest things are consumer DNC lists. Agents' file-status views today are per-lender portals (Floify or a lender app). None aggregates across independent MLOs, and none is governed by the agent's own consent.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA and state law:
- Consent is written, e-signed and per seller, and the agent can revoke it by any means.
- The agent's stop applies network-wide at once, and the D3 quiet hours and caps apply.

RESPA Section 8 and Reg X 1024.14:
- The passport and file view are free and identical for every agent. They aren't tiered by referrals, carry no LO-paid seat, and have no 'unlock with an MLO' feature.
- The file view shows only status on the agent's own transaction, which the borrower authorized. That's part of the MLO's own service, not a thing of value that defrays the agent's costs. [COUNSEL]

GLBA: file status is shared only under the borrower's revocable authorization, per file.

Antitrust:
- An agent's choice of MLOs is never shown to other MLOs.
- No reciprocity or referral counts are ever computed, as in idea 11's rule.

RETR terms: passport data replaces RETR as the input to automation. RETR stays display-only, a point the survey raised.
- **Moat:** It's a two-sided network. The more MLOs on the platform, the more of an agent's files and lender contacts it covers, so agents adopt it. The more agents hold passports, the more consented and lawful agent channels every MLO gets. A single-company CRM can't offer an agent a cross-lender view.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: (1) The 'request-to-connect' sent to an agent who hasn't consented is a commercial message. As an automated text to a cell number it raises state mini-TCPA risk (for example Florida's FTSA) and DNC issues, since agents' cells are often on the registry. (2) A free cross-lender file-status dashboard paid for by MLO subscriptions is a thing of value to referral sources unless it is strictly limited to borrower-authorized status on the agent's own transactions. It passes as designed, but feature creep, such as CRM or marketing tools for agents, would defray agents' costs (Reg X 1024.14(g)(1)(vi)). (3) Commission license lookups have terms-of-use limits. Redesign: Deliver the request-to-connect only inside the passport app or by one CAN-SPAM-compliant email, never by text or call. Freeze the passport's feature scope by counsel-approved charter to consent controls and borrower-authorized status only. / keep 7/10: Lender CRMs and data vendors (RETR, Model Match, MMI) treat agents as targets. I know of no cross-lender registry that the agent controls. The cold-start risk is high: agents won't come until many MLOs are on the platform. Redesign: Seed it from Agent Line and Partner Room status links, so the agent gets file value before being asked to set preferences. / keep 5/10: Cold-start problem: an agent won't claim a passport for a network of a few MLOs, and the cross-lender file view is only valuable once many of the agent's lenders are on the platform. A network-wide 'no new lenders' stop also cuts members' outreach, which is honest but works against adoption. Single-player value exists: an agent preference link for one MLO gives that MLO lawful, consented channels right away. Redesign: v1: a per-MLO agent preference and consent page (channels, purposes, quiet hours) linked from Agent Line and status texts, with stops written to the One Stop hash list. Add the cross-MLO file view and network identity once there are 25 or more members in one metro.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.3, 4.5, 5.1, 5.5), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 8 Agent Line, One Stop, idea 11 no reciprocity counts), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D2 consent log, D3 gate, D4 cross-LO stop), RETR Terms of Service (2026-09-17): https://retr.app/terms, Homebot agent seats: https://homebot.ai/pricing, Virginia SB 1339: https://www.privacyworld.blog/2026/01/key-changes-to-virginia-telephone-privacy-protection-act-take-effect/, Reg X 1024.14: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1024/subpart-B/section-1024.14 (not opened; host blocked)


## List Fingerprint: privacy-preserving overlap detection that enforces 'no lead is ever sold' (round 1, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**At import, every list is compared with every other member's imports by its blinded fingerprint, without anyone learning a single contact. A 'personal' list that heavily overlaps unrelated MLOs' lists is held as a likely purchased or shared list until a person reviews it.**

- **Problem:** D2 records where a list came from, but only on the MLO's word. Purchased aged-lead files, lead-vendor exports and a former employer's database taken by several departing LOs all look 'personal' to a single-tenant system. One mislabeled list brings its TCPA exposure with it (the survey: 330 TCPA cases in April 2026, Loanstream accused over 272,092 calls). It also brings trade-secret exposure: the poaching suits cited in idea 1.
- **How it works:** DATA: normalized phones and emails from D2's in-browser parser.

SYSTEM:
- The browser blinds each identifier through an oblivious PRF with the platform (RFC 9497), so the server never sees raw identifiers in this step and the client can't run a dictionary attack.
- A MinHash sketch of the blinded set is stored per import.

TRIGGER: every import, plus a re-check when new imports arrive.

CHECK:
- The sketch is compared with sketches from unrelated tenants: a different sponsor and team, with no recorded household or co-borrower link.
- Overlap above a calibrated band marks the import 'likely shared or purchased'. Its contacts default to 'unknown source, on hold', the same lock as D2's prior-employer hold.
- A separate pattern, the same list surfacing in several departing LOs' imports from one sponsor, raises a trade-secret caution that feeds idea 1's attestation flow.
- Nobody learns whose list overlapped or which contacts. Only the overlap band and the count of other imports are shown.

MLO: sees 'This list overlaps 38% with lists imported by 11 unrelated loan officers. That usually means a purchased or shared list. It's on hold. Attest where it came from or remove it.'

BORROWER: is spared calls from bought lists.
- **Technologies:** Oblivious PRF (RFC 9497) in the browser via WebCrypto plus a server OPRF endpoint, MinHash and LSH set-similarity sketches, D2 in-browser CSV parser and the hold and attestation flow (lib/contacts), Neon tables of sketches only, never identifiers, Calibration job for overlap thresholds
- **Why it's new:** From the survey and idea 1: Bonzo's and Total Expert's importers take whatever CSV they're given, BNTouch's switching guides cover field mapping, and none tracks provenance. Lead-buying platforms (ActiveProspect TrustedForm, Jornaya) certify consent for lead sellers and buyers. They exist to make bought leads usable, the opposite goal. I found no CRM that uses cross-customer overlap, with private set techniques, to detect bought or shared lists and enforce a no-purchased-leads rule. Only a platform holding many independent MLOs' imports can.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: the result can only tighten consent. A flagged list loses even 'relationship only' outreach until reviewed. Nothing is ever loosened by a non-match.

GLBA:
- Sketches are derived from identifiers and may still count as nonpublic personal information, so they're kept within the platform as service provider, for fraud and liability prevention (15 U.S.C. 6802(e)(3)), and never shown to other tenants. [COUNSEL]
- Sketches are deleted with the import if the MLO removes the list.

Trade secrets: the system never tells an MLO they may or may not solicit anyone. It records the overlap, the attestation and the lock, as idea 1 does.

No lead is sold: the mechanism exists to prove it, and it reveals no contacts.

Fair lending: overlap only, with no demographic or geographic inputs.

Antitrust: no competitive information is exchanged.
- **Moat:** Detection power scales with the number of imports on the network. With thousands of MLOs, a bought list can hardly enter without being caught, which makes 'no lead is ever sold' verifiable rather than a promise. Competitors with lead-centric business models (aged-lead dialing at Shape and Flair) can't adopt it without hurting themselves.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: It only tightens consent, which is good. GLBA: sketches derived from sponsor A's NPI used to flag sponsor B's import rely on the fraud and liability-prevention exception (6802(e)(3)), which is plausible. But small cohorts can leak: with 2 or 3 other tenants, an overlap band reveals that specific competitors hold these people. False positives will also hit legitimate lists, such as shared agent sphere lists and multi-lender shoppers. That isn't a violation, but it affects the fairness of the hold. Redesign: Show the band only when at least 10 unrelated tenants and a minimum overlap count contribute. Never reveal tenant counts below that threshold. Allow attestation plus spot-check release, and put a sponsor-agreement clause authorizing the cross-tenant fraud use. / keep 7/10: There is an adjacent precedent: email platforms (Mailchimp's Omnivore) use cross-customer signals to catch purchased lists. Doing it privately with OPRF and MinHash across mortgage tenants is new, and only a multi-tenant platform can do it. It is weak until there are many imports. Redesign: Name the Omnivore precedent honestly. Start with a plain server-side salted-HMAC overlap, and upgrade to OPRF only if counsel or security require it. / drop 3/10: Detection power needs thousands of imports from unrelated tenants. At launch scale it finds nothing. False positives are likely for legitimate overlaps: past clients of the same busy agent, families, team members who split, relocations. OPRF (RFC 9497) plus MinHash infrastructure is specialist crypto work for a small team. D2 provenance attestation and holds already block the main abuse. Redesign: Defer to 500 or more members. Now: simple in-tenant heuristics on import (no closing provenance, bulk age-band patterns, lead-vendor column names) that prompt for attestation.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D2 import, prior-employer hold, in-browser parsing), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 1 and poaching-suit sources), Mortgage TCPA surge (survey): https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/tcpa-class-action-targets-loanstream-over-272k-spam-calls ; https://tcpaworld.com/2026/06/04/april-tcpa-filings-off-the-chart-330-tcpa-cases-255-tcpa-class-actions-filed-in-april-2026-up-40-from-2025/, Flair aged-lead dialing: https://www.housingwire.com/articles/west-capital-flair-ai-voice/, RFC 9497 (OPRF): https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9497, GLBA 15 U.S.C. 6802(e): https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/6802 (not opened)


## Choice-First Territories with an antitrust firewall (a precondition for MLO ownership at scale) (round 1, high impact, ~3 weeks)

**Turn 'one originator per service area' into a default routing suggestion set by independent management under a published algorithm, never an exclusion. Bar member-owners from voting on territories, prices or comp, so an MLO-owned network can't become a horizontal market allocation.**

- **Problem:** MLOs owning the platform plus exclusive service areas is the fact pattern behind the Supreme Court's per se condemnations: United States v. Sealy (1967), where the licensees controlled the licensor that allocated their territories, and United States v. Topco (1972), a cooperative of independent grocers with exclusive territories. American Needle (2010) limits the single-entity defense where members are independent competitors. This is from my knowledge; the case pages couldn't be opened this session. The more MLOs own and govern the platform, the more 'one originator per area' looks like competitors dividing markets. Company-owned rivals don't face this, but they can't offer ownership. Solving it is what lets the founder's model scale.
- **How it works:** PRODUCT:
(a) Routing is a default, never an exclusion:
- Every borrower on LoanM8 can see and pick any platform MLO licensed for their property state, using the existing choose-state UI (random order, nothing pre-selected).
- 'Service area' means only where LoanM8 marketing routes by default.
- MLOs remain free to serve any borrower anywhere they're licensed, on or off the platform. The member agreement says so, with no non-competes between members.

(b) Territory decisions:
- Made by platform management, not members, using a published, versioned algorithm whose inputs are objective: borrower demand from routing gaps, licensing, stated capacity and response-time service levels.
- Each change is written to an append-only territory log with its inputs and its algorithm version.

(c) Governance firewall in the D12 ownership ledger:
- Ballots, councils and member communications are checked against reserved topics: territories, seat prices, any member's pricing or comp, lender terms.
- The ledger refuses to create a vote on them and logs the attempt.

(d) Data firewall: no member ever sees another member's pipeline, pricing, comp or which borrowers chose whom. Network features (ideas 1 to 5) share only privacy-preserving aggregates.

(e) An annual antitrust counsel review exported from the logs.

MLO: sees the default routing rules and their own position, and knows they can serve anyone.

BORROWER: always has a real choice. RECRUIT: a clear, lawful explanation of what 'one originator per area' means.
- **Technologies:** lib/routing.ts and the choose-state UI (MloChooser, PickerSheet), Versioned territory algorithm and an append-only territory log in Neon, Reserved-topic validator in the D12 ownership-ledger governance module, Role grants and the data firewall (idea 12 pattern), Exportable antitrust review packet (PDF)
- **Why it's new:** The survey found no MLO-owned platform. Lenders One's members are companies and Altisource manages it. eXp, Real and NEXA are company-controlled, so their territories and recruiting trees raise no cooperative allocation question. Nobody in mortgage has had to design 'member-owned plus routing' to survive Sealy and Topco, because nobody has combined them. The existing ideas doc rejected Lender Pulse over information-sharing antitrust risk, but never examined the territory rule itself under member ownership. That is the gap.
- **Compliance design:** Antitrust (Sherman Act section 1):
- No agreement among members on territories, customers, prices or comp.
- Management decides under objective published criteria, and decisions are logged.
- Borrower choice and members' freedom to serve anyone keep routing a non-exclusive default.
- The DOJ/FTC competitor-collaboration guidelines were withdrawn in December 2024 (WSGR, cited in the ideas doc), so there's no safe-harbor shortcut.
- [COUNSEL: antitrust, plus securities counsel on how governance rights are drafted]

RESPA: routing carries no payment.

Reg Z: territories never touch comp.

Fair lending: the routing algorithm uses no demographic or tract-demographic inputs, and the Fair Lending Commons (idea 2) tests it.

Copy: stays policy language ('one originator per service area'), never scarcity language (check:copy).
- **Moat:** It's the structural enabler that lets ownership scale from 2 MLOs to thousands without the network's main promise turning into its biggest liability. The longer it runs, the stronger the evidence: years of logged, objective territory decisions.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: (1) Antitrust: under Sealy (1967) and American Needle (2010), decisions by an entity controlled by competing members can be concerted action. Barring member votes helps, but if members elect the board or the trust protector, 'management decides' may still be attributed to them. A non-exclusive default for the JV's own traffic likely gets rule-of-reason treatment, which isn't fatal. (2) Missing piece, RESPA 8: the platform receives subscriptions from MLOs and routes consumers to the one default MLO per area. That is the paid-steering pattern the CFPB's 2023 digital comparison-platform advisory opinion targeted. Its 2025 status is unverified, possibly withdrawn, but the statute and state enforcement remain. If default placement is effectively what the subscription buys, the subscription can look like a referral fee. Redesign: Price subscriptions identically whether or not a member holds a default area, with no area premium ever. Let any licensed member opt out of default routing with no price change. Show consumers all licensed members with a neutral explanation and the disclosure that members pay platform fees. Write in governance documents that the territory algorithm is set by independent management and that members cannot instruct it through board elections. / keep 7/10: It isn't a product feature. It is a governance requirement that the ownership thesis depends on. It is new only because nobody else has combined member ownership with routing. Redesign: Treat it as a requirement inside D12 and the routing code, not as a marketed idea. / keep 7/10: Mostly policy, drafting and logging, so the build is small: a territory change log with algorithm version, and a reserved-topic block in the governance ledger. It protects the ownership thesis from a Sherman Act section 1 allocation problem that would otherwise grow with membership. MLOs don't use it weekly, but it removes a structural liability cheaply. The 'published algorithm' needs data (routing gaps, capacity) that is thin at first. Redesign: Ship the member-agreement language (no non-competes between members, borrower choice always) and the append-only territory log now. Start the algorithm as simple published criteria (licensing, stated capacity, response SLA). Get antitrust counsel review before the first equity issuance.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (section 4c routing, choose states, MloChooser), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (sections 2.4 and 4.8), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (Lender Pulse rejection; WSGR on the withdrawn guidelines: https://www.wsgr.com/en/insights/doj-and-ftc-withdraw-antitrust-guidelines-for-competitor-collaborations.html), United States v. Topco Associates, 405 U.S. 596 (1972): https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/405/596/ (fetch failed: host not allowed), United States v. Sealy, 388 U.S. 350 (1967): https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/388/350/ (not opened), American Needle v. NFL, 560 U.S. 183 (2010): https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/560/183/ (not opened), Lenders One (survey): https://stocks.observer-reporter.com/observerreporter/article/gnwcq-2026-3-2-lenders-one-unites-members-and-providers-at-2026-summit-welcomes-new-president


## Liquidity Ladder (round 1, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Every vested unit has a floor (it can be swapped for subscription credits), an annual window to sell it back for cash through an independent tender agent, and a member-to-member transfer path. That turns a paper stake into something an MLO can actually use, without ever tying value to loans.**

- **Problem:** An illiquid unit in a private company is the weakest part of any equity pitch to loan officers. eXp's and Real's stock is liquid only because they are public. reAlpha, One Real and NEXA tie value to staying employed and producing. A private platform's units can sit unsellable for a decade. Rule 144 holding periods, transfer restrictions and the 12(g) holder count also make informal secondary sales dangerous. Recruits from large companies will discount a stake to zero unless there is a credible, compliant path to value that doesn't depend on a sale of the company.
- **How it works:** Data: the D12 ownership ledger (vested units by holder), billing events, an annual independent valuation, and a reserve account. Rung 1, the credit floor (always on): a holder can redeem vested units for subscription credits at a published conversion rate set annually from the independent valuation, applied to the invoice of the payer of record. No cash moves, so the state 'paid only by your sponsor' concern does not arise, and the credit has nothing to do with loan activity. Rung 2, the annual tender window: a fixed share of the prior year's subscription revenue only (never loan, pricing or data revenue; the reward engine's database role cannot read anything else) funds a company repurchase offer run by an outside tender agent. It stays open at least 20 business days, as Regulation 14E is generally read to require for any tender offer. Priority goes first to estates, then to retirement from origination (NMLS license surrendered), then to everyone else pro rata. Rung 3, the member matching board: during the same window, holders who want to sell are matched with accredited members (or, after a Reg A Tier 2 round, any eligible member) under the company's right of first refusal. The D12 cap-table engine checks the holding period, per-buyer limits and the 12(g) count before any match settles through the transfer agent. What the MLO sees: their units, vesting, the current conversion rate to credits, the next window date and their place in the priority order. Units only, never dollar projections, with the statement that units may be worth nothing. What a recruit sees: the plan documents and the published reserve formula, not earnings claims.
- **Technologies:** Append-only ownership ledger (D12) behind the comp-firewall Postgres role, Stripe customer balance and credit notes for the credit floor, Registered transfer agent API for settlement and ROFR records, Outside tender agent or private-market tender platform for the annual window, Annual independent valuation report stored with a KMS-signed hash, Rules engine for Rule 144 holding periods, 12(g) counts and per-investor limits, Batch matching job (pro-rata allocation, priority queue)
- **Why it's new:** In the survey, every stake surveyed relies on a public listing (eXp's AGNT and Real's REAX) or has no stated liquidity at all (NEXA's servicing share, Loan Factory's RSUs, reAlpha's employment-gated RSUs). Lenders One and the CUSOs give institutions, not individuals, a stake. I know of no mortgage-technology vendor that offers a private-company unit both a non-cash floor and a scheduled, subscription-funded buyback. The survey's 'cap-table compliance engine' counts holders and exemptions. This idea is the liquidity mechanism that runs on top of it. The credit floor is the new piece: it gives units a use from the first vesting date and keeps all value inside the subscription relationship. Caveat: web search was exhausted and sec.gov could not be reached in this session, so the Regulation 14E, Rule 144 and Reg A secondary-sale points are from general knowledge and need counsel to verify.
- **Compliance design:** [COUNSEL: securities] Redeeming for credits, the company repurchase and member transfers are each securities transactions. Counsel picks the exemption and the tender procedure (Regulation 14E timing and disclosure; antifraud disclosure of the valuation method). Members must be told the reserve can be zero in a bad year. [COUNSEL: mortgage] Reg Z 1026.36(d): the reserve is funded only from flat subscription revenue, so redemption value can't move with any MLO's loan terms or volume. The comp firewall's CI privilege test also covers the reserve job. Whether redemption proceeds count as 'compensation' or as a return on equity (comment 36(a)-5.vi on bona fide returns) is a counsel question. If it is compensation, the 10% non-deferred profits limit may apply, so the default for active MLOs is the credit floor, with cash windows only where counsel clears them. State 'paid only by sponsor' rules: cash goes only to holders counsel clears per state (for example, retired holders no longer licensed). Active licensees default to credits paid to the payer of record. RESPA 8: agents can't hold units (the cap table only admits licensed MLOs and sponsors), so no value reaches a referral source. check:copy: no 'returns', 'income' or earnings language.
- **Moat:** The reserve grows with total subscription revenue, so each new member makes every existing unit more liquid. That is a network effect a competitor would need years of subscription history to copy. Each completed annual window adds to a track record of honored buybacks, and recruits can check that track record. A private competitor would have to change its capital structure to offer it, and a lender-owned CRM can't offer it at all.
- **Reviews:** keep 5/10: (1) The member matching board, which pairs sellers and buyers of the company's own units, likely makes the platform an unregistered broker-dealer or exchange (Exchange Act 15(a) and 5). An issuer can't run a secondary market for its own securities without a registered intermediary. (2) Units issued to customer MLOs are a securities sale. Rule 701 is unavailable because MLOs aren't employees or consultants, so Reg D, Reg CF or Reg A is needed. 12(g) holder counts bite with thousands of MLOs. (3) The tender window must follow Rule 14e-1, which is correctly noted. (4) For active licensees, cash redemption raises state 'paid only by sponsor' rules. The credit floor avoids that, but credits paid to a sponsor 'payer of record' are value flowing to the employer, which counsel must analyze under 1026.36(d). Sources here are training knowledge; sec.gov was unreachable. Redesign: Run all member-to-member transfers through a registered broker-dealer or ATS (a licensed private-market platform), with the issuer only exercising ROFR and transfer-agent functions. Default active licensees to credits only. Limit cash tenders to counsel-cleared holders, such as retired or unlicensed holders and estates. Choose the offering exemption before any unit vests. / keep 6/10: Stakes in eXp and Real rely on public listings. Private companies run tender offers through Carta and Nasdaq Private Market. Converting units to subscription credits as a floor is new and fits the state rules on who may pay an MLO. The securities complexity is heavy. Redesign: Launch with the credit floor only, and add tender windows after counsel clears them and the reserve has a track record. / keep 5/10: The credit floor is cheap and fits the subscription-and-tenure rule. The cash rungs don't work economically early: a reserve funded by a share of subscription revenue from a few dozen MLOs is tiny, while an independent annual valuation, a tender agent, Regulation 14E procedure and securities counsel cost tens of thousands of dollars a year (estimate). Matching boards add 12(g) and transfer-agent cost. Promising cash windows early also creates investor-relations risk if the reserve is zero. Redesign: v1 is the credit-floor rung only, with a conversion rate from an annual valuation and redemption into the payer-of-record invoice. Write the tender-window and member-transfer rungs into the plan documents as 'may be offered', and activate them only above a revenue threshold counsel and the board set.
- **Sources:** https://www.housingwire.com/articles/nexa-acquires-umortgage-casa/ (NEXA economics tied to staying at NEXA; survey), https://www.quiverquant.com/news/reAlpha+Tech+Corp+Launches+National+Loan+Officer+Recruitment+Program+with+RSU+Incentives (employment-gated RSUs; survey), https://www.barchart.com/story/news/1777802/exp-world-holdings-to-begin-trading-as-agnt-acquires-nexthome-to-launch-unified-platform-for-franchise-and-cloud-brokerage (eXp liquidity comes from its public listing; survey), https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_loan_originator_small_entity_compliance_guide.pdf (dividends Q&A, comment 36(a)-5.vi; survey), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/chapter-II/part-240/subject-group-ECFR4e3b0f2fbd2d1b1/section-240.14e-1 (Regulation 14E 20-business-day rule; canonical URL, NOT opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/chapter-II/part-230/section-230.144 (Rule 144 holding periods; canonical URL, NOT opened), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md idea 12 (platform-months engine and comp firewall this builds on)


## Steward Trust and Member Covenants (round 1, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**A purpose trust holds a golden voting share, so the platform can't be sold, have its data charter weakened, or raise seat prices beyond a published cap unless members vote yes. 'MLO-owned' becomes a promise no future board or acquirer can quietly undo.**

- **Problem:** The thing MLOs fear most about any vendor is that it changes owners. In the survey, Bonzo went to MMI, Surefire to ICE, Maxwell to PLACE, Floify to Porch, MeridianLink to Centerbridge and Structurely to CapStone. Each sale changed incentives on data, pricing and neutrality. An ownership plan that a later majority investor or acquirer can dilute or sell over makes 'the first platform MLOs own' a slogan rather than a structure. Venture-style capital (which the platform may need) normally comes with drag-along rights that would override members.
- **How it works:** Structure (per counsel): the operating company is a public benefit corporation, or an LLC with an equivalent stated purpose. A non-charitable purpose trust (Delaware, Oregon and several other states allow these) holds a small class of golden voting shares. The trust protector is elected by MLO unit holders, one member one vote, with tenure eligibility.
