Separately, a chat or voice agent that gathers the six Reg X/TRID application items (name, income, SSN, property address, estimated value, loan amount) can create an 'application' without anyone noticing. That starts the 3-business-day Loan Estimate clock (1026.19(e)(1)(iii)) and, if the loan doesn't proceed, may bring ECOA adverse-action duties. Licenses also change underneath the product. NMLS renewal runs Nov 1 to Dec 31, which is now. CE can be missed, sponsorships can end, and temporary authority runs out. Yet D3's licensed-states check reads a static list, so after a lapse M8 could keep naming and quoting for an MLO across a state line.
- **How it works:** DATA. The License Clock holds, for each MLO and state: license status, sponsoring company and its start date, renewal attestation, CE completion for the year, temporary-authority start and 120-day end, and any conditions. The sources are the MLO's own entry and a confirmation from the sponsor's NMLS company admin, plus a monthly NMLS Consumer Access lookup of that member's own record, which counsel must confirm fits the 'decide whether to continue doing business with a registered person' permitted use. Bulk monitoring of non-members is never done. config/licensing.ts stays the source for the public facts, and the Clock adds the 'as of now' dimension.

TRIGGER AND CLASSIFICATION. Every M8 turn, tool call, drafted text, ad and Command Center action is classified into a fixed taxonomy: (1) general education, (2) clerical or status, (3) presenting particular terms, (4) application-element collection, (5) negotiation. Deterministic rules come first: showing a get_pricing result is class 3, and asking for income or loan amount is class 4. A server-side classifier with a zod schema handles free text, and the output guard enforces the final decision.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES. Class 3 is allowed only when three things hold. (a) The Clock says the attributable MLO is active in the property state right now. (b) That MLO has signed, with a WebAuthn passkey over the canonical scenario hash from lib/pricing/canonical.ts, an 'authorization to present' for that scenario set and day. (c) The presentation carries the MLO's name and NMLS ID from config. Class 5 is always refused, so M8 never says 'I can do better' and hands off instead. Class 4 runs through an Application Meter that counts the six TRID elements gathered across the whole conversation and every channel. M8 is hard-stopped at five: it never asks for SSN (already redacted), and a sixth element triggers a handoff to the licensed MLO's application path in the POS. If a license lapses, the Clock flips routing (lib/routing.ts) to 'choose' or 'no licensed MLO yet' in that state within minutes. At the same moment it pauses that state's priced pages, ads and send-gate marketing, and puts a renewal card on Today 45, 15 and 3 days before Dec 31.

WHAT THE MLO DOES. Taps one passkey approval each morning for the day's priced scenario set, which can be bundled with the C9 pricing pull, and sees a Clock strip ('OR: renewal attested; CE 6 of 8 hours').

BORROWER. Every quote line reads 'Presented for [MLO], NMLS #…, licensed in [state]'. They can never be pushed into an application by chat.

BROKERAGE. A Licensed-Activity Ledger per conversation, answering the examiner's question 'which licensed individual originated this?'
- **Technologies:** WebAuthn passkeys (signature over scenario hash), lib/pricing/canonical.ts hashing, Server-side classifier with zod-validated output, lib/m8/guard.ts enforcement, lib/routing.ts state flips, D3 send gate licensing check, Neon append-only ledger tables, QStash scheduled Clock checks, NMLS Consumer Access (member's own record only, counsel-cleared)
- **Why it's new:** Today: D3 and most CRMs check licensed states from a static list. Total Expert's AI Sales Assistant identifies itself only 'when required by law or if prompted' (vendor page cited in the survey). Better's Betsy only reports what its pricing engine computed (ElevenLabs case study, vendor-published), which is the closest analog, but it is one lender with salaried staff and has no per-state, per-individual attribution. I found no CRM or AI-voice vendor that sorts AI output into SAFE Act activity classes, ties every terms presentation to a licensed individual's cryptographic sign-off, or counts TRID application elements to stop accidental applications. The renewal-season gating is new compared with Move-In Week in the ideas doc, which covers license transfer, not lapse. Verification note: this session's web-search budget was exhausted and ecfr.gov was outside the allowed network, so the regulation citations come from training knowledge (cutoff June 2026). Counsel must confirm them.
- **Compliance design:** SAFE Act and Reg Z: M8 never negotiates, never takes an application, and presents terms only as the agent of a named licensed MLO who authorized that exact scenario. [COUNSEL: whether a pre-authorized AI presentation satisfies 1026.36(f) and state licensing; the clerical exception's supervision requirement.] TRID: the Application Meter keeps M8 below the six-element definition in 1024.2(b) and 1026.2(a)(3). Rates appear only from a priced result with APR and the not-a-commitment line, as today. NMLS terms: Consumer Access is used only for the member's own record, never for solicitation or bulk monitoring [COUNSEL]. GLBA: Clock data is licensee data, not borrower NPI. Fair lending: the classifier reads utterances, never borrower attributes. Ownership: nothing here touches comp. The passkey step adds no per-loan payment.
- **Moat:** Each new state, sponsor and license type adds verified Clock rules. The activity taxonomy and red-team cases grow with every examiner question members get. Once brokers' examiners accept the Licensed-Activity Ledger as evidence, competitors would need the same per-individual signature chain to sell to those brokers.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Strong control. Risks: (a) whether an AI may 'offer terms' as agent of a licensed MLO under 1026.36(f) and state law is unsettled, and the passkey sign-off helps but isn't a safe harbor; (b) monthly NMLS Consumer Access lookups of members' own records likely fit the 'whether to do business' purpose, but automated retrieval may breach its terms of use; (c) the Application Meter duplicates Application Clock. Redesign: Use sponsor-admin attestation plus manual or terms-approved NMLS checks, with no scraping. Merge the Application Meter into Application Clock's ledger. / keep 8/10: I know of no CRM or AI-voice vendor that sorts AI output by SAFE Act activity class or ties each terms presentation to a passkey sign-off by a licensed individual. This is the strongest compliance moat in the batch, but counsel must confirm the legal theory. Redesign: Absorb Application Clock's element meter and Delegation Desk's activity taxonomy into this one licensed-activity layer. / keep 6/10: The License Clock (lapse means routing and marketing pause, renewal cards) is cheap and avoids a costly licensing breach. The cross-channel six-element meter is worth it if it absorbs Application Clock's ledger. Costly or friction-heavy parts: a free-text activity classifier on every turn, and a daily passkey sign-off that adds MLO friction for little marginal protection beyond 'rates only from a priced result'. Monthly NMLS Consumer Access lookups of members' own records depend on counsel confirming the terms of use. Redesign: v1: the License Clock with sponsor attestation, deterministic class rules (priced result equals presenting terms, application-element questions), and the merged Application Meter. Bundle passkey authorization into the existing daily pricing publish rather than a separate step. Add the free-text classifier later.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (loan originator definition and qualification; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1024/2/ (Reg X 'application' six items; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/19/ (Loan Estimate timing; not opened), https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/policies/NMLS%20Document%20Library/Consumer%20Access%20Terms%20of%20Use.pdf (permitted use; cited in survey), https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/knowledge/Products/nmls/pubs/ugIndState/licensing/individual/topics/licInd_mu4_temporaryAuthority.html (temporary authority; repo-sourced), https://www.totalexpert.com/features/ai-sales-assistant (disclosure 'when required'; survey), https://elevenlabs.io/blog/better (Betsy reports engine results only; vendor; survey), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D3 (static licensed-states check)


## Paired-Tester Lab: nightly matched-pair fair-lending tests of M8 itself (round 1, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**Synthetic borrower pairs that are identical except for one protected-basis signal (a name, Spanish rather than English, public-assistance income, age, a spouse's gender, a disability accommodation request) run through M8 chat, M8 voice and the text drafter every night. Any measurable difference in encouragement, products offered, options priced or handoffs blocks the release. Results are published for each model and prompt version.**

- **Problem:** Reg B 1002.4(b) bars discouraging applicants on a prohibited basis, including through oral statements. An AI that is a little warmer, mentions more options, or offers a live transfer more readily for some personas is discouraging the others, and no scoring-model audit will catch it, because the discrimination is in the conversation, not in a score. Academic work (a 2024 Lehigh University study that ran LLMs on mortgage underwriting vignettes, from memory) reported race-linked differences in LLM recommendations. Regulators and fair-housing groups have long used matched-pair testers to find exactly this in human loan officers. Federal disparate-impact enforcement is paused, but disparate treatment, which is what matched pairs reveal, is not, and state AGs and private plaintiffs remain. The plan's fair-lending check covers scoring models only.
- **How it works:** DATA. A versioned persona library in tests/, never in production. Each pair shares the same scenario: the same loan amount, state, credit tier as the borrower states it, and the same question script. They differ in one signal: a name associated with a race or ethnicity, an English or Spanish conversation, a retiree at 68 against a worker at 34, 'my wife' against 'my husband', public-assistance or Social Security income (a protected basis under ECOA), an accommodation request, or a property in a majority-minority tract against one outside it. Protected signals exist only in test fixtures. Production ranking still never takes them as inputs.

TRIGGER. Nightly, before every promote (alongside tests/m8-redteam-remote.spec.ts), on every model or prompt change, and on every owner-training-note change from /mlo/training.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES. Runs each pair through the chat API, and through the voice loop (TTS persona audio into the /api/m8-listen path, then M8's spoken reply transcribed). It also runs the play drafter and Today ranking with name-swapped contacts, where the assertion is identical rank. It measures options mentioned, priced options shown (which must be byte-identical, because buildPricingRun is deterministic), handoff and call-back offers, the share of turns with encouraging language and with hedging or discouraging language (scored by a separate rubric classifier, itself calibrated on labelled examples), reply length, follow-up questions and time to the first substantive answer. Paired statistical tests use preset thresholds. A significant gap fails the build in the same way check:copy does.

WHAT THE MLO OR OWNER DOES. Reviews a failed pair side by side and fixes the prompt or training note. The fix becomes a permanent regression pair.

BORROWER. Gets a more consistent M8. A public /ai page shows the pass history for each version, with no borrower data.

BROKERAGE. A quarterly fair-lending testing report to file with its compliance management system.
- **Technologies:** tests/m8-harness.ts and the red-team runners, Google TTS and Speech-to-Text loop already in lib/voice, Deterministic lib/pricing/build.ts, Rubric classifier with zod output, Paired statistical tests (McNemar and permutation) in vitest, Vercel preview share links for remote runs, Append-only results store with KMS-signed run reports
- **Why it's new:** The build plan (§2.2) runs a fair-lending check on scoring models. The survey proposes explainable Today scores and HMDA peer benchmarks. Neither tests the conversational agent's treatment of people. Matched-pair testing is the established enforcement and advocacy method for human lending (DOJ, HUD and NCRC testing programs, from training knowledge, not re-verified). I found no mortgage AI-voice or chat vendor (Sela, Flair, Total Expert, UWM Mia, Better Betsy) that publishes paired-test results for its agent. The survey notes that none publishes a consent methodology or independent outcome data. Verification note: search budget exhausted this session; the Lehigh study and the NCRC programs are cited from memory (MED confidence).
- **Compliance design:** ECOA and Reg B: testing for disparate treatment and discouragement on every prohibited basis, including age, marital status and public-assistance income, which race-only audits miss. Protected signals appear only in synthetic test data. Production models never receive them, which keeps the plan's 'no demographic proxies' rule intact. Using tract demographics only in tests fits the plan's 'audit or add, never exclude' rule. GLBA: no real borrower data is used. TCPA: no real calls are placed; the voice loop is internal. AI disclosure: verified as identical across personas, including the Spanish version. [COUNSEL: whether publishing results creates admissions; the Reg B self-testing privilege at 1002.15 may protect self-tests that meet its conditions, so counsel decides what is published and what stays privileged.]
- **Moat:** The persona library compounds. Every member market adds languages, local naming conventions and scenario types, and every incident becomes a permanent pair. A multi-year, version-by-version public record is something a new entrant can't fake. Brokerages can point examiners to it.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: Synthetic personas carry no privacy or TCPA exposure. It tests all ECOA bases, including age, marital status and public-assistance income. The only risk is publishing results. Public failures could be admissions, and publication may waive the Reg B 1002.15 self-test privilege, since that privilege's conditions include corrective action and non-disclosure. Redesign: Publish only pass/fail per version after remediation. Keep detailed results under counsel's privilege protocol. / keep 7/10: Generic LLM bias-testing tools exist (Holistic AI, Credo AI and others), and academic audits of LLM mortgage bias have been published. I know of no mortgage chat or voice vendor that gates releases on paired tests and publishes the results per version. It is cheap to build on the existing red-team harness. Redesign: Build it as an extension of tests/m8-redteam and run it before Fair Lending Commons. Counsel decides what gets published. / keep 8/10: Cheap and immediate: synthetic fixtures run through the existing chat API and harness, and a few hundred pairs a night cost little in model fees. It needs no data licensing and no network scale, and it gives sponsors and examiners concrete evidence. Cost centers: building and calibrating the rubric classifier, and the voice loop (TTS into STT), which adds cost and flakiness. Publishing results publicly needs counsel because of self-test privilege. Redesign: Ship chat-only pairs first, gated before promote like the red-team suite. Add voice pairs monthly rather than nightly. Keep results internal until counsel decides on publication.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/4/ (Reg B discouragement; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/15/ (self-testing privilege; not opened), https://ncrc.org/ (matched-pair lending tests; from memory, not opened), Lehigh University 2024 study on LLM mortgage underwriting bias (from memory; MED confidence, verify), /home/user/RATEM8/tests/m8-redteam.spec.ts and tests/m8-redteam-remote.spec.ts (existing harness), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md §2.2 (fair-lending check covers scoring models only), Market survey: AI voice segment (no vendor publishes consent methodology or independent outcome data), Verification: WebSearch budget exhausted; www.ecfr.gov blocked


## Ad Desk: pre-cleared marketing with rate binding and a targeting guard (round 1, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**Every social post, flyer, email, listing page, video script and paid ad an MLO makes is checked automatically against Reg Z trigger-term rules, the MAP Rule, state license-line rules and fair-housing targeting rules. Any rate in it is bound to a dated priced scenario and expires with that scenario. The sponsor's compliance officer approves from one queue, and every version is archived for the retention period.**

- **Problem:** Brokerage compliance officers review LO social posts and flyers by hand, or don't review them at all. Advertising is a standard state-exam item. The common failures are a rate without APR, a triggering term (payment, down payment, number of payments) without the required disclosures, a missing NMLS ID or state license line, and prohibited misrepresentations under the MAP Rule (Reg N). Fair-lending cases have used a lender's marketing footprint as evidence of redlining (the DOJ/CFPB Trustmark 2021 settlement, from memory). Paid social for credit must run under the platforms' special housing and credit ad rules. Solo MLOs have no tooling for any of this, and an advertised rate typically outlives the market it came from by weeks.
- **How it works:** DATA. A per-state ad-rules table in the same versioned format as D3 (required license lines, NMLS ID placement, Equal Housing statement or logo, state-specific notices), sourced from config/licensing.ts for each MLO's states. The Reg Z 1026.24 trigger-term lexicon and MAP Rule misrepresentation patterns. check:copy's banned phrases. Today's priced snapshots.

TRIGGER. An MLO drafts in Ad Desk (templates, or M8 drafting under the guard) or uploads an outside design. OCR extracts the text from images and PDFs.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES. (1) Finds every rate, payment, down payment and term. Each rate must link to a priced-snapshot ID. Ad Desk then writes the assumptions block (loan amount, LTV, credit tier, points, APR, date) from that snapshot and sets the ad's expiry to the snapshot's freshness window. When it expires, scheduled posts stop and live listing pages fall back to 'see today's example'. (2) Any trigger term automatically requires the 1026.24(d)(2) disclosure set, and the ad is blocked until it's present. (3) Inserts the MLO's and company's name, NMLS ID and state license lines from config, never typed by hand. (4) Paid ads: only through connected ad accounts, where Ad Desk requires the platform's housing or credit special ad category, refuses custom or lookalike audiences built from demographic or tract lists, and records the targeting spec. It compares the geographic footprint with the MLO's licensed service area and flags gaps that exclude majority-minority tracts within it. The flag is only ever acted on by adding coverage. (5) Routes to the sponsor's compliance queue. Low-risk, pre-approved templates with no trigger terms can auto-clear under the sponsor's written policy. (6) Archives the final rendering, rule versions, approver, publish locations and takedown date, immutably, for at least the MAP Rule's 24 months or the state's longer period.

WHAT THE MLO DOES. Writes, taps 'check', fixes the highlighted lines, and publishes to connected channels.

BORROWER. Sees ads whose numbers were true on the stated date and that can't outlive the market.

BROKERAGE. One approval queue for all its LOs and an exportable ad archive for exams.
- **Technologies:** Reg Z trigger-term and MAP Rule rule engine (extends scripts/check-copy.mjs), Priced-snapshot binding via lib/pricing/display.ts and canonical hashes, OCR and vision extraction for uploaded creative, Meta Marketing API, Google Ads API, LinkedIn and Google Business Profile posting APIs, Versioned per-state rules table (D3 format), Census tract boundary data (audit-only), Immutable object storage archive with hash chain, Sponsor compliance approval workflow
- **Why it's new:** Total Expert advertises marketing compliance approval workflows for enterprise lenders (vendor claim, not re-verified). Bank-side archiving vendors (Smarsh, Hearsay, from training knowledge) store and supervise social content but don't compute Reg Z disclosures from a priced engine. The survey's openings cover a rules changelog and an AI governance binder, not advertising. I found nothing for small brokers that binds an advertised rate to a dated priced scenario, expires it automatically and generates the trigger-term disclosures from the same record. The repo's check:copy and 'no rate outside a priced result' rule give LoanM8 a working start. Verification note: search budget exhausted; the regulatory details (1026.24, Reg N retention, platform ad-category rules) are from training knowledge and need confirming.
- **Compliance design:** Reg Z 1026.24 and the MAP Rule (12 CFR 1014): disclosures are generated, never typed, and a rate only comes from a priced result with APR. Fair lending: demographics are used only to audit footprint gaps and add outreach, never to exclude. Paid ads require the housing or credit special ad category. State law: license lines come from config. check:copy runs on every ad (no 'best rates', no urgency, no testimonials unless counsel clears each one). RESPA 8: co-branded listing ads must carry a co-marketing ledger entry with the cost split before they publish (plan §2.3). Retention meets the longer of federal and state periods. Ownership: no ad metric feeds any reward. [COUNSEL: state ad-rule table, auto-clear policy, testimonial and review rules.]
- **Moat:** The pre-approved template library grows with every sponsor approval, sharing templates and never data, so new members start with ads that have already been approved somewhere. The rules table gets better with every exam finding a member reports.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: It directly implements 1026.24 triggering-term disclosures, Reg N (MAP) 24-month retention, state license-line rules and housing and credit special-ad-category use (the HUD v. Facebook settlement lineage). Meta's credit category naming and rules have changed and must be checked. Auto-clear of templates must remain the sponsor's written policy. No violation found. Redesign: Re-verify each ad platform's special-category rules quarterly in the rules table. Retain for the longer of 24 months or state requirements. / drop 5/10: Marketing compliance review is shipped: Total Expert's approval workflows and rate flyers with live pricing, Hearsay and Smarsh for archiving, Red Oak in financial services. Binding each rate to a dated priced result with auto-expiry is the one new twist, and it is small. Redesign: Fold the rate binding and expiry into Quote Ledger as one priced-result binding service. Add ad archiving through the sponsor's existing vendor. / keep 6/10: Sponsors must review MLO ads, and rate binding with auto-expiry is a real differentiator built on existing pieces (the priced-snapshot store, check:copy, config license lines). Costly parts: paid-ad account connections (Meta and Google API approvals, special ad category handling already enforced by the platforms) and OCR on outside designs. Weekly use is likely for MLOs who post. Enterprise competitors exist (Total Expert's compliance workflows; vendor claim). Redesign: v1: text and template ads with trigger-term detection, generated 1026.24 disclosure blocks, license lines from config, snapshot-bound expiry, a sponsor approval queue and an archive. Defer paid-ad account connections and footprint audits.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/24/ (advertising and trigger terms; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1014/ (MAP Rule, Reg N; not opened), https://www.facebook.com/business/help/298000447747885 (Meta special ad categories; from memory, not opened), https://www.justice.gov/opa/pr/justice-department-and-consumer-financial-protection-bureau-secure-agreement-trustmark (Trustmark redlining settlement 2021; from memory, not opened), https://www.totalexpert.com/ (marketing compliance workflow; vendor claim), /home/user/RATEM8/scripts/check-copy.mjs (existing banned-phrase gate), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md §2 and §4b (no rates without a priced result), Verification: WebSearch budget exhausted; www.ecfr.gov blocked


## Complaint Radar: complaints found the day they happen, with SLAs and a legal hold (round 1, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**A classifier reads every inbound text, email, M8 chat and call transcript, voicemail and review for complaints, including the 'how did you get my number' and 'I told you to stop' kind. It opens a case in the sponsor's complaint register with an SLA clock, attaches the consent and provenance proof automatically, places a legal hold so retention jobs can't delete evidence, and pauses the play that caused it.**

- **Problem:** Most TCPA suits and regulator complaints start as an irritated reply that sits in an inbox. The 2026 mortgage TCPA wave counted in the survey (11 complaints in about two months, then nine more, per NMN) is a sign of how many there are. Brokerages, as the licensees, are expected to keep a complaint log and handle complaints, and exam request lists commonly ask for it (from training knowledge). CRMs treat these messages as ordinary conversations. Worse, LoanM8 deletes on schedule: redacted transcripts are kept 3 years, there's 'Delete my chat', and imported CSVs are deleted. Without a hold mechanism, a routine retention job could destroy evidence after a dispute begins. Destroying evidence after litigation is reasonably anticipated is spoliation.
- **How it works:** DATA. All inbound content already flowing through D4 (/api/twilio/inbound), M8 chat transcripts, voice transcripts (D9), email replies, and the MLO's own Google Business Profile reviews (owner-connected API).

TRIGGER. Each item is scored by a complaint classifier. It uses deterministic patterns ('how did you get my number', 'I said stop', 'lawyer', 'attorney general', 'CFPB', 'report you', 'discriminat') plus a model classifier with a zod schema for expressions of dissatisfaction, labelled by the CFPB product and issue taxonomy.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES. Opens a case: channel, quote, category, contact, the touches involved, and the MLO and sponsor compliance owner (both notified). It starts the SLA timers set by the sponsor (acknowledge, respond, close). Then it handles specific cases. (a) Provenance questions: attaches the D2 source tag, consent record and proof, DNC scrub date and gate decision for every touch to that number, so the MLO can answer truthfully in minutes. (b) 'I said stop': searches for any send after the earliest plausible revocation and, if one is found, raises a priority incident. (c) Discrimination allegations: routed at priority to the sponsor's compliance officer as a fair-lending matter. (d) Attorney or regulator mentions: places a LEGAL HOLD that suspends every retention and deletion job ('Delete my chat', transcript expiry, Book Deed export-and-delete) for that contact's records only, logged, until counsel lifts it. A borrower's deletion request on a held record gets a lawful explanation and is honored when the hold lifts. (e) Pattern alarm: if complaints for one play version cross a threshold, that play is paused for the MLO, and across all tenants if the cause is a shared template. (f) Prepares a draft response and evidence bundle, for a CFPB portal response if the sponsor has a company portal account, or for a state regulator's inquiry, which then hands off to the survey's demand-letter packet if one arrives.

WHAT THE MLO DOES. Gets a red Today card: 'Dana asked how you got her number. You imported her from personal contacts on 11-19; here is her consent record. Call her personally?'

BORROWER. Gets a fast human answer, not a drip.

BROKERAGE. A live complaint register across all its LOs, with root causes and SLA compliance, exportable for exams.
- **Technologies:** Complaint classifier (patterns plus model with zod output) on the D4 inbound path, CFPB complaint product and issue taxonomy, Neon case tables with RLS by sponsor tenant, Legal-hold flag enforced in every retention and deletion job, QStash SLA timers, Resend and Twilio notifications to the sponsor compliance owner, Google Business Profile reviews API, Play pause hooks (D7)
- **Why it's new:** Bank-side complaint-management software exists (for example Ncontracts' complaint management, from training knowledge). It serves bank compliance staff, it is fed by hand, and it isn't wired to a consent ledger or a send gate. Mortgage CRMs (Bonzo, Shape, BNTouch, Jungo) show replies as inbox threads. The survey's openings include a TCPA demand-letter responder and a litigation packet, which start after a demand letter arrives. Complaint Radar acts days or weeks earlier, at the first irritated reply. It adds the SLA register brokerages are examined on, play pausing, and a legal hold that reconciles LoanM8's deletion promises with evidence preservation, which nothing in the repo or plan does. Verification note: search budget exhausted; the NMN counts are from the survey, and the state complaint-log expectations are training knowledge (MED).
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: provenance answers come from real proof. 'I said stop' searches enforce immediate revocation. Pattern pauses stop repeat harm. Fair lending: discrimination allegations get priority routing, and the classifier uses no borrower demographics. GLBA: cases are sponsor-tenant scoped under RLS, and every read is audited. Retention: legal hold overrides deletion only for named records, with counsel's approval to lift it; deletion requests are answered lawfully. State privacy: deletion-right exceptions for legal claims vary by law [COUNSEL]. UDAAP: borrowers get a truthful, prompt answer. Ownership: complaint metrics never reduce or increase an MLO's units; units stay tied to subscription and tenure only. [COUNSEL: SLA defaults, state complaint-log rules, what goes into a CFPB response.]
- **Moat:** The classifier gets better with every labelled case across the network. Template-level complaint signals pooled across tenants, never contact data, catch a bad play before it reaches the next MLO. A brokerage that keeps several years of registers and root causes on the platform has an exam history it won't want to move.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: It is compliant. A legal hold that overrides deletion fits the CCPA's legal-claims exception (1798.105(d)) and similar state carve-outs. Automated reading of all inbound content is within the sponsor's service-provider scope. Review ingestion must follow Google Business Profile API terms. Discrimination routing is good. Redesign: Place holds only by counsel or a compliance officer, with expiry review. Send the borrower a lawful-retention notice when a held deletion request arrives. / drop 5/10: Complaint detection in calls and messages is shipped by contact-center analytics (CallMiner, NICE, Verint), and complaint registers by Ncontracts and others. The new part is wiring it to consent proof, pausing plays and placing a legal hold, which is valuable but is a D4 feature. Redesign: Build it as D4 inbound rules (complaint patterns lead to a case, the proof bundle, a play pause and a legal hold) rather than as a standalone product. / keep 7/10: Mostly cheap: deterministic patterns plus a small classifier on inbound content that already flows through D4 and the chat, a case table, timers and a legal-hold flag that the deletion jobs respect. State regulators expect a complaint log, and early detection of 'how did you get my number' heads off TCPA demands. Google Business Profile review ingestion adds API work and can wait. The automated CFPB-response drafting has low frequency. Redesign: v1: classifier, case register, SLA timers, provenance packet, revocation-miss search, legal hold and play pause. Add review ingestion and regulator-response drafting later.
- **Sources:** https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/mortgage-tcpa-lawsuits-surge-with-11-new-cases (survey), https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/tcpa-lawsuits-hit-nine-more-mortgage-lenders (survey), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/complaint/ (CFPB complaint process and taxonomy; not opened this session), https://www.ncontracts.com/ (bank complaint management; from memory, not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D2 (provenance tags), D4 (inbound path and cc_optouts), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md §1 (redacted transcripts kept 3 years, 'Delete my chat'), Verification: WebSearch budget exhausted; primary regulator hosts blocked


## Proof-Priced Coverage: a member purchasing group for TCPA, E&O and cyber insurance priced on verified controls (round 1, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Member brokerages and MLOs form a federal Liability Risk Retention Act purchasing group, and later possibly a member-owned risk retention group. They buy TCPA, E&O and cyber liability coverage together, underwritten on a standard, member-consented feed of the platform's control evidence instead of a questionnaire. The platform takes no commission.**

- **Problem:** TCPA class exposure is rising: 330 TCPA cases and 255 class actions were filed in April 2026 alone (Webrecon via TCPAWorld, cited in the survey), alongside AI-voice suits against mortgage lenders. Many cyber and E&O policies exclude TCPA claims or sublimit them (from training knowledge). Solo MLOs and small brokerages buy coverage on self-reported questionnaires, so a careful broker pays the same as a reckless one and often can't get TCPA cover at all. Large lenders self-insure or negotiate. Small originators can't.
- **How it works:** DATA. A controls feed computed from the platform's own logs, at tenant level and aggregate only: share of marketing touches with written consent on file, sends after revocation (target 0), DNC scrub freshness, Reassigned Numbers Database check coverage, quiet-hours blocks, AI-disclosure receipt rate, complaint rate and SLA compliance (from Complaint Radar), guard incidents, Paired-Tester Lab pass rate, Safeguards items (MFA, encryption, incident runbook tested). No borrower NPI ever leaves.

TRIGGER. A member opts in and signs a data-sharing consent naming the carrier and broker. The feed refreshes monthly and at renewal.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES. Produces a signed controls attestation (KMS-signed, hash-chained to the underlying logs so an auditor can sample them). It also shows the member a controls scorecard with the exact gaps that would change their terms ('7 sends in Q3 without a fresh DNC scrub').

WHAT THE BUSINESS DOES. Counsel and a licensed insurance broker form the purchasing group (registered with states under the LRRA). The broker places a group program with carriers who agree to price on the attested controls. At scale, members may form a risk retention group: a member-owned liability insurer, capitalized separately and governed by its own documents.

WHAT THE MLO OR BROKERAGE SEES. Quotes and a renewal checklist driven by their own controls. Claims-ready evidence comes from the same logs if a suit arrives.

BORROWER. Nothing directly. Indirectly, every member is pushed toward stricter consent behaviour.
- **Technologies:** Aggregated controls metrics from send gate, D4, Complaint Radar and Paired-Tester Lab, KMS-signed attestations with hash-chained log references, Member consent and data-sharing agreements (e-signature), Carrier and broker API or secure file delivery, Neon with a read-only metrics role (the D12 comp-firewall pattern)
- **Why it's new:** Security-posture-priced cyber insurance exists in tech (insurers such as Coalition or At-Bay price partly on scanned controls, and compliance platforms partner with insurers, all from training knowledge, not re-verified). In mortgage, I found no CRM or AI-voice vendor that turns its compliance logs into insurance terms. Sela, Flair, Shape and Bonzo, the high-volume dialing tools in the survey, have the opposite incentive. Only a network with many small originators can reach the scale an LRRA purchasing group needs. Ownership by MLOs makes a later risk retention group, which is itself member-owned, a natural fit rather than a profit centre for a vendor. Verification note: LRRA mechanics and TCPA exclusion practice are training knowledge (MED). Search budget exhausted.
- **Compliance design:** RESPA 8 does not apply: this is the originator's own business liability insurance, not borrower hazard insurance or any other settlement service, and no borrower is steered. Reg Z comp: no loan factor; a discount depends only on controls, never on loans or terms. Insurance licensing: the platform takes no commission or fee. If any value were ever received it would need a producer license, so it is designed out [COUNSEL]. A licensed broker places the coverage. The LRRA covers commercial liability only, and state registration applies [COUNSEL]. GLBA: aggregate metrics only, no NPI, member consent first. The ownership reward engine is separate. Any risk-retention-group equity is a distinct, counsel-structured instrument and is never mixed with subscription-tenure units [COUNSEL: securities status of RRG membership]. Antitrust: group purchasing of insurance by unrelated businesses is the LRRA's stated purpose, but counsel checks information exchange.
- **Moat:** Underwriting data gets better with each member-year of attested controls and loss history, and only this network has that history linked to the logs. As premiums fall for clean members, staying on the platform pays off and doesn't depend on lock-in. A member-owned risk retention group would be a second ownership asset competitors cannot copy without changing who owns them.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: Under the LRRA, purchasing groups can only buy liability coverage and must register in each state, and a risk retention group is a separately capitalized insurer whose membership interests may be securities. The platform must avoid any act that counts as soliciting or negotiating insurance. Sharing controls metrics among competitors to set group pricing is generally fine under McCarran-Ferguson and LRRA purposes, but needs a counsel check. TCPA statutory damages are often excluded or uninsurable in some states, so the copy must not imply coverage. It duplicates Evidence-Priced Coverage. Redesign: Keep only this version. The platform generates attestations, and the licensed broker and purchasing group, as separate entities, solicit, quote and place. Show no premiums in the platform, and put a counsel opinion on RRG equity before any formation. / keep 5/10: Control-priced cyber insurance exists (Coalition, At-Bay, Vanta and Drata insurer partnerships). A mortgage-specific LRRA purchasing group priced on consent telemetry is new but depends on scale and on insurers willing to take part. Redesign: Absorb Evidence-Priced Coverage. Start with a signed controls attestation export that a member can hand to any broker, and form the group later. / drop 3/10: A purchasing group under the LRRA needs enough members and premium to interest a carrier, and TCPA is often excluded or sublimited (training knowledge, not verified). A risk retention group needs capital and years of loss data. Neither happens at launch scale. The useful near-term piece is a signed controls attestation that answers sponsor vendor-diligence and cyber questionnaires. Redesign: Fold a signed controls-attestation export into the Compliance Center (consent coverage, revocation latency, DNC freshness, MFA and encryption). Revisit a purchasing group with a licensed broker at 300 or more members.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/chapter-65 (Liability Risk Retention Act, 15 U.S.C. 3901 et seq.; not opened this session), https://tcpaworld.com/2026/06/04/april-tcpa-filings-off-the-chart-330-tcpa-cases-255-tcpa-class-actions-filed-in-april-2026-up-40-from-2025/ (survey), https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/ai-marketing-calls-spur-suit-against-lender (survey), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1024/subpart-A/section-1024.2 (settlement service definition; survey, not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md idea 12 (comp firewall role pattern), Verification: WebSearch budget exhausted; insurance-market claims are training knowledge


## Exam Room: scoped live examiner access with statistical sampling and finding-to-recall remediation (round 1, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**A time-boxed, read-only room for a state examiner, the sponsor's internal auditor or a wholesale lender's QC reviewer. It is scoped to the items on the exam request letter. The examiner picks the sample seed. Every sampled decision replays with the exact rule version and evidence. Each finding becomes a 'recall' query that finds every affected record across the sponsor and tracks the fix.**

- **Problem:** State exams and lender audits of brokers run on request lists: marketing, telemarketing and consent records, complaint logs, LO comp plans, vendor oversight, AI use. Small brokers answer by exporting spreadsheets and screenshots for weeks. The examiner can't independently sample, so trust is low and follow-up requests multiply. When a finding lands ('this template lacked a disclosure'), nobody can quickly tell how many borrowers it touched, so remediation is guesswork. Exports such as the survey's governance binder, security packet and litigation packet are static. They don't let the examiner choose the sample, and they don't close the loop on findings.
- **How it works:** DATA. Everything already logged: the consent register and proofs (D2), send-gate decisions with rule versions (D3), messages and calls (D4 and D9), the Licensed-Activity Ledger, the Ad Desk archive, the Complaint Radar register, Paired-Tester reports, comp-firewall attestations, and the data map.

TRIGGER. The sponsor's compliance officer creates an engagement: examiner identity (passkey or verified email plus the agency domain), agency, date window, request-list items mapped to data scopes, and an expiry.

WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES. (1) Gives the examiner a scoped room: only the mapped items, for that sponsor tenant, for that window. Borrower NPI is masked by default and unmasked per record with a logged reason. Pages are watermarked with the examiner's name and time. (2) Sampling: the examiner enters a seed and sample size, and the system draws a reproducible random sample of contacts, touches or ads, stratified by state or play if asked. The sponsor can't pre-pick it. (3) Replay: for any sampled touch, it shows the gate inputs as they were, the rule version in force that day, the consent proof, the DNC check, quiet-hours math, the disclosure receipt and the licensed MLO. (4) Findings: the examiner or auditor logs a finding against records. The system turns its root cause (a template ID, a rule version, a play, an MLO, a date range) into a recall query. The query counts every affected record across the sponsor, pauses whatever is still live, and opens a remediation tracker with owners and due dates. Corrective outreach, if counsel orders it, still runs through the send gate. (5) Every examiner action is audit-logged. On close, the room locks and a signed engagement record is kept. An export bundle can also be produced for upload where the agency uses its own system (such as the CSBS State Examination System, from training knowledge).

WHAT THE MLO SEES. Only findings and remediation tasks assigned to them.

BORROWER. Nothing, unless counsel orders corrective notice.

BROKERAGE. Exams in days instead of weeks, and provable remediation.
- **Technologies:** Engagement-scoped RBAC on Neon RLS (examiner role, time-boxed), Passkey or verified-domain examiner sign-in (extends D1), Seeded reproducible sampling (stratified), Point-in-time replay from append-only logs and versioned rules, PDF and page watermarking, Recall query builder over template, rule, play and date keys, Remediation tracker with QStash due-date reminders, KMS-signed engagement records
- **Why it's new:** The survey's openings (AI governance binder, security-review packet, litigation evidence packet, rules changelog) are exports. Exam Room adds three things they don't have. The examiner controls the sample, so the result is credible. Any decision can be replayed exactly as it was made. Findings become recall queries with tracked remediation. Bank GRC suites have exam-management modules for banks (from training knowledge), but none is wired to a mortgage send gate, consent proofs and per-MLO licensing. I found no broker CRM that gives examiners scoped live access. Verification note: search budget exhausted; GLBA exception and CSBS SES details are training knowledge.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA: disclosure to the sponsor's examiners and auditors falls under the regulatory and examination exceptions (15 U.S.C. 6802(e)(8) and similar, counsel to confirm). Access is scoped, masked by default, logged and watermarked. Safeguards Rule: examiner access is a documented, time-limited access control. TCPA and state law: replay shows the rule version as it stood. Corrective outreach goes only through the send gate. Fair lending: Paired-Tester reports and footprint audits are viewable in scope, and privileged self-tests stay outside unless counsel releases them. Ownership: examiners may verify the comp firewall's attestation and its subscription-only basis. No reward data reaches loan tables, or the reverse. [COUNSEL: which items are produced live and which by export; privilege handling.]
- **Moat:** Once regulators and wholesale QC teams have run exams in the room, brokers on the platform face shorter, cheaper exams, which is a concrete reason for a brokerage to stay. Every request-list mapping and recall pattern members create is reused by the next member's exam.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Disclosure to examiners fits GLBA 6802(e)(8). Wholesale lender QC reviewers are not regulators. Their access must be limited to loans that lender funded, under the broker agreement or 6802(e)(1), not the broker's CRM book. Privileged self-tests must be excluded by default. Redesign: Add an engagement type for lender QC that is hard-scoped to that lender's own loan IDs. Exclude Paired-Tester and Fair Lending Commons privileged outputs unless counsel releases them. / keep 6/10: Auditor portals and GRC exam modules exist (Vanta and Drata auditor access, Ncontracts). Seeds chosen by the examiner, exact replay of gate decisions and recall queries are a new combination in mortgage. It is unproven whether state examiners will use live rooms. Redesign: Start with signed export bundles plus recall queries. Add live rooms once one regulator or wholesale QC team pilots them. / drop 3/10: State exams of a broker come every few years, and examiners work in their own systems (the CSBS State Examination System, recalled) from requested exports. They are unlikely to log into a vendor's live room. Watermarked scoped access, unmask logging and recall queries are a large build for a rare event. The recall-query idea is useful internally. Redesign: Build an exam export bundle in the Compliance Center: request-list mapping, a reproducible seeded sample the sponsor can hand the examiner, decision replay in the export, and an internal 'find all affected records' query for remediation.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/6802 (GLBA exceptions incl. (e)(8); not opened this session), https://www.csbs.org/ (CSBS and State Examination System; from memory, not opened), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (FTC Safeguards Rule; survey, not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md §4.9 (Compliance Center exports), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D1 to D4 (audit log, consent log, gate decisions, opt-outs), Market survey: regulatory horizon segment (rule versions on every decision; governance binder) and LOS segment (security-review packet), Verification: WebSearch budget exhausted; www.ecfr.gov blocked


## Lender Mail Room (round 1, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Each MLO forwards the notification emails their wholesale lenders already send (submitted, conditions, suspense, CTC, docs out, funded, bulletins) to a private address. Command Center checks the sender, parses each email into pipeline events and tasks, and the broker's pipeline reflects the lender's actual status without anyone re-keying it.**

- **Problem:** A brokered loan's real status lives in the lender's portal (UWM, Rocket Pro TPO, Kind, Pennymac TPO and the rest of the 11 first-mortgage lenders in lib/pricing/lenders.ts). The lender announces status by email, and the MLO or processor copies the conditions and milestones into the LOS and the CRM by hand. The integration routes have holes. ARIVE's Zapier triggers fire only on the user's own Primary-LO loans and need a Pro seat. Encompass says its webhooks are not guaranteed to be real time, and it deletes subscriptions it can't deliver to. Floify has no Zapier app. So the 'milestone guardian' (plan 5.5) and the D5 Today screen can be days behind the lender's own record, and the borrower calls to ask.
- **How it works:** DATA: each loan officer gets a per-tenant inbound address (inbound-parse vendor TBD) and sets one mail filter for the lender notification domains listed in config. TRIGGER: an email arrives. SYSTEM: (1) Authentication: DKIM must pass and align with that lender's domain in the config table, plus a DMARC pass. Anything else goes to quarantine and is never parsed, because spoofed 'clear to close' or 'wire update' emails are both fraud and prompt injection. (2) The lib/m8 redaction runs. (3) A versioned parser template for that lender, with fixture tests per lender, extracts the lender loan number, borrower surname, property street, event type, the condition list (ID, text, category, status) and the dates. For unknown formats, an AI fallback extracts into a zod schema, and every field must quote a substring of the email or it is dropped. (4) It matches to a loan inside the tenant by lender loan number, then by surname plus address. A low-confidence match goes to a one-tap 'which file?' card and is never guessed. (5) It writes loan_milestones and loan_conditions with source 'lender email', the message hash and the quote, then deletes the raw email. The original stays in the MLO's mailbox as the record. (6) Rule: an email event never sends anything by itself. It can only create Today cards or tasks, and any message still goes through the D3 gate. A new event also voids pending drafts about that file that were written from older status, and they are regenerated. (7) Bulletins (product, overlay or guideline changes) are matched against the open pipeline and pre-approvals by program and product fields: '2 of your files are on the product this bulletin changes; effective date X.' MLO: sees a 'Lender says' line on each file. Conditions split into borrower document requests, written in plain language for the MLO to approve, and processor tasks. A 'lender status vs LOS status' mismatch card shows the gap. BORROWER: gets an accurate, earlier status (through 5.5, approved by the MLO) and a plain-English list of what's still needed, not underwriter shorthand. AGENT: gets the weekly file summary (5.5), now based on the lender's own events.
- **Technologies:** Inbound email parsing (vendor TBD) with DKIM/SPF/DMARC verification, Per-lender versioned parser templates with fixture tests, Server-side AI extraction with zod schemas and quote anchoring, lib/m8 redaction, Neon loan_milestones / loan_conditions tables with row-level security, Durable job queue (QStash or Vercel Queues), D3 send gate and D5 Today, Gmail or Microsoft Graph connectors as a later option
- **Why it's new:** CRM pipeline data today comes from the LOS: Bonzo through hosted LOS integrations at $25/month (Bonzo help center, survey), Shape, MloFlo and Total Expert through RETR and LOS links, and ARIVE through Zapier, which is limited to the Primary LO's own loans. When the LOS lags the lender, the CRM lags too. Encompass even documents that its webhooks are not real time. Lender emails are the one channel every wholesale lender already sends to every broker, with no partner agreement needed. The prior research found no broker CRM that parses wholesale-lender notifications, checks the sender, and turns them into conditions and milestones. This session could not re-check that claim: the web search budget was used up and ecfr.gov was blocked. Treat 'nobody does this' as unverified and ask the major CRMs before marketing it.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA: lender emails contain borrower NPI. Command Center acts as the sponsor's service provider. Raw emails are deleted after extraction, only the extracted fields plus the hash and source quote are kept, every read is audited, and the mailbox stays the system of record for the sponsor's record keeping [COUNSEL: retention]. Emails are data, never instructions: the M8 rule applies, and the extractor has no tools. Fraud: an unauthenticated sender is quarantined. Any email that mentions wiring or changes payment instructions is never parsed into tasks, and it raises a 'verify by a known phone number' warning. Wholesale broker agreements may treat lender bulletins and pricing as confidential, so extracted data never leaves the tenant and is never pooled, ranked or compared across companies. That avoids the antitrust and confidentiality problem behind the rejected 'Lender Pulse'. Only parser code (formats, not content) is shared. TCPA: events create cards, and the D3 gate still decides every send. RESPA: no value moves. Fair lending: the same automation for every file. If a Gmail or Microsoft connector is added later, Google classes full mailbox read access as a restricted scope that needs a security assessment (training knowledge, MED). That's why forwarding by filter comes first: it sends only the lender mail.
- **Moat:** Every new lender format one member maps becomes a tested template for all members (formats only, never data), so coverage of wholesale lenders compounds with each MLO who joins. An enterprise CRM tied to one LOS (Surefire to Encompass) has no reason to build this for brokers. An MLO-owned network does: its members are the brokers who receive these emails.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: It is compliant under the sponsor's Safeguards program, with DKIM and DMARC quarantine. Risks: wholesale broker agreements may make pricing and bulletins confidential, and the design keeps them in-tenant. Record retention means raw emails can't be deleted from the MLO mailbox, which is the system of record, and that is handled. A future mailbox-scope connector triggers Google restricted-scope assessment. Redesign: Keep parser templates format-only, with no extracted values in shared fixtures. Have counsel confirm broker-agreement confidentiality per lender before bulletin matching is enabled. / keep 7/10: Generic email-parsing tools (Parseur, Zapier Email Parser) let brokers do this themselves. I know of no broker CRM that ships authenticated, lender-specific parsers that turn lender emails into conditions, but this is unverified. It is cheap, needs no partner agreement, and fills the LOS-lag gap. Redesign: Build early. Start with the 3–4 lenders the sponsor uses most, with fixture tests and the DKIM/DMARC quarantine. / keep 7/10: High weekly use and real time savings: every wholesale lender emails brokers, and forwarding by filter needs no partner agreement. Inbound-parse services are cheap, and DKIM/DMARC checks are standard. The economics risk is template upkeep: lender formats change, and some notifications only say 'log in to view conditions', which yields an event but no condition list. Expect a parser maintenance tax per lender. Wholesale agreements may restrict how lender communications are used, which needs checking. Redesign: Start with the 3 to 5 lenders the founding members use most, with fixture tests. Accept 'event only' extraction as a success. Use the quoted-substring AI fallback for the rest. Keep the per-tenant, no-pooling rule and the quarantine for unauthenticated or wire-related mail.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D3 send gate, D4 inbound handling, D5 Today), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md §4.6, §5.5, §6, /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (Lender Pulse rejected on antitrust), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work- (Primary-LO scope, per survey snippet), https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/changelog/222-major-release (webhooks not guaranteed real-time, per survey), https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/reference/webhook (subscription auto-deletion, per survey), https://zapier.com/apps/floify/integrations (no official Floify Zapier app, per survey), https://support.getbonzo.com/hc/en-us/articles/21542576428946-Integrations-LOS (Bonzo $25/mo hosted LOS integration, per survey)


## Deal Clock (round 1, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**One timeline per file that brings together the purchase contract's contingency deadlines, the lender's and LOS milestones, the TRID and ECOA timing rules, lock expiry and document age-outs. It works out the earliest closing the rules allow and flags a collision days before it happens, with a one-tap fix.**

- **Problem:** The deadlines that cost borrowers their earnest money or their lock are spread across four places. The financing and appraisal contingency dates sit in the purchase contract, which the agent's transaction-coordinator app tracks. Milestones sit in the LOS and the lender portal. The disclosure waiting periods are Reg Z rules. Credit reports, pay stubs and asset statements expire under investor rules. Nobody reconciles all of these for the broker MLO, so a 'Tuesday closing' with no CD issued, or a loan contingency that ends before the projected CTC, turns up at the last minute. That's when the MLO loses the most hours, with phone calls, extensions and relocks.
- **How it works:** DATA: (a) the purchase agreement and addenda. The borrower or their agent uploads them through the C20/Second Look upload path, under the borrower's logged authorization. They are parsed into a zod schema of deadlines (loan or financing contingency, appraisal contingency, inspection, closing or possession, seller-credit terms), every field quote-anchored, and the file is deleted after extraction. (b) LOS key dates and milestones (ARIVE Zapier 'loan date' and tracker triggers, Encompass milestones) and Lender Mail Room events. (c) The lock and its expiry. (d) An effective-dated rules table, built like the D3 state rules, with a citation and counsel status on each row. It covers: initial LE within 3 business days of application; LE received at least 7 business days before consummation; a revised LE within 3 business days of a changed circumstance or a lock; CD received at least 3 business days before consummation; the CD changes that restart the wait (APR past tolerance, product change, prepayment penalty added); rescission on a principal-dwelling refinance where it applies; the appraisal copy under ECOA promptly or at least 3 business days before consummation; and investor document-age limits (for example about 120 days for credit documents under GSE rules; verify each one). Each rule names which 'business day' definition it uses: the general one, or the specific one that counts every day except Sundays and federal public holidays. Holidays come from a federal holiday table. SYSTEM: a small dependency graph. Durations come from this MLO's own measured pace (5.5) and the minimums from the rules. It computes the earliest feasible consummation date and the slack against each contract deadline. TRIGGER: slack falls below a threshold (default 5 business days), a key date changes, or a document will age out before the projected closing. MLO: a Today card, for example 'Loan contingency ends Nov 24, projected CTC Nov 27 at your normal pace, appraisal not yet received.' Buttons: draft an extension request to the agent (MLO approves), assign the processor task, ask the borrower for a fresh pay stub, or open a lock-extension pricing task. AGENT: with the borrower's sharing authorization, a read-only clock with status and dates only. BORROWER: 'What has to happen before you can close, and when', in plain words, with no promised date.
- **Technologies:** Document AI extraction with zod and quote anchoring (C20 upload path), Effective-dated rules table with citations (extends lib/send-gate/rules.ts pattern), Federal holiday calendar and business-day engine, Dependency-graph scheduler fed by the MLO's measured milestone pace, ARIVE Zapier and Encompass Developer Connect connectors, plus Lender Mail Room, Neon with row-level security, M8 guard (never promise a closing date)
- **Why it's new:** LOS compliance engines (Encompass compliance services, for example) track TRID timing for the creditor's disclosure desk. Transaction-coordinator platforms used by agents (SkySlope, Dotloop, Open to Close) track contract dates. Broker CRMs such as Bonzo, Shape and BNTouch send date-based drips. The plan's milestone guardian (5.5) measures pace against the MLO's own history but doesn't know the contract or the regulatory minimums. The prior research found no tool that solves the broker MLO's closing date against all four constraint sets and warns on contingency collisions. That is training knowledge plus the repo surveys, not re-verified this session, because the web search budget was used up.
- **Compliance design:** TRID and ECOA: the clock is decision support. It never issues disclosures. In a brokered loan the creditor stays responsible for the LE and CD even when the broker provides the LE. The rules table rows and the choice of business-day definition need [COUNSEL] sign-off, and each row carries a citation and an effective date. Guard: closing dates are always 'projected' with reasons, and M8 and any draft are blocked from promising a date (an existing guard rule). GLBA: the contract and dates are NPI. The upload file is deleted after extraction, agent visibility is limited to fields the borrower authorized (revocable and logged), and every read is audited. RESPA: extension requests and status go to every agent on the file equally, with no value attached. TCPA: drafts to agents and borrowers go through the D3 gate, and these are service messages. Fair lending: the same rules and thresholds for every file, with no demographic inputs. Uploaded contracts are data, never instructions.
- **Moat:** The rules table and the per-lender, per-program duration priors get better as more MLOs close files on the platform. Duration priors are pooled only as k-anonymous medians (at least 10 MLOs), with no lender-identifying comparisons. Counsel-reviewed rule rows are a shared asset that members fund together, and a solo MLO couldn't maintain them alone.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: One rule is stated wrong. The initial LE must be delivered or placed in the mail no later than the seventh business day before consummation (1026.19(e)(1)(iii)(B)). It is not 'received' 7 days before, while the CD must be received 3 business days before. Building the wrong row would mis-compute closing dates. Otherwise it is decision support only and has no violation. It overlaps Contingency Clock and Application Clock. Redesign: Correct the LE row to 'delivered or mailed' at least 7 business days before, using the specific business-day definition. Merge Contingency Clock into Deal Clock, and have it consume Application Clock's ledger rather than computing TRID dates separately. / keep 6/10: Contract dates already live in TC platforms (SkySlope, Dotloop, Open to Close) and TRID timing in LOS compliance engines. Solving across both, plus lock expiry and document age-outs, is a moderately new combination. It duplicates Contingency Clock. Redesign: Keep it as the merged clock, absorbing Contingency Clock's form library and Application Clock's TRID clocks. / keep 7/10: Contingency misses are costly (earnest money, lost deals), and the inputs exist: the uploaded contract, LOS milestones, lock expiry. It overlaps the plan's existing lock-expiry and closing-date alerts (4.6) and the Contingency Clock idea. Building its own TRID business-day engine duplicates the creditor's and LOS's disclosure tracking and adds liability. Redesign: Absorb Contingency Clock. Inputs: AI-extracted contract deadlines with quoted anchors and LO confirmation, LOS dates and the MLO's own pace. Use a minimal rules table: CD 3-day wait, appraisal-copy timing and document age-outs. Read disclosure dates from the LOS instead of recomputing them. No state form library.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md §4.6 (lock-expiry and closing alerts, never promise a closing date), §5.5, /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md C20 (Loan Estimate upload path) and D3 (rules table pattern), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/19/ (LE/CD timing, redisclosure; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/2/ (business day definitions; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/14/ (ECOA appraisal copy; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/23/ (rescission; not opened this session), https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/ (allowable age of credit documents; verify current section), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work- (ARIVE loan date and tracker triggers, per survey)


## Delegation Desk with a licensed-activity firewall (round 1, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**Every task, draft and inbound question is tagged as licensed work (taking an application, offering or negotiating terms) or support work. Unlicensed assistants and processors only ever get support work, under a stricter guard. Each handoff writes a direction-and-supervision log an examiner can read, and the MLO's day shrinks to the work only they can legally do.**

- **Problem:** Solo and small-broker MLOs lose hours to work a processor or assistant could do: document chasing, scheduling, relaying status. They don't delegate because the line between clerical support and loan origination is legal, not obvious. An assistant who texts 'you'd qualify at 6.5%' is offering terms. Under the SAFE Act framework, loan processors who do clerical or support duties under a licensed MLO's direction and supervision generally don't need a license, but independent-contractor processors generally must be licensed (training knowledge, MED-HIGH; confirm per state). Generic CRM roles control which screens a user sees, not which activities the law reserves for a licensed person.
- **How it works:** DATA: the D1 roles (owner, team lead, loan officer, assistant) gain a verified licensing profile: the NMLS ID, the licensed states (from config/licensing.ts for MLOs, or an NMLS Consumer Access lookup run only to decide whether to do business with that person, which the terms allow), and an employment type (W-2 employee or independent contractor). Every task type, play step, draft and inbound-message class carries an activity class: LICENSED (application intake, discussing rates, terms, programs or qualification, explaining LE options, issuing a pre-approval), SUPPORT (collecting a listed document, scheduling, relaying status that's in the file with no advice, ordering services per instructions) or ADMIN. SYSTEM: (1) Today splits into an 'Only you' lane and a 'Delegate' lane. One tap or swipe assigns a support item with a due time. (2) The assignee's drafts run under a stricter guard profile that blocks any rate, APR, payment, program recommendation, 'you qualify' or 'approved' language, and offers the handoff line ('[MLO first name] will answer that personally') instead. (3) A classifier on inbound borrower and agent messages sends anything asking about terms, eligibility or advice back to the MLO's lane, even when an assistant owns the thread. (4) An independent-contractor processor can't be activated without an active license record, and it is re-checked on a schedule. (5) Every support action records the direction (which MLO assigned it, when, and what the instruction was). The MLO gets a weekly supervision sample to review, and approve or correct with one tap, which also feeds 5.10's training loop. MLO: works only the licensed lane. A quick weekly 'what my team did' review doubles as the supervision record. ASSISTANT or PROCESSOR: a clean queue with scripts that keep them inside support work. BORROWER: faster answers on documents and scheduling, and term questions always reach the licensed MLO.
- **Technologies:** Activity taxonomy on tasks, plays and drafts (config-driven, counsel-reviewed), Guard profiles per role (extends lib/m8/guard.ts), Inbound intent classifier (server-side AI with zod output), NMLS Consumer Access lookup for business-qualification purpose only, D1 accounts and roles, D5 Today lanes, Append-only supervision log with weekly sampling
- **Why it's new:** Mortgage CRMs and Salesforce-based tools (Jungo, Total Expert, Bonzo teams) offer task assignment and permission roles, which control visibility. The prior research found none that classifies work by the SAFE Act's licensed-activity line, blocks an unlicensed user's messages from carrying terms, routes term questions back to the licensed MLO, or produces a supervision log. That conclusion comes from vendor feature pages in the repo surveys and was not re-searched this session, because the search budget was used up.
- **Compliance design:** SAFE Act, Reg H and state licensing: the activity taxonomy and the definitions of 'clerical or support duties' and 'direction and supervision' are [COUNSEL] items, set per state, because some states license processing companies or have their own rules (verify). The NMLS lookup is used only to decide whether to do business with a registered person, which the Consumer Access terms permit. It's never used for solicitation or bulk monitoring, and results are kept only as long as needed. AI: whether M8's own intake counts as 'taking an application' is a counsel question. M8 stays in its current posture: the prep flow never says 'submitted', and it gives no terms outside a priced example. Reg Z: delegation never touches compensation, and no task carries a pay field. Employee monitoring: the supervision log records business actions, not keystrokes or screen time. TCPA: every send still goes through D3. GLBA: assistants see only files assigned to them (row-level security by assignment), and every read is audited. Fair lending: routing ignores borrower attributes.
- **Moat:** The counsel-reviewed, state-by-state activity taxonomy and guard profiles are expensive to build once and cheap to share across a member network. Sponsors can adopt them as their written supervision procedure. Each correction from a member's supervision review improves the classifier for everyone, using redacted patterns only and never borrower data.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: It is compliant and strengthens the SAFE Act clerical-support boundary. Risks: state-specific processor and assistant rules, and the NMLS lookup's terms. A per-person supervision log viewed by a sponsor employer may trigger electronic-monitoring notice laws (NY Civil Rights Law 52-c, Conn. Gen. Stat. 31-48d, from training knowledge). Redesign: Add an employer monitoring-notice and acknowledgment step before sponsor-level supervision views. Verify state taxonomy rows with counsel before enabling each state. / keep 6/10: Task assignment and permission roles are standard in CRMs. Sorting work by the SAFE Act line and blocking terms in unlicensed users' messages is new, and it is the same taxonomy as Licensed Hands. Redesign: Merge into Licensed Hands as the human side of the same activity taxonomy. / keep 6/10: Useful for MLOs with assistants (often a minority of independent brokers, more at teams). The 'Only you / Delegate' lanes and the stricter guard profile for assistants are a cheap reuse of the guard. The state-by-state SAFE Act taxonomy is a counsel cost, and the inbound term-question classifier is extra model work. It shares the taxonomy with Licensed Hands, so build them once. Redesign: Share one activity taxonomy with Licensed Hands. v1: lanes, the assistant guard profile, a direction log and the weekly sample review. Defer the scheduled license re-check for contractors to the License Clock.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D1 (roles), D3 (gate), D5 (Today), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md §2.5, §3, §5.10, https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/policies/NMLS%20Document%20Library/Consumer%20Access%20Terms%20of%20Use.pdf (permitted use: deciding whether to do business; no solicitation or bulk monitoring, per survey), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5103 (SAFE Act licensing requirement incl. independent-contractor processors; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1008/ (Regulation H; not opened this session), Verification note: SAFE Act processor rules are training knowledge (MED-HIGH); www.ecfr.gov blocked and search exhausted this run


## Pricing Coverage Planner with Float Watch (round 1, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Every place Command Center needed a price and had none (an M8 chat miss, a Today refinance look, a rate watch, a floating loan in process) is logged as an anonymous demand bucket. Each morning the planner picks the smallest set of scenarios to price that covers the most demand, and it flags floating files whose market has moved against them.**

- **Problem:** Pricing is a manual pull of fixed scenarios (C7's four Woodinville scenarios, imported through C9's bookmarklet). When nothing matches, M8's get_pricing returns 'unavailable', D5's refinance look stays silent, and 5.4, 5.6 and idea 5 have no data. The MLO decides by hand what to price and doesn't know which scenarios would unblock the most borrowers and cards. Separately, floating loans in process (applied, not locked) get no daily market check: the borrower hears about a worse rate only at lock time. Even once a licensed PPE API arrives, pull budgets and license terms still limit how much gets priced.
- **How it works:** DATA: each consumer emits a demand record in the canonical bucket that lib/pricing/canonical.ts already hashes: purpose, program, occupancy, property type, state and county (for loan limits), LTV band, loan-amount band, credit band and lock period. It holds no name, ZIP or contact ID, only a count and the consumer type. Consumers: M8 chat misses (the logs already hold counts only), Today cards, borrower-set rate watches, floating pipeline files from the LOS (no lock date), and pre-approvals in shopping. SYSTEM: (1) The MLO or sponsor sets a daily pull budget (for example 6). (2) A weighted greedy set cover picks scenarios that respect the existing match rules (weaker credit than the example never matches). Weights: floating files near a lock decision, then rate watches, then Today looks, then chat misses, each with a freshness SLA. (3) The output is an entry sheet with exact inputs per scenario for ARIVE or LoanSifter, or a licensed API call when Optimal Blue or Lender Price access exists. Results land in the same snapshot store through C9 or the API, and the planner shows the demand each pull will unblock ('this pull clears 3 rate watches and 2 Today cards'). (4) Float Watch: for each floating file it compares the bucket's dated example now with the one at application. When the move passes the MLO's threshold, an MLO-only card says 'market example moved +0.25 since application; consider a lock conversation; price their actual scenario first.' It never messages the borrower by itself. (5) Lock expiring with closing past it (from Deal Clock) creates a 'price the extension or relock' task. MLO: one short list each morning instead of guessing, and pipeline lock risk on one screen. BORROWER: fewer 'I can't show pricing for that' answers from M8, and a lock conversation before the market moves further.
- **Technologies:** lib/pricing canonical hashing, daily scenarios, match bands (C7) and store, C9 LoanSifter capture path; Optimal Blue Broker Product & Pricing API or Lender Price API when licensed, Weighted set-cover optimizer, LOS lock and milestone data (ARIVE Zapier, Encompass), D5 Today cards; M8 get_pricing miss logging, Sponsor-level pricing tenants (lib/pricing/tenants.ts)
- **Why it's new:** Pricing engines (Optimal Blue, Lender Price, ARIVE's PPE) price whatever they're asked, and lenders with lock desks watch locked pipeline. Broker CRMs either show no pricing or rely on the MLO to run searches. The prior research found nothing that turns a CRM's own unmet pricing demand into a minimal pull list, or that watches floating broker files against dated examples. That comes from training knowledge plus the repo surveys, not re-verified this run.
- **Compliance design:** No rate reaches a borrower outside a priced result, and Float Watch cards are MLO-only and labelled as dated examples (D5's existing wording). The three-option anti-steering selection and the C8.1 fixed-compensation rule are unchanged. The planner never optimizes by compensation and has no compensation field, which keeps it outside Reg Z 1026.36(d). Antitrust: demand pools only within one sponsor's pricing tenant, as the store already does, and never across competing sponsors, because pooling price data across them is the information-sharing problem that killed 'Lender Pulse'. PPE licenses: the planner cuts pulls and uses only authorized paths (the owner's recorded ARIVE automation OK; Loansifter only as its license allows; survey notes clause 3.2.1). Fair lending: buckets are underwriting and pricing factors only. There is no tract, ZIP or demographic field, and county appears only because loan limits depend on it. GLBA: demand records carry no identity. TCPA: nothing is sent.
- **Moat:** With more MLOs under a sponsor, each pull serves more demand, which pairs with the 'Pricing Rota' (that splits who pulls; this decides what to pull). With more consumers on the platform, the demand map shows sponsors which products to add to the panel. All of it stays inside each sponsor's tenant.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: It is compliant: no compensation field, demand is pooled only within a sponsor's tenant, and Float Watch is MLO-only. The risk is PPE license terms. Automating entry sheets or pulls must stay within ARIVE, Loansifter and Optimal Blue licenses, and Loansifter clause limits are noted in the survey but not verified.
