Reg Z advertising: any rate shown is a priced result with APR and the not-a-commitment line, and the guard enforces it. No comparison claims about the builder's lender (consistent with the Second Look 'never compares pricing' rule). GLBA: the contract is the buyer's NPI and is shared with the agent only under authorization. The network clause library keeps template text and fingerprints, never buyer names, prices or addresses. Fair lending: the same checks for every buyer. TCPA: service only. Licensing: priced examples only in the MLO's licensed states. Antitrust: builder templates are documents the builder hands to thousands of buyers; the library records clause text, not builder performance ratings.
- **Moat:** Large builders reuse a few contract templates across thousands of buyers. Once any member's buyer uploads a version, its fingerprint and flagged clauses are known network-wide, so the next buyer's agent gets instant flags. That library compounds with every MLO and market, and a single-lender CRM or a builder's captive lender has no reason to build it.
- **Reviews:** keep 5/10: RESPA 9 (12 U.S.C. 2608: a seller cannot require a particular title insurer as a condition of sale, with liability of three times the title charges) and the Reg X 1024.2 'required use' definition (an optional, true discount is not required use) are correctly cited. But the actor is a competing MLO with a direct financial interest in steering the buyer away from the builder's affiliated lender. (1) Stating 'federal law says a seller can't require…' next to a specific clause is applying law to facts, which risks UPL in several states, and interference or defamation claims from builders. (2) Putting the incentive's dollar value next to the MLO's own priced example is a comparative pricing claim, which conflicts with Second Look's 'never compares pricing' rule and creates Reg Z advertising and UDAAP exposure if the builder package is cheaper overall. (3) Most builder affiliate incentives are lawful optional discounts, so a flag-heavy UI overstates the legal risk. Redesign: Limit it to neutral extraction: list the clauses that mention lender, title or deadlines, with page references, plus a general explainer that is not tied to the clause ('what required use means'), with UPL review per state. Remove the side-by-side incentive valuation. If the borrower asks for a quote, give a standalone priced LE-style example with no comparison language. Do not characterize any clause as a violation. / keep 6/10: I know of no tool for buyer's agents or independent MLOs that reads builder contracts for affiliate-conditioned incentives and RESPA Section 9 title-designation clauses. Builder-side and captive-lender software has the opposite incentive. Novelty holds. The risks are UPL and relationships with builders, and contract volume per broker may be modest. Redesign: Ship the clause library and quoting extractor with strict 'quote and cite, never interpret' output. Start with the large public builders' templates in the licensed states. Keep the shared library limited to template fingerprints. / drop 3/10: The timing is wrong. Buyers usually bring an independent MLO in after they've signed the builder's contract, when incentive deadlines have already locked them in. Public builders report very high affiliate mortgage capture (from 10-K knowledge, not re-checked this run), so independent MLOs win few of these files. Unauthorized-practice-of-law review is needed in every state. The template library needs network scale, and use per MLO is rare. Redesign: Drop as a desk. If anything, add a 'new construction' checklist sheet with the RESPA Section 9 statement and an affiliated-business disclosure check that the agent can hand to the buyer, with no AI clause extraction.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/2608 (RESPA Section 9; host blocked this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1024/2/ (Reg X definition of required use; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1024/15/ (affiliated business arrangements; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1024/d/ (AfBA disclosure format; not opened), https://www.sec.gov/edgar/search/ (public builders' 10-K filings with mortgage capture rates; not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (Second Look never compares pricing)


## Screener Protocol and Verify-M8 (round 2, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**M8 knows when a phone's AI answers its call, or when a consumer's AI calls the loan officer, and follows a fixed, provable script for each. Borrowers can also confirm in one tap that a call or text claiming to be their loan officer really was.**

- **Problem:** More and more calls are answered by software before a person hears them. Apple's iOS 26 Call Screening asks unknown callers for their name and reason before the phone rings, Google Pixel has had Call Screen for years, and Google's 'Ask for Me' has an AI phone businesses for the user (all three from training knowledge; this session couldn't confirm them). Hiya's 2026 survey found 86% of people won't answer an unknown number and 1 in 4 have had a deepfake voice call. No mortgage AI-voice product says what its agent does when a screener answers. Does the AI disclosure count if only a bot heard it? Can marketing content be read to a screener? Is the call counted against frequency caps? Nor do they say what happens when a consumer's AI phones the loan officer and offers to 'consent' or hand over an SSN. Impersonation is the other side of the same problem: a scammer who sounds like the loan officer can push fake wire instructions or a fake 'rate lock fee'.
- **How it works:** **Data:** Twilio answering-machine detection, plus a fingerprint library of screener opening lines. The library holds only the screener's own script text (for example 'Hi, if you record your name and reason for calling…'), never the consumer's speech. It also uses the D3 gate decision and the consent record's purpose for each call.

**Outbound, when a screener answers:**
- M8 switches to a fixed 'screener card' that the guard has already checked. It says who is calling: M8, an AI assistant for [loan officer first name] at [sponsor], NMLS [number]. It gives the reason, taken from the consented purpose and never more specific than the consent. For a marketing-purpose consent it says only 'following up on your request to hear from [first name]'. It gives a callback number (47 CFR 64.1200(b) requires artificial-voice messages to identify the caller at the start and give a number) and 'say stop, or reply STOP'.
- It never pitches and never mentions a rate.
- If a person then picks up, M8 gives the full AI and recording disclosures again before anything substantive.
- If no person picks up, there's no voicemail pitch. At most one follow-up text is sent, and only with text consent.
- A screened call counts toward the frequency caps like any other call.

**Inbound, when a consumer's AI calls the loan officer's line:**
- M8 says it is an AI too.
- It answers only with public facts: who is licensed in which state, and a published dated example with APR and the not-a-commitment line, or 'no price available'.
- It refuses to take consent, application data, SSN, date of birth or account numbers from the AI, and it never texts or calls the number the AI calls from.
- It gives the AI a booking link to pass to its user, so the human starts the relationship.
- It logs the call as AI-mediated demand by area, never sold.

**Verify-M8:** every M8 call and text is listed in real time on the borrower's consent-passport page, for example 'M8 called you 2:14 PM about your requested check-in'. A borrower can text VERIFY to the loan officer's number, or open the page, to see whether a call or text really came from the network. If no call is logged, the page says 'that wasn't us' and shows a fixed warning: we never send wire instructions or ask for a fee by phone. That links to the existing wire-callback check.

**Each party's experience:**
- **Loan officer:** sees 'screened, not answered' as a separate outcome, so the dial counts stay honest.
- **Borrower:** a polite, complete message, and a way to catch impostors.
- **Real estate agent:** nothing changes.
- **Technologies:** Twilio Programmable Voice with answering-machine detection and Media Streams, M8 voice runtime (cascaded speech-to-text, then the guard, then text-to-speech for the screener card), lib/m8/guard.ts and redaction, D3 send gate and D4 opt-out list (cc_optouts), Consent passport page (plan 5.3) as the verification surface, STIR/SHAKEN full attestation and branded caller ID or Rich Call Data via the carrier, Speech-to-text classifier for screener scripts, /api/agent rules from AGENTS.md ('an agent cannot consent for a person')
- **Why it's new:** The market survey's 'answerability' opening covers branded caller ID and moments the borrower set up. Its Licensed Agent Gateway covers consumer AIs over MCP and the web, not over the phone. The taken 'closing week conductor' covers only the wire-instruction callback at closing. No mortgage CRM or AI-voice vendor publishes how it handles device screeners or inbound consumer AI callers. Total Expert's AI discloses only 'when required by law or if prompted' (https://www.totalexpert.com/features/ai-sales-assistant), and Flair and Sela publish dial volumes with no consent method. Rocket, UWM's Mia and Better's Betsy publish no screener policy. The first product to treat a screener as a defined compliance situation, and to give borrowers impostor verification across every loan officer, sets the pattern.
- **Compliance design:** **TCPA:**
- A screened call is still an artificial-voice call, so the gate needs the same prior express consent, and written consent for marketing.
- The identity statement and number come at the start (64.1200(b)), followed by an automated opt-out.
- 'Stop' spoken to the screener, or later by text, revokes on every channel immediately (strict build rule). This also covers FCC 26-67: the designated method is disclosed on every call, but any reasonable means is still honored.
- Screened calls count toward the frequency caps.

**AI disclosure:** given to the screener and again to the person, before any substance.

**Recording:** the screener card has no substance, and the all-party-state recording notice still comes before any human conversation.

**Consent from AIs:** an AI caller can never give consent, apply or supply nonpublic personal information. Only a human acting through the booking link can (E-SIGN 7001(h) attribution is unsettled, so treat AI-given consent as invalid).

**Rates:** no rate is spoken to a screener. Inbound, a rate is given only as a priced dated example with APR and the not-a-commitment line.

**GLBA:** the Verify page shows only that a call happened, its time and purpose category. It never shows loan data, and it needs the passport link or a texted code to open.

**Fair lending:** detection keys on the device's script, never on the consumer's voice. No voiceprints (BIPA, and the Texas and Washington biometric laws).

**No lead sold:** AI-caller demand is counted by area only.

**[COUNSEL]:** whether a disclosure delivered to a device screener satisfies state chatbot and AI disclosure laws (Utah, Maine, California), and the FCC 24-84 proposal if it's adopted.
- **Moat:** The screener-fingerprint library is the asset that compounds. It holds only device and vendor script text, which isn't nonpublic personal information, and it improves with every tenant's calls as Apple, Google, Samsung and carriers change their screeners. A solo loan officer on Retell or Vapi can't keep up with that. Verify-M8 also gets more valuable with network size: once borrowers know 'check any call at your loan officer's link', every member's calls become more trustworthy and impostors stand out. Big lenders verify only their own calls.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: This is mostly sound: a screened call is still an artificial-voice call, and 47 CFR 64.1200(b) requires identification at the start and a callback number. Five risks. (1) Recording or transcribing the screener exchange can capture the consumer's own speech, for example when they reply to the screener, before the recording notice. CIPA and all-party-consent states require consent before recording a confidential communication. (2) Inbound AI callers get a 'published dated example with APR' when the property state is unknown, which breaks the build-plan licensing rule (2.1) that M8 discusses loans only for licensed property states. (3) FCC 26-67 on designated revocation methods comes from an unverified single source. (4) Detecting screeners from 'device script text' uses ASR on the call, which is fine, but nothing voice-derived may be stored. (5) VERIFY responses must not confirm a relationship to an unverified texter, because the fact of a customer relationship is GLBA NPI. Redesign: Don't record or keep the screener leg beyond the detection result until the recording disclosure has been given. Inbound to AI callers, share only licensing facts and a booking link, and give price examples only after the AI states a licensed property state. Answer VERIFY only to the number M8 actually called, with the same generic wording whether or not a call exists ('check your link'). Merge Verify-M8 with Verified AI Caller. / keep 6/10: Answering-machine and voicemail detection are commodity features (Twilio AMD; Retell, Vapi and Bland all handle voicemail and IVR). Branded-call verification is sold by Numeracle, First Orion and Hiya. A defined compliance script for device AI screeners and a policy for inbound consumer AI callers is new and forward-looking (training knowledge; no vendor publishes one per the survey). Verify-M8 duplicates the VERIFY path in Verified AI Caller in this batch. Redesign: Turn the screener protocol into a D8 voice gate rule with a guarded screener card. Merge Verify-M8 and Verified AI Caller into one 'Verify a call' feature (call log on the consent passport plus VERIFY by text). Drop the watermark and C2PA pieces until a borrower-facing use is proven. / keep 7/10: Phone-side AI screeners (Apple's iOS call screening, Google Call Screen, carrier screeners) now intercept a large share of unknown calls, so a fixed, guard-checked screener script directly decides whether M8 outbound calls work at all. It is small to build on Twilio answering-machine detection plus a short script, and it needs no external data. The inbound 'consumer AI calls the LO' branch is speculative for now. Verify-M8 overlaps the same feature in Verified AI Caller. The screener fingerprint library as a moat is weak, since vendors change their scripts and the platform can only observe them. Redesign: Ship the outbound screener card (identity, number, opt-out, no pitch, one follow-up text only with consent) and the VERIFY lookup. Merge Verified AI Caller's verification into this. Defer the inbound consumer-AI branch until such calls actually show up in logs.
- **Sources:** https://www.hiya.com/newsroom/press-releases/state-of-the-call-2026-ai-deepfake-voice-calls-hit-1-in-4-americans-as-consumers-say-scammers-are-beating-mobile-network-operators-2-to-1 (from market survey), https://www.totalexpert.com/features/ai-sales-assistant (from market survey), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-64/subpart-L/section-64.1200 (64.1200(b) identification and opt-out; not opened this session, host blocked), https://www.nelsonmullins.com/insights/alerts/fcc-download/all/fcc-adopts-overhaul-of-tcpa-consent-revocation-rules-revoke-all-narrowed-exclusive-opt-out-methods-permitted-and-fraud-alert-exemption-expanded (FCC 26-67, from market survey), https://support.apple.com/guide/iphone/ (iOS 26 Call Screening; from training knowledge, host blocked this session, VERIFY), https://blog.google/products/search/ (Google 'Ask for Me' AI calls to businesses; training knowledge, VERIFY), https://www.zian.ai/fcc-nprm-24-84-ai-call-disclosure-rules/ (FCC 24-84 pending, from market survey), /home/user/RATEM8/AGENTS.md (an agent cannot consent for a person)


## Own Words: letters of explanation and gift letters the AI never writes (round 2, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**An AI interviewer collects letters of explanation and donor gift letters by voice or chat. It records the borrower's or donor's exact words, checks them against the uploaded documents, and builds a letter in which every factual clause traces back to something the person said. That speeds conditions up and blocks AI-written fiction from ever reaching a lender.**

- **Problem:** Letters of explanation (LOEs) for large deposits, employment gaps, address mismatches and occupancy, and gift letters from family donors, are among the most common conditions on a file. General-purpose AI makes it easy to write a fluent explanation the borrower never gave. That is a mortgage-fraud risk: 18 U.S.C. 1014 covers false statements to a 'mortgage lending business'. It is also the opposite of what a lender wants from an AI-assisted broker. Donors are third parties: the borrower can't consent to calls or texts on the donor's behalf, so chasing a donor by AI call or text is a TCPA problem. ARIVE turned off its document-AI beta on 2026-08-30 (market survey), so brokers on ARIVE have no first-party help here.
- **How it works:** **Data:** the condition text from the LOS (ARIVE Zapier triggers, Encompass webhooks), the borrower's uploaded statements, and, for gifts, the donor's own uploads. Conditions that come from a credit report stay in the LOS. Own Words sees only the lender's condition wording and stores no tradeline data.

**Trigger:** a new LOE or gift condition, or a deposit the borrower flags during a live doc session.

**What the system does:**
- **Interview:** after the AI disclosure, M8 asks open, non-leading questions such as 'What was the $8,400 deposit on March 3?' and records the answers verbatim (transcript plus an audio hash).
- **Draft:** builds the letter only from transcript spans, changing grammar alone. The borrower sees each sentence linked to what they said, with a diff of any wording change.
- **Consistency check:** the answer is compared with the documents (amount, date, source account). A mismatch is never smoothed over; it goes to the loan officer as 'explanation and document disagree'.
- **Red-flag stops:** answers that suggest occupancy misrepresentation ('we'll rent it out' on a primary-residence loan), an undisclosed debt, or a 'gift' expected to be repaid stop the interview and route the file to the loan officer. M8 doesn't coach, and it refuses requests to 'word it so underwriting accepts it'.
- **Sign and file:** the borrower reads it back or listens, then e-signs under an attestation that the statements are true. The letter goes to the condition in the LOS with the evidence attached.

**Gift letters:**
- The borrower forwards a one-time link to the donor themselves. The platform never collects the donor's number and never calls or texts the donor first.
- The donor opens M8 chat (AI disclosure first), answers the gift-letter items in their own words (name, relationship, amount, date of transfer, no repayment expected), e-signs, and uploads proof of the transfer.
- The donor can choose to receive one status text. Nothing else is ever sent to them.

**Each party's experience:**
- **Loan officer:** sees 'LOE ready, 3 sentences, all sourced', with one tap to submit.
- **Borrower and donor:** about 5 minutes, on a phone, at a time they choose.
- **Technologies:** M8 chat and consented voice (Google speech-to-text from C16, text-to-speech from C15), LLM extraction with sentence-to-transcript span provenance (a new guard rule: no unsourced fact), Document OCR and parsing of bank statements, E-SIGN signature capture with truthfulness attestation, ARIVE Zapier and Encompass webhooks for condition read and document write-back, Pass-along link pattern (no third-party number collected), lib/m8/redact.ts (account numbers redacted in the transcript)
- **Why it's new:** The taken 'live doc session' checks uploads on a call, and 'one entry intake' covers write-once intake. Neither produces a sworn narrative, and neither has a rule that the AI may not author facts. The market survey notes that AI loan processors (Friday Harbor, Addy, Candor, Vesta) are lender-side and publish no accuracy results, and that ARIVE paused its document AI. Generic AI letter tools draft explanations for the user, which is exactly the fraud vector. A verbatim, span-sourced LOE standard, plus a gift workflow that never contacts the donor unasked, is a new posture that lenders can rely on.
- **Compliance design:** **Fraud law:** 18 U.S.C. 1014 attestation. The AI never invents, adds or 'improves' a fact, and the guard rejects any sentence without a transcript span. Occupancy and undisclosed-debt signals stop the interview and route to the loan officer, never to auto-submit.

**TCPA:**
- Borrower calls are service calls with prior express consent.
- The donor is never called or texted unless they opt in on the page, and then only one status text.
- The borrower can't consent for the donor.

**AI disclosure** comes first for both borrower and donor. The **recording notice** comes first in all-party states.

**GLBA:**
- Transcripts and statements are nonpublic personal information, redacted, read-audited and tenant-isolated, and never used for training.
- Donor data is kept only on that loan file, under the sponsor's record-keeping rules.

**FCRA:** no consumer-report data enters the platform. Credit-derived conditions are handled only as the lender's condition text.

**ECOA:** the same questions are asked of every borrower for the same condition type.

**No rate talk.**

**[COUNSEL]:** the attestation wording, and whether span-sourced formatting counts as 'preparing' the letter for any state licensing rule.
- **Moat:** Lenders' underwriters come to recognize network LOEs as verbatim and evidence-linked, which earns members faster condition clearance. Each new member makes that reputation broader. The open 'verbatim LOE' format and its guard rule can be published as a network standard, the way the survey suggests for signed quotes. The question library per condition type improves with use across tenants, sharing questions and never answers.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: This is a strong anti-fraud design. A missing Reg B piece: when an LOE concerns a deposit from alimony, child support or separate maintenance, 1002.5(d)(2) requires telling the applicant that such income need not be revealed unless they want it considered. An interviewer asking 'what was the $8,400 deposit?' needs that notice. Donor data (bank statements, identity) belongs to a non-customer. That puts it outside GLBA's customer definition, and it may fall under state privacy laws (CCPA's GLBA exemption is data-level and may not cover the donor). Storing an audio hash is not a voiceprint, which is fine, but audio retention needs recording consent in all-party states. Redesign: Add the Reg B 1002.5(d)(2) notice to any deposit or income LOE question flow. Give donors their own short privacy notice, state-law rights handling and minimal retention tied to the loan file. Keep the 'no unsourced sentence' guard as a hard CI-tested rule. / keep 7/10: Generic AI LOE writers and POS gift-letter forms with donor e-signature already exist (Blend, Floify and others), so collecting LOEs and gift letters digitally is not new. The binding rule that every sentence traces to the borrower's own transcript, with document consistency checks and red-flag stops, is a materially better and defensible posture that I know of no vendor offering. Redesign: Keep it. Use the POS's existing gift-letter fields and conditions as the destination rather than a separate signing flow, and publish the verbatim LOE format as an open standard. / keep 7/10: LOEs and gift letters come up on nearly every file, and chasing borrowers for them costs processors and MLOs real hours each week. The build reuses the M8 chat, redaction and guard, plus a span-sourcing check. Limits: many wholesale lenders require their own gift-letter form, so output must fill the lender's form. Lenders won't give 'faster clearance' for network LOEs, so that moat claim is a vendor-style hope. Voice interview and audio hashing add cost early. Redesign: MVP in chat only: verbatim interview, span-linked letter, document consistency check, red-flag stop, e-sign, and PDF export to the LOS. Gift letters fill the lender's own form fields from the donor's answers. Add voice later.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/1014 (false statements to a mortgage lending business; training knowledge), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000319289-arive-ai-beta-doc-classification-extraction-update (ARIVE doc-AI beta turned off 2026-08-30, from market survey), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work- (ARIVE Zapier triggers, from market survey), https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/reference/webhook (Encompass document webhooks, from market survey), https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/ (B3-4.2-02 large deposits; B3-4.3-04 gift letter contents; training knowledge, VERIFY), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/7001 (E-SIGN, from market survey)


## Protective Pause: an exploitation and coercion circuit breaker for every AI channel (round 2, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**When a conversation shows signs of exploitation, such as a third party steering the borrower, cash-out proceeds headed to someone else, or scam scripts, every automated touch on that file stops. A human loan officer takes over with a private check-in, and the sponsor's anti-money-laundering officer gets a ready case file. The triggers are based on behavior only, never on age.**

- **Problem:** AI agents make it easy for a fraudster or a pushy relative to drive a refinance or cash-out through a vulnerable borrower. M8 'just answering questions' can become the tool of an exploitation scheme: a romance or government-impersonation scam pushing a HELOC, a 'helper' who answers every question, proceeds wired to a third party. FinCEN treats elder financial exploitation as a priority SAR typology (its 2022 advisory). Nonbank residential mortgage lenders and originators must run anti-money-laundering programs and file SARs (31 CFR 1029). Mortgage CRMs and AI-voice vendors optimize for conversion and have no stop rule. A naive fix ('flag older borrowers') would violate ECOA, which prohibits age discrimination.
- **How it works:** **Data:** M8 chat and call transcripts (already redacted), the loan officer's own calls only where already recorded under the plan, LOS events (a new title party, a change of disbursement instructions), and the borrower's own words.

**Triggers (conduct only, the same for everyone):**
- Another voice answering the borrower's identity questions, or audible coaching.
- A request to send loan proceeds to a non-borrower or a crypto account.
- A stated pressure source ('my adviser says I must do this today').
- Scam script phrases (government impersonation, 'investment opportunity', 'they told me not to tell my bank').
- The borrower contradicting the loan's purpose.
- A late request to add a non-occupant to title.

**What the system does:**
- Sets the file to 'protective pause' in the D3 gate. All plays, AI calls and drafted texts stop, except a service message the loan officer approves.
- Tells the loan officer the specific excerpts, with no score and no demographic inference.
- Offers a scripted, neutral, private check-in call by the human loan officer, never M8. The script offers the borrower a voluntary 'trusted contact' they choose.
- Builds a case file (timeline, excerpts, LOS events) for the sponsor's AML/BSA officer, who decides whether to file a SAR. The platform never files and never tells the subject.

**Clearing the pause:** the loan officer clears it with a logged reason, and every pause and clear is kept for the exam room.

**Each party's experience:**
- **Borrower:** their own loan officer checks in calmly, and nothing is denied by the system.
- **Agent:** nothing is shared with them.
- **Technologies:** Transcript classifier over redacted M8 chat and voice transcripts (rules plus an LLM, with human review), D3 send gate (a new 'protective pause' block reason), LOS webhooks (ARIVE Zapier, Encompass) for title and disbursement changes, Case-file builder with append-only audit (cc_audit), Paired tester lab (taken idea) to test the triggers for age-neutral outcomes, Compliance Center queue for the sponsor's AML officer
- **Why it's new:** The taken 'fraud signal exchange' is a Section 314(b) information-sharing consortium between institutions. 'Hardship lifeline' handles delinquency, and 'guard commons' protects M8 from attacks. None of them detects coercion of a borrower inside a conversation or stops automation on that file. Banks and broker-dealers run elder-exploitation programs (FINRA Rule 4512 trusted contacts, Senior Safe Act training). Mortgage CRMs and the AI-voice vendors in the survey (Sela, Flair, Structurely, Total Expert) publish dial and appointment metrics with no protective stop. A Texas AI-voice suit alleges an AI kept selling after repeated refusals (market survey), which is the failure this design prevents.
- **Compliance design:** **ECOA and Reg B:**
- Age, date of birth, marital status and voice-inferred traits are never inputs. The triggers are conduct phrases and events, applied the same to everyone.
- Pause outcomes are tested by matched pairs (taken paired tester lab) and kept immutably.
- A pause only delays automation. It never denies credit. Any adverse action stays with the creditor under Reg B.

**BSA/AML:**
- 31 CFR 1029.320 SAR duties belong to the sponsor or lender. The platform only supplies a case file.
- SAR confidentiality: nothing reveals to the subject that a SAR is being considered.

**Senior Safe Act (12 U.S.C. 3423):** its immunity may not reach nonbank mortgage companies [COUNSEL]. The training module is offered either way.

**TCPA:** the pause only removes touches. The human check-in is a service call under existing consent.

**Biometrics:** no voiceprints or speaker-ID embeddings (BIPA, Texas CUBI, Washington). 'Another voice' is detected from turn-taking in the transcript, not from biometrics.

**GLBA:** case files stay in the tenant and are read-audited.

**AI behaviour:** M8 never accuses anyone. It says only 'let me have [first name] follow up with you personally'.
- **Moat:** Trigger phrase lists and scam scripts change quickly, and an MLO-owned network can share the patterns (phrases and event types, never borrower data) across every tenant through the guard pipeline. A single-lender tool can't. Sponsors' compliance teams can approve a platform that can show it stops automation in these cases, which makes the platform easier to adopt broker by broker.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: (1) Age is a protected basis under ECOA. Conduct-based triggers ('another voice answering', hesitancy, confusion) will in practice fire far more often for older borrowers. If a pause delays processing or closing, that is a discouragement or disparate-impact risk, even though no credit is denied. (2) Detecting 'another voice' usually relies on speaker diarization, which creates speaker embeddings, arguably a voiceprint under BIPA, Texas CUBI and Washington RCW 19.375. (3) Offering a 'trusted contact' means sharing NPI with a third party, which needs the borrower's GLBA authorization. (4) The Senior Safe Act's immunity does not cover nonbank mortgage companies (correctly flagged). (5) RMLO SAR duties (31 CFR 1029.320) sit with the sponsor, so the case file must not leak to the subject (correct). Redesign: Limit the pause to automated marketing and AI outreach only, and never delay processing, disclosures or closing. Have the pause trigger a human contact within one business day. Use content-only triggers with no diarization, so no voice features are processed. Make the trusted contact a borrower-signed authorization limited to specific fields. Run the matched-pair fair-lending test on pause rates by age band using synthetic testers before launch. / keep 6/10: Exploitation detection exists in banking and brokerage (EverSafe, Carefull, bank call-center vulnerability flags, FINRA 4512 trusted contacts). I know of no mortgage CRM or AI-voice vendor with a conduct-based stop on automation. It is new to mortgage, and the 'stop automation, human takes over' design is materially better than the competitors' metrics-first voice products. Redesign: Implement it as a D3 gate state plus guard phrase patterns, not as a desk. Ship the trigger list with paired-tester checks and the SAR case-file export for the sponsor. / keep 5/10: Low frequency but low cost: it is a set of guard trigger rules plus a hold state in the D3 gate, both of which already exist. The detection is unreliable: 'another voice' from turn-taking in transcripts will give false positives, and scam phrase lists need upkeep. The SAR case-file builder has value only to sponsors with an AML program, and most small brokers' AML programs are thin. Not a revenue feature; it reduces risk. Redesign: Ship it as guard rules: proceeds to a non-borrower, a stated pressure source and scam phrases set a file to 'protective pause' and send a Today card to the LO with the excerpts. Drop turn-taking voice detection and the network phrase sharing until there is real incident data. The case-file export is a later add.
- **Sources:** https://www.fincen.gov/ (FinCEN Advisory FIN-2022-A002 on elder financial exploitation, June 2022; training knowledge, VERIFY), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-31/subtitle-B/chapter-X/part-1029 (AML program and SARs for nonbank residential mortgage lenders and originators, from market survey; host blocked this session), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/3423 (Senior Safe Act immunity and covered institutions; training knowledge, VERIFY scope), https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/4512 (trusted contact person; training knowledge), https://www.mondaq.com/unitedstates/consumer-law/1821332/ai-robocalls-tcpa-and-texas-converge-in-class-action (AI kept selling after 'no', from market survey), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/ (ECOA age as a prohibited basis; training knowledge)


## LOMA Desk: get a past client's house out of the mandatory flood requirement, when the facts support it (round 2, medium impact, ~5 weeks)

**An AI agent screens each client's property against FEMA flood maps and free elevation data. Where a structure may sit above the flood line, it assembles the free FEMA LOMA request for the borrower to file. It then tracks FEMA's decision and drafts the borrower's letters to the servicer and insurer.**

- **Problem:** Homes that are mapped inside a Special Flood Hazard Area, but actually sit above the base flood elevation, still carry mandatory flood insurance. That can cost the borrower hundreds to thousands of dollars a year in escrow. FEMA's Letter of Map Amendment (LOMA) is free and can be filed online by the homeowner. From training knowledge, not re-verified: the MT-EZ form for a single structure, the Online LOMC portal, and a decision typically within about 60 days of a complete request. Few borrowers know it exists, and the paperwork (deed or plat, tax map, sometimes an elevation certificate) defeats them. Under NFIP Risk Rating 2.0 a LOMA may not cut the NFIP premium much, but it removes the federal mandatory-purchase requirement, so the borrower can choose. Market survey facts relevant here: Cotality projects shortages for about 65% of escrow accounts in 2026, and NFIP authorization has lapsed during funding gaps.
- **How it works:** **Data:**
- FEMA National Flood Hazard Layer (free web services) for the zone and the base flood elevation where published.
- USGS 3DEP lidar elevation (free) for a screening estimate of ground height at the footprint. This is a screen, not a determination.
- The flood certificate in the loan file, or the borrower's flood declarations page.
- Recorded deed or plat from the borrower or public records.

**Triggers:**
- A closed loan whose flood certificate says SFHA.
- A past client who tells M8 their flood premium went up.
- A borrower request.

**What the system does:**
- Labels the case 'likely out as shown', 'possible with an elevation certificate' or 'unlikely', with the evidence.
- Assembles the MT-EZ package from the borrower's documents.
- The borrower files it under their own name in FEMA's Online LOMC. The platform is never the applicant.
- Gives a neutral way to find a licensed surveyor through the state licensing board's search, with no referral relationship.
- Tracks FEMA's case status from emails the borrower forwards.
- On a LOMA: drafts the borrower's request to the servicer to release the mandatory-purchase requirement, and their note to the insurer about cancellation or refund options under NFIP rules, which the borrower decides on.
- Removes the flood item from the escrow forecast once the servicer confirms.

**What M8 does:** on a consented service call, after the AI disclosure, it explains the map, the screen result, the cost (free to file, a surveyor if needed) and the timeline. It never promises a result and never advises dropping coverage.

**Each party's experience:**
- **Loan officer:** a service-only Today card ('3 clients may qualify for a LOMA').
- **Borrower:** a possible real saving from their loan officer, with no sales pitch.
- **Technologies:** FEMA NFHL web services (ArcGIS REST), USGS 3DEP elevation service, Geocoding and building-footprint match (open footprint data), Document OCR for the flood certificate and declarations page, M8 consented service voice and chat with the guard, QStash delayed jobs for the FEMA clock, Today cards (D5) and the escrow forecast (taken idea 10, Escrow Shock Shield) as consumers, E-SIGN for borrower letters
- **Why it's new:** The market survey's 'Insurance Readiness' opening flags missing flood insurance before closing. The taken 'coverage guard' prevents force-placed insurance after a non-renewal. Idea 10 forecasts escrow shocks. None of them helps a borrower lawfully get out of the mandatory requirement. LOMA help today comes from paid flood consultants and surveyors working with individual homeowners, not from the borrower's loan officer, and no mortgage CRM in the survey (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert, Homebot, Surefire) offers it. Homebot sends one-way public-record AVM email with no flood or escrow work (ideas file and survey).
- **Compliance design:** **RESPA 8:** no surveyor, flood consultant or insurer pays the loan officer or the platform anything, and the surveyor directory is a neutral, public, state licensing board search. Hazard and flood insurance are settlement services under Reg X 1024.2.

**Insurance licensing:** M8 explains requirements and FEMA's process only. It never recommends a carrier, a coverage level, or dropping flood coverage. The borrower decides with their insurer.

**TCPA:** this is a service purpose. AI calls need prior express consent, with the AI and recording disclosures first. The card isn't marketing and carries no refinance content.

**Fair lending:** screening is per property, for clients whose own file shows an SFHA certificate, or on request. It is never area or tract targeting, and flood or climate scores never enter contact ranking (survey: neighborhood risk scores as redlining proxies).

**Truthfulness:** the screen result is labelled an estimate. FEMA alone decides.

**GLBA:** the flood certificate and declarations page are nonpublic personal information under the tenant's audit. FEMA and USGS calls send only the address coordinates.

**No rate talk.**

**[COUNSEL]:** whether preparing the MT-EZ for the borrower touches any state's unauthorized-practice or surveying rules.
- **Moat:** Per-county document patterns (which recorder records satisfy FEMA, typical elevation-certificate needs) and screen-accuracy calibration build up with every member's cases. They contain no nonpublic personal information, only geography and outcome rates. The network's escrow, PMI-exit and LOMA services together make 'my loan officer saves me money after closing' a habit borrowers have only with this network.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: No likely violation. FEMA's MT-EZ and Online LOMC are designed for homeowners to file themselves, and NFHL and USGS 3DEP data are public. Three cautions. (1) A '3DEP likely out' screen shown to a borrower may lead them to drop or reduce coverage before FEMA decides. That raises UDAAP risk, and the lender may still require flood insurance at its discretion even after a LOMA. (2) Messages to past clients offering a free service from the MLO are probably not telephone solicitations, but under the FCC's dual-purpose doctrine any relationship-building content can make them telemarketing. Keep them strictly service. (3) Preparing a filing for someone else may touch surveying-practice rules if the platform estimates elevations, and the screen must not be presented as an elevation determination. Redesign: Hide 'likely out' screen results from the borrower until a surveyor's elevation certificate or FEMA's decision exists. Show the borrower 'possible; here is how to check' only. Add 'keep your flood policy until FEMA and your servicer confirm in writing'. / keep 5/10: Paid LOMA consultants, surveyors and some flood-determination vendors already help homeowners and lenders file LOMAs, and FEMA's Online LOMC is free and self-service. A screen run by the originating MLO for their own SFHA past clients is not offered by any CRM I know of. Novel delivery, but a small population and a real risk of over-promising from lidar screening. Redesign: Keep it as a service play on past clients whose flood certificate shows SFHA, with a conservative 'unlikely or possible' label. Defer the escrow-forecast integration. / drop 5/10: The data is free (FEMA NFHL, USGS 3DEP), and a removal saves a client hundreds to thousands of dollars a year, so the goodwill is strong. But only a small share of any book is in an SFHA, and most structure-based LOMAs need an elevation certificate from a paid surveyor, so 'likely out' hits are rare. The lender or servicer may still require flood coverage after a LOMA. Lidar screening accuracy at footprint scale is uncertain, and false hope is a risk. Use per MLO is once a year at most. Redesign: Make it an annual batch screen, not a desk. For past clients whose file shows an SFHA certificate, run an NFHL plus lidar check and generate a service card saying 'possible LOMA candidate, elevation certificate likely needed', with a link to FEMA's free Online LOMC. Skip MT-EZ assembly and FEMA status tracking at first.
- **Sources:** https://www.fema.gov/flood-maps/change-your-flood-zone (LOMA, MT-EZ, Online LOMC; training knowledge, host blocked this session, VERIFY), https://hazards.fema.gov/femaportal/onlinelomc/ (Online LOMC portal; training knowledge, VERIFY), https://www.fema.gov/flood-maps/national-flood-hazard-layer (NFHL services; training knowledge), https://www.usgs.gov/3d-elevation-program (3DEP elevation; training knowledge), https://www.cotality.com/resources/property-taxes-are-reshaping-servicing-operations (escrow shortages 2026, from repo research), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1024/subpart-A/section-1024.2 (hazard and flood insurance as settlement services, from market survey), https://www.fema.gov/flood-insurance (NFIP authorization lapses, from market survey)


## Project Book: a shared, dated record for every condo and HOA project (round 2, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**Each time a member's file touches a condo or HOA project, the facts about the building (never the borrower) become a dated, cited entry, so the next MLO in that building sees the problems before an offer is written.**

- **Problem:** Condo and HOA files tend to die late. The usual causes are a pending special assessment, critical repairs, litigation, an expiring master policy, too much commercial space or single-entity ownership, a lapsed FHA or VA approval, or a questionnaire that takes weeks to arrive. GSE project rules on critical repairs and special assessments tightened after Surfside, and states added inspection regimes (Florida SB 4-D milestone inspections and structural reserve studies, California SB 326 balcony inspections). Each MLO rediscovers the same building's problems one file at a time, usually after contract. No mortgage CRM models collateral, and the GSE project tools (Fannie Condo Project Manager, Freddie Condo Project Advisor) are lender-licensed, work per file, and say nothing about cost or timing.
- **How it works:** Data: (1) Project documents already in a member's file: the HOA questionnaire, budget, reserve study, master-policy declarations, milestone or balcony inspection letters, and the management company's contact. M8 extracts only project-level fields: special assessments with amounts and dates, a critical-repair flag, a litigation flag, the reserve percentage, master-policy carrier, limits and expiry, commercial-space share, owner-occupancy share, and the questionnaire's fee and turnaround. (2) Public lists where their terms allow: HUD's FHA approved-condominium lookup, with approval expiry, the VA condo report, and state inspection filings that are published as open data. Trigger: a property address enters a pre-approval, a listing kit, an Agent Line call or a new file, and it resolves to a known project. The system shows a Project Card listing each fact with its source, its date and an expiry ('stale after 90 days'). It lists what is still unknown, gives a pre-contract checklist, and warns when the FHA approval or the master policy lapses before the projected closing date. The MLO sees the risk before the buyer writes an offer and orders the questionnaire on day one. The borrower hears: 'This building's last questionnaire, dated March, noted a special assessment. Your loan officer will confirm the current status with the HOA.' The agent gets the same card free in the listing kit on equal terms, never sponsored by the MLO. Phase 2 adds an insurer-writing layer: which carriers bound master or HO-6 policies in the building recently, k-anonymous, used only to start insurance shopping earlier.
- **Technologies:** Document extraction with M8 under lib/m8 redaction, which drops unit numbers, owner names and loan data before anything leaves the tenant, A project entity resolver (address normalization plus legal-description and parcel matching), Freshness model: each fact has a source, an as-of date and a half-life, Public data ingesters (HUD FHA condo list, VA condo report, state open data), Contribution delay queue: a fact is released to the commons only after the contributing file closes or withdraws, or after 30 days, Today card and Agent Line integration
- **Why it's new:** The GSE tools are lender-side and per file. HUD and VA publish approval status only. Questionnaire vendors (management-company portals such as HomeWiseDocs or CondoCerts) sell the document each time and keep no cross-file memory. Market survey: no CRM in the segment (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert, Jungo, Surefire) handles collateral. It differs from the taken ideas: the contingency clock and deal clock track one file's deadlines, the builder completion desk covers new construction, and the coverage guard covers a past client's own policy. None of them builds a shared record of a building. The data only becomes useful at network scale, since a single MLO rarely sees the same building twice in a year.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA: only facts about the building are pooled. Unit number, owner and borrower names, loan amount and rate are removed in the tenant before any contribution, and the release is delayed and unattributed, so the commons never reveals that 'a member had a file in building X this week'. Document rights: the questionnaire and reserve study stay in the contributing file. Only extracted facts are shared, because facts aren't copyrightable, and documents whose license forbids even that are blocked [COUNSEL]. Nothing from GSE tools enters the commons unless their terms allow it. Fair lending: project status never feeds contact ranking, outreach targeting or any area-level score. It is used only for preparing the file at hand. The product offers no 'avoid these buildings' lists and no neighborhood roll-ups, and the fair-lending commons tests that no play uses it. AI: M8 says 'as of [date], per [source]' and never says 'eligible' or 'approved', because eligibility is the lender's decision. It never quotes a rate. RESPA 8: agents get the card free, identical for every agent, and the MLO pays nothing on any agent's behalf. Antitrust: the record contains no prices, fees charged by members, compensation or lender terms. Rewards: contributing earns nothing. Ownership rewards stay subscription- and tenure-only, and the comp firewall has no contribution field.
- **Moat:** Coverage and freshness grow with every closing in a building. A competitor with 1,000 MLOs on one LOS can't match a cross-sponsor network, because buildings change hands among brokers. A building's history (assessments, policy renewals, inspection outcomes) builds up over years and can't be bought later.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: (1) Contract terms, not copyright, are the main obstacle. HOA questionnaire, resale-certificate and condo-doc vendors (HomeWiseDocs, CondoCerts and management portals) commonly license documents for the single transaction. Contract law can bar even pooling extracted facts, so 'facts aren't copyrightable' doesn't settle it. (2) In small buildings (2 to 10 units), building-level facts such as special assessments and litigation can identify the owners, and fresh entries can reveal that a sale is in progress. (3) Pooled building-risk data across sponsors creates fair-lending risk if anyone uses it to avoid buildings that skew minority or LMI. The ban on roll-ups helps, but each MLO's own pre-contract warnings still decide where buyers are advised to buy. (4) Agents receiving a free card each time is fine only if it is equal and not tied to referrals. Redesign: Have counsel review each document source's license terms, and accept contributions only from sources whose terms allow derived facts. Set a minimum unit count, for example 20 or more, before a building's entries are pooled, and delay and coarsen dates. Add fair-lending monitoring to see whether project-card warnings correlate with tract demographics. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted: Fannie Mae's Condo Project Manager and Freddie Mac's Condo Project Advisor already give lenders a shared project-level record across lenders, with PERS and approval status, and questionnaire vendors (HomeWiseDocs, CondoCerts) sell the documents. These are lender-side, so a broker-side memory that pools facts across sponsors is new, but GSE data can't feed it, which limits it to member-extracted facts. Redesign: Start with a per-tenant project card (FHA/VA list status plus the file's own questionnaire facts) and ask wholesale lenders for CPM status. Pool facts across sponsors only after counsel clears document rights and the network has enough density. / drop 4/10: A real pain in condo-heavy markets (special assessments, reserve shortfalls, master-policy gaps after the post-Surfside GSE rules). But the value depends on cross-member repeat visits to the same building, which are rare until the network is large. Questionnaire and reserve-study vendor licenses (HomeWiseDocs, CondoCerts) may limit even extracting facts, which is unverified. GSE project tools (Fannie Condo Project Manager) are lender-only, and HUD's FHA condo list is public and already used. Redesign: Phase 1, per-tenant only: extract project fields from the MLO's own questionnaires and show a dated project card with an FHA list lookup and expiry warnings. Start cross-member pooling only after counsel clears the document licenses and member density in a metro supports it.
- **Sources:** https://entp.hud.gov/idapp/html/condlook.cfm (HUD FHA approved condominium lookup; not opened this session), https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b4-2.1-03/ineligible-projects (Fannie Selling Guide B4-2.1-03; DNS-blocked this session), https://www.flsenate.gov/Session/Bill/2022A/4D (Florida SB 4-D; not opened this session), https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/billNavClient.xhtml?bill_id=201920200SB326 (California SB 326; not opened this session), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 2.2 and 2.3, Market survey segment 1 (no CRM covers collateral), segment 3 (data licensing limits on derivative datasets)


## Shared Security Desk: the FTC Safeguards Rule run as a network, with cross-tenant fraud-domain blocking (round 2, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**Every small sponsor and MLO on the platform gets a working Safeguards Rule program (passkeys, scans, incident runbook, evidence binder, an optional shared Qualified Individual), and a spoofed domain or malicious attachment one member reports is blocked for every member within minutes.**

- **Problem:** Under the FTC Safeguards Rule (16 CFR 314), a nonbank broker needs a Qualified Individual, a written risk assessment, MFA, encryption, annual penetration tests, vulnerability scans every six months, an incident response plan and FTC notice within 30 days for breaches of 500 or more consumers. Solo MLOs and small brokers rarely have this, and sponsor security review is the real obstacle to bringing brokers onto a new platform (market survey, LOS segment). Their mailboxes are also the main target for real estate wire fraud. A one-off security packet answers the questionnaire. It doesn't run the program, and it doesn't learn from attacks on other members.
- **How it works:** Data: platform telemetry (sign-ins, passkey coverage, device checks, admin actions, read-audit logs), plus technical threat indicators that members' connected mailboxes and the Counterparty Atlas produce: sender domains, lookalike registrations, attachment hashes, URLs and IPs, with no message content. Triggers: a member flags a phishing email, the mailbox connector finds a lookalike of an atlas counterparty's domain, or the scheduled scan or test calendar comes due. What the system does: it shares the indicator network-wide after automated checks and a confirmation threshold, quarantines matching emails in every member's mailbox, and puts a banner on any file whose emails touched it. It runs the Safeguards calendar (scans, a pen-test vendor slot bought for the whole network, staff training, access reviews), keeps the incident runbook with the 30-day FTC clock, and generates each sponsor's binder: risk assessment draft, controls, evidence, a list of service providers and the annual report to the board. A sponsor can also name the platform's security lead as its Qualified Individual, which 314.4(a) allows for a service-provider employee as long as the sponsor keeps responsibility and oversight. The MLO sets up a passkey once and then barely sees the desk. The borrower sees fewer spoofed 'your wire instructions changed' emails succeed. The sponsor's compliance officer gets a binder that is always current.
- **Technologies:** WebAuthn passkeys and phishing-resistant MFA for every account (extends D1), Mailbox connectors (Microsoft Graph and Gmail API) with indicator extraction inside the tenant, Threat-indicator sharing in STIX/TAXII format, with confidence scoring and a confirmation threshold, Lookalike-domain and newly registered domain detection (RDAP, homoglyphs), Evidence collection from audit logs into a generated Safeguards binder, Network-bought vulnerability scanning and penetration testing
- **Why it's new:** Vendor security packets (market survey idea) are static documents. Managed security providers sell to firms one at a time and know nothing about mortgage counterparties. The taken fraud signal exchange is a Section 314(b) AML consortium about borrower and transaction fraud, not cyber indicators, and the taken closing week conductor checks wires per file. A shared indicator network pays off only at scale. A shared Qualified Individual plus bought-together testing turns a fixed compliance cost into a subscription feature, and that only works because many small sponsors share one program.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA and Safeguards: indicators are technical only (domains, hashes, IPs). Message bodies, borrower NPI and recipient lists never leave the tenant. The sponsor stays the financial institution responsible for its program, and the platform is a contracted service provider under 314.4(f) oversight, with the Qualified Individual appointment in writing [COUNSEL]. Privacy and wiretap: mailbox scanning runs only on business mailboxes the member connects, with a notice to the member's own staff, and is limited to security purposes. No AI training and no retention beyond the indicators [COUNSEL: ECPA provider and consent exceptions, CIPA]. Information-sharing law: the design doesn't depend on the federal Cybersecurity Information Sharing Act of 2015's liability and antitrust protections, whose status after its 2025 sunset I couldn't verify this session. Only indicators are shared, which keeps antitrust risk minimal [COUNSEL]. Antitrust: no prices, terms or customer information. Reg Z comp: the shared program is part of the flat seat, never priced per loan. Copy: no 'guaranteed secure' language (check:copy).
- **Moat:** Detection gets better with every member mailbox and every atlas counterparty. Each sponsor's binder fills with years of evidence that is costly to rebuild elsewhere. Buying penetration testing for the network lowers per-sponsor cost, which compounds as the network grows.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Legally sound. Under 314.4(a) a Qualified Individual may be a service-provider employee if the institution keeps responsibility, designates a senior member to oversee, and requires the provider to keep a program. The 30-day FTC notification applies to notification events involving 500 or more consumers (effective 2024). Two cautions. (1) Scanning connected mailboxes and auto-quarantining across tenants is acting on members' communications, which needs ECPA consent from the member as provider or party, notices to staff, and care in all-party states. (2) Quarantining based on another member's report without human confirmation could withhold legitimate borrower emails and delay a closing. CISA 2015's status after its 2025 lapse is uncertain, and the design correctly doesn't rely on it. Redesign: Make cross-tenant quarantine 'banner plus hold for review' rather than silent removal, with a fast release path. Get a written Qualified Individual appointment and oversight acknowledgement from each sponsor. Get counsel sign-off on the ECPA and state wiretap consent language before the mailbox connector ships. / drop 3/10: Refuted. Mortgage-focused managed IT and vCISO providers already sell FTC Safeguards programs to small brokers, including Qualified Individual services, risk assessments and pen tests. Email security vendors (Proofpoint, Abnormal, Microsoft Defender) already share threat indicators across customers, and FS-ISAC exists. It is not a differentiator and it pulls the platform into managed security. Redesign: Publish the platform's own Safeguards evidence for sponsors as part of vendor management, and partner with an MSP for the rest. No shared Qualified Individual and no mailbox scanning. / keep 6/10: Real, recurring need: small brokers are covered by the FTC Safeguards Rule, which requires a written program, a Qualified Individual, annual pen testing, periodic vulnerability scans, a board report and FTC notification of qualifying events. Most small shops don't have these, and they would pay for them. A shared program priced into the seat is a credible differentiator. But mailbox scanning and cross-tenant indicator blocking duplicate what Microsoft 365 and Google Workspace already do, and they bring ECPA and wiretap scope. Acting as the Qualified Individual adds liability. CISA 2015 status after its 2025 sunset was not verified this run. Redesign: MVP: a Safeguards binder generator (risk assessment template, controls mapped to platform evidence, service-provider list, incident runbook with the 30-day FTC clock, annual report), passkeys, and a network-priced pen-test and scan vendor slot. Defer mailbox scanning, indicator sharing and the shared-QI offer until counsel and insurance are in place.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (FTC Safeguards Rule; from market survey, DNS-blocked this session), https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/ftc-safeguards-rule-what-your-business-needs-know (FTC guidance; not opened this session), https://www.cisa.gov/resources-tools/resources/cybersecurity-information-sharing-act-2015-procedures-and-guidance (CISA 2015; status not verified this session), https://www.ic3.gov/AnnualReport (BEC losses; not opened this session), Market survey segment 2 opening on a security-review packet for sponsors


## Language Commons: member-reviewed, multilingual M8 with a glossary that covers the whole loan (round 2, medium impact, ~5 weeks)

**Bilingual licensed MLOs across the network review and version M8's explanations, scripts and glossary in each language, built on the FHFA mortgage translations, so a borrower who chooses Spanish, Vietnamese, Chinese, Korean or Tagalog gets reviewed language from first chat to closing, and any gap is stated up front.**

- **Problem:** Limited-English-proficient borrowers are underserved, and an AI that translates on the fly makes mistakes on mortgage terms that become UDAAP and fair-lending problems: marketing in a language but servicing only in English, or mistranslating 'pre-approval' as approval. One MLO can review one language at most. Uncommon languages never get reviewed. The 'Live Interpreter Bridge' in the ideas doc translates calls live and leaves the quality question unresolved.
- **How it works:** Data: the FHFA Mortgage Translations glossary and translated documents as the base, M8's response templates and play scripts, and reviews from bilingual NMLS-licensed members (accept, edit or flag, with a reason). Trigger: a borrower picks a language. The language is offered to every borrower and never inferred from a name or location. What the system does: M8 uses only reviewed glossary terms and reviewed templates for that language. Every reply carries the glossary version, and unreviewed phrasing falls back to English with a note. A 'language coverage' line states which steps are available in that language (chat, texts, documents, closing) and which are English-only, before the borrower relies on it. The red-team suite (guard commons) runs in each language, including rate-without-APR and promise checks. The MLO sees the borrower's language preference and which steps are covered. The borrower gets accurate, consistent language with an honest statement of its limits.
- **Technologies:** A versioned multilingual glossary seeded from the FHFA Mortgage Translations, A review workflow restricted to NMLS-verified bilingual members, with inter-reviewer agreement scoring, M8 constrained generation (glossary-locked terms) and per-language guard tests, Language-preference field and coverage map per step, Google speech and voice for supported languages (C15 and C16 runtime)
- **Why it's new:** UWM Mia added Spanish for one wholesaler (market survey). Generic voice vendors translate without mortgage review. FHFA provides static documents, not a conversational system. The taken guard commons pools safety failures, not language quality. Reviewed coverage of less common languages appears only when the network has enough bilingual licensed reviewers.
- **Compliance design:** Fair lending and ECOA: language is offered to everyone and never inferred or used in ranking. In-language marketing is allowed only where in-language service is available or the gaps are disclosed up front, which follows the CFPB's 2021 statement on serving limited-English-proficient consumers (its current status after the 2025 guidance withdrawals is unverified, so treat it as best practice) [COUNSEL]. Documents: the official disclosures (LE, CD) stay controlling, and translations are labeled as aids. State translation rules such as California Civil Code 1632 are checked per loan type [COUNSEL]. AI disclosure comes first in every language. Rates appear only from priced results, with APR and the not-a-commitment line translated from reviewed templates. TCPA: opt-out words are honored in every supported language as a reasonable revocation method (extends D4). Rewards: reviewers earn no economic credit. Reviews are recognized only with a badge, so comp and ownership stay subscription- and tenure-only.
- **Moat:** The number of reviewed languages and the depth of review grow with the network's bilingual membership. A reviewed, versioned glossary and per-language red-team results build up over time, and they become a recruiting draw for bilingual MLOs.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: (1) California Civil Code 1632 requires a translated copy of the contract or disclosures, in Spanish, Chinese, Tagalog, Vietnamese or Korean, when certain loans, including residential-real-property loans arranged by a broker under 1632(b)(4) as I recall, are negotiated primarily in that language. Offering M8 in those languages can trigger that duty, and labelling translations as 'aids' doesn't cure it. (2) The federal LEP guidance landscape changed in 2025: EO 14224 designated English as the official language and revoked EO 13166. CFPB and FHFA LEP materials, including the FHFA Mortgage Translations clearinghouse and the URLA language preference question, may have been withdrawn or changed. I could not verify this; search was exhausted. Building on FHFA materials whose status is unknown is a source risk. (3) Any rate or APR in translation must exactly match the English guard. (4) Under the TCPA, STOP-equivalents in each language are reasonable revocation (good). Redesign: Before launching any language, have counsel map the state translation-of-contract duties (CA 1632, plus similar rules such as Oregon's for certain loans). Turn on a language only when the loan's own disclosures can be delivered as the law requires, or else block 'negotiation' in that language. Verify the current status of the FHFA glossary and license terms before using it. Run guard and red-team in each language as a release gate. / keep 5/10: Partly existing: FHFA Mortgage Translations, UWM Mia's Spanish support, and many vendors offering machine translation or Spanish AI. Reviewed coverage across a whole loan, with a coverage line stated up front and a red-team run per language, is not known to ship. It is a moderate gain whose value depends on recruiting bilingual MLOs. Redesign: Start with Spanish only, on the FHFA glossary, with the coverage line and per-language guard tests. Add more languages only when reviewer supply exists. / drop 4/10: Spanish demand is large and commercially real. Vietnamese, Korean, Tagalog and Chinese are niche per MLO. Volunteer bilingual reviewers with no economic credit won't sustain review across all templates and red-team suites. Every added language multiplies guard and red-team maintenance. The FHFA Mortgage Translations glossary is a free base (from training knowledge; not re-checked this run). Redesign: Do Spanish only, reviewed by paid bilingual licensed staff or contractors, with the FHFA glossary, a coverage line and translated STOP and opt-out words. Add another language only when a member cohort commits reviewers and file volume.
- **Sources:** https://www.fhfa.gov/mortgagetranslations (FHFA Mortgage Translations; not opened this session), https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_lep-statement_2021-01.pdf (CFPB LEP statement 2021; not opened this session, status unverified), https://www.mpamag.com/us/specialty/wholesale/uwm-expands-mia-ai-assistant-with-spanish-language-capability/575330 (repo source), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (Live Interpreter Bridge), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (C15 voice and C16 mic runtime)


## Contributor Lane: units for bona fide platform work, never for loans or for buying a seat (round 2, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**MLOs earn equity units by doing reviewed, time-boxed work that makes the platform better for everyone: play templates, M8 red-team cases, cited state-rule rows, script translations, beta testing. The grants run under a consultant agreement, which is the one equity route the survey found still open to people who aren't employees.**

- **Problem:** The survey found that Rule 701 is likely closed to subscribing MLOs because they are customers, not employees or consultants, and that the SEC never adopted the platform-worker rule. It also found that units handed out for paying a subscription look like a sale of securities: the subscription fee becomes partly an investment, so Reg D, Reg CF or Reg A paperwork applies to every seat. Idea 12 (platform-months) and the planned two-lane plan reward tenure and purchases, but nothing gives an MLO equity for the expertise only working MLOs have. That expertise (what a good past-client play sounds like, which state rule actually bites, which prompt breaks M8) is what keeps the product ahead.
- **How it works:** Data and trigger: a contribution registry, built as Git-backed pull requests or an in-app equivalent, lists open bounties. Each bounty has a published, fixed unit value set by rubric and time-box, not by outcome. Examples: verify one state's quiet-hours and mini-TCPA row with primary citations; write 10 red-team cases in the format of tests/m8-redteam-cases.ts; draft a play's copy that passes check:copy and the guard; translate and back-translate a disclosure script; run a 2-week beta of a new connector and file a structured report. Members choose each quarter, one member one vote and never weighted by units or production, which bounty categories get the next quarter's fixed unit budget. What the system does: it routes each submission to two reviewers. State-rule and disclosure rows also go to counsel, because nothing reaches the D3 rules table or the prompt without counsel. Accepted work merges with contributor credit shown in the play gallery. The D12 ownership ledger then records a grant tagged 'Rule 701, consultant, services: <bounty id>'. It tracks the Rule 701 12-month cap and the $10M enhanced-disclosure threshold, holds each person to a per-year unit cap, issues a 1099 and keeps the signed consulting agreement. What the MLO does: picks bounties from a 'Build with us' tab, signs the contributor agreement once, and sees their units, vesting and plan documents, with no dollar projections. Recruits see contributor credits on plays and red-team cases, which shows them a platform built by working MLOs. Borrowers and agents see nothing, except that the scripts and guards they meet are better.
- **Technologies:** Git-backed contribution registry (GitHub PRs or in-app review queue), tests/m8-redteam harness accepting member cases, D3 rules table with counsel_status per row, check:copy and lib/m8/guard.ts as automated reviewers, D12 ownership ledger with exemption tags and Rule 701 cap counters, E-signature for contributor agreements, Stripe or payroll 1099 reporting, One-member-one-vote ballot for quarterly bounty budgets
- **Why it's new:** The survey found that every equity or revenue-share program for licensed professionals pays for transactions or recruiting: eXp (5% of commissions in stock), Real and One Real (RSUs gated on production or a recruit closing), reAlpha (trailing production), NEXA (10 bps per recruit loan plus servicing cash flow) and Epique (first-sale stock). None pays MLOs in equity for improving the shared product. Idea 12 pays for recruits' subscription months, and the survey's two-lane plan covers earned tenure units and purchased units, but neither uses the consultant route or turns members into the R&D team. I found no mortgage technology vendor with a member-contributed, counsel-reviewed rules and red-team commons. Caveat: web search and law.cornell.edu were unavailable this run, so the Rule 701 details below are from training knowledge and still need checking.
- **Compliance design:** Securities: Rule 701(c) covers consultants and advisors who are natural persons providing bona fide services that are not tied to a capital-raising offer and do not promote a market for the issuer's securities. Its 12-month limits are the greatest of $1M, 15% of total assets or 15% of the class outstanding, with enhanced disclosure above $10M. Exchange Act Rule 12g5-1(a)(8) keeps holders of compensatory plan securities out of the 12(g) holder count (training knowledge, unverified; counsel to confirm). So the lane never pays for recruiting, for promoting the units, or for paying a subscription, and the subscription stays a flat fee with no units attached. Reg Z 1026.36(d): grants are for software services unconnected to any transaction, with no loan, pricing or pipeline field anywhere in the bounty schema. The idea-12 comp firewall's database role and CI privilege test also cover the grant engine. State 'paid only by your sponsor' rules (Texas SML guidance, Cal. B&P 10137, Arizona) appear to concern pay for licensed origination activity, and this pays for non-licensed software work [COUNSEL per state]. Where counsel says no, the MLO's grant routes to the payer of record as a subscription credit, or the lane is closed in that state. RESPA 8: agents can't contribute or hold units (survey: keep agents off the cap table). Copy: no earnings claims, no 'commission', and units are described as possibly worthless. GLBA: bounties never touch borrower data, and red-team cases are synthetic only. Fair lending: play copy contributions go through the fair-lending check before they ship.
- **Moat:** It compounds. Each accepted rule row, red-team case and play improves every tenant at once, and only a network of working MLOs can produce that content at volume. Contributors own part of the thing they built, so leaving means walking away from vesting units and from credit for work everyone uses. A large competitor can hire staff to write plays, but it can't offer its customers equity for doing it.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: Securities. Rule 701 covers consultants who are natural persons providing bona fide services not tied to capital raising, as stated. But it also requires a written compensatory plan, and as I recall the consultant must provide services to the issuer as an individual. State blue-sky law needs its own exemption: California 25102(o) requires a plan that meets 10 CCR 260.140.41 to .46, and other states vary. A large group of customer-consultants getting equity looks like an offering to customers. Pricing grants with no FMV/409A-style valuation creates tax and securities-disclosure risk. Licensing comp: state rules that an MLO may be paid only by their sponsor are often written broadly as 'any compensation' (for example the Texas SML rules and Cal. B&P 10137 for DRE licensees), not only for origination. Paying licensed MLOs any compensation from a non-sponsor entity may be barred in some states whatever the work is. Mentor-type bounties near recruiting look like recruiting rewards. Redesign: Before launch, get a per-state counsel matrix on whether a non-sponsor may pay a licensed MLO for non-origination work. Where it can't, route the grant through the sponsor or close the lane. Use a written 701 plan with a valuation, per-state blue-sky exemptions and per-person caps. Exclude any bounty that touches recruiting or onboarding of other MLOs. Keep units fully separate from subscription status. / keep 6/10: Equity or tokens for product contributors exist outside mortgage (open-source bounty and Rule 701 consultant grants at startups). The survey found no mortgage program that pays MLOs for improving the shared product rather than for production or recruits. It is novel in its segment and serves the founder's ownership goal directly. Securities and state comp risk are high, and the Rule 701 details are unverified this run. Redesign: Keep it, but make counsel's Rule 701 and state comp opinion a gate before build. Start with red-team cases and cited rule rows only, with a small fixed unit budget. / drop 4/10: It fits the founder's 'MLOs get a stake' goal, but the economics and legal cost are heavy for a small team. It needs a Rule 701 written compensatory plan, a 409A valuation, per-grant consulting agreements, 1099s, cap-table administration, per-state licensing review and two reviewers per submission. Reviewing bounty content (rule rows, red-team cases) costs staff time on par with writing it. Weekly MLO participation will be a small minority. The Rule 701 details are from training knowledge, not re-verified this run. Redesign: Fold it into the D12 ownership plan as a small, capped category, not its own lane. Launch only after counsel approves the plan, with a few high-value bounty types (cited state-rule rows, red-team cases) and fixed unit values. Staff writes the rest.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 2.4 and 4.8, /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md idea 12, https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/chapter-II/part-230/subject-group-ECFR6e651a4c86c0174/section-230.701 (canonical; not opened this run, host blocked and search budget exhausted), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/chapter-II/part-240/subject-group-ECFR4dd95ad5b8f4fd1d/section-240.12g5-1 (canonical; not opened), https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1495932/000110465926019145/expi-20251231x10k.htm (survey: eXp equity tied to commissions), https://mortgageorb.com/one-real-mortgage-launches-compensation-and-stock-awards-programs-for-los (survey), https://nationalmortgageprofessional.com/news/nexa-removes-guardrails-its-100-revenue-model (survey), https://www.cooley.com/news/insight/2020/2020-12-01-sec-proposes-changes-rule-701-form-s8 (repo-cited: platform-worker proposal never adopted)


## Lift-Out Guard: team moves where each person decides alone and the reward engine can't pay for raiding (round 2, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**When several MLOs from one company look at the platform, the system structures their move so that every departure is an individual decision. Recruiter credit is blocked wherever it could reward soliciting current coworkers in breach of a covenant, and the platform keeps a timestamped record that it never induced a breach.**

- **Problem:** In mortgage, recruiting happens through team and branch moves, and the litigation follows: diverted leads, employee non-solicits, Gmail forwarding and breach of loyalty by a departing team lead (Griffin v. West Capital, Citizens v. SoFi, Stockton, CCM; repo research). A platform that pays recruiters (idea 12's platform-months, even as credits) can become evidence in a tortious-interference or raiding suit if a team lead earns credit for bringing colleagues while still employed alongside them. The mobility protocol and Move-In Week cover one person's book and license transfer, but nothing protects the group dynamics, or the platform's own reward engine, from becoming the raiding tool.
