- **How it works:** Data and trigger: the join flow detects several pending applicants from the same current sponsor, by self-declared current employer and NMLS company ID that they enter themselves (no NMLS scraping). What the system does: (1) Recruiter-link guard: a recruiter-of-record link can't be created between two people who share a current sponsor, or where the recruiter attests a non-solicit of employees covering the recruit, until that covenant's end date. Those joins carry no recruiter credit. (2) Separate rooms: there are no group invites or team join links. Each applicant's join conversation and documents are private, and nobody, including the team lead, sees who else joined until each person's own start date. (3) Covenant intake in the browser: the applicant tags clauses in their own agreement without uploading it, covering customer and employee non-solicits, notice period, garden leave, database ownership and return-of-property duties. Those dates feed D2 hold states and the send gate, plus a 'questions for your own employment lawyer' sheet with citations to state statutes (for example Cal. B&P 16600, WA RCW 49.62, OR ORS 653.295, Tex. Bus. & Com. Code 15.50), stating no conclusions. (4) Clean-exit checklist with attestations: employer devices and data returned, no forwarding to personal email, no exported lists, signed before the seat activates. (5) An evidence log of every platform contact with each applicant, kept for counsel. What the MLO sees: a calm, step-by-step individual plan. What the destination sponsor sees: readiness status per person, never the old company's data.
- **Technologies:** Join-flow clustering on self-declared employer and NMLS company ID, Comp firewall (idea 12) recruiter-link constraint and CI test, In-browser clause tagging (no upload, network calls blocked, same pattern as Zero-Upload Book Check), D2 hold states and the D3 send gate for covenant dates, Per-applicant access control and delayed visibility, Append-only evidence log with KMS-signed export
- **Why it's new:** Taken ideas cover the individual mover: the mobility protocol (taking a book lawfully), Move-In Week (license transfer) and Zero-Upload Book Check (auditing a book privately). None addresses coordinated group departures, the recruiter's own exposure, or the risk that the platform's reward engine itself becomes evidence of raiding. Competitors' recruiting rewards (NEXA's per-recruit bps, One Real's recruit RSUs, Loan Factory's 'cash or RSUs' for referring an LO; survey) actively reward bringing colleagues, with no covenant check visible in their public terms.
- **Compliance design:** Restrictive covenants and tort risk: the platform never advises that anyone may solicit anyone. It records attestations and dates and points to the person's own lawyer [COUNSEL: employment law in each state; FTC non-compete rule status, which from training knowledge was set aside in Ryan LLC v. FTC (N.D. Tex. 2024) and whose appeal the FTC later dropped, unverified this run]. Reg Z and state comp rules: recruiter rewards stay subscription-and-tenure-only credits (idea 12) and are now also covenant-gated. Antitrust: the guard blocks raiding incentives but never coordinates with employers on not hiring anyone, so there's no no-poach agreement. Any applicant may join on their own. GLBA and trade secrets: no prior-employer data is uploaded, D2 holds stay on, and attestations are required. TCPA: covenant dates become send-gate blocks, so outreach can't start early. Copy: policy language only, no scarcity or 'bring your team' pitches.
- **Moat:** Sponsors who join through the network get fewer departure lawsuits, which makes the platform the destination their compliance teams prefer. Over time the evidence log and covenant data, kept per person and never pooled, give the platform a record of clean moves that a raiding-incentive competitor can't claim. It also protects the ownership plan from being reclassified as a recruiting bounty.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: This is protective. The platform's unilateral decision to withhold recruiter credit is not a no-poach agreement. (1) Detecting that several applicants come from the same sponsor and then deliberately holding back coordination can itself become evidence that the platform knew of a group lift-out, so the evidence log cuts both ways in discovery. (2) The 'questions for your lawyer' sheet must avoid conclusions (UPL). (3) Ryan LLC v. FTC set aside the FTC non-compete rule in 2024, and the FTC later dropped its appeal (from memory, unverified). State law governs. Redesign: Keep the log factual and limited to the platform's own contacts. Have counsel set its retention, and keep it under privilege where possible. Never show the destination sponsor that applicants share a prior employer. / keep 5/10: No known platform gates recruiter credit on covenants or keeps an evidence log of individual decisions. Competitors (NEXA, One Real, Loan Factory) reward recruiting outright. It is novel and protective, but it is a defensive policy layer, not a feature that attracts users, and it overlaps Move-In Week and idea 12. Redesign: Fold it into Move-In Week and idea 12's recruiter-of-record logic: the covenant-gated credit, separate join rooms and attestations. No standalone product. / keep 5/10: Small build on top of existing D2 holds and the recruiter-of-record link: a covenant gate on recruiter credit, no group links and attested clean exits. It protects the ownership plan from being recast as a recruiting bounty and lowers lawsuit risk, which matters to sponsors. It triggers rarely and earns no revenue directly, so it is a guardrail, not a product. Redesign: Ship it as join-flow rules inside D12 and the mobility protocol: block recruiter credit between people at the same current sponsor or under an attested employee non-solicit, add the covenant tagging and clean-exit attestation, and keep the evidence log. No separate UI.
- **Sources:** https://www.housingwire.com/articles/griffin-funding-alleges-west-capital-lending-former-los-diverted-borrower-leads/ (repo-cited), https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/citizens-sues-sofi-as-fights-over-talent-pipelines-persist (repo-cited), https://www.housingwire.com/articles/stockton-mortgage-gmail-lawsuit-2/ (repo-cited), https://www.housingwire.com/articles/ccm-enforcement-action-non-solicitation/ (repo-cited), https://nationalmortgageprofessional.com/news/nexa-removes-guardrails-its-100-revenue-model (survey), https://apply.workable.com/leadling/jobs/view/2B59E9EC2A.md (survey: Loan Factory cash or RSUs for referring an LO), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 12, Move-In Week, Zero-Upload Book Check)


## Honest Twin Trial: a recruit runs the real Today screen and send gate on a synthetic copy of their book, built from numbers they type in (round 2, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**A prospective MLO types a few rough facts about their book (closings per year, loan mix, how many have provable text consent) and gets a fully synthetic twin with fake names. They then see exactly what Command Center would do with it on day 1, day 30 and day 90, including how much is blocked and why.**

- **Problem:** Recruits decide based on demos with perfect sample data, then find on day one that their imported book is almost entirely 'relationship only' and M8 can't call anyone. That gap destroys trust, and it is the main finding of the repo's idea review. The recruit also can't safely try the product on their real book: exporting it from their employer's CRM may itself breach their agreement. So the honest answer to 'what will this do for me?' is unavailable just when they're choosing between Command Center and a large company.
- **How it works:** Data: no file at all. The recruit types estimates (about 400 closings since 2017; 60% purchase; states WA and OR; roughly how many contacts have written text consent, 'probably none'; agents they work with, as a count). Trigger: 'Try it on a twin' on the package page. What the system does: it generates a synthetic book in the browser with seeded fake names (clearly marked synthetic), realistic dates and an all-relationship-only consent state unless the recruit says otherwise. It then runs the real, unmodified lib/today/rank.ts, the D3 send-gate decide() and the Re-permission Sprint simulator over it. The output is a 90-day storyboard: 'Day 1: 0 contacts M8 may call; 7 you should hand-dial this week, and why. Day 30: if the sprint gets the response rates you enter, N contacts give written consent.' The recruit sets the response-rate slider and the system supplies no default success rate. Every blocked action shows the gate's plain-English reason. What the recruit experiences: the actual product logic, the actual compliance blocks, and a realistic picture with no data risk. What the sponsor or platform learns: nothing about the recruit beyond an opt-in 'contact me' button. The twin isn't stored server-side.
- **Technologies:** Client-side synthetic data generator with a seeded PRNG, lib/today/rank.ts and lib/send-gate/gate.ts run unchanged in the browser, Re-permission Sprint simulator with recruit-set response rates, Design-system storyboard UI (components/ui/*), Network-call blocking on the trial page, as in Zero-Upload Book Check
- **Why it's new:** Zero-Upload Book Check (taken) audits a real book locally. The twin uses no real data at all and runs the full product, Today, the gate and the sprint, as a simulation, which also sidesteps the employment-agreement risk of exporting a real book. Vendor demos in the survey (Shape, Bonzo, Total Expert) use sample data and advertise outcome lifts, such as Flair's dial and handoff counts, without denominators. Showing a recruit the blocks before they join is the opposite of the 'reactivate your database with AI' pitch the survey found across Empower LO, Structurely and Flair.
- **Compliance design:** No data risk: no file, no names and no employer data, and the twin never leaves the browser, so there are no GLBA, trade-secret or non-solicit issues. Copy and earnings claims: the storyboard shows only counts of contacts and actions, never dollars, loans or income, and response rates are the recruit's own inputs, labelled hypothetical. check:copy runs on all trial strings. TCPA: the simulation applies the real gate and so teaches the consent rules, and nothing is ever sent. Fair lending: the generator never synthesizes demographic or tract attributes. Ownership: the trial shows the plan documents as they stand, with units 'as approved by counsel' and no projections.
- **Moat:** Weak on its own, but it strengthens the core trust position. The trial stays truthful automatically because it runs the shipped code, so every improvement to ranking, the gate or the sprint shows up immediately and competitors can't fake it with a curated demo. It turns the platform's compliance strictness from an onboarding shock into a reason to choose it.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Low compliance risk: synthetic data stays in the browser, and the output shows counts only, never dollars. One remaining risk: even counts ('N contacts give written consent by day 30') at slider values the recruit sets could be read as implied outcome claims in recruiting materials. The FTC earnings-claim and business-opportunity concerns apply only weakly here because no money is shown. Synthetic generators must never produce realistic real-looking names that collide with real people's PII. Use obviously fake names. Redesign: Default the slider to 0%, and label every projection 'your hypothetical input; not a prediction'. Use clearly synthetic names (for example 'Sample Contact 041'). / drop 3/10: Sandbox demos on synthetic data are standard in SaaS, and the taken Zero-Upload Book Check already gives recruits a no-risk audit. Running the shipped gate code is a nice honesty touch, but the idea itself says it is weak on its own. Redesign: Add a 'synthetic book' option to Zero-Upload Book Check that runs the same code, rather than building a separate trial. / keep 6/10: Cheap: client-side synthetic data generation running shipped code (rank, gate, sprint simulator). It answers the recruit's real question, 'what will this do with my book on day 1', without data or non-solicit risk. It is a sales aid, so its value depends on recruiting volume, and outcomes rest on the recruit's own slider inputs. Low ongoing cost. Redesign: Build it once the Today ranking, the gate and the re-permission sprint are stable. Keep it browser-only, with counts only and no dollars.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D3 (send gate), D5 (Today ranking), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (finding 1: imported books are relationship only; idea 2 sprint; Zero-Upload Book Check), https://www.housingwire.com/articles/west-capital-flair-ai-voice/ (survey: outcome counts without consent methodology), https://www.structurely.com/news/insights/how-mortgage-teams-can-reactivate-dormant-leads-with-ai-powered-outreach (repo-cited)


## Before You Sign: a second opinion on anything secured by the home (round 2, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Before a past client signs a PACE assessment, a solar loan, lease or PPA, a home equity investment, contractor financing or a sale-leaseback offer, they text a photo of it to their loan officer. M8 explains in plain words what it does to their home's title, to a future refinance and to a future sale, and the loan officer follows up personally. Nobody is paid for any outcome.**

- **Problem:** Homeowners get pitched products secured by their home at the door, by contractors and online: PACE assessments (California is the largest market and one of LoanM8's states), solar arrangements with UCC fixture filings, home equity investments (Point, Hometap, Unlock, Splitero), and equity-stripping sale-leasebacks. They usually see these without anyone on their side. The damage often shows up years later. A PACE lien generally has to be paid off before a Fannie, Freddie or FHA refinance or a sale, a solar fixture filing holds up closing, and an HEI settles at a share of future value the owner never priced. The loan officer, the one professional who knows the borrower's first lien, hears about it after it's recorded. The CFPB's PACE rule brought PACE under Reg Z ability-to-repay (final December 2024, effective about 2026-03-01; MED, host blocked this session), and its January 2025 HEI spotlight flagged high effective costs. Neither rule gives the homeowner a second opinion at the moment they decide.
- **How it works:** DATA: the offer the borrower sends: a photo or PDF texted to the loan officer's number, uploaded in M8 chat, or forwarded by email. It is treated strictly as data, never instructions. The borrower's own first-lien facts (idea 4 or the POS). An encumbrance rules table in config, with citations, counsel status and effective dates: agency and FHA treatment of PACE liens; GSE solar rules for leased panels, PPAs and fixture filings; how HEI settlement and subordination work; and state home-equity-sale and distressed-conveyance statutes such as Cal. Civ. Code 1695 and WA RCW 61.34 [MED, verify]. Today's priced snapshots for the HELOC or cash-out alternative, through idea 5's planner.
TRIGGER: always the borrower. They send the document or ask "is this a good idea?". An annual service message, sent to every consented past client alike, reminds them the service exists.
SYSTEM: a server-side extractor (zod schema, redaction first) pulls the lien type, recording and priority, payment and term, settlement formula, transfer and subordination clauses, and any cooling-off or rescission rights. It then produces a one-page "what this does to your home" summary: whether it records a lien and with what priority, what a future refinance or sale would require ("paid off first", "provider consent needed"), and the effective cost over the borrower's own horizon. Figures are labeled estimates and an HEI's cost is shown as a range. Where it fits, it shows an honest side-by-side with alternatives: a HELOC or cash-out from priced results, or a neutral explainer while a HELOC program isn't approved for display. Doing nothing is always one of the options. The summary never says "don't sign"; it explains, then names free help (a HUD-approved housing counselor, the state regulator, an attorney).
MLO: gets a priority Today card with the summary ("Ana sent a PACE contract, $38k at 8.9%, recorded ahead of her mortgage. She plans to sell in 3 years.") and calls personally. A red flag (a sale-leaseback, a deed transfer clause, signs the borrower is being pressured) moves the card to the top and suggests the regulator or counselor links.
BORROWER: gets the explanation within minutes, inside their own deadline, from someone who already knows their loan. The file is deleted after extraction.
- **Technologies:** Twilio MMS inbound plus M8 chat upload (D4 inbound route), AI vision extraction with zod-validated schemas and lib/m8 redaction, Encumbrance rules table in config (effective-dated, counsel-signed), Idea 5 equity planner and lib/pricing priced snapshots, M8 output guard (no rate outside a priced result, no disparagement), Today card priority lane, HUD housing-counselor locator link and state regulator table
- **Why it's new:** What exists: PACE administrators, solar installers and HEI providers sell directly. Consumer guides (CFPB spotlights, state warnings) are generic. Homebot shows equity, not the effect of a specific offer. Idea 5 (equity planner) starts from a cash need the loan officer surfaces, and the Equity Planner and HEI-comparison openings compare financing choices. This idea is reactive and protective: it reads the third party's actual contract against the borrower's real first lien and answers "what does this do to my next refinance or sale?" before signature. I found no loan officer CRM, servicer tool or consumer app that does this. That finding comes from my memory and the surveys, not re-verified live: search budget exhausted, consumerfinance.gov and ecfr.gov blocked in this session.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: borrower-initiated. The annual reminder is a service message to consented contacts only, and the follow-up call is personal. Any financing alternative offered by M8 call or text needs prior express written consent. RESPA 8 and state insurance and anti-rebating laws: the platform and loan officer accept nothing of value from PACE administrators, installers, HEI providers or contractors, and never refer the borrower to them. Reg Z: alternatives are shown only from priced results with APR and the not-a-commitment line. HELOC trigger terms follow 1026.16, and HELOC programs are not displayed until reviewed. Loan officer pay: a platform comp-parity attestation means the loan officer's pay doesn't change whether the borrower signs, takes a HELOC, refinances or does nothing (HELOCs fall outside 1026.36(d), so this is platform policy) [COUNSEL]. UDAAP and disparagement: factual explanations only, with no claims about providers, and the guard blocks pejoratives. Unauthorized practice of law: labeled an explanation, not legal advice, with attorney and counselor pointers [COUNSEL]. Fair lending: PACE and HEI sales have a history of concentrating on older and minority homeowners, so the service is announced the same way to every past client and is never targeted by age, neighborhood or any proxy. Any red-flag rule uses only what's in the document. GLBA: the uploaded contract is encrypted, redacted, deleted after extraction, and every read is audited. AI disclosure precedes the M8 explanation.
- **Moat:** Each new document type adds extraction templates and rule rows: a provider's settlement formula, its subordination policy, a county's PACE program terms. These are drawn from public contracts and program documents, never from borrower data, so every member's borrowers benefit from every other member's files. The rule table is effective-dated and counsel-signed, which a solo loan officer can't afford to maintain. And the habit it builds, "text my loan officer before I sign anything on my house", makes the originating loan officer the borrower's first call for the life of the loan, which large lenders' call centers can't replicate.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: This is a protective design and borrower-initiated. Four risks. (1) The MLO has a conflict of interest: the alternatives offered (HELOC, cash-out) are the MLO's own products, so a 'second opinion' that steers to them is UDAAP-risky. Comp parity helps but doesn't remove the bias in how the explanation is presented. (2) Explaining the legal effect of a PACE, HEI or sale-leaseback contract is close to UPL. (3) Since the CFPB PACE rule (EGRRCPA 307, effective 2026-03-01 per my training; current status unverified), PACE is Reg Z credit with ATR, so explanations must be accurate on TILA rights. (4) WA RCW 61.34 and Cal. Civ. Code 1695 are correctly identified as distressed and home-equity sale statutes. Redesign: Always lead with 'doing nothing' and free third-party help (a HUD counselor, the state regulator), and put the MLO's own products last. Disclose 'your loan officer offers some of these alternatives' on every summary. Have counsel approve the per-document-type templates for UPL. / keep 7/10: Only generic consumer guides exist (CFPB and state warnings). PACE administrators, solar installers and HEI providers sell directly, and no LO tool or consumer app reads the third party's actual contract against the borrower's real first lien before they sign (training knowledge). It is novel and protective and builds a 'text my LO first' habit. UPL and disparagement need care. Redesign: Keep it. Absorb Junior Lien Keeper's PACE path. Start with PACE and solar lease/PPA, where the lien and refinance effects are clearest, and add HEI and sale-leaseback later. / keep 5/10: Borrower-initiated and infrequent, but it reuses the existing document parser and M8, so the cost is mainly the encumbrance rules table (PACE treatment, solar leases and UCC fixture filings, HEI settlement terms, state equity-sale statutes). The retention value is real and hard to copy. Risks: UDAAP and unauthorized-practice-of-law exposure if the explanation edges into advice, and few borrowers will think to send the contract before signing. Redesign: MVP: four document types (PACE, solar lease or PPA, HEI, sale-leaseback), a one-page 'what this records and what a refi or sale needs' explainer, priced alternatives only when available, and an LO card. Announce it in the annual past-client service message.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/final-rules/residential-property-assessed-clean-energy-financing-regulation-z/ (CFPB PACE rule; URL from memory, host blocked this session; effective date about 2026-03-01 is MED), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/data-research/research-reports/ (CFPB home equity contracts spotlight, January 2025; MED, from the survey), https://app.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=61.34 (WA Distressed Property Conveyances Act; not opened), https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/codes_displayText.xhtml?lawCode=CIV&division=3.&title=&part=4.&chapter=2.&article= (Cal. Civ. Code 1695 home equity sales contracts; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/40/ (HELOC rules; from the survey), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 5 equity planner), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md section 4b (HELOC panel and display rules)


## Home Record Watch: deed fraud, surprise liens and the lien-release clock (round 2, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**With the owner's consent, Command Center watches the public record on each past client's own home. A deed or lien they didn't expect sends an alert the same day with next steps. When a loan the loan officer refinanced is paid off, it runs the state's statutory clock for the old lender to record the lien release and drafts the borrower's demand letter if the release is late.**

- **Problem:** Seller-impersonation and deed fraud target homes owners don't watch, and owners usually find out at sale. Mechanic's, HOA, judgment, PACE and UCC solar liens appear without the owner noticing. Then there's a gap almost nobody covers: when a refinance pays off the old loan, the old lender must record a release or reconveyance within a state deadline (for example Cal. Civ. Code 2941 and Ariz. Rev. Stat. 33-707, which carry statutory damages; MED, verify). Missed releases cloud the title and block the next sale or refinance years later. The originating loan officer closed the refinance but never checks that the release recorded. Many counties offer free recording-alert services that few owners know about. Consumer "title lock" subscriptions are sold directly to homeowners (vendor marketing), with no tie to the person who knows the loan.
- **How it works:** DATA: (1) a county table in config listing which recorders offer free owner-notification or property-fraud-alert enrollment, with the signup URL and what each covers [VERIFY per county; examples recalled are LA County, Maricopa, Harris]. (2) Where a county has none, a licensed recorded-document feed for that one address only (First American DataTree or ATTOM under an enterprise, multi-tenant license whose permitted use covers verifying ownership and lien information, which is the DataTree EULA's own stated purpose). (3) The payoff date and old lender, taken from the loan officer's own refinance closings (POS) or the borrower's Closing Disclosure. (4) A lien-release statute table by state, with the deadline, who must record, penalties and citations, counsel-signed.
TRIGGER: the borrower opts in on the consent passport with a new service purpose, "home record alerts". Every past client is offered it the same way at closing and once a year. Watches run per address, never as lists of owners.
SYSTEM: first, it deep-links the borrower to their county's free alert so the borrower enrolls themselves, and the platform stores nothing it doesn't need. Otherwise it checks the licensed feed weekly. New deed or ownership document: a same-day service text plus an urgent loan officer card, with steps (the county recorder's fraud contact, local law enforcement, the owner's title insurance claim line from their closing package, a link to the FBI IC3 report form). New lien: a plain-language explanation of what was recorded and by whom, and the Before You Sign summary if it's a PACE or solar filing. Payoff: when a refinance funds, a clock starts on the old lien's release. On the statutory day plus a grace period, with no release recorded, it drafts the borrower's own demand letter to the old lender or servicer citing the state statute, and tells the loan officer.
MLO: sees "2 releases overdue" and "1 deed alert" on Today, calls personally on deed alerts, and gets a closing-quality metric: the share of their refinances whose old liens released on time.
BORROWER: gets an alert they asked for, about their own home, with exact next steps, and can cancel any time.
- **Technologies:** County recorder alert table in config (effective-dated, sourced), Licensed recorded-document API (DataTree or ATTOM) under a multi-tenant enterprise license, Data-rights ledger and use gate (per-field license tags; property data never a credit factor), Consent passport with a new service purpose, POS payoff events (ARIVE Zapier or webhooks) and the CD parser, State lien-release statute table, PDF letter generation; D4 texting through the D3 send gate, QStash scheduled watches
- **Why it's new:** What exists: county free alerts, which owners have to find for themselves. Consumer title-monitoring subscriptions sold direct (vendor claims; not verified this session). Property-data vendors selling owner-named lists to prospect from, the opposite of this idea. Title and servicer post-closing departments, which work for the lender, not the owner. Nothing in the taken list watches recorded documents or the lien-release deadline. Refund Radar tracks money owed back, not recorded releases. No mortgage CRM surveyed (Bonzo, Shape, BNTouch, Jungo, Total Expert, Homebot) monitors the client's title or checks that the old lien released after the loan officer's own refinance. Not re-verified live: search budget exhausted.
- **Compliance design:** Data licensing: watches run only for an owner who asked, one address at a time, never as lists of individuals. That respects Washington's commercial-use bar on agency lists of individuals (RCW 42.56.070, AGO 2019 No. 3) and similar rules, and the use gate enforces it [COUNSEL per state]. The vendor license must expressly permit multi-tenant use for ownership and lien verification, because store EULAs are internal-use only. FCRA: recorded data is never used as a credit-eligibility factor (DataTree and RETR terms both bar it), so the use gate blocks that path. TCPA: service purpose with consent. Nothing in an alert mentions refinancing; any later marketing needs prior express written consent. GLBA: the address and loan link are nonpublic personal information, held under row-level security with audited reads, and alerts never reveal one client's records to another loan officer. RESPA 8: no payments from title agents, insurers or monitoring vendors, and pointers to them are neutral and free. Unauthorized practice of law: the demand letter is the borrower's own, worded from the statute and counsel-approved, labeled not legal advice. Fair lending: offered identically to every past client. California DROP and Daniel's Law suppression run before any watch is created, and the platform never sells or shares the watch list, which keeps it outside data-broker registration [COUNSEL]. AI disclosure on any M8 explanation.
- **Moat:** Every member adds counties to the alert table and statute rows to the release table, and a solo loan officer could never keep 3,000 recorder formats and 50 statutes current. The licensed feed is bought once for the whole network, priced into the flat subscription, and never tied to loans. Fraud patterns seen across watched homes (document types, timing) can feed the Section 314(b) fraud exchange under that idea's controls. And the on-time-release metric becomes a closing-quality standard sponsors adopt.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: This is a sound owner-consented, address-by-address design that respects RCW 42.56.070 and the CA DROP and Daniel's Law checks. (1) Vendor licenses (DataTree, ATTOM) must expressly permit multi-tenant monitoring for consumers. Standard EULAs are internal-use. (2) Section 314(b) participation is open only to FinCEN-registered financial institutions. The platform cannot participate itself, and sharing watched-home data must run through sponsors under 314(b) and only for suspected money laundering or terrorist financing. (3) The 'closing-quality metric' (share of releases on time) depends on old servicers, not the MLO, and if used in marketing it is a misleading claim. It duplicates Release Watch. Redesign: Merge with Release Watch. Route any fraud-pattern sharing only through sponsor 314(b) registrations. Keep the release metric internal. Get the multi-tenant license term in writing before any feed is used. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted: many county recorders run free owner-notification alerts, consumer title-monitoring services (Home Title Lock and others) are sold direct, and lender and title companies track lien releases. Combining them is materially better: the originating MLO enrolls the client in the free county alert and runs the statutory release clock with a ready demand letter after their own refinance. The licensed recorded-document feed is costly and depends on its license. Redesign: Merge Release Watch in. Phase 1: deep-link enrollment in county alerts plus the release clock from the MLO's own payoffs. Buy the licensed feed only for counties with no free alert, once there is demand. / keep 4/10: The cheapest part is strong: many county recorders run free property-fraud alert enrollments (examples recalled: LA County, Maricopa, Harris; not re-verified this run), so deep-linking the borrower costs nearly nothing. The paid feed for other counties (DataTree or ATTOM, multi-tenant terms unconfirmed) costs money per watched address and produces almost no hits. The release clock overlaps Release Watch and title agents' own work. Redesign: Phase 1: an opt-in 'enroll in your county's free alert' link offered at closing and once a year, plus a release-confirmation task (merged from Release Watch). Buy a recorded-document feed only after license terms permitting multi-tenant ownership monitoring are confirmed in writing and a cost per address is known.
- **Sources:** https://dnastore.firstam.com/content/pdf/DNA_Online_Store_EULA_2.9.2023.pdf (DataTree EULA: ownership and lien verification use, no FCRA or credit use; from the survey), https://www.attomdata.com/solutions/delivery/property-data-api/pricing/ (ATTOM API from $500/month; from the survey), https://www.azleg.gov/ars/33/00707.htm (Arizona release of mortgage; not opened, MED), https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/codes_displaySection.xhtml?lawCode=CIV&sectionNum=2941 (California reconveyance; not opened, MED), https://app.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=42.56.070 (WA commercial-purpose list bar; from the survey), https://www.ic3.gov/ (FBI complaint portal for real estate fraud), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D2 to D4 (consent purposes, send gate, Twilio)


## Churn Guard: no refinance outreach the borrower can't benefit from (round 2, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Before any refinance card, rate-watch text or M8 mention reaches a past client, the send gate checks the borrower's own note against the hard windows. It blocks the outreach with the reason and the earliest lawful date, and every refinance that does go ahead comes with a borrower-signed record of what they gain. The hard windows are VA IRRRL seasoning and 36-month recoupment, FHA streamline seasoning and net tangible benefit, cash-out seasoning, HOEPA flipping limits, any state net-benefit rule, and the wholesaler's early-payoff clawback window from the loan officer's own broker agreement.**

- **Problem:** When rates dip, refinance outreach to recent borrowers is the fastest money in mortgage and the fastest way to hurt a borrower. VA's anti-churning statute (38 U.S.C. 3709: 210 days, 6 payments, fee recoupment within 36 months), FHA streamline seasoning and net tangible benefit rules, and Ginnie pooling seasoning exist because lenders churned veterans in 2016–2018. Wholesale broker agreements commonly claw back the broker's pay if a loan pays off within about 120–180 days (MED, varies by lender). Today these limits are checked at underwriting, if at all, after the borrower has already been pitched. Rate-watch and refinance-ripeness features (plan 5.4 and 5.6, Today's refinance card in D5) are exactly what could churn a client if nothing gates them.
- **How it works:** DATA: the borrower's note date, first payment date, payments made, program, rate and fees, from the POS or verified loan facts (idea 4). A program-rules table in config: VA 3709, FHA streamline seasoning and the net-tangible-benefit test, GSE cash-out seasoning, HOEPA 1026.34(a)(3) refinance limits, and state net-benefit or anti-flipping statutes, each with citation, effective dates and counsel status. A private per-tenant lender-terms table, entered by the loan officer from their own broker agreements: the EPO window and early-payment-default terms. It is never pooled. Today's priced snapshots for the costs and rate of the candidate refinance.
TRIGGER: every refinance-purpose decision in the D3 send gate (cards, rate watch, Time Capsule topics about refinancing, M8 call or text drafts) and every new refinance application in the POS.
SYSTEM: adds a gate rule. Inside a window, the touch is blocked with a plain reason ("VA seasoning: earliest IRRRL note date 2027-04-02" or "Your lender's 180-day early-payoff window ends 2027-01-14") and a private reminder is set for the loan officer on the earliest date. Outside the windows, the card leads with recoupment computed from the priced example (fees ÷ monthly savings, months to break even) and the program's benefit test. If the test fails, the card says so and nothing is sent. At application, it generates a one-page Benefit Record with the old and new payment, rate, costs, break-even month, total-interest change over the borrower's stated horizon, and the program test result. The borrower signs it electronically and keeps a copy; it's stored as evidence.
MLO: sees why a client is suppressed and when they become eligible, and avoids clawbacks they didn't track.
BORROWER: never gets a refinance pitch that can't pay off for them. When they do refinance, they hold a signed record of the benefit.
- **Technologies:** D3 send gate rule plugin with effective-dated rule rows, Program-rules table in config (VA, FHA, GSE, HOEPA, state), Per-tenant private lender-terms table (EPO and EPD), lib/pricing priced snapshots, APR and amortization, POS application events (ARIVE), E-SIGN signature capture and PDF Benefit Record, Today card suppression reasons
- **Why it's new:** What exists: lenders and wholesalers enforce seasoning at underwriting or pooling, and EPO clawbacks are enforced afterwards by contract. CRMs and AI dialers (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert, UWM's Mia, Flair) run rate-drop and past-client campaigns on dates and rate thresholds, and the surveys show no outreach-layer gate on seasoning, recoupment or EPO windows. Plan 5.4 computes break-even, but nothing blocks outreach inside a legal or contractual window or produces a signed borrower benefit record. This was the survey's Churn Guard opening, refined here to cover every refinance channel and the Benefit Record. Not re-verified live: search budget exhausted.
- **Compliance design:** VA, FHA and Ginnie: the rules are enforced from the borrower's own note, as a stricter platform default, counsel-confirmed. HOEPA: flags any refinance of a high-cost loan within a year where the benefit test fails. Reg Z loan officer pay: the EPO table protects the sponsor's broker pay from clawback, but the gate never ranks or steers by pay. Suppression is purely about the borrower's eligibility and benefit, and pay never enters the ranking. TCPA: blocked touches are never sent, and permitted ones still pass consent, quiet hours and do-not-call checks. Reg Z advertising: every rate in a card or draft comes only from a priced result, with APR and the not-a-commitment line. Fair lending: the same rules for every borrower in a program. Program eligibility (VA, FHA) is a lawful program fact, not a proxy, and the fair-lending check runs on the ranking that remains. Antitrust and confidentiality: lender terms stay private to each tenant, because broker agreements are often confidential and pooling them is the rejected Lender Pulse pattern. E-SIGN: the Benefit Record is signed under E-SIGN 7001(c) consent. GLBA: audited reads.
- **Moat:** A counsel-maintained, effective-dated table of federal, program and state refinance limits becomes the rule set sponsors and their investors trust. Because the platform is owned by loan officers, "no churn" can be written into the member covenant and published as a standard large lenders' retention desks can't credibly adopt. Each new state, program and Benefit Record adds evidence for exams and for defending a complaint. A network-level churn metric, the share of refinances inside 12 months that failed a benefit test (target zero), becomes a quality signal sponsors and warehouse lenders can rely on.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: These are strong rules: the VA 38 U.S.C. 3709 seasoning (210 days and 6 payments) and 36-month recoupment, FHA streamline seasoning and NTB, HOEPA 1026.34(a)(3), and state net-benefit laws (from memory). One real issue: suppressing outreach because of the broker's EPO clawback window lets the MLO's compensation decide whether a borrower who would benefit hears about a refinance. That is not a 1026.36(e) violation, since it isn't steering among options, but it is a conflict, and it must never block borrower-initiated requests. The Benefit Record must not read as a waiver or a promise. Redesign: Have EPO and contractual windows suppress only MLO-initiated marketing. A borrower-initiated inquiry is always answered, with the earliest-date reason disclosed to the borrower where the borrower's own benefit test passes. Label the Benefit Record 'estimate as of [date]; not a guarantee'. / keep 7/10: Seasoning and net-tangible-benefit checks are already enforced at pricing and underwriting by PPEs, LOS VA NTB tools and compliance engines (training knowledge), so the rules themselves aren't new. Applying them at the outreach layer across every channel, with EPO windows from the broker's own agreements and a borrower-signed Benefit Record, isn't offered by CRMs or AI dialers. That makes it materially better, it is cheap given D3, and it fits the brand. Redesign: Keep it as a D3 gate rule. Hand the program rules to the Guideline Ledger so there is one cited table. / keep 8/10: Clear economics. EPO clawbacks in broker agreements (commonly a 120–180-day window, from industry knowledge) cost the broker its whole comp on a loan, and VA's seasoning and 36-month recoupment rule and FHA's streamline seasoning and net-tangible-benefit test are hard blocks that waste outreach if ignored. It is a small rules table plus a D3 gate rule using note dates the platform already holds. The Benefit Record also defends against churning complaints. Caveat: Wave Ready describes Churn Guard as a survey opening, so the novelty is the cross-channel gate and the Benefit Record, not the concept. Redesign: Ship it early as a D3 gate rule. Start with VA, FHA and the private EPO table, and add HOEPA and state net-benefit rows after counsel confirms them. Make the signed Benefit Record optional at first, and required once counsel signs off.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/38/3709 (VA anti-churning; HIGH), https://www.hud.gov/program_offices/housing/sfh/handbook_4000-1 (FHA streamline seasoning and net tangible benefit; HIGH), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (loan officer compensation; from the survey), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1026/subpart-E/section-1026.34 (HOEPA refinance limits; host blocked this session), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/7001 (E-SIGN consumer consent), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D3 and D5 (send gate, Today refinance card), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md sections 5.4 and 5.6


## Second-Lien Desk: subordination and draw-end for every HELOC on the book (round 2, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**Command Center knows every HELOC behind a past client's first mortgage, including the automated programs on the panel (Aven, HELIX, NFTYDoor). It starts the subordination request the day a first-lien refinance begins, warns the borrower a year before the draw period ends and payments jump, and shows the loan officer when folding the HELOC into a new first lien beats keeping both, priced and with the same pay either way.**

- **Problem:** Fintech HELOCs (Figure, Aven, HELIX, NFTYDoor) are creating millions of second liens with no relationship layer after funding (survey). Two problems follow. First, when rates fall and the past client refinances the first mortgage, the HELOC lender must agree to subordinate or the HELOC must be paid off. Subordination turnaround is unpredictable and can outlast the rate lock (MED, industry practice), and nobody starts it early. Second, when a HELOC's draw period ends, the payment can multiply as it starts amortizing, and the prime rate it floats on moves (6.75% after the December 2025 cut, MED). Borrowers rarely see that coming. HELOCs sit outside Reg Z's loan officer pay rules (1026.36(d) covers closed-end credit only), so pay could legally differ between a HELOC and a cash-out refinance. That's a steering incentive nobody neutralizes.
- **How it works:** DATA: HELOC facts from the loan officer's own HELOC closings (POS) or the borrower's HELOC statement or agreement, uploaded through the idea 4 parser with a new document type. Fields: lender, credit line, balance, index and margin, draw-period end, repayment term, lien position, subordination policy. The prime rate from FRED (series DPRIME, public). A per-program subordination playbook: contact, request form, fee, documents, maximum combined loan-to-value for subordination, typical steps. These are process facts, built from program documents and each member's files. Texas home-equity rules for TX homesteads (Tex. Const. art. XVI §50(a)(6) and (t)).
TRIGGER: (1) a first-lien refinance application in the POS, or a refinance card for a client with a known HELOC. (2) 12, 6 and 3 months before draw-end. (3) A change in the prime rate. (4) The borrower asking.
SYSTEM: (1) On day 1 of a refinance it builds the subordination package checklist, drafts the request with the borrower's authorization, starts a clock, and compares the expected timeline with the lock period. If the timeline is tight, it tells the loan officer before locking and hands the date to the lock planner. (2) Draw-end: it computes the post-draw payment from the borrower's own terms and sends a service-only explanation with the options: keep paying, refinance the HELOC, fold it into the first lien. Priced examples appear only where available and approved. (3) Consolidation ripeness: it compares keeping first lien plus HELOC with one new first lien, over the borrower's horizon, using priced results only, and Churn Guard's benefit test applies. (4) Texas homesteads: it enforces 80% combined loan-to-value, one home-equity loan per 12 months, the 12-day notice and the 2% fee cap before any option is shown.
MLO: sees "3 HELOCs reach draw-end in 2027; 1 subordination at risk on Tuesday's lock".
BORROWER: is never surprised by a payment jump or a refinance that stalls over a second lien.
- **Technologies:** Idea 4 document parser with HELOC statement and agreement types, FRED API (DPRIME) for prime rate changes, Per-program subordination playbook table, POS refinance application events and lock dates, lib/pricing amortization and priced snapshots, Texas home-equity rule rows in the D3 rules table, Consent passport service purpose; D3 send gate and D4 texting, Comp-parity attestation in the comp firewall
- **Why it's new:** What exists: HELOC originators (Figure, Aven and others) run the product but not the borrower's broader loan picture. Servicers send the required draw-end notices. Title and processors chase subordinations late, by hand. Reset Radar (taken) covers first-lien ARM resets and buydown step-ups from the note. This covers the open-end second lien: its subordination dependency inside the next first-lien transaction, its draw-end payment change, and pay parity. The CRMs surveyed have no awareness of HELOCs (survey: "no awareness of servicers, escrow, seasoning, EPO windows, HELOCs or assumptions"). It turns the survey's Second-Lien Lifecycle opening into a buildable desk tied to LoanM8's own HELOC panel. Not re-verified live: search budget exhausted.
- **Compliance design:** Reg Z: HELOC advertising trigger terms (1026.16) and closed-end trigger terms are enforced by the guard. Rates appear only from priced results, with APR and the not-a-commitment line. HELOC programs stay undisplayed until they have display consent or reviewed terms (CLAUDE.md 4b), and they are never described with first-mortgage assumptions (no tri-merge credit, no W-2s; one bureau, Plaid income). Loan officer pay: a platform comp-parity rule and quarterly attestation show the loan officer's pay is the same for a HELOC, a cash-out refinance or keeping both, because 1026.36(d) doesn't reach HELOCs [COUNSEL]. Texas: 50(a)(6) and (t) limits are hard blocks. TCPA: draw-end and subordination messages are service messages; consolidation offers are marketing and need prior express written consent. Fair lending: the same triggers for every HELOC holder, with no inference from neighborhood or demographics. GLBA: the borrower authorizes the subordination request, and every read is audited. HPPA: no credit data is used. Antitrust: the playbook holds process facts only. Turn-time statistics stay private per member unless counsel clears pooling.
- **Moat:** Every subordination a member runs improves the playbook for that HELOC lender (forms, addresses, documents, maximum combined loan-to-value), and the fintech HELOC wave keeps adding programs no loan officer can track alone. Because the platform also sits in front of the next first-lien refinance, it is the only place both liens' timelines meet. And as HELOCs grow, comp parity, proven by the same firewall as the ownership plan, becomes a compliance standard sponsors can point to.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: No likely violation. The Texas 50(a)(6) limits are correctly identified: 80% CLTV, one per 12 months, a 12-day notice, and a 2% fee cap that excludes certain items (from memory). (1) The HELOC programs (Aven, HELIX, NFTYDoor) are not yet reviewed for display, so draw-end messages must not name them as alternatives. (2) Consolidation suggestions are marketing and need PEWC. (3) Comp parity across HELOC and cash-out is platform policy only, because 1026.36(d) doesn't reach HELOCs, so enforce it technically. (4) A subordination request drafted for the borrower needs written authorization. Redesign: Enforce HELOC display gating via CONFIG flags. Make comp parity an attested firewall rule. Keep draw-end notices limited to the borrower's own terms, and add option content only under marketing consent. / keep 5/10: Subordination processing is already done by title companies and outsourced subordination services, and HELOC servicers send the draw-end notices. Starting the subordination on day 1 at the broker, with a lock-timing check, plus comp parity, is a real workflow gain no CRM offers, but it overlaps Junior Lien Keeper in this batch. Redesign: Merge with Junior Lien Keeper into one Junior Lien desk. Keep the day-1 subordination start, the lock-timing warning and the Texas 50(a)(6) blocks. / keep 6/10: Subordinations are a known cause of delay on refinances, and starting them on day 1 and checking them against the lock period saves real time and lock-extension fees on active files. That part has weekly value for refi-heavy MLOs. Draw-end and consolidation touches depend on knowing about HELOCs on past clients, which most books don't record. The per-lender subordination playbook is upkeep. The HELOC programs on LoanM8's panel aren't reviewed for display yet, so priced comparisons are blocked for now. Redesign: MVP: on any refi with a known second lien, generate a subordination checklist and request draft, start a clock and warn the LO before locking. Enforce the Texas 50(a)(6) blocks. Add draw-end notices only for HELOCs the MLO closed or the borrower uploaded.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (1026.36(d) scope, closed-end only; from the survey), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/40/ (HELOC rules; from the survey), https://statutes.capitol.texas.gov/Docs/CN/htm/CN.16.htm (Texas home-equity lending §50(a)(6) and (t); from the survey), https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/DPRIME (prime rate series), https://www.federalreserve.gov/monetarypolicy/openmarket.htm (late-2025 rate cuts; MED), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md section 4b (Aven, HELIX, NFTYDoor rules)


## Claim Desk: from the insurance check to a rebuilt home (round 2, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**When a past client has a fire, flood, hail or wildfire loss, M8 and their loan officer guide them through what servicers leave opaque: endorsing a claim check made out jointly to the borrower and the servicer, the investor's staged loss-draft release rules, the document package, and NFIP, FEMA and SBA help. It tracks every disbursement, and if the servicer stalls it drafts the borrower's own formal request.**

- **Problem:** After a covered loss on a mortgaged home, the insurer's check is usually made out jointly to the borrower and the servicer (the mortgagee clause). The servicer deposits it and releases money in stages under investor rules (Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac, FHA, VA), with inspections and paperwork the borrower has never seen. Rebuilds stall and contractors walk. LoanM8's five states have active wildfire, flood and hail exposure (the 2025 Los Angeles fires, Pacific Northwest wildfires, Texas hail and flood, Arizona monsoon). The plan's Disaster Desk only quiets marketing. Coverage Guard handles non-renewal and force-placed insurance. Hardship Lifeline handles missed payments. Nothing helps the borrower get their own insurance money released.
- **How it works:** DATA: FEMA declarations (OpenFEMA API) by county. The borrower's loan facts, including investor and servicer. The investor can be confirmed by the borrower through the public Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac loan lookup tools. A loss-draft rules table in config by investor and loan status: thresholds for an initial release, inspection triggers, staged disbursement, interest on held funds where a state requires it [VERIFY current Fannie and Freddie servicing guide sections and any California rules]. Uploads from the borrower: the claim letter, the check image, the contractor's bid and contract, lien waivers. A help-programs table: NFIP Increased Cost of Compliance (up to $30,000, MED), FEMA Individual Assistance non-duplication, SBA disaster home loans, forbearance options handed to Hardship Lifeline.
TRIGGER: the borrower tells M8 or the loan officer about a loss, or a FEMA declaration covers a past client's county. In that case the Disaster Desk's quiet mode applies and the loan officer gets a service-only call list. Nothing automated reaches the borrower without service consent.
SYSTEM: M8 (AI disclosure first) explains the process for this borrower's investor. It builds the document checklist for this servicer's loss-draft department, from a growing per-servicer library. It tracks each release against the rules table and the borrower's rebuild milestones. When a release is late, it drafts the borrower's written request for information or notice of error under Reg X 1024.35 and 1024.36 [COUNSEL: which servicing errors loss drafts fall under], and the existing "hold for you" agent can wait in the servicer's queue. When the rebuild finishes, it closes out with a service note: escrow re-analysis, insurance re-rating, and Coverage Guard for the renewal.
MLO: sees "2 past clients in the Maricopa declaration have open claims; $84k held by servicer, next inspection Friday", calls personally, and is the one person who knew what to do.
BORROWER: gets a step-by-step plan, a running tally of what's been released and what's held, and their own letters ready to send. There's no insurance advice and no contractor or adjuster referral.
- **Technologies:** OpenFEMA disaster declarations API, Idea 4 document parser with claim, check and contractor document types, Loss-draft rules table by investor in config (effective-dated, sourced), Per-servicer loss-draft package library, Reg X 1024.35 and 1024.36 letter drafting, Hold-for-you servicer queue agent (reused), Disaster Desk quiet mode in the D3 rules table, Consent passport service purpose; D4 texting
- **Why it's new:** What exists: servicers' loss-draft departments, built to protect the collateral. Public adjusters and contractors, paid by the claim. Generic FEMA and insurance-department guides. Disaster Desk in the ideas doc only pauses marketing. Coverage Guard covers non-renewal. Hold-for-you waits on hold. The survey's Claim Check Desk opening identified this service gap. I found no loan officer CRM, servicer-independent tool or Homebot-style product that runs the borrower's own loss-draft release against investor rules. Not re-verified live: search budget exhausted, and FEMA and agency guide pages weren't opened this session.
- **Compliance design:** Insurance licensing: no carrier recommendations, no coverage interpretation beyond what the borrower's own documents say, no claim negotiation. It explains the process, and the borrower acts. Public adjuster and contractor referrals: none. The platform and loan officer accept nothing of value from adjusters, contractors, restoration firms or insurers (state anti-rebating and solicitation rules; RESPA-style platform policy even though post-closing claims aren't settlement services) [COUNSEL]. TCPA: service purpose only during a disaster. Marketing stays quiet in declared counties (Disaster Desk), and any later refinance or recast talk needs prior express written consent. Reg Z: no rate outside a priced result. Fair lending: offered to every affected past client, with nothing ranked by neighborhood. GLBA: claim documents are nonpublic personal information, encrypted, redacted, deleted after extraction, and every read audited. Unauthorized practice of law and public-adjuster rules: the borrower's own Reg X letters use counsel-approved wording and are labeled not legal advice. AI disclosure before M8 speaks. No promises about timing, and the guard blocks any.
- **Moat:** Each claim adds to the per-servicer loss-draft package library and the investor rules table: process facts, not borrower data. A network across many disaster-prone states gets ready faster with every event, which a solo loan officer can't achieve. The clients it helps through the worst week of homeownership are the strongest retention there is, and that relationship belongs to the loan officer, not the servicer or a wholesaler's AI.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: (1) Public adjuster statutes in many states (for example Florida 626.854 and California Ins. Code 15006/15027, from memory) define adjusting broadly enough to cover helping an insured with an insurance claim for compensation. The design must stay strictly on the servicer's loss-draft release, not the insurer's claim. (2) Messaging during disaster declarations: some states restrict solicitation after a disaster, and the FCC's emergency-purpose exception doesn't cover a mortgage broker. Keep to service, with consent. (3) Whether Reg X 1024.35/36 covers loss-draft handling is unsettled (flagged). (4) NFIP Increased Cost of Compliance coverage, up to $30,000, is correct per memory. Redesign: Put a hard scope rule in the guard: no statements about claim value, coverage or negotiation with the insurer. Use only servicer loss-draft process and documents. Add a per-state check on post-disaster solicitation rules. Send only service messages to consented contacts, and give FEMA-declared counties manual MLO calls only. / keep 5/10: Servicer loss-draft departments, public adjusters and nonprofit guides (United Policyholders' recovery roadmap) already cover the process. A borrower-side tracker of loss-draft releases against investor rules, run by the originating MLO, is not known to ship. Events are infrequent, so it is a retention play, not a daily feature. Redesign: Build it as an extension of Disaster Desk: an investor rules table plus Reg X letter templates. Skip building a per-servicer library until claim volume justifies it. / drop 4/10: Rare events per MLO, but large goodwill when they happen. The investor loss-draft rules (Fannie and Freddie servicing guides, state interest-on-held-funds rules) and a per-servicer package library are heavy upkeep for a feature used a few times a year, concentrated in disaster states. It sits next to taken Disaster Desk, Coverage Guard and Hold-for-you. Like Claim Check Desk, it is a survey opening. Redesign: Fold it into Disaster Desk as a borrower guide: a plain explainer of jointly payable checks and staged release, a document checklist, links to the NFIP, FEMA and SBA programs, and the counsel-approved Reg X request template if a release stalls. No per-servicer library at first.
- **Sources:** https://fema.gov/about/openfema/faq (OpenFEMA API; from the ideas doc), https://www.fema.gov/floodplain-management/financial-help/increased-cost-compliance (NFIP ICC; not opened, MED), https://www.knowyouroptions.com/loanlookup (Fannie Mae loan lookup for borrowers; not opened), https://myhome.freddiemac.com/blog/homeownership/disaster-relief-mortgage-assistance-faq (Freddie Mac disaster help; from the ideas doc), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1024/35/ (Reg X error resolution; from the survey), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (Disaster Desk; idea 4)


## Witnessed Ledger: tamper-evident consent and send-gate records with outside witnesses (round 2, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Every consent, revocation and send-gate decision is hashed into an append-only Merkle log whose signed checkpoints are co-signed by parties the platform doesn't control (each sponsor's compliance officer, outside counsel, an RFC 3161 timestamp authority). Any record can then be shown, in a lawsuit or an exam, to have existed unaltered on the date it claims.**

- **Problem:** TCPA defense turns on proof of consent, and plaintiffs increasingly argue that consent records were fabricated or backdated after the fact, a known problem with lead-generation certificates. Today a CRM's consent table is only as credible as the vendor's database admin. D2's append-only cc_consents log and D3's gate log stop the application from overwriting rows, but they can't prove to a court or examiner that nobody with database access rewrote history. An MLO-owned platform has a particular credibility problem: its owners are the people whose conduct is being judged, so its own records need outside witnesses.
- **How it works:** Data: the existing append-only consent events (D2), send-gate decisions with their inputs and rule version (D3/D4), opt-outs (cc_optouts) and AI-call disclosure receipts. Each event becomes a leaf: a salted hash commitment of the canonical event (same canonical-hash approach as lib/pricing/canonical.ts). No borrower data goes into the log itself; the payload stays in the tenant's encrypted row. Trigger: every write. System: leaves go into a per-tenant Merkle tree (Certificate Transparency style, RFC 6962). Every 10 minutes the platform signs a checkpoint (tree size plus root hash) and sends it to witnesses: (1) a witness service run by each sponsoring brokerage's compliance officer, a small container or a hosted key they hold; (2) outside counsel's witness; (3) an RFC 3161 timestamp authority. Each witness checks that the new tree is an append-only extension of the last one it signed (a consistency proof), then co-signs. A rewrite of history would need every witness to collude. Evidence export: for one phone number, the system produces the records, the inclusion proof for each, the co-signed checkpoints and a draft custodian certification for a records custodian to sign [COUNSEL: whether this supports authentication under FRE 902(13)/(14) and business-records foundation under 803(6), and the state analogues]. MLO: sees a green 'witnessed' mark on each consent and gate decision, and can export a contact's evidence bundle in one tap. Borrower: their consent receipt (the consent passport link) carries a short verification code, and a public verifier page confirms the receipt was logged on that date without revealing anything else. Deletion: a privacy deletion destroys the payload and its salt (crypto-shredding). The hash leaf stays, so the log remains consistent but can no longer be linked to the person.
- **Technologies:** Merkle transparency log (RFC 6962 / Trillian or a Sigstore Rekor-style log), Witness cosigning of checkpoints (C2SP tlog-witness style), RFC 3161 trusted timestamping, KMS-held signing keys per tenant, Neon append-only tables (existing cc_consents, cc_audit, cc_optouts), Salted hash commitments and crypto-shredding for deletion, Existing canonical hashing pattern from lib/pricing/canonical.ts, Static public verifier page
- **Why it's new:** What exists: consent-certificate vendors (ActiveProspect TrustedForm, Verisk Jornaya) record web sessions for lead sellers and buyers. The certificate belongs to the vendor and the lead buyer, not the borrower, and session replay itself now draws California CIPA wiretap claims (per the adjacent-technology survey). Mortgage CRMs (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert, Jungo, BNTouch) market consent fields and audit logs, but none of their public material describes externally witnessed, append-only proofs (per the CRM survey's player gaps). Transparency logs are proven technology in Web PKI (Certificate Transparency) and software supply chains (Sigstore). Applying them to TCPA consent, with the borrower's own brokerage as a witness, is new as far as this research found. Verification limit: this session's web search budget was exhausted and law.cornell.edu was blocked, so the FRE 902(13)/(14) wording and the status of proposed FRE 707 on machine-generated evidence (published for comment in 2025, per training knowledge) were not re-checked on 2026-11-18. The idea also differs from the taken 'consent archaeology' (old evidence read on the LO's device), from the 'TCPA demand-letter responder' and 'litigation evidence packet' openings (both assemble records but can't prove those records weren't altered), and from 'exam room' (access, not integrity).
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: the log proves records weren't altered after they were logged. It doesn't prove the consent was genuine, so it sits alongside the existing capture evidence (an E-SIGN e-signature, a texted yes), never in place of it. Marketing copy must say exactly that and must not claim 'TCPA-proof'. GLBA and FTC Safeguards: only salted hashes leave the tenant, witnesses see no nonpublic personal information, and witness services go on the sponsor's service-provider list. State privacy deletion (CCPA, Oregon and others): crypto-shredding removes the link to the person while the log stays consistent [COUNSEL: whether a hash with its salt destroyed still counts as personal information]. Retention: payloads follow the counsel-set schedules (TSR 5 years for telemarketing records per the regulatory survey). No lead is sold: the log carries no contact data, so it can't be resold. RESPA, Reg Z and HPPA aren't touched. Evidence certifications are signed by a human custodian, never generated as legal conclusions [COUNSEL].
- **Moat:** Every new sponsor adds a witness, so the log gets harder to forge as the network grows, and no single company, including the platform, can rewrite it. A competitor owned by a data vendor or an LOS vendor can copy the code but can't easily get its customers' compliance officers to run witnesses against it. Years of witnessed history also can't be recreated later: a platform that starts witnessing in 2026 holds evidence a 2028 entrant can't have for the same contacts.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: No violation. Salted hash commitments with crypto-shredding are a sound privacy pattern, and the design correctly says it proves integrity, not genuine consent. FRE 902(13)/(14) (2017) exist, and proposed FRE 707 was published in 2025 (unverified). Two risks. (1) Whether a hash with a destroyed salt is still 'personal information' under CCPA is open, so it needs counsel. (2) The public verifier page lets anyone holding a code confirm a consent exists, which reveals a customer relationship (NPI) if codes leak. Redesign: Make verifier codes high-entropy and rate-limited, and have the page confirm only 'a record with this code exists dated X', with no name or number. Never market it as 'TCPA-proof'. / keep 6/10: TrustedForm and Jornaya certify sessions for lead buyers. Hash-anchored audit logs exist (RFC 3161 timestamping, Guardtime, Chainpoint-style anchoring), and some consent-management vendors offer immutable logs, so tamper-evidence itself isn't new. Using the borrower's own sponsor as a co-signing witness for TCPA consent is a novel combination that is materially better than vendor-held certificates. Redesign: Simplify v1 to a per-tenant Merkle log with RFC 3161 timestamps and an optional sponsor witness key. Add more witnesses later. Avoid saying 'TCPA-proof'. / keep 5/10: TCPA litigation risk is the platform's biggest legal exposure, and records that are provably unaltered help a defense. But courts usually admit business records with a custodian declaration. Asking every sponsor's compliance officer to run a witness service is an ops burden few small brokers will take on. A full CT-style multi-witness log is more than the value requires at launch. The FRE 902(13)/(14) and proposed FRE 707 status were not re-verified this run. Redesign: MVP: an append-only hash chain over consent and gate events, with a daily root timestamped by an RFC 3161 TSA and published or anchored externally, plus crypto-shredded payloads and one-tap evidence export. Add sponsor or counsel witnesses later as an opt-in for large sponsors.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D2 append-only consent log, D3 send gate, D4 cc_optouts), /home/user/RATEM8/lib/pricing/canonical.ts (existing canonical input hashing), https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6962 (Certificate Transparency; canonical URL, not opened this session), https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3161 (Time-Stamp Protocol; canonical URL, not opened this session), https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/fre/rule_902 (FRE 902(13)/(14); host blocked this session, unverified), https://news.inman.com/2026/02/27/class-action-accuses-lender-of-unsolicited-ai-generated-cold-calls/, https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/mortgage-tcpa-lawsuits-surge-with-11-new-cases, https://www.goodwinlaw.com/en/insights/publications/2025/02/alerts-practices-dpc-ftec-ai-voice-products-subject-to-california-invasion-of-privacy-claims


## Law as Tests: counsel-signed rule cases, replay of past decisions and a public conformance report (round 2, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**Every rule in the send gate ships with plain-language test cases that counsel writes and signs. CI refuses any build where the gate disagrees with counsel. When the law changes, the platform replays months of logged decisions under the new rule and hands counsel the exact list of past touches that would now be blocked.**

- **Problem:** A compliance engine is only as good as the match between its code and what counsel actually decided, and that match is invisible to brokers buying the software. A real gap already exists. D3 defaults to an 8 AM–8 PM window, while the regulatory survey reports Connecticut's sales-call and text window as 9 AM–8 PM (Public Act 23-98, from memory, to be confirmed). The rules also keep moving: FCC Order 26-67 takes effect 2026-11-25 per the voice survey, Virginia's 10-year STOP memory, Texas SB 140 registration. When a rule changes, nobody tells an MLO which of last month's touches would now be problems, or whether a self-correction is warranted.
- **How it works:** Data: the D3 rules table (lib/send-gate/rules.ts), gate decisions logged with their full input snapshot (contact state, consent basis, local time, caps, DNC age, license state) and the rule version. Authoring: counsel writes cases in a structured plain-English form in /mlo/rules, for example 'Given a CT recipient, a marketing text, permission in writing, 8:30 AM local, expect BLOCK, reason: quiet hours, cite PA 23-98'. Counsel signs a case set with a passkey-bound key, which records who approved what and when. CI: `npm run verify` runs every signed case against lib/send-gate/gate.ts, and any divergence fails the build. Mutation testing nightly flips each rule (an hour, a cap, a state) and checks that at least one signed case catches it; uncaught mutations become a 'missing test' list for counsel. Rule change: a proposed rule version runs in shadow. The system replays the last 6–12 months of logged decision inputs under it and produces (a) a look-back list of touches allowed then and blocked now, grouped by contact and play, for counsel to decide whether any outreach, suppression or disclosure is owed, and (b) a forward impact estimate ('this blocks 4% of next week's planned texts'). Publication: each gate release has a conformance report: number of signed cases per state and channel, pass rate, the counsel approval date and the changelog. Sponsors subscribe to it. MLO: sees 'rules v2026.11.25 approved by counsel on [date]', and any contact affected by a look-back shows a card saying why. Borrower: nothing changes, except fewer bad touches.
- **Technologies:** Structured legal test-case DSL (YAML or JSON with zod schema), Passkey or KMS signing of counsel-approved case sets, Vitest in npm run verify (existing), Mutation testing of the pure gate function (Stryker or a custom mutator), Decision-input snapshots in the D3/D4 audit log, Shadow evaluation and historical replay job on QStash, Versioned rules table with effective dates, Published conformance report page
- **Why it's new:** The regulatory survey proposed effective-dated rule versions on every decision. This builds on that substrate, and what's new is three things it lacks: counsel-authored executable cases that gate every build, mutation testing that proves the case set has no blind spots, and historical replay that turns a rule change into a concrete remediation list. Vendors (Gryphon, PossibleNOW, DNC.com) sell rule libraries for call centers, and mortgage CRMs claim 'built-in compliance'. None of their public material shows counsel-signed tests or a replay look-back (per the CRM and regulatory survey player gaps, which are inferences from marketing). The repo's own gap shows why it's needed: D3's 8 AM default against Connecticut's reported 9 AM start would have been caught by a single counsel-written case. Verification limit: the Connecticut window and Texas SB 140 details come from the regulatory survey's memory-based notes and weren't re-verified this session (the search budget was exhausted).
- **Compliance design:** TCPA and state mini-TCPAs: the gate stays fail-closed, tests only confirm it, and a signed case can only tighten behavior unless counsel signs a relaxation with a reason. Look-back output is privileged work product for counsel [COUNSEL: structure the replay as performed at counsel's direction, so attorney-client privilege and work-product protection are preserved]. It's a list for counsel to judge, never an automatic outreach, and it never re-contacts anyone without the gate's current approval. Revocation by any reasonable means stays a hard rule, and no test case can relax it. GLBA: replay runs inside each tenant on snapshots already kept for the audit log. Reports share only counts. No loan data, no Reg Z or RESPA surface. 'No rate outside a priced result' gets its own signed cases against lib/m8/guard.ts.
- **Moat:** Signed cases add up. Every state change, every counsel decision and every incident becomes a permanent case that future releases must pass. A new entrant starts with zero, and an MLO-owned network can spread one counsel panel's cost across every member. Replay value also grows with history: the longer a tenant has been on the platform, the more precisely it can measure the effect of a new rule.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound engineering. Connecticut's 9 AM to 8 PM window (PA 23-98) matches my memory. The risk is the look-back replay. A list of past touches 'that would now be blocked' is not privileged by default: work product needs anticipation of litigation, and compliance analyses are often discoverable. It would be a ready-made exhibit for TCPA plaintiffs. Where the law changed prospectively, those touches were lawful when sent, but the list invites mischaracterization. Redesign: Have outside counsel request each replay in writing, run it in a counsel-controlled workspace, label the output privileged, and limit retention. Present the output as forward impact by default, and produce the look-back list only on counsel's request. Publish case counts only in conformance reports. / keep 6/10: Test cases for rules engines and compliance-as-code are standard engineering practice, and call-compliance vendors (Gryphon, PossibleNOW) ship maintained rule libraries, so the idea isn't novel as a market feature. The counsel-signed cases that gate the build, and especially the historical replay that produces a look-back list for counsel, are new and valuable for D3. Redesign: Treat it as a D3 engineering requirement rather than a product: signed cases in CI plus replay. Make the public conformance report optional. / keep 8/10: High return for little build. The repo already runs vitest in npm run verify, and the gate is a pure function, so signed cases and nightly mutation tests are cheap. They would have caught the 8 AM vs Connecticut 9 AM default. Replaying logged decision inputs turns a rule change into a remediation list. The main cost is counsel's time to write cases. The public conformance report is optional marketing. Redesign: Ship signed case files under tests/, enforced in verify. Use passkey-signed approval only if counsel will use it; a reviewed PR with counsel's sign-off works at first. Replay after 3 months of logged inputs. Treat the public report as optional.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/lib/send-gate/rules.ts and /home/user/RATEM8/lib/send-gate/gate.ts (D3), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D3 default 8 AM–8 PM window), https://www.nelsonmullins.com/insights/alerts/fcc-download/all/fcc-adopts-overhaul-of-tcpa-consent-revocation-rules-revoke-all-narrowed-exclusive-opt-out-methods-permitted-and-fraud-alert-exemption-expanded, https://www.dlapiper.com/en/insights/publications/2026/10/fcc-tcpa-rule, https://www.privacyworld.blog/2026/01/key-changes-to-virginia-telephone-privacy-protection-act-take-effect/, https://www.manatt.com/insights/newsletters/tcpa-connect/florida-amends-ftsa, https://capitol.texas.gov/BillLookup/History.aspx?LegSess=89R&Bill=SB140 (not opened this session)


## Independent Control Attestation: a CPA-examined report on the platform's TCPA, consent and comp-firewall controls, fed by continuous evidence (round 2, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**Engage an independent CPA firm to run a SOC 2 Type II examination plus an AT-C 315 compliance examination of specific assertions ('no AI call or marketing text leaves without written consent on file', 'opt-outs take effect on every channel within 60 seconds', 'the reward engine can't read loan tables'). Controls stream evidence to the auditor automatically, and every sponsor receives the report.**

- **Problem:** A brokerage that puts its MLOs on a new platform becomes responsible for overseeing that vendor (FTC Safeguards Rule service-provider oversight, 16 CFR 314.4(f)), and for TCPA liability the vendor's conduct creates. Today the evidence available to a small broker is the vendor's own marketing. Self-made security packets and governance binders, both proposed in the surveys, still ask the buyer to trust the seller. Compliance officers and warehouse lenders trust an independent auditor's opinion, which no mortgage CRM or AI-voice vendor offers on TCPA or comp controls.
- **How it works:** Assertions: management writes specific, testable assertions. (1) The send gate is the only path to Twilio and email (a network egress check plus code-boundary lint). (2) No M8 call or marketing text without written consent and a DNC check under 31 days. (3) Opt-out latency is under 60 seconds across every loan officer (from D4's cc_optouts). (4) The comp firewall's Postgres role has no SELECT on loan, pricing or pipeline tables (from idea 12's CI privilege test). (5) No borrower nonpublic personal information in logs or analytics.
