(6) The AI disclosure is spoken before substance on 100% of M8 calls (from transcript checks). Evidence: each control emits machine evidence into an evidence store: daily privilege dumps, gate decision samples, latency histograms, the egress allow-list and red-team run results (tests/m8-redteam*). Auditor access: a read-only evidence API that returns statistically sampled records with tokenized contact IDs, so the auditor sees proof, not borrower data. The auditor tests design and operating effectiveness over a 6–12 month period and issues (a) a SOC 2 Type II report (security, confidentiality, privacy) and (b) an AT-C 315 opinion on the compliance assertions. Any control failure opens an incident and a corrective-action record the auditor can see. Sponsor: downloads the report, bridge letters and the platform's complementary user-entity controls ('you must not upload purchased lists') from the sponsor console, ready to file. MLO-owned twist: the member audit committee, not management alone, engages the auditor and receives the management letter, and a summary goes to all members. MLO: sees a 'controls examined through [date]' mark they can show their broker. Borrower: nothing changes.
- **Technologies:** SOC 2 Type II examination (AICPA Trust Services Criteria), AT-C section 315 compliance attestation, Continuous control monitoring and evidence store (Neon plus object storage with retention), Read-only, sampled, tokenized auditor evidence API, CI privilege test and ESLint import boundary (from the comp firewall), Network egress allow-list for Twilio, Resend and voice vendors, Existing red-team suites tests/m8-redteam.spec.ts and remote runner, Sponsor console with report distribution under NDA
- **Why it's new:** Mortgage CRMs and AI-voice vendors market compliance features. The CRM, voice and regulatory surveys found none advertising an independent examination of TCPA consent or LO-comp controls; most at most hold a generic SOC 2 (that is inferred from public marketing and not re-checked this session). The survey openings proposed a self-produced 'security review packet' and an 'AI governance binder'. This idea differs because an independent CPA tests the controls over time and gives an opinion, which compliance officers can rely on in a vendor review. It's also distinct from the taken 'gate warranty' (a financial promise) and 'proof-priced coverage' (insurance priced on controls): the attestation is the independent evidence those programs would need. Verification limit: AT-C 315 and SOC 2 come from training knowledge of the AICPA attestation standards. This session's search budget was exhausted, so current AICPA wording wasn't re-opened.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA, consent, revocation, DNC and AI disclosure: the assertions restate the build plan's §2 rules as testable claims. Reg Z 1026.36(d) and the 10% rule: assertion 4 attests that the reward engine can't see loan terms, which supports the claim that ownership rewards are subscription and tenure only [COUNSEL: securities and comp]. GLBA and the Safeguards Rule: the auditor receives sampled, tokenized evidence under NDA as a service provider, and no raw nonpublic personal information leaves the tenant. Copy: an opinion is described exactly as issued, never as 'certified compliant' or 'guaranteed', and check:copy blocks those words. The examination covers the platform's controls, not each MLO's own conduct, and the report says so through complementary user-entity controls. RESPA, HPPA and fair lending: optional further assertions (no consumer-report data in the schema; fair-lending tests run per model version) can be added in year 2.
- **Moat:** An examination needs a 6–12 month operating period, so a competitor that starts later is at least that far behind. Evidence automation lowers the audit cost per member as the network grows, so the MLO-owned platform can give a solo broker an institutional-grade report that broker could never pay for alone. Brokerages that file the report once in their vendor-management records have one less reason to switch to an unattested vendor.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: No violation. SOC 2 Type II and AT-C 315 compliance attestation are appropriate engagements (from memory; AICPA text not re-checked). Two risks. (1) Marketing must describe the opinion exactly as issued, never 'certified compliant'. A qualified opinion must be disclosed if marketing continues. (2) Assertion 4 supports, but does not prove, that ownership rewards are subscription- and tenure-only for securities and Reg Z purposes. Auditor independence rules apply if the same firm advises. Redesign: Have check:copy block 'certified', 'compliant' and 'guaranteed' near the attestation. Have the member audit committee engage the auditor under an engagement letter, keeping advisory services with a different firm. / drop 4/10: Not novel. SOC 2 Type II is standard among mortgage tech vendors (Total Expert, Blend and others), AT-C 315 compliance examinations are an established AICPA service used in other regulated industries, and continuous-evidence platforms (Vanta, Drata) already stream control evidence to auditors. It is useful for sales later, but it isn't a differentiator. Redesign: Plan a SOC 2 Type II through a Vanta- or Drata-style evidence platform once revenue supports it. Add AT-C 315 assertions for the gate and firewall in year 2. Not a launch idea. / keep 6/10: Sponsors and lenders running vendor reviews will eventually ask for SOC 2, so a Type II report is a sales requirement, not a moat. The cost is significant for an early company: tens of thousands of dollars a year for the audit plus a compliance-automation tool (Vanta or Drata class), from industry knowledge. The AT-C 315 compliance opinion adds cost and few buyers know to ask for it. It is premature before paying sponsors exist. Redesign: Phase it. Wire the evidence automation now (privilege dumps, gate samples, opt-out latency), since it also feeds Law as Tests. Start a SOC 2 Type I when the first sponsor's vendor review requires it, then Type II. Add the AT-C 315 assertions only if sponsors or investors value them.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 12 comp firewall and CI privilege test), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D3 gate as sole path, D4 cc_optouts), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (FTC Safeguards Rule; not opened this session), https://www.aicpa-cima.com/resources/landing/system-and-organization-controls-soc-suite-of-services (SOC suite; canonical URL, not opened this session), https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/201301_cfpb_final-rule_loan-originator-compensation.pdf, https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/tcpa-lawsuits-hit-nine-more-mortgage-lenders


## Sunlight Receipts: a 'why did I get this?' link on every message, with the consent used, who has looked at your file, and a dispute button (round 2, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**Every text, email and M8 call summary carries a short private link showing the borrower the exact reason for the contact, the consent record it relied on (date, how given, proof excerpt), a role-level log of who viewed their record, and three buttons: stop, 'I never agreed to this', and correct my data. A dispute freezes contact across the whole network within seconds and gets a human answer with the evidence.**

- **Problem:** Most TCPA suits start with a person who believes they never agreed to be contacted and has no easy way to find out why. So the first thing the business hears is a demand letter. The consent passport (plan 5.3) lets a borrower set future choices, but it doesn't explain a specific message or show the evidence behind it, and nobody shows borrowers who inside a company has accessed their file. The FTC Safeguards Rule already requires logging authorized users' activity (16 CFR 314.4(c)(8)), so the data exists but stays hidden.
- **How it works:** Data: the D3 gate decision for each touch (play, reason, rule version, consent basis), the D2 consent event and its proof reference, the D1 cc_audit access log (reads by role: 'your loan officer', 'an assistant on their team', 'M8 (automated)', 'compliance review'), and the Witnessed Ledger verification code. Trigger: every outbound touch gets a signed, expiring, unguessable link (shortened in texts, with the 10DLC campaign samples updated to include it). Borrower view: 'You got this text because you asked for an annual review on 2026-03-02 by replying YES to [number]. Proof: your reply, logged and witnessed [code].' Then 'Who has viewed your file in the last 90 days', by role and date. Then the buttons. Stop revokes every channel and every loan officer via D4. 'I never agreed' records a dispute: all contact stops network-wide (the One Stop hash), the record is placed under legal hold, and a 48-hour compliance task opens. The reply either shows the borrower the proof, or confirms the error, deletes the record if they want, and logs a root cause against the import source or play. Correct my data fixes wrong-number and name records. Identity: the link opens only for the number or email it was sent to, plus a one-time code, and shows no loan data. MLO: a per-play and per-import 'dispute rate'. A spike auto-pauses that play or quarantines that import source, which feeds D2's provenance tags. Sponsor and auditor: dispute rate and time-to-resolution become monitored controls.
- **Technologies:** Signed expiring links (HMAC or JWT) with one-time code step-up, D1 cc_audit access log rendered by role, D2 consent events and proof references, D3 gate decision records, D4 opt-out propagation and the One Stop salted-hash suppression, Legal hold flag on contact records, Resend and Twilio (existing), with 10DLC sample-message updates, QStash task queue for 48-hour compliance SLAs
- **Why it's new:** What exists: CRMs add 'Reply STOP' and CAN-SPAM unsubscribe links, and preference centers let people pick channels. The consent passport (plan 5.3) is a preference center done well. Taken ideas cover complaint detection ('complaint radar') and examiner access ('exam room'). None shows the person, for one specific message, the reason, the evidence of consent and an access log, or turns 'I never agreed' into a structured, evidence-backed dispute before it becomes a demand letter. Access transparency exists in cloud computing (for example, cloud providers' access-transparency logs for customer admins), but not for borrowers in mortgage, as far as this research found. Verification limit: competitor features come from the CRM survey's player gaps, which were themselves built from search snippets. No new searches were possible this session.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: revocation is honored by any reasonable means and at once. The dispute button is extra, never the exclusive method, even after FCC 26-67 allows designated methods (effective 2026-11-25 per the voice survey). The dispute freeze covers every channel and loan officer. GLBA: the page shows the borrower only their own consent and role-level access events, never staff names or other contacts, and access to it is authenticated to the channel the message went to [COUNSEL: Reg P and state privacy rules on disclosing access logs]. State privacy laws: the 'correct' and 'delete' buttons double as the CCPA, Oregon and Connecticut rights flows, with sponsor record-keeping holds where a loan is active. Legal hold: a dispute preserves records before any deletion request is processed, and counsel sets the order of operations. 10DLC: the link domain is a first-party LoanM8 domain (no public URL shorteners) and appears in the registered samples. Copy: neutral, no admission language without counsel's template.
- **Moat:** Dispute outcomes become a network-wide quality signal. Import sources and plays that produce disputes are found and fixed faster as more MLOs use the platform, and the root-cause history can't be bought. Borrowers who learn that LoanM8-network messages always explain themselves are more likely to answer, which improves consent yield for every member.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: This is strong pro-consumer transparency. (1) Reassigned numbers: a link sent to a number that has changed hands, opened with an OTP to that same number, shows the new holder the prior person's consent record. That discloses NPI (the existence of a relationship, the request topic). Check the FCC Reassigned Numbers Database before sends, and keep receipt content minimal. (2) Showing consent proof excerpts before a dispute is resolved can leak the other party's data if the proof contains it. (3) The dispute button must never become the exclusive revocation method (correctly stated). Adding links to every message changes the 10DLC campaign samples (handled). Redesign: Query the Reassigned Numbers Database before marketing sends and before showing receipt contents. Show only the date, the channel and how consent was given, with proof excerpts released after a human review. Use first-party link domains only. / keep 6/10: Pieces exist elsewhere: 'Why am I seeing this ad' (Meta, Google), the Kantara consent receipt spec, preference centers (OneTrust, PossibleNOW) and cloud access-transparency logs. No mortgage product shows the person the consent behind one specific message and turns 'I never agreed' into a structured dispute with a network-wide freeze. That is materially better. Links in texts can hurt 10DLC deliverability. Redesign: Keep it. Put the link in emails and call summaries always, and in texts only where the carrier campaign allows, otherwise via a 'reply WHY' keyword. Reuse the consent-passport page. / keep 5/10: The dispute-before-demand-letter flow has real legal-cost value, and the auto-pause of disputed plays or imports is a cheap, strong control. But a link in every text adds SMS segments (cost on every send) and can hurt carrier filtering. Role-level access logs shown to borrowers add GLBA and Reg P questions for little benefit. Most borrowers will never tap it. Redesign: Put the 'why did I get this' link in the consent receipt, the first message of each play and the STOP confirmation, not in every text. Keep the dispute freeze and per-play or per-import dispute-rate auto-pause. Defer the access-log view.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D1 cc_audit, D2 consent log, D3 gate reasons, D4 opt-outs across loan officers), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (§5.3 consent passport, §4.9 data requests), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (Safeguards Rule 314.4(c)(8) activity logging; not opened this session), https://www.manatt.com/insights/newsletters/client-alert/fcc-replaces-revoke-all-rule-and-permits-callers-to-designate-an-exclusive-revocation-method, https://tcpaworld.com/2026/06/04/april-tcpa-filings-off-the-chart-330-tcpa-cases-255-tcpa-class-actions-filed-in-april-2026-up-40-from-2025/, https://www.faegredrinker.com/en/insights/publications/2025/6/launching-text-campaigns-ins-outs-of-10dlc-registration


## Retention Clock and crypto-shred vault: every record keeps exactly as long as the strictest applicable rule requires, then is provably destroyed (round 2, medium impact, ~5 weeks)

**Each record gets its own encryption key and a computed retention date that resolves overlapping obligations (Reg Z 1026.25, TSR 5-year telemarketing records, Virginia's 10-year opt-out memory, state books-and-records rules, legal holds and privacy deletion rights). When the date passes, or a valid deletion arrives, the key is destroyed and a signed deletion certificate is issued, while opt-out suppression hashes and held records survive.**

- **Problem:** Brokerages face two opposite risks with a CRM vendor: deleting too early (an examiner asks for advertising or telemarketing records the vendor already purged; a 10-year Virginia STOP is forgotten and the person is texted again) and keeping too long (breach exposure under the Safeguards Rule's 30-day FTC notice for 500+ consumers; state deletion requests; CCPA ADMT obligations from 2027). Today these rules live in different laws with different clocks, and a CRM either keeps everything forever or deletes on request without checking holds. When an MLO changes sponsors, it's unclear whose clock applies.
- **How it works:** Data: every stored object (contact, consent event, message, call recording, transcript, gate decision, uploaded-document extraction) is tagged with its record class, the governing entity (the sponsor, the platform or the MLO personally), property state, channel, and whether it is GLBA nonpublic personal information. A counsel-maintained retention table (versioned and tested like the Law as Tests cases) maps each class and jurisdiction to minimum and maximum periods with citations. Examples: Reg Z 1026.25 (2 years generally, 3 for LO-comp records), TSR telemarketing records (5 years per the regulatory survey), the counsel-set transcript period (the chat currently uses 3 years), Virginia STOP suppression (10 years), sponsor books-and-records rules [COUNSEL per state]. Mechanics: each record's payload is encrypted with its own data key wrapped by a tenant KMS key (envelope encryption). A nightly job computes each record's earliest lawful destruction date, the latest of all minimums and holds, capped by any maximum, and destroys keys when it arrives. Conflicts resolve by rule: a legal hold or a dispute (Sunlight Receipts) always wins; an active loan with the sponsor keeps the sponsor's copy. A borrower deletion request removes what the law allows at once and schedules the rest, with a letter explaining what is kept and why. Suppression: opt-outs survive as salted phone hashes for their legal period even after the contact record is shredded, so 'delete me' never turns into 're-text me'. Sponsor change: the old sponsor's copies of its own records stay on its clock and keys, and the MLO's relationship-only contacts move to the new tenant with fresh keys, driven by D2 provenance. Output: signed deletion certificates (counts by class, key IDs destroyed, logged in the Witnessed Ledger) and a retention report for exams and vendor reviews. MLO: sees 'kept until' on each record and why. Borrower: the deletion confirmation lists exactly what is kept, for how long, and the citation.
- **Technologies:** Envelope encryption with per-record data keys and a tenant KMS, Crypto-shredding by key destruction, Versioned retention table with counsel citations and signed test cases, Nightly retention job on QStash, Legal-hold and dispute flags, Salted-hash opt-out suppression list (cc_optouts), Deletion certificates logged in the Witnessed Ledger, Record-class tagging extending D2 provenance tags
- **Why it's new:** What exists: the plan's §4.9 has export and delete with sponsor holds; the regulatory survey proposed a per-record GLBA-versus-state-privacy tag (the legal basis); the taken 'complaint radar' includes a legal hold. None of them computes a per-record destruction date from overlapping federal, state and contractual clocks, destroys data cryptographically with a certificate, or keeps suppression alive after deletion. Enterprise records-management tools (for example SEC 17a-4 WORM archives for broker-dealers) handle retention but aren't mortgage-aware and don't model TCPA suppression or the sponsor-MLO split. Verification limit: the TSR 5-year rule, Virginia's 10-year period and Reg Z 1026.25 periods come from the regulatory survey and training knowledge. No primary-source re-check was possible this session (search budget exhausted, eCFR not reached).
- **Compliance design:** GLBA and the FTC Safeguards Rule: keeping less shrinks breach scope. The rule's data disposal provision (no longer than 2 years after last use unless needed, per training knowledge of 314.4(c)(6)) is a ceiling the table must respect [COUNSEL]. TCPA, the TSR and Virginia: suppression hashes outlive the record, so no deletion can bring back a revoked number. Reg Z 1026.25: LO-comp and loan records stay with the sponsor for the required period, and the reward-engine ledger keeps its 3-year comp-adjacent records. State privacy (CCPA, Oregon, Connecticut): deletion runs at once where no exemption applies, with GLBA data-level exemptions noted per record, and the borrower is told what is kept and why. Legal holds always win over deletion. No lead is sold: shredded data can't be resold, and certificates prove it. Ownership: nothing here touches loan terms or rewards.
- **Moat:** The retention table becomes a maintained, counsel-signed body of law across 50 states and every record class, funded once by the network instead of by each small broker. Each sponsor that adopts it reduces its breach exposure and exam risk, and moving away means re-creating both the table and the key architecture. Deletion certificates logged over years are a trust asset a new vendor can't backfill.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: No violation. The cited periods match my memory: Reg Z 1026.25 (2 years generally, 3 years for LO comp), the 2024 TSR amendment to 5 years, Virginia's 10-year do-not-call honoring (Va. Code 59.1-514), and the Safeguards 314.4(c)(6) 2-year disposal ceiling with exceptions. Missing classes to add: ECOA 1002.12 (25 months), HMDA (3 years for the LAR), state MLO books-and-records periods (often 3 to 4 years), and Reg X servicing-adjacent records. The 'maximum' caps must never cut short a sponsor's own retention duty. Redesign: Add the ECOA, HMDA and state books-and-records rows. Have a legal hold always win, and have the sponsor's own retention policy override platform maximums for the sponsor's copies. / drop 4/10: Mostly refuted as a differentiator. Records-retention engines with policy conflict resolution and legal-hold precedence already ship (Microsoft Purview retention labels, OneTrust, Smarsh and Global Relay archiving), and crypto-shredding with per-record keys is a standard pattern. The mortgage-specific retention table and suppression that survives deletion are good architecture, not a market innovation. Redesign: Build it as core architecture (retention table, envelope keys, suppression hashes) inside D-series infrastructure, and don't market it as a feature. / keep 5/10: Retention and deletion are required (Safeguards Rule disposal, Reg Z 1026.25, TSR records, state privacy deletion), and suppression that survives deletion is essential. But per-record data keys mean KMS cost and latency at scale, and per-record conflict resolution across 50 states is more legal table than an early team needs. The counsel-maintained table is the real cost. Redesign: Use a key per tenant and per record class with a monthly date bucket, not per-record keys. Start with five record classes and the five launch states. Keep salted-hash suppression and legal-hold precedence, and issue deletion certificates as simple signed logs.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (§2.2 data rules, §4.9 data requests), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D2 provenance tags, D4 cc_optouts; C4 transcripts kept 3 years), https://www.privacyworld.blog/2026/01/key-changes-to-virginia-telephone-privacy-protection-act-take-effect/, https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-310 (TSR recordkeeping; not opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (Safeguards Rule disposal and breach notice; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/25/ (Reg Z record retention; not opened this session), https://cppa.ca.gov/regulations/ccpa_updates.html (CPPA ADMT; not opened this session)


## File Drift Engine: field changes that start compliance clocks (round 2, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**Every change to a loan field in the LOS (rate, loan amount, program, fees, income, property type) is compared old value to new value. Each change starts the clocks it legally triggers: a revised Loan Estimate, a new Closing Disclosure waiting period, an AUS re-run, and a re-check of the pre-approval letter. All of them land on one Today card with the deadline.**

- **Problem:** Brokers lose real money and hours to disclosure timing they track in their heads. A rate lock, a loan-amount change or a program switch can each require a revised Loan Estimate within 3 business days. If that window is missed, the original fee figures stay binding and any increase in a zero-tolerance or 10% fee becomes a cure the broker or lender pays within 60 days of closing. A late change to the APR, product or prepayment penalty restarts the 3-business-day Closing Disclosure wait and moves closing. Income or asset edits made after the last AUS run leave stale findings, so the lender suspends the file. A pre-approval letter can stay with the agent after the scenario that supported it has changed. LOS tools flag some of this inside the LOS. None ties field-level changes across ARIVE, Encompass and nCino to one clock screen that also drafts the borrower and agent messages through a send gate.
- **How it works:** Data: the existing connector layer already plans signed, stored-raw webhooks. The Encompass Enhanced Field Change webhook returns field, old value and new value (it needs ICE support to enable and carries PII, 214 KB chunks). nCino webhooks are HMAC-signed. ARIVE's Zapier triggers fire on loan trackers, loan dates and loan updates for the Primary-LO's own loans, so for ARIVE the engine keeps a snapshot of each loan and works out the change itself. Lock confirmations and third-party invoices the MLO forwards are parsed into the same change stream.

Rules: a versioned rules table, the same pattern as the D3 state rules, maps each kind of change to its clocks. Examples:
- Rate lock after the initial Loan Estimate: revised LE within 3 business days of the lock.
- A changed circumstance or a borrower-requested change: revised LE within 3 business days of learning of it, and received no later than 4 business days before consummation.
- Fee increases: classified into the zero-tolerance, 10%-aggregate or unlimited bucket.
- APR beyond tolerance (1/8 for a regular loan, 1/4 for an irregular one), a product change or an added prepayment penalty: a new Closing Disclosure waiting period.
- Income, assets, liabilities, loan amount or occupancy changed after the last AUS timestamp: re-run AUS before submission.
- A change that breaks the scenario behind an outstanding pre-approval letter: a re-check card.

Business days use the Reg Z definitions, with a federal-holiday calendar per rule version.

What the MLO sees: one Today card per file ('Rate locked Tue 2:14 PM. Revised LE due Fri. Reason to document: rate lock. Fee changes since the last LE: appraisal +$75, 10% bucket, still inside the limit.'). Two buttons: 'Open in LOS' and 'Record reason'. The reason is stored with the change that caused it and the rule version, which builds the changed-circumstance file an examiner asks for.

Borrower and agent: an optional, approved service text through the D3 gate ('Your updated Loan Estimate is on the way; nothing for you to do yet'). The text never states a closing date or a rate unless it comes from a priced result.

The engine never edits the disclosures. It tells the licensed person what is due and why.
- **Technologies:** LOS webhooks (Encompass Enhanced Field Change, nCino HMAC webhooks, Blend events), ARIVE Zapier triggers with diffs against a local snapshot, Effective-dated rules table (D3 pattern) with a business-day and holiday calendar, Parsing of lock confirmations and invoices with field validation, Durable job queue (QStash or Vercel Queues) for idempotent replay, D3 send gate for the borrower and agent service texts
- **Why it's new:** Today TRID timing lives inside each LOS's own disclosure-tracking screens, such as Encompass's disclosure tracking and ARIVE's disclosure tools (product knowledge, not re-checked this session). Each covers only loans in that LOS and doesn't link fee changes to AUS staleness or pre-approval letters. CRMs (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert, Jungo per the market survey) read milestones at best, not field changes. The taken 'application clock' covers the moment an application exists and the initial Loan Estimate across AI channels. 'Refund radar' finds money owed after closing. This idea sits between them: the changes after application that create cures and delays, caught in time to prevent them. Sources: Encompass Enhanced Field Change webhook (https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/docs/efc-webhook-user-setup-guide), and Encompass webhooks being not guaranteed real-time (https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/changelog/222-major-release), which is why the engine also re-reads loans on a schedule.
- **Compliance design:** - TRID (12 CFR 1026.19(e)(3)(iv), (e)(4), (f)(2)): the clocks are stated as advice to the licensed originator. The engine never generates, sends or suppresses a disclosure, and the creditor or broker of record still issues them. [COUNSEL: confirm the trigger table and the business-day definitions before launch.]
- GLBA: field diffs carry NPI, so they are encrypted, stored per tenant behind row-level security, audited on read, and never logged in plain text. The raw EFC payloads are purged after normalization.
- Fair lending: demographic and GMI fields are excluded from the diff stream at the connector, so they never reach rules or AI.
- AI: drafts follow the M8 guard (no rate outside a priced result, no closing-date promise).
- Messages go through the D3 gate as service-purpose only.
- Rule-version stamping gives an exam trail of which rule applied on which day.
- **Moat:** The trigger and tolerance table is costly to get right across five states, three LOSes and dozens of lender overlays. Each member's resolved edge case (for example, 'a county transfer-tax change was ruled unlimited tolerance by counsel on date X') becomes a counsel-approved rule row for everyone. That is shared rules logic, not shared loan data. After a year the platform holds the most complete operational TRID rules library a broker can buy, and incumbents tied to one LOS can't match the cross-LOS coverage.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: No violation. The rules are correctly identified: revised LE within 3 business days of a changed circumstance or a lock, and received no later than 4 business days before consummation; a new 3-business-day CD wait for APR inaccuracy (1/8 and 1/4), a product change or an added prepayment penalty. One correction: TRID uses two business-day definitions. LE delivery uses the 'general' definition (days the creditor's offices are open for business). Waiting periods use the 'specific' definition (all calendar days except Sundays and federal holidays). An APR decrease can be accurate under 1026.22(a)(4). GMI exclusion and GLBA controls are correct. Redesign: Encode both business-day definitions per rule, plus the overstated-APR exception. Have counsel sign the trigger table with signed test cases (Law as Tests). / drop 3/10: Refuted (training knowledge). Encompass Disclosure Tracking and changed-circumstance tools, ARIVE's disclosure tools, and compliance engines (ICE Mavent, ComplianceEase, Wolters Kluwer) already track tolerance buckets, revised-LE timing and CD re-disclosure triggers inside the LOS where the disclosure is issued. A parallel clock outside the LOS risks disagreeing with the system of record. Redesign: Drop it as a desk. At most, mirror the LOS's own disclosure-due dates onto the Today card and link the AUS-stale and pre-approval-letter re-check, which the LOS doesn't connect. / keep 6/10: TRID cures and missed revised-LE deadlines cost real money, and catching AUS staleness and broken pre-approval scenarios saves time. But in the wholesale broker channel the lender's disclosure desk often issues revised LEs and CDs and tracks changed circumstances itself. ARIVE also has disclosure tracking. ARIVE Zapier triggers don't give field-level diffs, so the engine must snapshot. The Encompass EFC webhook needs ICE enablement and serves mostly larger shops. The value per broker MLO is moderate, and the trigger table needs careful counsel review. Redesign: MVP on ARIVE snapshots only: lock event to revised-LE due date, fee changes classified by tolerance bucket, AUS-stale flag and pre-approval re-check, shown as Today cards with 'record reason'. Add Encompass later for sponsor tenants that already have EFC enabled.
- **Sources:** https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/docs/efc-webhook-user-setup-guide, https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/changelog/222-major-release, https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/reference/webhook, https://developer.ncinomortgage.com/mortgage/docs/webhooks, https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work-, https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1026/subpart-C/section-1026.19 (canonical; host blocked in this session, not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/compliance/compliance-resources/mortgage-resources/tila-respa-integrated-disclosures/ (canonical; not opened)


## Lender Switch Kit: re-broker a file in an afternoon (round 2, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**When a file has to move from one wholesale lender to another (suspended, a guideline miss, a better priced option the borrower picks, a lender leaving the panel), the kit assembles the move. It requests the appraisal transfer or FHA case transfer, plans the credit reissue, re-maps the document package to the new lender's checklist, tracks the Reg B notice from the old creditor, and starts the new creditor's Loan Estimate clock.**

- **Problem:** Moving a brokered loan is one of the most time-consuming things a broker does, and it happens at the worst moment, mid-contract. The steps are scattered. An appraisal transfer letter is needed from the first lender. FHA case numbers must be transferred, and VA valuations reassigned. The credit report must be reissued rather than re-pulled. Documents must be restacked to the new lender's submission checklist. The new creditor issues its own Loan Estimate. The first creditor's application needs a withdrawal or adverse-action outcome. The borrower and agent have to be told what's happening without promises. Each step is a separate email, portal and phone call, and a missed one costs days of the contingency period.
- **How it works:** Trigger: a loan's lender field changes in the LOS, or the MLO taps 'Move this file' on a pipeline card and picks the destination lender from their panel.

What the system does:
- It generates a move plan from a per-lender recipe table maintained by members and counsel. The table holds the appraisal transfer letter template and where to send it, whether the destination accepts transferred conventional appraisals, the FHA Connection case-transfer step, VA valuation transfer, credit reissue (the original credit vendor and the reissue fee) and the destination's submission checklist and file naming.
- It re-maps the borrower documents already in the LOS or POS into the new checklist and lists only the true gaps, so the borrower is asked for nothing twice.
- It opens two clocks. The first is the new creditor's Loan Estimate (3 business days once the new creditor has the application; the broker may issue on the creditor's behalf). The second is the Reg B action-taken record for the first creditor (withdrawn, or an adverse-action notice where required).
- The 'third-party chase agent' (already taken) can be handed the transfer-letter follow-ups. This kit decides what needs chasing.

What the MLO sees: a checklist card with each step, its owner, its status and a 'send' button for each request email or text, plus a running 'days added to closing' estimate drawn from the contingency clock.

Borrower: one approved service text explaining that the loan is moving to a different lender, what they need to do (usually nothing) and that a new Loan Estimate will arrive. No rate is stated unless from a priced result, and the reason for the move is not described as a denial unless it is one.

Agent: a neutral file-status update through Agent Line, if the borrower has approved sharing (partner room).
- **Technologies:** Per-lender recipe table (versioned, counsel-reviewed), LOS and POS document index plus checklist re-mapping (classifier with MLO confirmation), Email and portal request templates sent from the MLO's own mailbox via OAuth, TRID and Reg B clocks from the File Drift Engine rules table, D3 send gate for borrower texts, Contingency clock integration (taken idea) for days-added estimates
- **Why it's new:** LOSes with lender marketplaces (ARIVE, per the market survey) help submit to a lender but don't run a lender-to-lender move as a workflow with transfer letters, case transfer, reissue and the Reg B record. CRMs don't touch it. I found no product that sells it as a workflow. This is from product knowledge only: the search budget was exhausted this session, so it is not verified against current vendor pages. The taken 'third-party chase agent' waits on hold for HOAs, insurers and payoff desks. It doesn't plan a re-broker or own the disclosure and Reg B consequences of changing creditors.
- **Compliance design:** - TRID: the new creditor's Loan Estimate is a new disclosure obligation. The kit shows the due date and never treats the old LE as carrying over. [COUNSEL]
- Reg B (12 CFR 1002.9): the original creditor's action on the first application is recorded, including whether the third-party-notice provision for brokered applications covers the notice. [COUNSEL]
- Appraisal independence (12 CFR 1026.42): the kit only requests transfers and never contacts the appraiser to influence value.
- RESPA 8: no fees to anyone for transfers beyond actual third-party charges, which are logged.
- Anti-steering (1026.36(e)): when the move is pricing-driven, the reason and the options shown are recorded, with no comp figures in the reason field.
- GLBA: documents move only between the broker and creditors under the borrower's application authorization, and every release is logged.
- **Moat:** The per-lender recipe table is operational know-how that improves with every member move, for example 'Lender X needs the transfer letter on letterhead with the AMC name'. It is procedural, so it can be shared across sponsors without the antitrust problem that sank Lender Pulse, as long as it never includes turn times, pricing or comp. [COUNSEL: antitrust review of the shared fields.] A solo broker never builds this. A lender-owned tool has no reason to help a file leave.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Mostly sound. Reg B 1002.9(g) allows a broker or third-party notice on behalf of multiple creditors, and the new creditor's LE is a new obligation. Two gaps. (1) Anti-steering: lender-paid comp to the broker can differ by lender. A pricing-driven move to a lender paying higher broker comp, without presenting 1026.36(e)(3) safe-harbor options, is steering. Recording the reason without comp is not enough, and a comp-blind field doesn't prevent a comp-motivated choice. (2) The borrower should affirmatively consent to the application going to a new creditor, which also supports permissible purpose for the credit reissue and the GLBA 502(e) 'requested by consumer' basis. Redesign: Run a safe-harbor options check on every voluntary move, comparing comp at the old and new lender privately in the compliance role. Require a borrower e-consent step ('send my application to Lender B') before any transfer or reissue. / drop 4/10: Core mechanics already exist. ARIVE's marketplace lets a broker resubmit the same file to another lender, appraisal platforms (Mercury Network, Reggora) support appraisal transfers, and credit vendors handle reissue. A per-lender recipe checklist plus the Reg B and new-LE reminders is a modest gain. Redesign: Make it a 'move file' checklist play using the ARIVE and Zapier triggers, with a small per-lender recipe table. Route its clocks to the LOS rather than a separate engine. / keep 5/10: A real pain point when it happens: appraisal transfers, FHA case transfers, credit reissue and redisclosure. Brokers move files between lenders often enough that a checklist saves hours each time, though not weekly for most MLOs. The per-lender recipe table needs upkeep, and its sharing must stay procedural only. A small build if it stays a checklist generator. Redesign: MVP: a move-plan checklist with templated appraisal-transfer and case-transfer requests, a credit reissue note (from the Credit Cost Router), a new-LE clock and a Reg B action-taken record. Grow per-lender recipes from member use and keep them procedural only.
- **Sources:** https://www.arive.com/integration-partners, https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/9/ (canonical; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/19/ (canonical; not opened this session), https://www.hud.gov/program_offices/housing/sfh/handbook_4000-1 (FHA case and appraisal transfer; canonical; not opened), https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/ (appraisal transfer provisions; canonical; not opened)


## Appraisal Desk: valuation clock, value-gap plan and a borrower-initiated ROV (round 2, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**From order to delivery, the desk tracks the appraisal against the ECOA copy-delivery clock and the purchase contract. On a low value it lays out the options the borrower and agent actually have. When the borrower asks, it assembles the reconsideration of value request in the form the GSEs and the 2024 interagency guidance expect. Nobody contacts the appraiser to push a number.**

- **Problem:** For brokers the appraisal is the least visible and most deal-killing step. The lender's AMC orders it, the broker learns status by email, and a low value arrives with days left on the appraisal contingency. MLOs then improvise. Some call the AMC or appraiser in ways that brush against appraisal-independence rules. Others miss that the borrower must get a free copy of every valuation promptly, or 3 business days before consummation. Some don't know that borrower-initiated reconsideration of value (ROV) is now a standardized lender process, with the interagency ROV guidance of July 2024 and GSE requirements to have a borrower-initiated ROV process. The bias-concern path inside an ROV is a fair-lending matter that needs careful, neutral handling.
- **How it works:** Data: appraisal-ordered, inspection, received and waiver milestones from the LOS and POS connectors. The appraised value and condition flags come from the LOS field-change stream. The contract price and the appraisal-contingency date come from the contingency clock (taken). Comparable-sales suggestions come only from the borrower's or listing agent's own submission (MLS access goes through the agent). No AVM is used as a value argument.

Triggers and actions:
1. Order placed: a Today card tracks days to inspection against this MLO's own normal pace. It shows whether the file got a GSE appraisal waiver or value acceptance, and records the ECOA notice-of-right delivery date (due within 3 business days of application).
2. Appraisal received: the desk starts the copy-to-borrower clock. The copy must be free, delivered promptly upon completion or 3 business days before consummation, whichever is earlier, unless a timing waiver is on file at least 3 business days before. It drafts the delivery message.
3. Value below contract price: a value-gap card sets out the neutral options. These are renegotiate, cover the gap in cash (with an LTV and MI effect, priced only from a priced result), change program, or submit an ROV. Every number is labelled and dated.
4. Borrower asks for an ROV: M8 walks the borrower through it in chat. It collects their reasons, up to the number of alternative comparables the lender's form allows, and any concern that the valuation was affected by bias, routed verbatim and never paraphrased. It builds the lender's ROV package and records the submission date and the lender's response deadline.

What the MLO does: approve and send. The MLO never edits the borrower's bias concern and never contacts the appraiser.
- **Technologies:** LOS and POS milestone and field-change connectors, ECOA valuation timing rules in the effective-dated rules table, M8 chat flow for structured ROV intake (guard: no value promises), Per-lender ROV form templates, Contingency clock (taken) for deadline alignment, D3 send gate for service-only borrower and agent messages
- **Why it's new:** Lenders and AMCs run ROV intake on their own side after the 2024 guidance. Broker CRMs and LOSes treat the appraisal as one milestone. I found no broker-side tool that runs the ECOA copy clock, the value-gap decision and a compliant borrower-initiated ROV together. This is from product knowledge only, because the search budget was exhausted this session and live vendor pages were not checked. Distinct from the taken 'contingency clock' (contract deadlines against the loan's pace) and 'offer math desk' (seller credits and buydown limits). This is the valuation workflow itself.
- **Compliance design:** - ECOA valuation rule (12 CFR 1002.14): notice-of-right and copy-delivery clocks, free copies, and waiver timing. [COUNSEL]
- Appraisal independence (12 CFR 1026.42, plus the Fannie and Freddie appraiser independence requirements): the platform has no 'contact appraiser' action at all. ROVs go to the lender only, through its process.
- Fair lending: bias concerns are passed through in the borrower's own words with a timestamp. Value-gap options are the same for every borrower. No neighborhood or demographic inputs exist anywhere in the flow.
- MLS data: comparables come only from what the agent or borrower submits under the agent's own MLS license (see the market survey MLS findings).
- AI disclosure precedes the M8 ROV intake. No rate or payment is shown without a priced result.
- Records of each clock are kept for the sponsor's retention period.
- **Moat:** Per-lender ROV form and response-time data builds up as members file ROVs. That is procedural, not performance data across competitors. [COUNSEL: antitrust.] The sanitized, aggregated rate at which ROVs succeed by reason category could later show members where ROVs are worth filing. That would be MLO-only, never public advertising, and needs minimum group sizes.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: The ECOA 1002.14 rules are correctly identified: notice of right within 3 business days of application, a copy promptly upon completion or 3 business days before consummation whichever is earlier, and the waiver timing. It has no 'contact appraiser' action and records the borrower's bias concern verbatim. One error: it says comps come from the 'listing agent'. The listing agent represents the seller, and the buyer's agent is the appropriate source; the seller's agent supplying comps to support price raises conflict questions. It overlaps Value Gap Desk and ROV Desk. The 'success rate by reason category' moat risks ROV-success advertising and fair-lending sensitivity. Redesign: Use this as the merged Appraisal Desk, combined with ROV Desk's borrower-filed factual checks. Take comps from the buyer's agent or the borrower. Keep ROV outcome statistics internal, with minimum cells, and never in marketing. / drop 4/10: Duplicate of Value Gap Desk in this batch. The ECOA notice-of-right and copy-delivery clocks are already tracked by the LOS and the appraisal portals, and lender and AMC ROV intake already exists. Redesign: Merge into Value Gap Desk. Keep only the value-gap options page and the copy-delivery reminder as part of it. / keep 6/10: The best of the three appraisal ideas, and the merge target. The ECOA copy-delivery clock and the value-gap options card are cheap and used on every purchase file. Brokers depend on the lender's ROV channel, and ROV success rates are reportedly low, so the payoff is time saved and compliance, not changed values. Purchase-heavy MLOs will use it a few times a month. The GSE and FHA ROV specifics (one borrower ROV, comp limits) are from memory and need checking. Redesign: Merge Value Gap Desk (agent comp upload link, no-contact tags for appraisers and AMCs, pressure-language guard) and ROV Desk (factual-error checklist, borrower's own words) into this. Ship the copy clock and the gap card first, and the ROV package builder second.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/14/ (canonical; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/42/ (canonical; not opened), https://www.federalregister.gov/ (Interagency Guidance on Reconsiderations of Value of Residential Real Estate Valuations, July 2024; search there, not opened), https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/ (borrower-initiated ROV requirements; canonical; not opened), https://guide.freddiemac.com/ (ROV bulletin; canonical; not opened)


## Credit Cost Router: pull once, reissue right, disclose the real fee (round 2, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**The platform tracks only metadata about each credit order: when, which bureaus, which vendor, its cost, its expiry and the borrower's authorization. It never stores report contents. It tells the MLO when to soft-qualify, when to hard-pull, when to reissue instead of re-pull for a second lender, when a report expires before closing, and what the Loan Estimate should say the credit fee is.**

- **Problem:** Credit report costs for mortgages rose sharply after FICO's 2025 pricing changes. As I recall (not re-verified this session), FICO announced a direct-license program in October 2025 with a per-score fee plus a per-funded-loan fee, while tri-merge stayed the GSE norm and FHFA allowed VantageScore 4.0 from July 2025. Brokers routinely pay twice: they pull early for a borrower who isn't ready, re-pull when a report ages past the investor's document-age limit, or let a second lender re-pull instead of reissuing. Every hard pull also used to spray the borrower with trigger-lead calls, which the HPPA has now narrowed. The credit fee is in the Loan Estimate's zero-tolerance bucket in most broker setups, so under-disclosing it means a cure.
- **How it works:** Data captured, metadata only:
- order date and type (soft prequal or hard tri-merge)
- vendor and reissue ID
- bureaus
- invoice cost (forwarded invoice or vendor portal export)
- the report's age limit for the target program, from the Guideline Ledger
- the borrower's written authorization timestamp (from POS e-consent)

No scores, tradelines or report PDFs ever enter Command Center. They stay in the LOS or the credit vendor, which keeps the 'no consumer-report data' certification from the market survey intact.

Triggers and actions:
1. A new lead or Not-yet-desk (taken) file. Router advice: 'soft-qualify first; hard-pull when the borrower has a property or a 30-day target' (MLO's choice).
2. A file moves lenders (Lender Switch Kit). The card says 'reissue report #… to the new lender, fee $X, do not re-pull'.
3. The report's age passes the investor limit before the projected closing date (from the Deal or Contingency clock). A card appears 10 days ahead: 'plan a refresh or supplement, cost $Y, tell the borrower'.
4. LE prep: the credit fee shown is the actual invoiced or expected cost, with a zero-tolerance flag. The File Drift Engine catches any later increase.
5. After any hard pull, M8 can send the borrower an approved, service-only explainer. It says what was pulled, that it counts as one inquiry for rate shopping inside the scoring window, how to use the federal prescreen opt-out (optoutprescreen.com), and that LoanM8 never sells their data.

What the MLO sees: a 'credit' chip on each file with age, expiry and cost, plus monthly spend and avoided re-pulls in the hours-back ledger (taken; this feeds it).
- **Technologies:** Credit-vendor invoice parsing or portal exports (metadata only), Schema-level block on consumer-report fields (role and column policy), Guideline Ledger document-age rules, LOS loan-date triggers, M8 service explainer through the D3 gate, Optional pooled reseller contract negotiated by the platform
- **Why it's new:** Credit vendors and LOS integrations handle ordering and reissue mechanics, but they are sold by the people who bill per pull. Nothing on the broker side optimizes timing and reuse across lenders or ties report age to the projected closing date. I found no CRM that does this; Bonzo/MMI sells credit-based alerts instead, per the market survey. The taken 'not yet desk' re-screens stalled files without reusing the credit report. This idea manages the cost and timing of credit orders themselves, and it deliberately keeps report contents out of the platform.
- **Compliance design:** - FCRA: permissible purpose and the borrower's authorization are recorded before any pull. Report contents never enter the platform; only order metadata does.
- HPPA: the router never requests, receives or uses prescreened lists. The borrower explainer helps them opt out.
- TRID: credit-fee disclosure follows the tolerance rules, and increases are caught by the File Drift Engine.
- Pass-through: the borrower is charged the actual third-party cost only. Per-program allowable-fee rules (FHA and VA) sit in the rules table. [COUNSEL]
- Pooled purchasing: savings reduce the subscription or borrower pass-through cost. They are never paid to MLOs per loan, so Reg Z comp is unaffected and the ownership rewards stay subscription and tenure only. [COUNSEL: RESPA 8, Reg X 1024.14, antitrust on joint purchasing.]
- GLBA: authorization records are NPI and get the standard controls.
- **Moat:** Collective buying is the MLO-owned advantage. A network of members can negotiate one credit-reseller rate card and reissue terms that a solo broker can't, and the router proves the savings per file. It also strengthens the platform's 'no consumer-report data' attestation, which incumbents owned by data vendors can't credibly copy.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: (1) RESPA 8 risk in the pooled purchasing. Credit reporting is a settlement service. If the credit vendor's volume terms reduce the platform's subscription price, or give the platform any rebate, marketing fee or credit, the vendor is giving the platform and its members a thing of value for steering members' credit orders. That is a kickback under 8(a). Only price reductions passed straight through to the per-report charge on the borrower's LE are clean. (2) The FHA and VA allowable-fee rules require actual cost (flagged). (3) The borrower explainer saying a pull 'counts as one inquiry inside the scoring window' depends on the scoring model version (14 vs 45 days), so it must be accurate. HPPA (effective 2026-03) is handled because the router never uses prescreened lists. Redesign: Ban any vendor-to-platform consideration. Have negotiated pricing appear only as a lower per-report cost passed through to the borrower at actual cost, documented per file. Get counsel review of the vendor contract under 8(a)/(b) and antitrust (joint purchasing). Word the inquiry explainer generically ('multiple mortgage inquiries in a short period are usually treated as one by common scoring models'). / drop 4/10: Mostly existing. Credit vendors (Xactus, Informative Research, MeridianLink-connected resellers) offer soft-pull prequalification and reissue, LOSes track document and credit expiration, and trade groups negotiate member credit pricing. The new part is pooled network purchasing, which is a business arrangement, not software. Redesign: Drop the router. Add a credit-expiry date to the pipeline card. Pursue a negotiated network credit rate card as a business-development item, with counsel on RESPA. / keep 7/10: Credit costs have risen sharply since FICO's 2023–2025 per-score price increases (from industry knowledge, not re-verified this run), so avoided re-pulls, reissues to the new lender and expiry planning save brokers real dollars on every file. It stores metadata only, so no FCRA data is in scope, and the build is small. Pooled reseller pricing is the MLO-owned economic edge, but RESPA and antitrust questions on joint purchasing need counsel, and the invoice data needs forwarding or a vendor export. Redesign: MVP: a credit chip per file with age, expiry against the program limit and the projected close, cost and reissue ID, plus reissue prompts tied to the Lender Switch Kit. Negotiate a network reseller rate card once there are 50 or more seats.
- **Sources:** https://support.getbonzo.com/hc/en-us/articles/34656798987538-MMI-One-MonitorBase-Alert-Types, https://mycomplianceresource.com/hppa-and-trigger-leads-are-your-controls-built-for-an-examination-or-an-explanation/, https://www.optoutprescreen.com/ (canonical; not opened), https://www.fhfa.gov/ (VantageScore 4.0 acceptance announcement, July 2025; canonical; not opened), https://www.fico.com/ (Mortgage Direct License Program announcement, October 2025; canonical; not opened)


## Guideline Ledger: cited, effective-dated lender rules with a pipeline impact run (round 2, high impact, ~7 weeks)

**A versioned library of agency guide text (Fannie, Freddie, FHA, VA, USDA) and the sponsor's wholesale lenders' overlays, each rule stored with its citation and effective date. M8 answers scenario questions only by quoting rules. When a rule changes, the ledger re-runs every open file and live pre-approval against the new version and lists the ones affected before the change takes effect.**

- **Problem:** Scenario research is a daily time sink. Can this self-employed borrower with a 1-year history and a non-warrantable condo go anywhere on my panel? Guidelines change constantly: Fannie Selling Guide announcements, Freddie bulletins, HUD Mortgagee Letters, VA circulars, the annual FHFA conforming loan limits (2026 baseline $832,750, as I recall), and each wholesaler's overlays. MLOs learn of a change when a file gets suspended. Generic AI chat about guidelines is risky because it answers from memory with no citation or date. Existing guideline chat (ARIVE keeps a guideline chat, per the market survey) is tied to one LOS and doesn't check your own pipeline against what's changing.
- **How it works:** Data:
- Agency guides and announcements, which are public.
- Each sponsor's lender overlay matrices and bulletins. The sponsor uploads them, or forwards lender emails, which overlaps with the taken 'lender mail room' only at intake. This idea is the rule store and the impact run.
- FHFA loan-limit files by county.

Each document is split into rules: a condition expressed on a small set of loan fields (FICO band, DTI, LTV/CLTV, occupancy, property type, income type, reserves, loan amount by county), the verbatim text, a source URL, a version and effective-from/to dates. Every parse is reviewed by a licensed member before the rule goes live, which is the moderation queue.

Two uses:
1. Scenario fit. The MLO types or says a scenario. The ledger returns which programs on their panel appear to fit and which don't, each with the quoted rule and its effective date. Where no rule covers the question, it says 'not covered; ask the AE'. M8 is only allowed to answer from retrieved rule text, which is an extension of the output guard: no citation, no claim. The rule set checked is saved with the file, which supports the anti-steering record of options checked.
2. Change impact. When a new version lands, a nightly job evaluates open pipeline loans and outstanding pre-approvals, using the fields already in the LOS. It produces cards such as '3 files fall outside Lender X's new condo overlay effective Dec 1; 1 can still be submitted before then.' New county loan limits produce 'these pre-approvals are now conforming or high-balance'.

The MLO decides everything. The ledger never decides eligibility and never touches pricing.
- **Technologies:** Document parsing into structured rules, plus retrieval-augmented M8 with a citation-required guard, Effective-dated rule store (same versioning as D3 and the File Drift Engine), Nightly evaluation over normalized loan fields (MISMO-style names), FHFA county loan-limit dataset, Member review queue for rule parses, Today cards for impact results
- **Why it's new:** Guideline search exists inside individual systems: ARIVE's guideline chat, GSE guide search, and lender AE portals. AI 'guideline bots' answer without effective dates or citations to the lender overlay. None that I know of runs a change against the MLO's own open files and pre-approvals before the effective date. That is from product knowledge only; the search budget was exhausted this session, so it is unverified against current vendor pages. Distinct from taken ideas: 'ad desk' is about marketing pre-clearance, 'pricing coverage planner' is about pricing snapshots, and 'lender mail room' is triage of lender correspondence. The ledger is the cited rule store and its impact engine.
- **Compliance design:** - AI: M8 may only state a rule it can quote, with source and date, and must say a guideline fit is not an approval. Agency and lender decisions stay with the creditor and its AUS. The guard rejects uncited eligibility claims.
- Fair lending: rule conditions may reference only underwriting fields. Protected characteristics and geographic demographic proxies are not representable in the schema. County appears only for loan limits and state-law rules.
- Anti-steering (1026.36(e)): the scenario-fit log records the creditors checked and the options shown, so the safe harbor can be documented. Comp is never an input or a displayed column.
- Copyright and terms: agency guides are public. Lender overlays are used only inside the sponsor's tenant unless the lender's terms permit sharing. [COUNSEL: per-lender confidentiality clauses in broker agreements.]
- Antitrust: only public agency rules are shared network-wide. Lender overlays are shared across sponsors only where the lender publishes them to all brokers. No pricing or turn-time data is shared.
- **Moat:** Rule parsing is the expensive part, and it is shared. Every member's review of a new bulletin improves the ledger for every other member with that lender. The library's completeness and freshness grows with the number of MLOs, which a single-LOS or single-wholesaler tool can't match. A wholesaler-owned tool covers only its own guide.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: No likely violation. (1) Lender overlays and matrices are often confidential under broker agreements, so cross-sponsor sharing only where the lender publishes to all brokers is right. Contract terms must be checked per lender. (2) An M8 'fit' answer that a borrower sees could be read as a prequalification. Keep it MLO-facing, or wrap it in 'not an approval' language. (3) Including county creates a geographic field. It must stay limited to loan limits and state law (stated). Redesign: Keep scenario-fit output MLO-only in v1. Have a licensed member and counsel review each rule before it goes live, and enforce per-lender confidentiality flags in the schema. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted. Guideline search and AI guideline chat already ship (ICE AllRegs, ARIVE guideline chat, GSE guide search tools, lender AE bots), and PPEs check eligibility across lenders. Re-running open files and pre-approvals against a rule change before its effective date is not known to ship and is materially useful. Parsing overlays is expensive and lender terms may restrict it. Redesign: Narrow it to an impact run on agency changes and county loan limits first, using public data. Merge Scenario Commons into it as the scenario-fit mode, and add overlays per sponsor only where the broker agreement permits. / keep 5/10: Parsing lender overlays and bulletins into structured rules is very expensive. Overlays change often, broker agreements may make them confidential, and pricing engines and LOSes (ARIVE guideline chat, Loansifter-style search) already offer guideline search. Agency guides are public and stable enough to structure. The cheapest high-value piece is the FHFA county loan-limit impact run, since the annual limits come out each late November and affect every open pre-approval. Redesign: Phase 1: agency guide rules (Fannie, Freddie, FHA, VA) with citations, plus the loan-limit impact run over open files and pre-approvals. M8 answers only from quoted text. Add per-sponsor lender overlays later, uploaded and reviewed by the sponsor and kept in its tenant.
- **Sources:** https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000319289-arive-ai-beta-doc-classification-extraction-update, https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/ (canonical; not opened this session), https://guide.freddiemac.com/ (canonical; not opened), https://www.hud.gov/hudclips/mortgagee-letters (canonical; not opened), https://www.fhfa.gov/data/conforming-loan-limit (canonical; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (anti-steering; canonical; not opened)


## Comp Record and Funding Reconciler (round 2, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Each sponsor's broker comp agreements with every wholesale lender are stored as versioned documents. For every funded loan, the reconciler checks what should have been paid against what the Closing Disclosure and funding statement show, flags short pays and dual-comp risks, and keeps the Reg Z comp records for the required retention period. It is completely separate from the platform's ownership rewards.**

- **Problem:** Brokerages reconcile lender-paid compensation by hand against funding wires and CD figures, and short pays go unnoticed. The bigger risk is regulatory. Reg Z requires the creditor or loan-originator organization to keep records of comp paid and the comp agreement (1026.25(c)(2), 3 years), forbids dual comp when the borrower pays the broker directly (1026.36(d)(2)), and forbids pay based on loan terms (1026.36(d)(1)). Different comp percentages across lenders are the steering incentive the 1026.36(e) safe harbor exists to neutralize. Small brokers rarely have a clean record of which agreement version applied to which funded file.
- **How it works:** Data:
- The sponsor's comp agreements per lender (uploaded PDFs, parsed into percentage, minimum, maximum, effective dates and lender-paid versus borrower-paid election). Each is reviewed by the sponsor's admin before use.
- The funded-loan event, loan amount and CD Section A broker-comp line from the LOS connector.
- Funding statements or wire remittances the sponsor forwards.

The reconciler computes the expected comp for each funded loan. That is a fixed percentage of the amount of credit, within min and max, which the commentary permits as long as the percentage doesn't vary with the amount. It compares expected against the CD and the funding statement, then raises cards:
- 'short paid $412'
- 'CD shows both borrower-paid and lender-paid comp' (dual-comp alarm)
- 'agreement expired before lock date'
- 'comp % differs across your panel; make sure the safe-harbor options were presented' (links to the Guideline Ledger log)

The sponsor's admin sees a monthly reconciliation and an exportable comp-record binder per lender and per year. The individual MLO sees only their own files' status (paid, pending, disputed), and the dispute email is drafted for the sponsor's ops lead.

Hard firewall: these tables live in a separate schema that the ownership and subscription-reward engine cannot read. That is enforced by database roles and tested in CI. Platform rewards stay subscription- and tenure-only, and nothing in the reconciler feeds any reward.
- **Technologies:** Comp agreement parsing into versioned terms (admin-confirmed), LOS funded-loan and CD field connectors, Funding statement and remittance parsing, Separate Postgres schema with role-based isolation from the ownership ledger, plus a CI test, Exportable record binder (PDF/CSV) for exams, Anti-steering cross-check with the Guideline Ledger scenario log
- **Why it's new:** Broker accounting is done in spreadsheets or general accounting tools, and LOSes show the comp line but don't reconcile it against agreements and wires or build the 1026.25(c)(2) binder. This is from product knowledge only; the search budget was exhausted this session, so I could not verify current vendor features. The taken 'comp firewall and platform-months reward engine' (idea 12) governs the platform's own rewards to MLOs. This idea governs the sponsor's loan compensation records and proves the two never touch.
- **Compliance design:** - Reg Z: 1026.25(c)(2) record retention, 1026.36(d)(1) (no terms-based pay; the fixed-percentage method is recorded with the agreement), 1026.36(d)(2) (dual-comp alarm), 1026.36(e) (safe-harbor cross-check when comp differs by creditor). [COUNSEL]
- State rules on who may pay an MLO: the reconciler records only payments to the sponsor entity, never to individual MLOs, matching the payer-of-record finding in the market survey.
- RESPA 8: comp flows are creditor-to-broker per agreement. The reconciler has no field for payments to agents or other third parties, and any attempt to record one is blocked.
- GLBA: loan amounts and CD data are NPI with standard controls.
- Ownership firewall: no join path from comp data to the ownership or reward tables, enforced by database grants and tested.
- **Moat:** The reconciler is the evidence that an MLO-owned platform keeps loan economics and ownership economics apart. Large companies that tie equity to production can't offer that record. Over time each sponsor's history of reconciled, exam-ready comp records makes switching away costly, and the platform's ownership plan easier for counsel and regulators to approve.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: No violation. It correctly applies 1026.36(d)(1) (a fixed percentage of the credit amount with min and max is permitted under the commentary), the dual-comp alarm under (d)(2), and 1026.25(c)(2) three-year retention. Recording payments only to the sponsor entity matches the state payer-of-record rules. A minor point: individual MLOs' visibility into the sponsor's lender agreements should follow the sponsor's confidentiality obligations. Redesign: Keep the database-role firewall tested in CI. Let the sponsor decide what lender-agreement detail its MLOs see. / drop 4/10: Refuted (training knowledge; lbaware.com was blocked). LO and broker compensation systems (LBA Ware CompenSafe) and mortgage accounting tools (Loan Vision, LOS-integrated accounting) already calculate comp, reconcile it against funding, and keep comp records. The firewall proof between loan comp and ownership rewards is valuable, but it's already idea 12's CI privilege test. Redesign: Integrate with the sponsor's existing comp or accounting system. Keep only the dual-comp alarm from the CD, and the database-role proof under idea 12. / keep 5/10: Sponsors must keep Reg Z comp records (1026.25(c)(2)), and dual-comp alarms are a cheap compliance win. Short pays happen but are uncommon, and broker comp usually arrives on the CD at funding through title, so reconciliation finds little money. LO comp calculation tools already exist (for example LBA Ware CompenSafe, from training knowledge; not re-verified), so the 'nothing exists' claim is wrong. The main value is evidence that loan comp and ownership rewards are kept apart. Redesign: MVP: store comp agreements per lender, compute expected comp versus the CD broker-comp line, raise dual-comp and expired-agreement alarms, and export a retention binder. Keep the separate schema and CI grant test. Skip wire-remittance parsing until sponsors ask for it.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/25/ (canonical; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (canonical; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/2026-04-08/interp-36/, https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_loan_originator_small_entity_compliance_guide.pdf, https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/who-owns-client-data-only-the-contract-knows


## Lawful Lanes: day-one channel plan with sponsor-attributed business relationships (round 3, high impact, ~3 weeks)

**From the moment a recruit's book is imported, each contact shows the legal ways to reach them today, the date each one opens or closes, and a day-one plan ordered by which window closes first. It also fixes a real defect: an 'established business relationship' belongs to the company the loan closed with, not to the loan officer.**

- **Problem:** VERIFIED IN REPO: the D3 gate (lib/send-gate/gate.ts line 117, test at gate.test.ts:48) allows a hand-dialled marketing call to any past client whose loan closed in the last 18 months, without checking which company that loan closed with. Under the FCC and FTC do-not-call rules, an established business relationship (EBR) is between the consumer and the seller entity. It does not carry over to a new sponsor, and it reaches affiliates only where the consumer would reasonably expect it to. So when a recruit imports 'my own closings' from their old brokerage, the new sponsor has no EBR with those people. Calling DNC-listed numbers on that basis is an exposure the platform would hand every recruit on day one. The flip side: a recruit's book still has lawful lanes open today, but nobody shows the loan officer which ones, for whom, or for how long. Those lanes are email, mail, inbound inquiries, the sponsor's own recent closings, written permission, and possibly the personal-relationship rule.
- **How it works:** Data: the D2 contact, its provenance tag and loan record, plus one new required field on each loan, closedWithEntityId (the sponsor or lender of record at closing). It is filled from the POS, or picked by the loan officer on import from a short list ('this brokerage', 'my previous company: name', 'not sure', where 'not sure' counts as not this company). Also used: the D4 opt-out list, the DNC scrub date, consent records, and any non-solicit end date from the clean carry-over attestation.

The system computes a lane set for every contact, with an opens-on and closes-on date per lane:
- (a) Purchase EBR with the current calling entity: 18 months from the last transaction, only when closedWithEntityId matches the calling sponsor or a counsel-approved affiliate.
- (b) Inquiry EBR: 3 months from the contact's own inquiry or application to this entity. A past client who texts the loan officer's number or calls the checkup line opens it, and the system starts the clock from that inbound event.
- (c) Written permission (prior express written consent) from the consent passport.
- (d) The personal-relationship basis, only if counsel confirms how it applies (family, friend or acquaintance of the caller). The loan officer attests per contact, it covers hand-dialled calls only, and it can never cover M8 or texts.
- (e) Email under CAN-SPAM.
- (f) Postal mail.
- (g) Inbound only: non-solicit locked, or no lawful outbound lane.
A do-not-call request to the entity ends that contact's EBR at once, and D4's stop list already feeds this.

The gate change: rule 2 of decide() takes the lane set instead of loanClosedOn. A failed lane check returns a reason such as 'Your loan with them closed at [prior company]. That relationship doesn't carry over to [sponsor]. Record their permission first, or wait for them to reach out.'

The day-one plan (Today, first login after import):
- 'Call by hand now. Your sponsor's EBR ends soon': people sorted by days until it expires. The aim is to ask in that call for written permission through the passport link, before the window closes.
- 'Inbound lane is live': the checkup number, and the loan officer's own number if it can move (see below).
- 'Email lane': size and warm-up status.
- 'Mail lane': contacts with no email.
- 'Locked until [date]'.

Bring-your-number check: the import asks whether the loan officer's mobile number is personally owned or belongs to the prior employer. Only a personally owned number may be ported or hosted on the platform's Twilio account. An employer-owned number is never ported, so inbound past-client texts keep going to the old company and are not taken.

What each party sees:
- The loan officer: lanes and their dates. No lead list, and no advice that a given person may be solicited.
- The borrower: nothing until a lawful touch.
- Counsel: a rule row per state where state do-not-call laws define EBR differently.
- **Technologies:** lib/send-gate/gate.ts lane-set refactor (pure function, unit tests), Neon: closedWithEntityId on the loan, plus a lane_windows view, D2 provenance and attestation tables, D4 cc_optouts and DNC scrub records, Consent passport e-signature link, Twilio number porting and Lookup (carrier type and ownership questions), QStash jobs that alert as a window closes, Today ranking (lib/today/rank.ts) gains EBR-expiry urgency
- **Why it's new:** CRMs mark a contact as 'past client' and stop there. The industry habit is to treat any past client as fair game, which is the lawsuit pattern behind the 2026 mortgage DNC wave: NMN counted 11 suits in about two months, and Loanstream is accused over 272,092 calls to DNC numbers. No CRM in the survey ties an EBR to the entity the loan closed with, starts the 3-month inquiry clock from an inbound event, or turns lane expiry into a day-one call order. This is also a correction to this repo's own D3 gate. VERIFICATION NOTE: the web search budget was used up and ecfr.gov was outside the session network, so the rule text (47 CFR 64.1200(f)(5) EBR definition, the 18-month and 3-month windows, affiliates, termination by a do-not-call request; the TSR's parallel 16 CFR 310.2(q); and the personal-relationship definition and its scope) is from model knowledge, not opened this session.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA and TSR do-not-call: EBR is computed per seller entity and ends on a do-not-call request. EBR never covers artificial-voice calls to mobile numbers (plan 2.1), so M8 and texts still need consent. Personal relationship is off until counsel confirms its scope, attestation-only, and limited to hand-dialled calls. State mini-TCPAs get their own EBR rows in the D3 table (Florida, Oklahoma, Texas, Indiana-style narrower definitions) [COUNSEL]. Non-solicit and trade-secret law: the system never tells the loan officer they may solicit anyone. It shows only the legal channel basis and the loan officer's own attestation. Employer-owned numbers are never ported. HPPA: no credit data. GLBA: lane data stays in the tenant and every read is audited. No lead is sold: lanes are computed per loan officer and never pooled.
- **Moat:** Each new sponsor, state rule and counsel ruling becomes a lane-rule row that every member uses. Inbound events across the network keep inquiry clocks accurate without any outbound volume. A platform whose recruits start compliant on day one recruits better than one whose recruits inherit their old company's exposure.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: The core rule holds. An EBR runs to the seller entity (47 CFR 64.1200(f)(5), 18 months after a transaction or 3 months after an inquiry, ended by a do-not-call request) and only exempts calls from the DNC rule, so it is right not to use it for artificial voice or texts. I found three gaps. (1) A bare inbound text ('hi') may not be an 'inquiry about the seller's products or services', so any inbound event opening a 3-month clock overstates the basis. (2) Some state DNC laws define EBR more narrowly or not at all (Indiana, for example), and Texas's telephone-solicitation registration rules have their own exemptions.
