(3) The 'call by hand before the EBR expires' ordering is still telemarketing: each call needs the time-of-day, caller ID and company-specific DNC rules, and the prior employer's loan record may itself be the employer's protected customer data. Not re-verified this round (search budget used up). Redesign: Open the inquiry clock only when the inbound message is classified as a product or service inquiry and the loan officer confirms it. Otherwise record it as 'inbound, reply only'. Put state EBR rows behind counsel sign-off and set the default for unknown states to 'no EBR'. Every hand-dial goes through the D3 time, caller-ID and internal-DNC checks. Feed loan-record fields from a prior employer into the lane calculation only when the clean carry-over attestation covers that source. / keep 6/10: The search budget was used up, so this comes from knowledge and nothing was opened this session. Partly refuted: telemarketing compliance suites that mortgage lenders use already track EBR per seller entity, with 18-month and 3-month expiry and DNC-request termination. Examples are Gryphon (gryphon.ai), PossibleNOW DNCSolution (possiblenow.com) and DNC.com. Three parts are new or still needed: tying EBR to the entity the loan closed with for a recruit's imported book, the check on whether the loan officer owns their mobile number, and a call order sorted by which window closes first. The fix to D3's loanClosedOn rule is a real correctness bug either way. Redesign: Present this as a D3 correctness fix plus a recruit day-one view, not as a new EBR engine. Use or copy the commercial EBR and state-DNC rule data (Gryphon or PossibleNOW feeds) instead of hand-building state rows. Keep the personal-relationship lane off until counsel rules on it. / keep 8/10: This survives the economics review. It fixes a real defect in D3: rule 2 grants an established business relationship (EBR) from loanClosedOn without asking which entity closed the loan. It needs only one new field plus attestation, builds in about 2 weeks, and every recruit sees it in week one. Two weak spots. First, the POS won't supply closedWithEntityId for loans closed at a prior company, so in practice the field comes from the loan officer's attestation; the 'not sure means no' default is right. Second, in a brokered loan it is unclear whether the 'seller' that holds the EBR is the broker or the creditor. Also be clear that EBR is only an exemption from do-not-call. It never authorizes texts or artificial-voice calls to mobiles (D3 already requires consent for those). So the real day-one value is the ordered list of hand-dialed calls and the passport ask, not a broad unlock. Not re-verified: the web search budget was exhausted and law.cornell.edu and ecfr.gov were unreachable. 47 CFR 64.1200(f)(5) (18 months after a transaction, 3 months after an inquiry, ended by a do-not-call request) and the 'personal relationship' definition are from memory. Redesign: Ship it as a D3 change: replace loanClosedOn with a lane set. Attest closedWithEntityId at import, with a default of 'not this company'. Leave lane (d), personal relationship, off until counsel rules, including who the seller is for brokered loans. The day-one plan is a single sorted list on the D5 Today screen, not a new screen.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/lib/send-gate/gate.ts (line 117: EBR basis from loanClosedOn with no entity check), /home/user/RATEM8/lib/send-gate/gate.test.ts (line 48), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D3 entry: 'A loan officer may hand-dial a past client whose loan closed in the last 18 months'), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-64/subpart-L/section-64.1200 (EBR, telephone solicitation and personal relationship definitions; not opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-310 (TSR EBR definition 310.2(q); not opened this session), https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/mortgage-tcpa-lawsuits-surge-with-11-new-cases, https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/tcpa-class-action-targets-loanstream-over-272k-spam-calls, /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 1 clean carry-over, idea 2 checkup line)


## Home Pass: the past client's loan officer on their lock screen, through Apple and Google Wallet (round 3, high impact, ~3 weeks)

**At closing, or from any re-permission touch, the borrower adds a Wallet pass that holds their loan officer's card, their own home dates and the checkup number. It updates itself with service-only facts and the borrower can delete it any time. The channel needs no phone-number consent and keeps working after a number change.**

- **Problem:** Most of an imported book is 'relationship only'. Phone numbers go stale: idea 1 removes reassigned numbers, and many clients change carriers. After closing, the servicer owns the monthly touch, so the client forgets who their loan officer was by the time they refinance, buy again or get asked for a referral. Emails get filtered. Texts need consent the book doesn't have. The loan officer has no lawful, persistent, opt-in presence on the client's phone.
- **How it works:** Issuance: a signed .pkpass (Apple) and a Google Wallet Generic pass, offered as an 'Add to Wallet' button on the closing-day M8 message, the re-permission email, the checkup line's follow-up link, the borrower's private My Mortgage page and M8 chat.

Front of the pass:
- the loan officer's name, photo, NMLS number and sponsor (rendered from config/licensing.ts, never typed)
- one-tap call and checkup line
- the home's 'next date' field

Back of the pass:
- PMI request window (from the PMI Exit Concierge or verified loan facts)
- escrow analysis month
- home anniversary
- the borrower's own Time Capsules
- 'Share my loan officer', which opens the existing pass-along card flow and never collects a friend's number
- privacy and licensing lines, and 'Remove this pass' instructions

Updates: a pass web service on Vercel pushes field changes via APNs for Apple, and the Wallet API for Google. Changing a field with a changeMessage shows a lock-screen notice, such as 'Your PMI request window opens in 30 days'.
- Every update is a send-gate decision on a new channel, 'wallet', with purpose service only, logged like any other touch.
- Marketing content, rates and 'refinance now' copy are blocked by the guard on this channel.
- If the loan officer changes sponsors, the pass updates the sponsor and licensing lines (the homecoming-routing idea), so the client always holds the loan officer's current, licensed identity.

What the loan officer sees: on Today, '212 past clients carry your Home Pass', plus pass-originated calls and checkup bookings. Those calls are inbound inquiries, which open the Lawful Lanes inquiry window.

What the borrower experiences: a quiet card that surfaces at useful moments (relevantDate on the escrow month) and never asks for anything. Deleting it ends the channel at once, and the pass service records the unregister call as a revocation.
- **Technologies:** Apple PassKit (pass signing certificate, pass web service, APNs updates, changeMessage, relevantDate), Google Wallet API (Generic pass, object updates, messages), Vercel route handlers for the pass web service, D3 send gate with a new 'wallet' channel (service only), lib/m8/guard.ts on all pass text, config/licensing.ts for identity lines, Verified Loan Facts and the PMI Exit Concierge for the date fields
- **Why it's new:** Mortgage past-client tools use email (Homebot), texting (Bonzo, Shape) or AI dialling (Flair, Sela, UWM Mia). None of the CRMs in this survey issues a borrower-held Wallet pass with live service dates; that is from the survey and the earlier idea review, not a fresh search. Wallet passes are common in airlines, retail loyalty and events, not in mortgage servicing or origination. What's different: a persistent, borrower-installed channel tied to the loan officer, not the servicer, and it survives phone-number changes and sponsor changes. VERIFICATION NOTE: Apple's and Google's current pass-notification policies and rate limits weren't re-checked this session (search budget used up). Confirm on developer.apple.com and developers.google.com before the build.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA governs calls and texts to telephone numbers. A Wallet pass update is a push through Apple or Google, not an SMS, but whether any reading of TCPA or a state law reaches it is a counsel question [COUNSEL]. So the channel is service-only by design, with no marketing or rate content, and the guard enforces that. No rate ever appears on a pass (no priced result is attached). Apple's and Google's platform policies on promotional notifications are followed and checked again before build. Every pass shows the AI-free identity and NMLS and sponsor licensing lines from config. GLBA: the pass carries dates and names only. No balance, rate or account number is on the pass, because passes can show on a locked screen. Fair lending: offered to every closed borrower the same way. RESPA: nothing of value is attached. Revocation: deleting the pass, or turning off its notifications, ends updates, and that is honored as a channel revocation.
- **Moat:** Each closing adds a pass that stays. The installed base of passes is the loan officer's portable, borrower-chosen relationship asset. It's worth more on a platform where it follows the loan officer across sponsors, which large companies that own the borrower relationship won't offer.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: A Wallet push is probably outside the TCPA. The real problems are elsewhere. (1) When the loan officer changes sponsors, the pass rebrands to the new sponsor but keeps pushing dates taken from the old sponsor's loan file. That reuses the old sponsor's customer NPI for a new entity (Reg P reuse limits under 1016.11, plus trade-secret and non-solicit exposure), and the rebrand is in effect a marketing contact for the new sponsor. (2) Apple's and Google's Wallet policies limit promotional and financial-account passes, and Google's Generic pass terms need checking. (3) A lock-screen 'PMI window opens' notice still reveals that a mortgage relationship exists to anyone who sees the phone. Redesign: On a sponsor change, freeze the pass data that came from the old file and show 'your loan officer has moved; tap to keep them'. Push nothing further until the borrower re-opts in for the new entity. Keep notification text generic ('An update about your home is ready') with the details behind the pass. Confirm Apple and Google pass policies in writing before the build. Record pass deletion as a revocation for every channel the pass fed. / keep 5/10: From knowledge, not searched this session. Wallet business cards are common for loan officers already: Blinq, HiHello and Popl all offer 'Add to Apple Wallet'. Loyalty and event passes with push-updated fields are routine through PassKit-style vendors. I know of no mortgage tool that issues a borrower-held pass with service dates that update and that follows the loan officer across sponsors. Expect low adoption. Apple limits pass-update notifications and treats promotional use as a policy risk. Whether TCPA reaches the channel is an open question. Redesign: Ship it as a cheap add-on to the closing-day message, not as a main channel. Show only the dates on the pass, and send changeMessage notices only for service dates the borrower picked. Count how many borrowers install and keep the pass before investing in the pass web service. Recheck Apple's and Google's notification policies first. / keep 5/10: It's cheap: an Apple developer account at $99 a year, a pass-type certificate, APNs pushes at no cost, and a Google Wallet issuer account. A small team can build it in 2 to 3 weeks. Economically, adoption and usefulness are doubtful. A mortgage pass gets updated only a few times a year (escrow, PMI, anniversary), and passes with little to do tend to get deleted. No data shows borrowers keep a loan officer card in Wallet. Google limits how often message notifications can be pushed (I recall about 3 per 24 hours); I couldn't confirm this because developers.google.com was unreachable. Apple's pass guidelines discourage marketing use. Pass-originated calls will be rare, so the '212 past clients carry your pass' metric is vanity. The claim that the channel 'needs no consent' is untested. Redesign: Make it a closing-package extra, not a channel strategy: one 'Add to Wallet' button on the closing-day message. Use service-only field updates, no more than 4 a year. Measure 90-day retention on the first 50 closings before building the update service, sponsor-change re-rendering or the Today metrics.
- **Sources:** https://developer.apple.com/documentation/walletpasses (PassKit pass updates and changeMessage; not opened this session), https://developers.google.com/wallet/generic (Google Wallet Generic passes and messages; not opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-64/subpart-L/section-64.1200 (TCPA scope: calls and texts to telephone numbers; not opened this session), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (ideas 4, 6, 9: the loan facts, capsules and PMI dates the pass displays), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (facts from config; the guard; no rates outside a priced result)


## Investor Book Desk: 1031 exchange clock, delayed-financing and seasoning windows for past clients who own rentals (round 3, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**Past clients who own investment property get a portfolio view of the hard dates that decide whether their next loan works: the 45-day and 180-day exchange deadlines, delayed-financing and cash-out seasoning windows, and lease-renewal dates that DSCR files need. Each date becomes a service card the loan officer acts on.**

- **Problem:** A loan officer's most valuable past clients are often small investors with 2 to 10 doors who refinance, buy and exchange repeatedly. Their transactions run on statutory clocks the loan officer doesn't track. A 1031 exchange fails if the replacement property isn't identified in 45 days or received within 180, and financing is the usual bottleneck. A cash buyer can lose the delayed-financing exception by missing its window. A DSCR refinance stalls when the lease expired last month. Every CRM in the survey treats an investor like a homeowner, with anniversaries and rate drops. The ideas already taken (move-up sequencer, keep-and-rent desk, next home plan) are about owner-occupants.
- **How it works:** Data:
- Occupancy and loan purpose from the POS (ARIVE, Encompass) on the loan officer's own closings, or from what the borrower tells M8 or uploads: a closing statement, a lease, or the qualified intermediary's (QI) exchange agreement.
- The property address from public record, used only for the client's own properties.
- A lender-terms table: each wholesaler's DSCR, delayed-financing and seasoning rules, cited and dated (the guideline-ledger pattern).

Triggers:
- The borrower says 'I'm selling the duplex' to M8 or on a call, or uploads a sale contract.
- One of their properties appears as a listing (RETR). That prompts a personal call only, never an automated one.
- A lease end date in their record is 60 days out.
- A cash purchase closes, which starts the delayed-financing window.

What the system does:
- 1031 Clock: day 0 is the relinquished property's transfer date (from the borrower's settlement statement). It plots day 45 (identification) and day 180 (or the tax return due date if earlier) and lays the replacement-loan timeline back from those. For each candidate property: pre-approval, appraisal order, DSCR or full-doc path, and the latest safe application date.
- With the borrower's direction (the partner-room pattern), the QI and the borrower's tax adviser get a read-only date view.
- Seasoning and delayed-financing windows per property, per lender, with citations.
- Lease-renewal reminders framed as 'documents your next DSCR file will need'.

What the loan officer sees: 'Exchange clock: Lee duplex sold 10/02. ID deadline 11/16 (passed, 3 identified). Replacement must close by 03/31. Latest safe application for a 30-day DSCR file: 02/20.'

What the borrower gets: a page with their own dates and a plain-language 'what your QI and tax adviser decide versus what your loan officer handles'. M8 explains the financing steps only.
- **Technologies:** POS and LOS occupancy and purpose fields via the connector interface, The Second Look and Verified Loan Facts document parser, with new types: settlement statement, lease, QI exchange agreement, A lender-terms table in config with citations and effective dates, QStash deadline jobs, RETR listing CSV for the client's own properties, The partner-room borrower-directed sharing model, lib/pricing for dated DSCR or investment examples, where a priced result exists
- **Why it's new:** Exchange deadlines are tracked by QIs and tax advisers, and investor CRMs (real-estate-investor tools) track deals, not the financing clock. Mortgage CRMs (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert, Jungo) have no investor-portfolio or exchange-deadline model in the survey. The difference is that the loan officer's past-client book becomes a set of investor portfolios with the statutory and investor clocks that decide funding. VERIFICATION NOTE (search budget used up, not re-checked this session): the 1031 deadlines are IRC 1031(a)(3) and Treas. Reg. 1.1031(k)-1. Fannie Mae's delayed-financing exception (within about 6 months of a cash purchase) and the cash-out seasoning rules (6 months on title, and a 12-month rule for the existing first lien) come from model knowledge of Selling Guide B2-1.3-03 and must be confirmed in the current Guide.
- **Compliance design:** Licensing: no tax or legal advice. M8 and the page say the QI and tax adviser decide exchange eligibility, and the system only shows dates the borrower supplied. Reg Z: credit primarily for business purposes, including non-owner-occupied rentals, is generally outside TILA (12 CFR 1026.3(a)), so 1026.36(d) may not reach these loans. The platform still applies the same flat, term-blind pay policy, and the reward engine never sees loans [COUNSEL]. ECOA and Reg B still apply to business credit, and investor status is used for timing only, never ranking by demographics. SAFE Act and state licensing: some states require a mortgage license for some business-purpose residential loans, so the licensing check still runs [COUNSEL]. TCPA: a business-purpose call to a mobile is still a TCPA call, so the same gate and Lawful Lanes apply. RESPA 8: no payment to or from QIs or advisers, and sharing is borrower-directed. GLBA: the borrower's documents are deleted after extraction and every read is audited. No rate outside a priced result.
- **Moat:** Every member's investor closings add lender-terms rows (DSCR lease rules, seasoning quirks) that the whole network can cite. Repeat investors stay with the loan officer whose desk keeps their clocks, which compounds with tenure on the platform.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: The structure is sound: it shows dates the borrower supplied and leaves eligibility to the QI and the tax adviser. The 'property appears as a listing (RETR)' trigger conflicts with the survey's reading of the RETR terms, which bar solicitation and rules-engine use, so a call prompted by that feed is likely a terms violation. 'Latest safe application date' can read as a promise of timing. Business-purpose loans fall outside TILA (1026.3(a)) but stay under ECOA, FCRA, state licensing, TCPA and GLBA, and calling a mobile about a new purchase loan is still telemarketing. Not re-verified this round (search budget used up). Redesign: Drop the RETR listing trigger, or use it only after RETR confirms in writing that the use is permitted. Rely instead on the borrower's own statements, uploads and requested reminders. Label every computed date as 'planning estimate, confirm with your QI'. Treat lease-renewal reminders that mention a new loan as marketing that goes through the gate. / keep 5/10: From knowledge. Qualified intermediaries' exchange software and DSCR lenders' scenario tools already track 45/180-day deadlines and seasoning, so the clock itself is not new. I know of no mortgage CRM that lays the financing timeline backward from the exchange deadlines across the loan officer's own investor clients, so the combination is new. The audience is narrow, and the borrower must supply settlement statements for it to work. Redesign: Cut it to the 1031 clock plus latest-safe-application date, fed only from documents the borrower uploads. Put the seasoning and delayed-financing rules in the shared guideline ledger rather than building a separate lender-terms table. Ship it after the core past-client features. / drop 4/10: It serves a niche. Most loan officers' past-client books contain few active 1031 exchangers, and the qualified intermediary already runs the 45-day and 180-day clock. The expensive part is a per-wholesaler DSCR, seasoning and delayed-financing table, cited and dated. Wholesalers change overlays often, and for a small team that is a guideline-ledger maintenance burden. The data is mostly borrower-supplied (settlement statements, leases), so coverage will be thin. Weekly use is unlikely outside investor-focused loan officers. The Fannie Mae B2-1.3-03 figures are from memory and weren't re-verified (search budget exhausted). Redesign: Fold it into the existing Time Capsule and guideline ledger. Let a borrower or loan officer set an exchange day 0, and the system adds the day 45, day 180 and latest-safe-application dates. Add lender seasoning rows only when a member cites them from a real file. Don't build a portfolio view until investor loan officers ask for one.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/26/1031 (45 and 180 day rule at 1031(a)(3); not opened this session), https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/26/1.1031(k)-1 (deferred exchange rules; not opened this session), https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/ (B2-1.3-03 cash-out and delayed financing; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/3/ (business-purpose exemption; not opened this session), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (4.6 connector interface; 2.4 comp rules)


## Pocket Loan Officer: a connector the past client installs in their own AI assistant (round 3, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**The past client adds a 'My mortgage with [loan officer]' connector to ChatGPT, Claude or Gemini. When they ask their assistant 'can I afford a pool?' or 'should I refinance?', it answers from their real loan facts and a dated, priced example, and hands them to their own loan officer. All contact is inbound, consent is never accepted from the AI, and the borrower can revoke it in one tap.**

- **Problem:** Past clients now ask their own AI assistant before they call anyone. The assistant knows nothing about their loan, so it guesses from advertised rates, or the conversation goes into whichever large funnel is built into the assistant (Zillow and Rocket-style apps, per the survey). The loan officer loses the moment of intent without ever knowing it happened. The Licensed Agent Gateway idea in the survey serves anonymous shoppers. Nothing serves the loan officer's existing, identified client inside the assistant that client already uses.
- **How it works:** Install: from the My Mortgage page, Home Pass or a checkup-line link, the borrower adds the connector (a remote MCP server per tenant) and signs in with a passkey or magic link bound to their contact record. They pick scopes: loan facts, home dates, ask my loan officer. A short plain-language screen says what the assistant will be able to read, that the assistant company handles the conversation under its own terms, and how to revoke.

Tools, all read-mostly:
- get_my_loan_facts: from Verified Loan Facts or the POS. Redacted, no account numbers.
- get_home_dates: PMI window, escrow month, capsules.
- get_dated_example: returns a priced, dated example with APR, points, the not-a-commitment line and a signed quote ID, or 'no price available'. Never a free-text rate.
- ask_my_loan_officer: creates a handoff with the borrower's own question. This is an inquiry, so it starts the Lawful Lanes inquiry clock. It returns a link the human opens in their own browser to choose callback consent per channel. The tool itself can never accept consent, an application, an SSN, a date of birth or documents, which matches AGENTS.md: 'an agent cannot consent for a person'.
- share_my_loan_officer: the pass-along card link.

Every tool result is structured data with a fixed disclosure field: 'Data from LoanM8 for [loan officer], NMLS [n], [sponsor]. Not a commitment to lend. M8 is not part of this conversation.'

What the loan officer sees: on Today, 'Dana asked her assistant about a pool remodel; she requested a callback Tuesday after 5', with the borrower's own question.

Auditing: every read is in the GLBA audit log, and the borrower's private page lists each assistant session that read their data, with a revoke button.
- **Technologies:** MCP server per tenant (stateless 2026-07-28 revision recorded in AGENTS.md; MCP authorization over OAuth 2.1), OAuth with passkeys (WebAuthn) bound to the D2 contact, Assistant connector and app surfaces (OpenAI Apps SDK on MCP; remote MCP connectors in other assistants), lib/pricing build and canonical hash for signed dated examples, lib/m8/guard.ts and redaction on every tool output, GLBA read-audit log, The Lawful Lanes inquiry clock and the consent passport
- **Why it's new:** The survey found no mortgage loan officer offering a borrower-installed, authenticated connector over the borrower's own loan. nCino's MCP servers are for lenders and their loan officers. Survey opening 1, the Licensed Agent Gateway, is an anonymous shopping front door. LoanM8's /api/agent/* endpoints are anonymous calculators. This idea is an identified past client's own record, installed by that client, with every answer priced or refused and every contact started by the borrower. VERIFICATION NOTE: assistant connector availability and terms as of 2026-11-18 weren't re-checked (search budget used up). The OpenAI Apps SDK on MCP (Oct 2025) is from the survey, and the MCP revisions are from /home/user/RATEM8/AGENTS.md.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA: the borrower directs disclosure of their own nonpublic personal information to the assistant provider. That uses the exception for disclosure with the consumer's consent or at their direction (15 U.S.C. 6802(e)(2)), with data minimized (no account numbers or balances unless the borrower picks that scope), every read audited, and revocation instant [COUNSEL]. Reg P notice update. TCPA: no outbound contact. Any callback needs the borrower's own e-signed consent in their browser, never consent relayed by the AI. AI disclosure: the platform isn't the chatbot, and every result carries the identity and not-M8 line. State chatbot and AI laws: reviewed for whether supplying data to a consumer's own assistant triggers anything [COUNSEL]. No rate outside a priced result, enforced by the tool schema. RESPA 8 and Reg Z advertising: dated examples carry the trigger-term disclosures. Prompt injection: tool outputs are data only, and inbound questions are treated as data, never instructions. The ATTORNEY_REVIEW_LIST requires counsel review before any MCP surface ships.
- **Moat:** Every installed connector is a durable, borrower-chosen link between a client's assistant and their own loan officer. It's portable across sponsors on a member-owned platform. As assistants become where people shop, the network whose members' clients already have their loan officer installed keeps the intent that large funnels would otherwise capture.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: Disclosure directed by the consumer (15 U.S.C. 6802(e)(2)) supports sharing the borrower's own loan facts. The binding rule 'an AI never quotes a rate outside a priced result' cannot be enforced once get_dated_example hands a rate to ChatGPT, Gemini or Claude: the third-party model can paraphrase it, round it, drop the APR or call it a recommendation, and the platform cannot control or guard that output. That is a Reg Z trigger-term and UDAAP risk the platform supplied the data for. Once the data is disclosed it also falls under the assistant vendor's retention and training terms, which GLBA reuse limits do not reach. Redesign: In v1, get_dated_example returns no rate figures, only a signed link to a hosted priced page that carries APR, points and the not-a-commitment line. Keep loan facts minimal (no balance or rate unless the borrower picks that scope, with a warning). Publish only to assistant platforms whose app terms bar training on tool results. Counsel review before any MCP surface ships, as ATTORNEY_REVIEW_LIST already requires. / keep 6/10: From knowledge, not re-checked. Consumer assistant apps from mortgage and real estate brands, such as Zillow's ChatGPT app, are anonymous shopping front doors. nCino's MCP servers are for lender staff. I know of no connector that a borrower authenticates over their own closed loan and that hands off to their own loan officer. It looks new. The open risks are adoption and the GLBA consumer-direction analysis. Redesign: Build it on the Verified Loan Facts vault with the same tool schema as Own-AI Bridge (one MCP codebase, with borrower and loan-officer scopes). Gate it behind counsel review per ATTORNEY_REVIEW_LIST. Start with read-only get_home_dates and ask_my_loan_officer, and add priced examples only after L4b. / drop 3/10: Demand fails first. Very few past clients will find a per-tenant MCP connector, install it, sign in and pick scopes for their mortgage. Consumer connector directories (ChatGPT apps, Claude connectors) require app review and impose their own terms. The build is moderate (an OAuth MCP server per tenant, scopes, an audit page, a signed-quote service) for close to zero weekly use. GLBA disclosure to assistant vendors, the consent-direction exception and counsel review of the MCP surface add fixed cost before any return. get_dated_example depends on pricing that is a daily manual pull covering a few fixed scenarios, so most answers would be 'no price available'. Connector terms as of 2026-11 weren't re-checked (search budget exhausted). Redesign: Defer it. If it's revisited, make it a read-only 'my home dates plus ask my loan officer' endpoint reached from the My Mortgage page, and build it only after Own-AI Bridge proves the MCP stack and the AGENTS.md review is done.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/AGENTS.md (section 4: MCP spec revisions and SDK versions; agent cannot consent for a person), /home/user/RATEM8/ATTORNEY_REVIEW_LIST.md (review required before any MCP surface), https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification (authorization; not opened this session), https://openai.com/index/introducing-apps-in-chatgpt/ (Apps SDK on MCP; via the market survey), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/6802 (GLBA exception at (e)(2); not opened this session), https://developer.ncinomortgage.com/mortgage/docs/lo-mcp-server (lender-side MCP, for contrast)


## Ungated Review Desk: every closed client asked once, the same way, with replies that never disclose the loan (round 3, medium impact, ~2 weeks)

**Command Center asks every closed borrower for a public review at one fixed moment, with no incentive and no screening by satisfaction. Unhappy replies go to the loan officer to fix, never away from the review site. M8 drafts public responses that never confirm the person is a customer. That turns the book into inbound referrals without the review-gating and privacy traps most loan officers fall into.**

- **Problem:** Referrals from a past-client book now run largely through public reputation: Google Business Profile, Zillow lender reviews and similar. The common practice creates three exposures:
- Review gating: asking only clients who scored you highly. The FTC's 2023 Endorsement Guides treat procurement that distorts reviews as deceptive, and Google's policy prohibits selective solicitation.
- Incentives: gift cards for reviews, restricted by the FTC's Consumer Reviews and Testimonials Rule.
- Replying to a review with loan details ('glad we got you that 30-year fixed!'), which discloses that the person is a customer. Under Reg P that fact is itself nonpublic personal information.
LoanM8's own check:copy bans testimonials in site copy, so the platform needs a lawful path to reputation that lives on third-party sites.
- **How it works:** Trigger: funding date plus 30 days (one fixed point for everyone, configurable by the sponsor), from the POS or the loan officer marking it closed.

The ask: the same message to every funded borrower, through a lawful lane: email under CAN-SPAM, or a text only if marketing-text consent exists, because a review request is treated as marketing.
- It links to the review platforms the loan officer has connected.
- No incentive, no sweepstakes, no 'if you're happy' wording.
- A pre-ask satisfaction survey is never used to decide who gets the ask.

The 'something wasn't right' path: the same message has a second link where the borrower can tell the loan officer directly. It does not replace the review link, which stays in the message. Those replies open a Today card and feed the complaint-handling flow.

Replies: when a new public review appears (Google Business Profile API for the loan officer's own profile, or pasted in), M8 drafts a response under a GLBA filter.
- The draft never confirms a loan, terms, dates, the property or that the person is a client.
- It thanks them and invites them to call.
- For negative reviews it offers an offline conversation and never argues facts in public.
- The loan officer approves every reply.

Records: a log proving every funded borrower got the same single ask (who, when, channel), for an FTC, state examiner or platform inquiry.

What the loan officer sees: ask coverage (100% target), replies awaiting approval, issues raised privately. Never a star-rating leaderboard used for ranking clients.

What the borrower experiences: one polite ask, then silence.
- **Technologies:** POS funding events via the connector interface, D3 send gate (marketing purpose) and Resend and Twilio through it, Google Business Profile API (reviews, replies) for the loan officer's own listing, An M8 drafting model with a GLBA disclosure filter added to lib/m8/guard.ts, An append-only ask log in Neon, check:copy rules applied to the ask and reply templates
- **Why it's new:** Reputation tools (Birdeye, Podium, Experience.com and similar) are known for 'request a review' flows, and some offer sentiment-first funnels that route happy customers to public sites, which is the gating pattern. The survey's mortgage CRMs don't advertise an ungated, proof-logged process or GLBA-filtered review replies. The new part is building the FTC rule, platform policy and the Reg P 'customer relationship is NPI' point into the workflow, with a log that proves equal treatment. VERIFICATION NOTE (search budget used up, not re-checked this session): FTC Rule 16 CFR Part 465 (effective 2024-10-21: fake reviews, conditional incentives, insider reviews, review suppression), the 2023 Endorsement Guides (16 CFR 255) on distorting procurement, Google's review policy on selective solicitation, and Reg P's treatment of customer-relationship information as NPI are from model knowledge.
- **Compliance design:** FTC 16 CFR 465 and 16 CFR 255: no incentives, no gating, no suppression, no insider reviews without disclosure (loan officers' family and staff are flagged), and proof that everyone was asked equally. GLBA and Reg P: replies never confirm the relationship or any loan detail, and the guard blocks names, terms and dates. Mortgage advertising: reviews stay on third-party platforms and are not quoted in LoanM8 copy, consistent with check:copy's testimonial ban, unless counsel clears a specific use under Reg N (12 CFR 1014) and state advertising rules [COUNSEL]. TCPA: the ask is treated as marketing, so the send gate and Lawful Lanes apply. RESPA 8: nothing of value is given for reviews or referrals. Fair lending: the same ask for every funded borrower, no selection.
- **Moat:** The ask log and reply filter get stronger with every sponsor's and counsel's review. A network-wide record that every member asks ungated is a credible trust signal for brokers' compliance teams, and it's evidence the platform's members don't game reputation.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Consistent with the FTC rule at 16 CFR 465 (no conditioned incentives, no suppression), the Endorsement Guides, Google's ban on selective solicitation, and Reg P's treatment of the customer relationship as NPI. The remaining risks are small. Many state mortgage advertising rules treat solicited reviews the platform displays as testimonials, which this design already avoids. A borrower whose loan funded but who later complained should still get the ask. A public reply that thanks the reviewer by name can itself confirm the relationship. Redesign: The reply template should not use the reviewer's name or any wording such as 'thanks for working with us'. Use a generic acknowledgment that invites them to call the office. Keep the ask log under the sponsor's books-and-records retention. Do not drop borrowers from the ask because they have an open complaint. / drop 3/10: Refuted, from knowledge. Experience.com (formerly Social Survey, experience.com) is mortgage-specific and already sends an automatic post-closing survey and review request to every funded borrower through LOS integrations. Google and FTC Part 465 already push vendors away from review gating, and Birdeye (birdeye.com) and Podium dropped gated funnels. Birdeye and others ship AI-drafted review replies, including privacy-filtered and HIPAA-safe reply modes in healthcare. The equal-ask log is a small increment. Redesign: Don't build it as its own feature. Integrate with an existing review platform, and add two small things in Command Center: a send-gate rule that the ask goes once to every funded file, and a GLBA phrase filter on reply drafts. / keep 7/10: This passes. Reviews drive inbound business, the trigger is a deterministic funding date plus 30 days, the build is small (a template, a log, a drafted reply behind a GLBA filter), and loan officers would approve replies a few times a month. Practical limits. Access to the Google Business Profile reviews API requires an application, and many individual loan officers don't have their own verified profile. Their sponsor may control the brand listing, and for many loan officers Zillow or Experience.com reviews matter more and can only be requested through those platforms. Reviews can't be quoted in LoanM8 copy (check:copy bans testimonials), so the payoff is only on third-party sites. 16 CFR 465 (effective 2024-10-21) and Google's policy against selective solicitation are from memory and weren't re-verified. Redesign: Version 1: the equal-ask scheduler, the proof log, the private 'something wasn't right' link, and reply drafting from pasted reviews. Add the GBP API only for loan officers with verified profiles. Add deep links for Zillow and Experience.com but don't integrate with them.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-465 (Consumer Reviews and Testimonials Rule; not opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-255 (Endorsement Guides; not opened this session), https://support.google.com/contributionpolicy/answer/7400114 (Google prohibited and restricted review content; not opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1016 (Reg P definition of nonpublic personal information; not opened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1014 (Reg N, Mortgage Acts and Practices advertising; not opened this session), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (check:copy bans testimonials in site copy)


## Clean Sender: a portable, authenticated email lane that gets a recruit's first message to an imported book into the inbox (round 3, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Before the first re-permission email goes out, each loan officer gets an authenticated sending domain they can take with them. The list is cleaned against bounces, traps and network-wide unsubscribes, sending ramps up on a schedule, and a complaint budget can pause the campaign for that loan officer alone. Email is the main lawful day-one lane, and it only works if it lands.**

- **Problem:** Idea 2's Re-permission Sprint and the Lawful Lanes plan depend on email, the one outbound channel that needs no prior consent under CAN-SPAM. A recruit's imported book is the worst case for deliverability: years-old addresses, role accounts, recycled spam traps, and a brand-new sending identity. Mailbox providers now enforce bulk-sender rules. Blasting 2,000 old contacts from a new domain gets the domain throttled or junked on day one. In a shared-reputation setup, one member's dirty list also hurts every other member. That is a network risk unique to a member-owned platform.
- **How it works:** Sending identity: either the loan officer's own domain (bring your own domain, guided DNS setup) or a per-tenant subdomain. Own domain is the default, because a domain's reputation moves with the loan officer when they change sponsors.
- SPF, DKIM and aligned DMARC are configured and checked before any send.
- BIMI is optional once DMARC enforcement is reached.
- The domain is verified in Google Postmaster Tools.

List hygiene before the first send:
- syntax and MX checks, role and disposable-address flags
- a verification-vendor pass [VENDOR TBD]
- the network-wide hashed unsubscribe and opt-out list (the One Stop pattern, so an unsubscribe given to any member is honored by all)
- provenance locks from D2 and the Lawful Lanes 'inbound only' contacts removed

Ramp: a staged warm-up schedule per domain, most engaged and most recent clients first (closings in the last 2 years before older ones). It auto-throttles when bounces exceed about 2% or complaints exceed about 0.1% of a batch (thresholds set by the owner).

Complaint budget: a new D3 rule. When a loan officer's rolling complaint rate nears the mailbox-provider ceiling, the gate blocks further marketing email for that loan officer until reviewed. Other members are unaffected, because reputation is isolated per domain.

Every marketing email carries:
- RFC 8058 one-click List-Unsubscribe, honored at once across channels for email
- the sponsor's physical address
- the licensing line from config

Fallback: addresses that hard-bounce move to the mail lane (a postcard with the consent-passport QR code).

What the loan officer sees: 'Email lane: 288 reachable, 41 removed (bounce risk), warming: day 3 of 10, inbox placement on seed tests 9/10.'

What the borrower sees: one readable email from a domain that matches the loan officer, with one-click unsubscribe.
- **Technologies:** Resend custom domains and webhooks (bounce, complaint), DNS automation and checks (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, optional BIMI), Google Postmaster Tools and Microsoft SNDS monitoring, An email verification vendor [VENDOR TBD], RFC 8058 one-click unsubscribe, The D3 send gate with a complaint-budget rule, The network hashed opt-out list, A print-and-mail API for the bounce fallback
- **Why it's new:** Email service providers offer warm-up and verification as generic tools, and the CRMs in this survey send email, but none is described as isolating reputation per member, letting the sending domain follow the loan officer across sponsors, or making a complaint budget a compliance gate tied to consent provenance. The new part is that deliverability becomes a send-gate rule and a portability feature for an ownership network. VERIFICATION NOTE (search budget used up, not re-checked this session): Gmail and Yahoo bulk-sender requirements from February 2024 (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, one-click unsubscribe, honoring within about 2 days, spam rate under 0.3%) and Microsoft Outlook.com's 2025 requirements for high-volume senders are from model knowledge.
- **Compliance design:** CAN-SPAM: accurate header and From, a physical address, a working unsubscribe honored well within 10 business days (the platform does it at once). Email is never a back door to texting or calling: an email reply grants no phone consent, and the consent passport link is the only path. TCPA: not applicable to email, but the network opt-out list applies across channels where a person said stop. GLBA: list hygiene vendors receive only email addresses under a service-provider agreement, with no other personal information, and are listed in the Safeguards vendor inventory. No lead is sold: suppression uses salted hashes and never reveals one member's contacts to another. Fair lending: ramp order uses recency and engagement only, never demographics or geography proxies. State privacy: prospects who never applied get state-privacy rights handling.
- **Moat:** Domain reputation builds with every clean month, and it belongs to the loan officer, so long-tenured members carry a deliverability asset that new entrants and departing staff at large companies can't. Network-wide suppression gets more complete as membership grows, which lowers every member's complaint rate.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: CAN-SPAM is met. Two compliance gaps. (1) A personal sending domain owned by the loan officer conflicts with state licensing and advertising rules. Many states require ads to carry the licensed company's name and NMLS number, require domains and trade names to be on the sponsor's NMLS record, and require the sponsor to supervise and keep every advertisement. An email domain owned by the loan officer and outside that control is a supervision and books-and-records gap. (2) Network-wide hashed unsubscribe matching across unaffiliated companies tells member B that its contact is known elsewhere. For borrower emails that are NPI, that is a cross-tenant disclosure unless it runs as a compliance-only service-provider use. CAN-SPAM 7704(a)(4)(A)(iv) lets an opt-out list be transferred only for compliance purposes. Redesign: Before the first send, require the domain to be listed as an approved website or DBA on the sponsor's NMLS record, with the sponsor holding DNS admin co-access and archive copies. Portability means the loan officer keeps the domain and the sponsor's oversight ends at departure. Run network suppression through the Commons Gatekeeper as a compliance-only use that returns 'suppress' and nothing else. Never return a match count or which tenant the person came from. / drop 3/10: Refuted, from knowledge. Per-tenant authenticated domains, SPF/DKIM/DMARC setup wizards, list verification (ZeroBounce, NeverBounce), staged warm-up, RFC 8058 one-click unsubscribe and automatic pausing on complaint rates are standard in SendGrid, Postmark, Mailgun and Validity Everest. Because the domain belongs to the loan officer, reputation already moves with them when they bring their own domain. Network suppression overlaps the One Stop idea. Redesign: Treat it as required infrastructure for the D-series email lane, not as a differentiator. Use the ESP's subuser and domain features and add one D3 rule: block marketing email for a loan officer when their complaint rate nears the ceiling. / keep 8/10: It has strong economics. Email is the main lawful day-one lane, and an unwarmed bulk send from a new domain to an old book will bounce and draw complaints. Resend or SES support many verified domains. Verification vendors charge fractions of a cent per address, and warm-up is a scheduler. A small team can build it. Caveats. Many sponsors require loan officers to send from the company domain for supervision and books-and-records reasons, so a portable 'own domain' may not be allowed, and the per-tenant subdomain is likely the common case. Seed-test inbox placement tools are an extra subscription cost. The Gmail and Yahoo 2024 rules (DMARC, one-click unsubscribe, spam rate under 0.3%) and Microsoft's 2025 rules are from memory and weren't re-verified. Redesign: Default to whatever sending domain the sponsor approves, and offer the loan officer's own domain only where the sponsor permits it. Ship the DNS checks, list hygiene, the network suppression list, the warm-up ramp, and the complaint-budget rule in D3. Make seed testing optional.
- **Sources:** https://support.google.com/a/answer/81126 (Gmail sender guidelines; not opened this session), https://senders.yahooinc.com/best-practices/ (Yahoo sender requirements; not opened this session), https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8058 (one-click unsubscribe; not opened this session), https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/can-spam-act-compliance-guide-business (not opened this session), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 2 re-permission sprint relies on email in throttled batches; One Stop opt-out), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D4 opt-out list cc_optouts; Resend already in use)


## Value Trail: every thing of value to a partner, captured when the card is swiped (round 3, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Each partner lunch, gift, event, sponsorship, desk rental or marketing services payment is captured from the loan officer's own business card feed and sorted into a Reg X 1024.14(g) category. Each quarter a review that only the compliance role can see compares that value over time against referrals, which the loan officer can never see or tune toward.**

- **Problem:** Most RESPA Section 8 exposure sits outside the CRM. Lunches, closing gifts to agents, open-house sponsorships, event tickets, desk rent paid to a brokerage and marketing services agreements are paid from the loan officer's card and recorded nowhere that compliance can see. The plan's co-marketing ledger (section 2.3) only sees costs someone types into it. The referral-blind value firewall (already taken) only covers values the platform itself sets. The August 2023 CFPB action the plan cites (Mayer Brown alert) went after promotional events and marketing services agreements used as disguised referral fees, which is exactly this off-platform spend. A sponsor's compliance officer usually learns about it from an exam or a lawsuit.
- **How it works:** Data: the loan officer links only their business card accounts, through Plaid Transactions or a CSV or issuer export (Amex, Ramp, Brex). Personal accounts are never linked. Optionally they add their own calendar (Google or Microsoft, with their own OAuth) and forwarded receipts. Trigger: a new transaction at a restaurant, gift, event, florist, printing or advertising merchant, or a recurring payment to a real estate brokerage. What the system does: it matches the charge to a calendar event and its attendees to propose which partner or partners benefited. It suggests one category: (a) a normal promotional or educational activity not conditioned on referrals, (b) payment for services actually performed, at a fair market value documented against an attached contract and proof of performance, (c) a co-marketing share that is routed into the existing ledger, or (d) unclassified, which needs review. Sponsor rules run as soon as the charge is categorized. Examples: a per-partner rolling value cap, a hard block on any gift whose memo or timing ties it to a closing ('closing gift for agent'), and a block on marketing services agreements with no proof of performance. What the loan officer does: confirms or corrects the match in one tap from Today. They never see referral counts beside the spend. Each quarter, under a separate database role, the compliance review lines up each partner's value timeline against that partner's referred-file timeline (counts and lagged correlation), flags concentration or value that rises after referrals, and writes a review memo for a human. It never sends an automatic warning to the loan officer that would teach them to game it. The partner sees nothing. Output: a per-partner annual RESPA binder for the sponsor's file and for exams.
- **Technologies:** Plaid Transactions or card-issuer CSV/API (business accounts only), Google Calendar and Microsoft Graph calendar read (loan officer's own OAuth), Receipt email parsing with the M8 document explainer (receipts treated as data, never instructions), Neon with a separate compliance-only Postgres role and row-level security, same pattern as the idea 12 comp firewall, Rules table in config, counsel-signed and effective-dated, Append-only audit ledger with signed PDF exports
- **Why it's new:** Expense tools such as Expensify, Ramp and Brex categorize spending for accounting and know nothing about RESPA. Mortgage CRMs, as Total Expert and others market them, split co-marketing costs that are entered by hand. I know of no product that captures partner spend automatically at the swipe, maps it to the Reg X 1024.14(g) safe harbors, and runs a value-against-referral review that only compliance can see. These market claims come from model knowledge as of mid-2026. They were not re-checked this round: the shared web-search budget was exhausted, and ecfr.gov and consumerfinance.gov were blocked in this session. It complements, and does not repeat, the co-marketing ledger (plan 2.3) and the referral-blind value firewall: both of those govern value that passes through the platform, while this one finds value that never touched it.
- **Compliance design:** RESPA Section 8 and Reg X 1024.14: the tool documents and blocks. It never approves a payment as legal. Categories and caps come from counsel-signed rule rows [COUNSEL], including whether the CFPB's 2020 RESPA Section 8 FAQs on gifts, promotional activities and marketing services agreements are still current (they replaced Compliance Bulletin 2015-05 as I recall; not verified this round). The correlation review runs only under the compliance role. The loan officer's tools have no field that pairs spend with referrals, so the platform never helps anyone optimize spend per referral, and no reciprocity scores are kept (same reasoning as Relay). GLBA: the card data is the loan officer's own business data, not borrower nonpublic information. Partners are business contacts. Charges marked personal are dropped and never stored. Reg Z 1026.36(d): the loan officer's comp is untouched, and nothing in the reward engine reads this ledger. Copy never calls anything a 'referral fee' and always passes check:copy. State privacy: partner names are kept for business purposes, retained for the counsel-set period, and covered by the retention vault.
- **Moat:** The merchant and pattern library (what a desk-rental agreement, an open-house sponsorship or a marketing services agreement invoice looks like) improves with every member's confirmations. Only de-identified category labels are shared; prices are never shared, which avoids antitrust exposure from exchanging input-cost information among competitors [COUNSEL]. Sponsors adopt it company-wide because it gives them an exam-ready Section 8 binder that no CRM produces, and that pulls their loan officers onto the platform.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: The design is aligned with RESPA 8 and Reg X 1024.14. The main risk is evidentiary: a platform-generated value-against-referral correlation memo is discoverable evidence that can establish a Section 8 'agreement or understanding' if privilege does not attach. Linking card feeds can also pull in sponsor-owned corporate cards and shared cards that hold other employees' spending, and the 2020 CFPB Section 8 FAQs need re-verifying after the 2025 guidance withdrawals. Redesign: Run the quarterly correlation review at the direction of counsel, with the memo addressed to counsel, so privilege can attach. Let the sponsor choose whether the review runs at all. Link only cards the loan officer owns or that the sponsor authorizes in writing. Drop unrelated cardholders' lines at ingest. / keep 6/10: From knowledge. Pharma already does this: Sunshine Act aggregate-spend systems (IQVIA, Veeva, Concur integrations) capture transfers of value to physicians from expense feeds and categorize them. Mortgage tools such as Total Expert only split co-marketing costs that are entered by hand. I know of no mortgage product that maps card spend to the Reg X 1024.14(g) categories and runs a value-against-referral review that only compliance can see. It is new in mortgage and has a proven pattern to copy. Redesign: Cite the pharma aggregate-spend design (match to attendees, transfer-of-value categories, annual report) as the template. Start with CSV and issuer export before Plaid. Keep the correlation review behind the compliance role, with counsel-signed categories. / drop 4/10: The incentives are wrong. The loan officer bears the friction (linking business cards, confirming matches), and only the sponsor's compliance role gets the benefit, so voluntary adoption will be low. At many brokers, spend runs through company cards or reimbursements the loan officer doesn't control. The value-versus-referral correlation needs reliable referral attribution and enough partners per loan officer to mean anything, and a solo loan officer has neither. Plaid Transactions also adds a per-account fee and a third-party data flow. The CFPB 2020 RESPA FAQ status wasn't verified. Redesign: Shrink it to a sponsor-mandated feature: forwarded receipts or a CSV of partner spend go into the existing co-marketing ledger, with category tags, per-partner caps and an annual binder export. Leave out live card feeds and the correlation engine until a sponsor with 20 or more loan officers asks for them.
- **Sources:** https://www.mayerbrown.com/en/perspectives-events/publications/2023/08/respa-enforcement-is-back-the-cfpb-takes-aim-at-marketing-and-promotional-activities (cited in repo ideas doc), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1024/subpart-B/section-1024.14 (Reg X 1024.14; not opened this round, host blocked), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/compliance/compliance-resources/mortgage-resources/real-estate-settlement-procedures-act/real-estate-settlement-procedures-act-faqs/ (RESPA FAQs; not opened this round, host blocked), https://plaid.com/docs/api/products/transactions/ (not opened this round), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 2.3, /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md idea 12 and Relay


## Licensed Partner Check: no file data goes to a professional who isn't who they say they are (round 3, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**Before any borrower data or file status reaches an agent, CPA, attorney, financial advisor, insurance producer, contractor or another loan officer, the platform ties that person to a public license record. It also freezes sharing the moment their contact route changes in the middle of a file.**

- **Problem:** Partner Room, the confidential intro rail and Agent Line (all already taken) share nonpublic information with professionals on the borrower's say-so. Nothing checks that the 'agent' or 'divorce attorney' on the file is actually licensed, still in good standing, or the same person who emailed yesterday from a new domain. Real estate business email compromise is a long-running FBI IC3 category; this round I could not re-check the yearly loss figures. A lapsed or disciplined license is also a sponsor-risk fact the loan officer usually never sees. The counterparty atlas (taken) covers servicers, HOAs, title agents and insurers, but not the individual professionals who refer.
- **How it works:** Data: public license registries, one per profession. State real estate commission lookups. NASBA CPAverify for CPAs. State bar directories for attorneys. SEC IAPD (including its public bulk data) and FINRA BrokerCheck for advisors. State insurance department lookups for producers. State contractor boards. NMLS Consumer Access for other loan officers, one lookup at a time, which is the permitted use of deciding whether to do business, and never bulk monitoring. Each source carries its terms in the data-rights ledger. Where automated lookup is not allowed, the loan officer gets a pre-filled manual lookup link and records the result. Trigger: a professional is added to a file, the borrower names one in Partner Room, or a scheduled recheck runs at file open, at clear-to-close and before closing week. What the system does: it records the license number, status, expiry, any public discipline flag and an 'anchor' contact taken from the registry or the firm's own site. Sharing turns on only when the license is active and the borrower's per-field direction is on file. If the partner's email domain, phone number or any payment or wire-related instruction changes, sharing pauses and Today shows 'Call the anchor number to confirm.' M8, on every channel, never accepts a new contact route from an inbound message. An expired or suspended license switches sharing off and gives the borrower a neutral notice. What the loan officer does: one-tap confirmations, plus an occasional manual lookup. What the partner experiences: a one-time 'confirm your license' step that is the same for every partner, free, and not tied to referrals.
- **Technologies:** Per-registry adapters (IAPD bulk data, CPAverify, state commission and bar lookups, NMLS single lookups) behind the data-rights use gate, Twilio Lookup for line type and carrier change on partner numbers, DMARC/SPF/DKIM and domain-age checks on partner email, Effective-dated rules table by profession and state, The existing GLBA read-audit log and Partner Room sharing grants, Today cards and the D3 send gate for any outbound confirmation
- **Why it's new:** Wire-verification products such as CertifID are sold for the closing wire, mostly to title agents (vendor positioning). Mortgage CRMs let a loan officer add any 'partner' by name. I know of no mortgage CRM that gates borrower-data sharing on the partner's live license status across professions, or that quarantines sharing when a partner's contact route changes. This is from model knowledge as of mid-2026, not verified live this round because the search budget was exhausted. It is distinct from the counterparty atlas (institutions, not licensed individuals) and from the shared security desk (fraud domains), which it reuses rather than repeats.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA: disclosures to these partners rely on the consumer-directed exception (15 U.S.C. 6802(e)(2), consent or direction of the consumer) and are logged per field [COUNSEL]. Safeguards Rule: license and identity binding is an access control that goes in the sponsor's written security program. Registry terms: NMLS Consumer Access permits use only to decide whether to do business and bans solicitation, scraping and bulk monitoring, so lookups happen one at a time, only for a professional already on a borrower's file, and are never used for recruiting or prospecting. Each other registry's terms are recorded and enforced [COUNSEL to review BrokerCheck and state-commission terms]. RESPA Section 8: the check is identical and free for every partner, never tiered or 'unlocked' by referrals. Fair lending: not used for any borrower decision. The discipline flag is shown only to the loan officer and is never used to message the partner's clients.
- **Moat:** Anchors confirmed by members (a firm's real main line, a brokerage's real domain) build into a network directory of verified professionals that is only as reliable as the number of loan officers confirming it. A spoofed agent domain caught on one file is held for every member. Partners verify once and are trusted across every Command Center loan officer they work with, which gives agents and CPAs a reason to prefer files run on the platform.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound overall. Watch the registry terms: BrokerCheck, CPAverify and several state commissions restrict automated or commercial queries, so the manual-link fallback is required, not optional. The 'neutral notice' telling a borrower their agent's or contractor's license is inactive can amount to defamation or interference if the registry data is stale, and it can read as steering the borrower away from their chosen provider. Redesign: Before any notice to the borrower, require the loan officer to confirm the license status on the live registry page and record that check. Word the notice as 'we paused sharing with [name] until their license is confirmed; you can still choose them'. Re-check registry terms every year. / drop 5/10: Partly refuted, from knowledge. CertifID and similar tools already verify identity and quarantine changed contact routes for wire fraud. Comergence and NMLS lookups vet counterparties, and license-lookup data is sold through ARELLO-type feeds. Making professional-license status a gate on sharing data with someone on a file is a modest increment, and it largely repeats the taken counterparty atlas, partner room and shared security desk. Redesign: Fold it into Partner Room as two rules: an active-license check where the registry allows automated lookup, and a sharing pause when a partner's contact route changes. Don't build it as its own product or a per-profession adapter fleet. / keep 6/10: The valuable, cheap part is the quarantine: sharing pauses when a partner's contact route changes, plus anchor numbers. That targets wire and impersonation fraud. The registry part fails on cost. There are dozens of state real estate commissions, bars and contractor boards, most have no API, terms vary (BrokerCheck limits automated use), and every adapter needs upkeep. Lookups will mostly fall back to manual links, which loan officers will skip. Per-file sharing with partners is weekly work, so the gate will be used. Redesign: Version 1: the route-change quarantine, anchor numbers, and an M8 rule never to accept new contact details from an inbound message. Add real estate license checks for the 5 licensed states only, with manual lookup links and the result recorded. Defer CPA, attorney, insurance and contractor adapters.
- **Sources:** https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/policies/NMLS%20Document%20Library/Consumer%20Access%20Terms%20of%20Use.pdf (via the market survey), https://adviserinfo.sec.gov/ (SEC IAPD; not opened this round), https://brokercheck.finra.org/ (not opened this round), https://cpaverify.org/ (not opened this round), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/6802 (GLBA exceptions; not opened this round), https://www.ic3.gov/AnnualReport/Reports (IC3 annual reports; figures not re-checked), /home/user/RATEM8/lib/send-gate/gate.ts and PATCHLOG.md D3–D4 (send gate and Twilio Lookup already built)


## Both Sides Clear: co-marketing packets cleared under both regulators (round 3, medium impact, ~5 weeks)

**When a loan officer co-hosts a class or co-brands a guide with a financial advisor, CPA, attorney, insurance agent or builder, the platform produces both sides' compliance evidence. That is the RESPA cost split and Reg Z ad check for the loan officer, plus a ready submission packet for the partner's own compliance officer under their own profession's rules.**

- **Problem:** Loan officers' best non-agent partners are regulated by someone else, and their compliance departments usually say no to joint marketing. Without a packet, the partner's reviewer can't approve. Advisors at broker-dealers need FINRA Rule 2210 review and retention. Registered investment advisers work under the SEC Marketing Rule (206(4)-1, endorsement and compensation disclosures). CPAs work under the AICPA Code (1.520, commissions and referral fees disclosed to the client) and state boards. Attorneys work under ABA Model Rules 7.1–7.3, including 7.2(b)(4) on non-exclusive reciprocal referral arrangements. Insurance producers face state anti-rebating rules. On the loan officer's side, the same piece triggers RESPA Section 8 (each side pays its share) and Reg Z 1026.24 (trigger terms). The ad desk (taken) clears only the loan officer's side.
- **How it works:** Data: the content draft, the co-marketing ledger split, the partner's profession and firm type (which the partner chooses), and a rule pack per profession signed by counsel and effective-dated. Trigger: the loan officer starts a co-branded piece, webinar, class or event with a partner. What the system does: (1) Loan officer side: runs the existing ad desk (Reg Z trigger terms, rates only from a priced result with APR and the not-a-commitment line, NMLS and sponsor lines) and the co-marketing ledger split, measured by exposure and showing who paid what. (2) Partner side: generates a PDF and ZIP for the partner's compliance officer. It contains the final locked content, the cost split showing the partner paid its proportional share (so nothing of value flowed to them), a signed statement that there is no referral payment and no exclusive reciprocal referral arrangement, the disclosures their regime typically asks for (for example a registered investment adviser's endorsement disclosure if counsel finds one applies, or a CPA's client-disclosure language), and a retention copy for their books and records. (3) Version lock: any edit after approval re-runs both sides. Attendee sign-ins at a class capture each person's own E-SIGN consent with the AI disclosure, and the class roster is never imported as a call list. What the loan officer does: picks the partner and the template, and sends the reviewer link. What the partner experiences: a free reviewer link, the same for every partner, with an approve or request-changes button whose outcome is recorded.
- **Technologies:** Ad desk and check:copy guard (MLO side, reused), Co-marketing ledger (plan 2.3, reused), Per-profession rule packs in an effective-dated config table (FINRA 2210, SEC 206(4)-1, AICPA 1.520, ABA 7.2, state insurance rebating), PDF/ZIP packet generation with KMS-signed hashes, E-SIGN consent capture at class sign-in feeding D2 consent records, Reviewer-link workflow with an append-only approval log
- **Why it's new:** Advisor-side review tools (Smarsh, Red Oak, Global Relay) serve only the advisor's firm, and mortgage ad-review tools serve only the lender (vendor positioning, from model knowledge). I know of no product that produces evidence acceptable to both regulators for a single cross-industry piece, or that makes RESPA cost-share proof part of the partner's own submission. This was not verified live this round because the web-search budget was exhausted. It is distinct from the taken ideas: the ad desk is the loan officer's side only, the confidential intro rail covers confidentiality in introductions rather than marketing, and the CPA income desk and advisor liquidity planner are file tools, not marketing.
- **Compliance design:** RESPA Section 8: every joint piece goes through the ledger, and the partner pays its proportional share. The platform has no field for counting or balancing referrals between the pair, and the packet states the relationship is non-exclusive [COUNSEL: whether any reciprocal-referral understanding is a thing of value under Reg X 1024.14]. Reg Z 1026.24: rates only from a dated priced result with APR and the not-a-commitment line. Each partner regime's pack is counsel-signed and marked as platform policy, not legal advice to the partner. The partner's own compliance officer decides. TCPA: attendees opt in themselves, partner rosters are never imported as consent, and the D3 send gate applies. Fair lending: the class venue and audience can be chosen to add outreach only, never to exclude anyone, and that use is logged. Copy: no earnings claims, no 'referral fee' language, and check:copy runs on the content.
- **Moat:** With the partner's consent, the template library learns which content each firm type's compliance reviewers approved. Over time a member's advisor partner at a given broker-dealer gets a template that firm already cleared. Approval outcomes stay within that firm's own reps. This makes non-agent partnerships, which large lenders run through enterprise marketing departments, workable for a solo loan officer, and the library compounds with every member.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: Lawful as documentation. Risks: (1) An advisor who co-markets with a loan officer and refers clients is a Section 8 referral source, and under the SEC Marketing Rule (206(4)-1), referrals going the other way can be compensation for endorsements. The packet's 'non-exclusive' statement does not cure an actual reciprocal understanding. (2) Writing 'the disclosures their regime typically asks for' into another firm's compliance submission drifts toward giving legal or compliance advice to a non-client. (3) FINRA Rule 2210 needs a principal's pre-approval for broker-dealer reps. Redesign: Limit the partner packet to facts: the final content, the cost-split ledger, a signed statement that no referral payment is made, and a version hash. Leave out regime-specific disclosure language, or label it 'for your compliance officer to decide'. Block any piece whose ledger shows the partner paying less than its share. / keep 4/10: From knowledge. Advisor-side review tools (Red Oak, Smarsh, Global Relay) and lender ad review are each one-sided. I know of no cross-regulator packet, so it is new. Demand is doubtful: broker-dealer and RIA compliance officers usually insist on their own intake systems, and solo loan officers rarely co-market with advisors. Redesign: Shrink it to an export: the loan officer's ad-desk approval plus a ledger cost-split PDF that the partner can attach to their own firm's review system. Skip per-profession rule packs until demand is shown. / drop 3/10: It's low frequency and has a doubtful buyer. Loan officers rarely co-brand with financial advisors or CPAs. When they do, the partner's compliance office (FINRA 2210 or SEC marketing rule review) runs its own workflow and won't accept a third-party packet in its place. Rule packs signed by counsel for each profession are an expensive fixed cost for a handful of uses a year. The useful piece already exists in the co-marketing ledger and ad desk. Redesign: Drop it. Add a 'cost-split proof' PDF export to the existing co-marketing ledger that the loan officer can attach to whatever the partner's compliance team asks for.
- **Sources:** https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/2210 (not opened this round), https://www.sec.gov/investment/marketing-faq (SEC Marketing Rule FAQ; not opened this round), https://pub.aicpa.org/codeofconduct/ (AICPA Code 1.520; not opened this round), https://www.americanbar.org/groups/professional_responsibility/publications/model_rules_of_professional_conduct/rule_7_2_communications_concerning_a_lawyers_services_specific_rules/ (not opened this round), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/24/ (Reg Z advertising; host blocked this round), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md sections 2.3 and 4.7


## Workplace Lane: employers as partners who pay, never partners who get paid (round 3, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**Hospitals, school districts, unions and cities offer a homebuying benefit through the network. The employer pays the platform a flat fee. Employees opt themselves in, see every licensed loan officer for their property state in random order, and have any employer housing grant documented to the program's rules. The employer sees only aggregate participation.**

- **Problem:** Employers and unions are among the most trusted referral partners for first-time buyers, but the usual arrangement is a RESPA trap. A lender gives the employer a 'free' benefit or a sponsorship in exchange for access to employees, and the employer is in a position to refer. Solo loan officers can't sign a hospital system or a multi-state employer, and large lenders lock those programs into one funnel. Employer-assisted housing grants and forgivable loans also confuse underwriting: is it a gift, a second lien, or does it have to be repaid if the employee leaves?
- **How it works:** Data: an employer agreement (flat annual fee per eligible employee, paid to the platform), the employer's housing-assistance program terms if any (amount, forgiveness schedule, repayment on separation, lien or no lien), and each employee's own opt-in. No employee roster is ever uploaded. Trigger: an employee opens the employer-branded entry. That is a LoanM8 or another tenant's front end, AI disclosure first. What the system does: M8 education for the employee's stated property state. A choice-first list of the network's loan officers licensed there, in random order with nothing pre-selected (the choose-state routing pattern already in lib/routing.ts). An employer-program card that documents the grant as a gift or a subordinate lien under the program's terms, with the letter and the program document stored for the file. Optionally, the employee can connect their own payroll account (Truv or Argyle) under their own revocable authorization. The employer receives a quarterly aggregate only: employees who opened education, chose a loan officer or closed, each suppressed below a minimum count of 10, with no names or file facts. What the loan officer does: works the file like any borrower who chose them. They pay nothing beyond their flat seat. What the employee experiences: a benefit they started themselves, a real choice of loan officer, and a clear statement that their employer can't see their file.
- **Technologies:** Multi-tenant front-end entry with employer branding (config, not code), lib/routing.ts choose mode (random order, nothing pre-selected), E-SIGN consent capture into D2 consent records (employee self opt-in only), Employer program rules table (gift versus subordinate lien, repayment triggers) feeding the file, Truv or Argyle under the borrower's own authorization (optional), Aggregate reporting with minimum-count suppression; Stripe invoicing to the employer
- **Why it's new:** Large lenders market employer and affinity programs, and employer-assisted housing is an established GSE and housing-finance-agency concept (from model knowledge; not verified this round because the search budget was exhausted). Those programs usually route employees to one lender, often with sponsorships flowing toward the employer. I know of no program where the employer is the payer, the employee sees a choice-first list of licensed independent loan officers across states, and the employer gets nothing but thresholded aggregates. It is distinct from the taken benefits commons, which covers small sponsors' own staff benefits, from the counselor and down-payment-assistance bridge (housing counselors and DPA programs), and from the advisor liquidity planner.
- **Compliance design:** RESPA Section 8 and Reg X 1024.14: the employer is in a position to refer, so nothing of value goes to the employer from the platform or any loan officer. The employer pays fair market value for the benefit. There are no per-loan fees anywhere, because a per-loan charge to a loan officer for employer-channel borrowers would be a referral fee. Version 1 has no special pricing or 'employee discount', to stay clear of Reg Z 1026.36(d) and pricing-concession issues [COUNSEL]. Fair lending and ECOA: the benefit is open to every eligible employee, there is no demographic or geographic targeting, and the routing list is random. TCPA: employees opt in themselves, a roster is never consent, and the D3 send gate applies. Privacy: the employer never receives nonpublic information or individual status.
