That protects GLBA and also prevents the employer from using housing data in employment decisions. State employment-privacy laws go in config [COUNSEL]. Licensing: routing only to loan officers licensed in the employee's property state. AI: disclosure before the first M8 turn. Rates come only from a priced result.
- **Moat:** Each employer signed is a channel for every licensed member in that employer's states. A multi-state employer needs coverage only a network has, so the lane grows as more loan officers join in more states. That is the reverse of the large-lender model, where the employer deal belongs to one lender. Employers renew because the aggregate report shows real use without the liability of seeing files.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: The employer is in a position to refer, and the platform then routes employees to member loan officers who pay it seat fees. That holds up under RESPA 8 only if seat pricing is plainly fair market value for software and nothing varies with the employer channel. Several states also license lead generators or brokers that route consumers to lenders, so the platform may need a license or exemption in some states. An employer housing grant is generally taxable wages, and how it is documented affects the employer's payroll obligations. Random listing of loan officers who pay for seats can still look like a paid comparison-shopping platform: the CFPB's 2023 advisory opinion on those platforms may have been withdrawn in 2025 (unverified), but the statute stands. Redesign: Price seats with no employer-channel add-on, ever, and document a fair-market-value study. Get a state-by-state counsel review of lead-generator and broker licensing for the routing function. Have the employer's benefits counsel own the grant's tax and payroll treatment. The platform only stores the documents. / keep 5/10: From knowledge. Employer homebuying benefits already exist: Landed for educators, programs that large lenders and banks offer through employers, and employer-assisted-housing programs with housing-finance agencies. These usually route employees to one lender. A choice-first, multi-state list of independent loan officers, with the employer paying and seeing only thresholded aggregates, looks new and fits the network. Sales cycles are long, and the absence of a discount weakens the employer pitch. Redesign: Pilot it with one multi-state employer or union, using LoanM8's existing choose-state routing. Have counsel decide RESPA and Reg Z before any employer pricing, and keep the aggregate-only report. / drop 3/10: The business model fails. Employer homebuying benefits are usually free to the employer because a lender funds them. Asking hospitals or school districts to pay a flat fee per eligible employee means competing with free and going through enterprise procurement and benefits-broker channels. That needs a B2B sales team the platform doesn't have, and sales cycles of 6 to 18 months. Aggregate-only reporting with small-cell suppression is sound but doesn't create demand. Redesign: Reshape it as a free affinity entry point. Any employer, union or association can link to a branded LoanM8 or tenant landing page with the AI disclosure and random, choice-first routing. Keep no fee, the RESPA rule that nothing of value goes to the employer, and aggregate counts only. Revisit paid tiers if employers ask.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1024/subpart-B/section-1024.14 (not opened this round, host blocked), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (Reg Z LO comp; host blocked this round), /home/user/RATEM8/lib/routing.ts (choose-state routing: random order, nothing pre-selected), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md sections 2.1–2.4, Market survey segment 'Adjacent technology' (Argyle and Truv entries, MED confidence)


## Expiry Stack: every document and verification laid against the projected closing, refreshed at the latest safe moment (round 3, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**An agent that knows when each pay stub, bank statement, credit report, appraisal, VVOE, payoff and insurance binder on a file stops being usable, lays those dates against the projected closing, and asks the borrower for each refresh once, on the day the new document exists.**

- **Problem:** Brokered files slip in the final two weeks because items age out between application and closing: a statement period ends, the credit report or appraisal passes its age limit, the VVOE window opens and closes, a payoff letter goes stale. Underwriters catch it as a new condition. The borrower is then asked for the same kind of document two or three times, often before the new one even exists, such as the next pay stub or the month-end statement. Today's POS reminder lists track whether something was received. They don't track how long it stays good for this program, this lender and this closing date.
- **How it works:** DATA: upload metadata from the existing upload path (document type, statement period end and pay date, pulled by a vision model on upload; the content is data, never instructions); LOS key dates (application, projected closing, lock expiry) from ARIVE through Zapier; the program (Conv, FHA, VA, USDA) and the wholesale lender, from the file. An effective-dated age-limit table, with agency defaults, lender overlays and counsel/ops status, reuses the D3 rules-table pattern. TRIGGER: nightly, and whenever the closing date moves, each item's 'good through' date is compared with the projected closing plus a buffer. SYSTEM: (1) Builds one stack per file, with each item's expiry, the reason and the citation. (2) Plans each refresh for the earliest day the replacement exists: the day after the borrower's stated payday for a stub, or the first business day after the statement cycle closes. (3) Merges overlapping asks into one request. (4) Drafts a service-only message ('Your May statement ages out on Jul 2 under your loan program; your projected closing is Jul 9. Your June statement should post around Jul 1; here's the upload link'). The draft goes through the D3 send gate as a service text, or as an M8 call only if the borrower chose AI calls. Third-party items (payoff, VVOE readiness, insurance binder) go to the third-party chase agent, which is already planned. MLO: a red/amber/green stack on Today and on the file, one-tap approve for each drafted ask, and a 'closing date moved' recompute. BORROWER: fewer, predictable asks, each saying why and when, with one upload link. Text never collects SSN, DOB or account numbers.
- **Technologies:** ARIVE Zapier triggers (loan date, tracker) for key dates, Vision/LLM extraction of statement period and pay dates with a zod schema, Effective-dated rules table (D3 pattern) with lender overlays, D3 send gate and D4 Twilio service texts, Optional M8 voice (cascaded STT-LLM-TTS so the guard screens every sentence), Today card (D5) and the nightly precompute
- **Why it's new:** Vendor POS tools (Floify, Blend, BeSmartee) send document request lists and nag reminders, and AI processors in the survey (Vesta, Friday Harbor, Candor) are lender-side or replace the LOS. The survey's 'condition chaser' chases conditions after the underwriter issues them. This prevents the stale-document condition before it exists and times each ask to when the source document is created. That part is an inference from public marketing, because this session couldn't search: the shared search budget was used up, and selling-guide.fanniemae.com and www.ecfr.gov failed DNS, so the agency age limits below are recalled, not re-read. As commonly understood, Fannie Mae B1-1-03 limits most credit documents to 4 months before the note date, the Fannie VVOE must be within 10 business days before the note date, and appraisals have their own age and update rules (1004D). FHA and VA have their own limits. All of them go into the table as counsel- and ops-confirmed rows, never hard-coded. It differs from the taken 'file drift engine' (field changes that start compliance clocks), 'contingency clock' (purchase contract deadlines) and 'live doc session' (checking uploads on a call): this is a forward schedule of when evidence expires.
- **Compliance design:** Service purpose only. Drafts can't mention rates, refinancing or new products (check:copy plus the guard), so they stay informational under the TCPA, and they go out only with service text consent or as replies inside a thread the borrower started. AI calls need AI-call consent, with the AI and recording disclosures first. Projected dates are always labelled 'projected', and the existing 'never promise a closing date' guard applies. Ages and overlays come from cited, effective-dated rows that counsel or ops approves. The same rules apply to every borrower on the same program and lender (consistent treatment, ECOA), and the logs prove it. No demographic or proxy inputs. GLBA: the extracted metadata is NPI, so it is encrypted, tenant-isolated and read-audited, and the original file follows sponsor retention. Uploads are links, never SMS attachments.
- **Moat:** Each underwriter rejection of a document as stale, recorded as a fact ('Lender X rejected a 58-day-old statement on a non-QM bank-statement loan'), corrects that MLO's overlay table. With counsel approval and the MLO's opt-in, those rule facts become candidate rows in the shared guideline ledger. Lender performance is never compared across companies (the Lender Pulse rejection stands). More files make the expiry predictions sharper, and every MLO benefits.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: These are service messages to an applicant about their own file, which is low TCPA risk with prior express consent, and the design is consistent with Reg B and GLBA. It substantially duplicates the freshness-plan half of Clean Submit, which matters for scoping rather than compliance. Age limits (Fannie B1-1-03 at 4 months, VVOE within 10 business days) are recalled from memory, not re-read. Redesign: Merge with Clean Submit into one document-age engine and one rules table. Keep the rows signed off by counsel and ops, and keep the service-only guard. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted, from knowledge. Lender LOS eFolders (Encompass document expiration dates, LendingPad, Byte) and several POS tools already track document expiry and alert staff. The new part is timing each refresh request to the day the replacement document exists (after payday or the statement cycle), on the broker side, through the consent gate. It largely duplicates Clean Submit. Redesign: Merge with Clean Submit into one 'File Freshness' feature: lint against the lender's checklist before submission, plus the age-out refresh plan. Put the age rules as cited rows in the guideline ledger. / keep 7/10: It's worth doing: stale-document conditions are a weekly cost, and the logic is deterministic. The practical gap is data. Command Center isn't the POS, so document metadata exists only for files whose uploads go through the platform. ARIVE via Zapier is unlikely to expose the projected closing date and document dates reliably. It overlaps almost completely with Clean Submit's freshness plan, so the two must be one feature. The age limits (Fannie B1-1-03, 4 months; VVOE within 10 business days) are from memory and must go into the rules table as rows ops confirms. Redesign: Merge Clean Submit's freshness plan into this as one feature. Start with the documents the platform actually holds plus loan-officer-entered key dates, with sourced default rows for Conventional, FHA, VA and USDA. Add lender overlays only as members cite them.
- **Sources:** https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b1-1-03/allowable-age-credit-documents-and-federal-income-tax-returns (canonical; DNS-blocked this session, not opened), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work- (from survey: ARIVE Zapier loan date and tracker triggers), https://help.floify.com/en/articles/16300950-how-to-create-a-custom-integration (from survey), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D3 and D4 (send gate, service texts), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 4.6 (milestone guardian, key dates)


## Spoken Consent Ceremony: E-SIGN-grade permission captured by voice on calls the borrower started (round 3, high impact, ~3 weeks)

**When a person calls in, or is on a live call with their loan officer, and says 'sure, you can text me', the platform runs a short counsel-scripted consent ceremony: an exact disclosure, a spoken read-back, audio-hash evidence and an instant written copy by text. It becomes a provable consent record, not a note.**

- **Problem:** Consent is the bottleneck. Every imported contact starts as 'relationship only' (D2), and M8 can't text or call them until proof exists. In practice the best moment to get permission is a live conversation, but a spoken 'yes' is only the loan officer's word ('attested'), not prior express written consent. So the MLO sends a link afterwards, and many people never click. Web-form consent certificates that rely on session replay have their own wiretap (CIPA) exposure, per the survey.
- **How it works:** SCOPE: only inbound calls (the person called the MLO line, the Checkup Line or the Agent Line) and live MLO calls, where the MLO taps 'Ask permission'. Never on an outbound AI call. TRIGGER: the caller asks to be texted or called, or the MLO taps the button. SYSTEM: (1) The AI and recording disclosures were already given before any substance (all-party states per D3). (2) M8, or a recorded counsel-approved prompt, reads the exact versioned disclosure: who will contact them, which channels, which purposes (service, marketing, M8 AI-voice calls as artificial voice), that consent isn't a condition of any loan or purchase, the usual frequency, that message and data rates may apply, and how to revoke ('say stop, reply STOP, or tell anyone, any way'). (3) It asks for a single affirmative read-back: the person states their name and 'I agree', with an optional keypress. (4) It stores the disclosure version id, the exact audio segment and its SHA-256 hash, the transcript with ASR confidence, the caller ID with STIR/SHAKEN attestation level, the timestamp and the actor. (5) Below a confidence threshold, an ambiguous answer, or any hesitation, nothing is recorded and M8 offers the text link instead. (6) If texts were agreed, the system immediately texts a copy ('Here's what you agreed to on your call at 2:14 PM: [link]. Reply STOP anytime'). The link shows the disclosure text and the audio clip. A new D2 evidence type, 'voice_signature', is ranked above 'attested'. D3 treats it as written consent only in states where the counsel-signed table says a voice signature qualifies (federal, FTSA, Virginia, Oklahoma and so on, each a row). Elsewhere it counts as prior express consent for informational use only. MLO: a 'Permission captured' toast and the evidence on the contact's permission grid. BORROWER: one sentence on a call they chose, and a copy in their pocket.
- **Technologies:** Twilio Voice (inbound), ConversationRelay or recorded prompt playback, Speech-to-text with word-level confidence, SHA-256 audio hashing plus the append-only consent log (D2), STIR/SHAKEN attestation from the carrier's call metadata, D3 per-state rules table (new column: voice signature counts as written), D4 Twilio confirmation text
- **Why it's new:** The FCC's 2012 TCPA order (FCC 12-21), as commonly cited, said E-SIGN-compliant prior express written consent can be obtained by 'email, website form, text message, telephone keypress, or voice recording'. This session could not re-read it because search was exhausted, so counsel must confirm. Call centers use IVR keypress consent, and lead-gen uses web certificates (TrustedForm, Jornaya), which the survey flags for CIPA session-replay risk. I found no mortgage CRM in the survey with a voice-signature ceremony tied to per-state acceptance, versioned disclosures and an instant written copy. Survey players (Total Expert, Bonzo, Shape, Flair, Sela) publish no consent methodology. It differs from the taken 'consent passport' (one tap online), 'consent archaeology' (old messages as evidence) and 'witnessed ledger' (tamper evidence for records): this is a new way to create the proof, live, by voice.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: never solicited on an outbound AI call, so asking for marketing consent can't itself become an unconsented marketing call. Consent is separate by purpose and channel, never bundled with a loan action, never a condition. AI disclosure first. Revocation stays 'any reasonable means, honored immediately', even if counsel adopts an FCC 26-67 exclusive method for disclosures. State mini-TCPAs: a per-state counsel row decides whether a voice signature counts as 'written'. Where it doesn't, the record is only prior express consent. Recording law: the notice comes before any substance, and the consent clip is kept with the TSR 5-year telemarketing record retention (counsel-set). The rest of the call follows the redacted transcript policy. Biometrics: the hash is of the audio file, and no voiceprint or speaker embedding is ever made (BIPA, Texas CUBI, Washington). CIPA: voice vendors are on zero-retention, no-training terms. GLBA: the clip is NPI, so it is encrypted and read-audited. Copy passes check:copy and the guard (no rates, no promises).
- **Moat:** Each disclosure version's network-wide revocation rate, complaint rate and any demand-letter outcome (gathered by the taken complaint radar and evidence features) shows counsel which wording holds up. The state acceptance table improves with every counsel review, and members get the result without paying for it separately. A solo MLO can't build or defend this alone.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: FCC 12-21 recognizes voice recording as an E-SIGN-compliant way to get prior express written consent, and limiting the ceremony to inbound and live calls avoids the outbound-AI problem. Risks: (1) 'Live MLO calls' include outbound hand-dialed calls, and on those the consent ask is only safe if the call itself was lawful (EBR or consent, DNC-checked). (2) Florida's FTSA and some other states require a 'signature' and may not accept voice, which the design already handles with state rows. (3) Hashing the audio is fine, but producing a transcript with ASR confidence from a vendor still needs CIPA-safe vendor terms. (4) A text sent right after the call to confirm marketing consent should go out only once the consent is recorded. Redesign: Allow the ceremony on outbound live calls only when the gate shows the call itself had a lawful basis. Default unknown states to 'informational only'. Keep the immediate confirmation text, and have it double as a STOP path. / drop 4/10: Refuted in substance, from knowledge. The third-party verification (TPV) industry, used for energy, telecom and insurance sales (Calibrus, VoiceLog and others), already records scripted verbal consent with read-back, versioned scripts and stored audio. Call-center IVRs capture keypress and voice consent for TCPA. A per-state acceptance table and a copy texted afterward are small increments. Redesign: Fold it into the consent passport as an evidence type. Copy the TPV pattern (fixed script, read-back, stored clip and hash, texted copy) and add the state rule rows. Don't present it as a standalone innovation. / keep 5/10: The legal basis is plausible: I recall the FCC's 2012 order allowing voice recording or keypress as an E-SIGN signature, but I couldn't re-read it. The marginal value over the cheaper path is small. That path: on the same call, the loan officer taps 'send permission link' and the borrower taps once in the consent passport, which needs no recording infrastructure, ASR confidence thresholds or audio retention. The ceremony needs call recording, all-party notice, transcript storage and state-by-state acceptance rows, and the platform has no voice calling yet (D1 to D5 are text only). Its main use is for people who can't or won't use a link. Redesign: Ship 'send permission link during the call' first. Add the recorded voice ceremony later, only for inbound calls on lines that are already recorded with notice, and only where counsel's state rows accept it.
- **Sources:** https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-12-21A1.pdf (FCC 2012 TCPA order; canonical, not opened this session), https://www.nelsonmullins.com/insights/alerts/fcc-download/all/fcc-adopts-overhaul-of-tcpa-consent-revocation-rules-revoke-all-narrowed-exclusive-opt-out-methods-permitted-and-fraud-alert-exemption-expanded (from survey: FCC 26-67), https://www.privacyworld.blog/2026/01/key-changes-to-virginia-telephone-privacy-protection-act-take-effect/ (from survey: Virginia SB 1339), https://www.goodwinlaw.com/en/insights/publications/2025/02/alerts-practices-dpc-ftec-ai-voice-products-subject-to-california-invasion-of-privacy-claims (from survey: CIPA and AI voice), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/7001 (E-SIGN), /home/user/RATEM8/lib/contacts/service.ts (attested evidence type today)


## Helper Lane: every non-borrower on a file is their own consent subject, with a scoped role and a hard marketing block (round 3, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**Gift donors, private landlords, non-borrowing spouses, adult children helping a parent, interpreters and attorneys-in-fact each enter the file only through the borrower. Each gets their own consent, privacy notice and role-limited view. M8 never calls or texts them without their own permission, and they can never become a marketing contact.**

- **Problem:** Real files depend on people who aren't the borrower: the donor who must show the transfer, the private landlord who must verify rent, and, in community-property states, the non-borrowing spouse. Four of LoanM8's five states (WA, CA, AZ, TX) are community-property states, where FHA and VA, as commonly understood, consider the non-borrowing spouse's debts. Today MLOs and bots text these people from the borrower's word alone. That means sharing the borrower's NPI with no consent record, and an AI call to a donor's cell is an artificial-voice call to someone who never consented. Afterwards the donor quietly ends up in the CRM as a 'lead'.
- **How it works:** DATA and TRIGGER: in M8 chat, the portal or a call, the borrower adds a helper and picks a role (gift donor, private landlord, non-borrowing spouse, family helper, interpreter, attorney-in-fact) and a scope: 'only what I ask them for', 'file status', or 'nothing but their own task'. SYSTEM: (1) The first contact comes from the borrower, never the platform: a share-sheet link the borrower sends from their own phone. The platform's first outbound message to a stranger is therefore never unsolicited. (2) The helper opens a page with their own privacy notice, their own per-channel consent (including a separate M8 AI-call choice) and their task list from a role template. Donor: the donor's own words in the gift letter (the taken 'own words' feature), proof the funds left, and transfer evidence. Landlord: a VOR form. Non-borrowing spouse: only the items the program and state property law require, per a counsel-maintained template. Attorney-in-fact: upload of the POA for the sponsor's review. (3) What M8 may say to a helper is limited to the scope the borrower granted, and each disclosure of borrower NPI is logged as 'at the consumer's direction'. (4) Helpers are stored as a separate subject type with their own retention clock and deletion rights. They can't be imported, exported into a book, added to plays, or ranked on Today, enforced in the D3 gate as a role-level block. (5) Either the borrower or the helper can end access with one tap, effective instantly across channels. MLO: a Today card ('Donor hasn't uploaded transfer proof; projected closing in 9 days'). HELPER: a page that asks for exactly their part and shows nothing else. If anything suggests pressure or coercion, the case goes to the taken protective-pause circuit breaker.
- **Technologies:** Role-scoped sharing links (signed, expiring, revocable tokens), Separate subject type in the D2 store with its own consent log and retention, D3 gate role block (helpers never enter marketing purposes), Twilio texts and optional M8 voice for helpers who consent, Counsel-maintained role templates by program and state, Upload path with vision extraction (content is data, never instructions)
- **Why it's new:** POS systems invite co-borrowers, and some let a real estate agent see status. The taken 'partner room' covers professionals on the file, and 'family transition desk' covers estates. I found nothing in the surveyed CRMs or POSs that treats donors, landlords and non-borrowing spouses as separate consent subjects, with role-scoped disclosure and a structural ban on becoming leads. That is an inference: search was exhausted this session, so vendor feature pages couldn't be checked. The survey's TCPA findings (AI voice is artificial voice; 2026 AI-voice class actions) show why calling a third party's cell without their own consent is the risky habit to engineer out.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: every helper needs their own consent. The borrower can't consent for them, and an agent can't consent for a person (AGENTS.md). GLBA and Reg P: disclosure of the borrower's NPI only at the borrower's direction (the consent exception, recalled as 12 CFR 1016.15(a)(1), not re-read this session), logged per disclosure. Reg B: a non-borrowing spouse is never required to apply or co-sign beyond what Reg B allows, such as signatures needed to create a valid lien or rights under state property law. The template is counsel-signed, and the role appears only when the borrower adds it or the program and state require it. Marital status is never inferred or used in any ranking. State privacy: helpers who never applied are state-privacy subjects with access and delete rights (the survey's per-record regime tag). 'No lead is ever sold', extended: helpers are never even farmed. Elder and exploitation signals go to protective pause.
- **Moat:** The role templates (which documents each role must give, by program, lender and state) are maintained once for the whole network and corrected by every file that uses them. Borrowers and the people who help them learn that bringing family into a LoanM8 file never turns them into a sales target, and that trust compounds.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Well designed for TCPA and GLBA. Watch Reg B 1002.5(c)-(d): any request for information about a non-borrowing spouse must fit the listed exceptions (community property state, reliance on the spouse's income, alimony, a signature needed for the lien). Donor and landlord data may not be GLBA data at all, because they are not the lender's consumers, so CCPA and other state laws can apply in full. The privacy notice to helpers must reflect that. Redesign: Classify helper data under state privacy regimes by default, not as GLBA-exempt. Tie the spouse template's questions to the Reg B exception that applies, by state and program. / keep 5/10: From knowledge. POS tools already invite co-borrowers and some send gift-letter e-sign requests to donors, and servicers keep authorized-third-party records. I know of none that treats donors, landlords and non-borrowing spouses as separate consent subjects with a structural block on marketing them. That is a sound compliance design but only modestly new. Redesign: Build it as a subject type inside the D2 schema and the D3 gate (role-level block on marketing), and reuse Partner Room's scoped-view UI. Keep it small: the donor and VOR templates first. / keep 5/10: The cheap, high-value core is a separate helper subject type that is never importable, exportable, eligible for plays or rankable. That is one schema type plus a D3 role block, about a week of work, and it prevents real TCPA exposure from calling gift donors. The role templates by program, lender and state (non-borrowing spouse items, POA review) are expensive to maintain and duplicate what the POS (ARIVE, Floify) already does with gift letters and VORs. Redesign: Build the subject type, the marketing block, the borrower-sent share link and separate consent for each helper. Leave document collection to the POS and drop the role-template library.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1016/section-1016.15 (Reg P consent exception; DNS-blocked this session, not opened), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1002/section-1002.7 (Reg B signature rules; not opened), https://www.hud.gov/program_offices/housing/sfh/handbook_4000-1 (FHA non-borrowing spouse in community-property states; not opened), https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/ai-marketing-calls-spur-suit-against-lender (from survey: AI-voice TCPA suit), /home/user/RATEM8/AGENTS.md (an agent cannot consent for a person)


## Scenario Runner: the broker's lender-shopping round, run by an agent and filed as the anti-steering record (round 3, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**For any file outside a plain priced box, an agent sends a de-identified scenario from the MLO's own email to the lenders the MLO picks, reads the replies into structured eligibility with cited guidelines, and assembles the Reg Z anti-steering option set from priced results only, so the borrower sees real options and the file holds the proof.**

- **Problem:** For non-QM bank-statement, ITIN, non-warrantable condo, recent credit-event or mixed-use scenarios, broker MLOs still email or call three to six account executives or scenario desks. They wait hours or days, then compare inconsistent answers by hand, and the comparison is rarely documented. Meanwhile, for a broker the Reg Z anti-steering safe harbor (12 CFR 1026.36(e)) depends on presenting options from creditors the originator regularly does business with: as commonly read, the lowest interest rate, the lowest rate without risky features, and the lowest total origination points, fees and discount points. Few brokers can show that record for an edge-case file.
- **How it works:** TRIGGER: the MLO taps 'Shop this scenario' on a file, or pricing returns nothing eligible. SYSTEM: (1) Builds a de-identified scenario card with no name, SSN, DOB, account numbers or street address. It holds only state and county, occupancy, property type, loan purpose, LTV/CLTV band, credit score band, income documentation type, reserves band, DTI band and the specific question. Protected characteristics are never fields; immigration or ITIN status appears only if the borrower raised it, and as a creditworthiness fact only. (2) The MLO approves the card and the lender list from their own sponsor-approved roster (lib/pricing/lenders.ts already holds the panel). (3) Sends one message per lender from the MLO's own mailbox (Gmail or Microsoft Graph, scoped to one label), or through a lender's published scenario form where its terms allow. It never logs into a lender portal by automation (the survey's Supergood/ICE terms caution). (4) Reads replies into a schema: eligible (yes, no, conditional), program name, cited guideline or overlay, required documents, and pricing only if the lender gave a priced result, stamped with time and source. Follow-up questions from AEs go to the MLO, never answered by the AI. (5) Builds the option set by extending the existing lib/pricing/antiSteering.ts selector to these results. Options come only from priced results, each with APR and the not-a-commitment line. Lenders with no price are listed as 'eligible, not priced'. (6) Files a dated worksheet to the loan record. MLO: one comparison screen; they choose what to present. BORROWER: the options with neutral, published criteria, never an AI 'pick'.
- **Technologies:** Gmail API or Microsoft Graph (MLO-delegated OAuth, label-scoped), LLM extraction to a zod schema, with the guard on any borrower-facing text, lib/pricing/antiSteering.ts and buildPricingRun() for the option set, Loansifter Broker Product & Pricing API, Lender Price API or the manual ARIVE pull for prices, Sponsor lender roster and a PDF worksheet attached to the file via ARIVE notes or tasks
- **Why it's new:** PPEs (Optimal Blue/Loansifter, ARIVE's marketplace, Lender Price) price products they hold, but edge-case eligibility still runs on AE email and phone. The survey found AI voice aimed at borrowers (UWM Mia, Sela, Flair) and AI processors on the lender side, with no tool that runs the broker's B2B shopping round and files it as the safe-harbor record. That is an inference: search was exhausted and www.ecfr.gov failed DNS, so the 1026.36(e)(3) wording is recalled and must be confirmed by counsel. LoanM8 already selects safe-harbor options for priced scenarios (CLAUDE.md §4b); this extends that discipline to the part of the market no engine prices.
- **Compliance design:** Reg Z anti-steering: the ranking never sees the MLO's compensation from any lender (the comp-firewall pattern from idea 12), and the worksheet shows the criteria. Reg Z comp: nothing here touches pay. RESPA 8: no lender can pay for placement or ordering, and the criteria are published (the survey's 'neutral, published ranking criteria'). GLBA: de-identified before application; after application, NPI goes to a chosen lender only to process the borrower's requested transaction, and the step is logged. Fair lending: no protected-class or proxy fields on the card; the same card template for every borrower; options are never filtered by who the borrower is. Antitrust: each MLO sees only their own replies; no cross-tenant lender ratings (the Lender Pulse rejection holds). The AI never states a rate to the borrower outside a priced result. Email to AEs is business-to-business, so TCPA doesn't apply, and CAN-SPAM doesn't either (these are transactional business messages).
- **Moat:** With each MLO's opt-in and counsel review, cited guideline facts from AE replies ('Lender X allows 12-month bank statements for 1099 borrowers per overlay doc dated ...') become candidate rows in the taken guideline ledger. The network's map of who does what in edge cases grows with every scenario, without ever ranking lenders' performance. A large retail lender has no reason to build a broker's multi-lender record.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound. The anti-steering safe harbor (1026.36(e)(3)) needs options from a significant number of the creditors the loan officer regularly does business with, plus a good-faith belief the consumer likely qualifies. A worksheet that leaves out regular lenders will not support the safe harbor. Small-county scenario cards (county, property type, LTV and credit bands) can re-identify the borrower. CAN-SPAM covers B2B email, but these inquiries are not commercial advertisements, so the conclusion holds. Redesign: Have the worksheet warn when the selected lenders exclude regularly used panel lenders. Coarsen geography to state, or to a county band, where county population is small. Record which lenders were asked and which answered. / keep 6/10: From knowledge. Scenario desks and AI guideline-search tools for brokers exist, as do lender scenario forms and ARIVE's marketplace. I know of no product that sends de-identified scenarios from the loan officer's own mailbox, parses AE replies into structured eligibility, and files the result as the anti-steering worksheet. That combination looks new. The risks are that AE replies are messy and that lender terms on automated outreach are unknown. Redesign: Ship the record-keeping half first: a structured worksheet the loan officer fills from AE replies, with AI-assisted parsing. Add sending from the mailbox only after checking each lender's broker-agreement terms. / keep 6/10: Real weekly time savings for non-QM and edge-case loan officers. Obstacles. AE replies are mostly unpriced and often come by phone, so the claim of 'an anti-steering option set from priced results' will usually fall short: 1026.36(e)(3) needs options from a significant number of creditors the originator regularly works with, and unpriced replies don't qualify. Reading Gmail needs a restricted scope, and Google's yearly CASA security assessment costs thousands of dollars. Microsoft Graph is easier. Many wholesalers now have scenario desks or portals that the tool can't automate under their terms. Redesign: Version 1: build the de-identified scenario card, send it from the loan officer's mailbox (Graph first), parse replies into structured eligibility, and file a dated worksheet. Label it a 'shopping record', and call it a safe-harbor set only when enough priced results came back. Defer Gmail until the cost of the restricted-scope assessment is justified.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1026/subpart-E/section-1026.36 (Reg Z anti-steering safe harbor; DNS-blocked this session, not opened), https://digitalmarketplace.optimalblue.com/product-overview (from survey: Broker Product & Pricing API), https://supergood.ai/api-report-card/arive (from survey: portal-automation risk), https://mortgagetech.ice.com/publicdocs/mortgage/encompass-partner-connect-api-terms-and-conditions.pdf (from survey: no bypassing the platform), /home/user/RATEM8/lib/pricing/antiSteering.ts


## Equal Ear: M8 voice that hears every borrower equally well, with accommodations on request and a release gate on speech equity (round 3, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**M8's voice runtime is tested nightly for how accurately it hears different kinds of speakers, and it can't ship a model that widens the gap. On calls it switches to slower turn-taking, read-back and a text mirror whenever the call itself shows trouble or the person asks, without ever inferring who the caller is.**

- **Problem:** Voice AI doesn't hear everyone equally. Koenecke et al. (PNAS 2020) reported that commercial speech recognizers made roughly twice as many word errors for Black speakers as for white speakers; the figures are recalled and pnas.org was blocked this session. Endpointing tuned for sub-second replies (the survey's latency race) also cuts off people who pause, stutter, speak English as a second language or use relay services. In mortgage, a misheard number becomes a wrong file fact, a failed consent capture or an abandoned call. An AI channel that consistently serves some groups worse is a fair-lending risk (discouragement under ECOA and the Fair Housing Act) and a disability-access risk, and it is invisible in the latency benchmarks vendors publish.
- **How it works:** (1) ACCOMMODATION ON REQUEST: every M8 call and the consent passport offer 'Want me to slow down, wait longer, or switch to text? Just say so.' The stored preference is functional ('longer pauses', 'text mirror', 'relay-friendly'), never the reason. Relay-friendly mode works with TRS and captioned-telephone users: longer silences, no early hang-up, no voicemail detection false positives. (2) SIGNAL-TRIGGERED FALLBACK: when the call's own signals degrade (ASR confidence below a threshold, the person repeating themselves, two barge-in cutoffs), M8 lengthens endpointing, slows down, reads facts back, offers a text mirror and offers the loan officer. It is triggered by what happens on the call, never by a prediction about the caller. (3) NO SILENT WRITES: any fact M8 captures (a consent, a number, a date, an appointment) needs an explicit read-back yes, and low-confidence facts are never written to the file. (4) NIGHTLY EQUITY EVAL: public, diverse speech corpora (for example CORAAL for African American English, accented-English sets, stuttering sets such as SEP-28k) run through the exact production pipeline (telephony codec, STT, endpointing, guard). Measures: word error rate, fact-capture accuracy and cutoff rate per corpus segment. (5) RELEASE GATE: a new STT or voice model, or an endpointing change, can't go live if any segment's gap widens past a counsel-set threshold. This plugs into the survey's 'deprecation shield' model-swap pipeline. (6) PRODUCTION METRICS WITHOUT DEMOGRAPHICS: outcome, opt-out and complaint rates by accommodation used and by fallback triggered, shown on the Compliance Center.
- **Technologies:** Cascaded STT-LLM-TTS runtime (Twilio ConversationRelay, Pipecat or LiveKit) with tunable endpointing and VAD, Word-level ASR confidence and barge-in telemetry, Offline evaluation harness on public speech corpora (CORAAL, SEP-28k and others), run nightly alongside tests/m8-redteam, D4 Twilio text mirror, Compliance Center report per model version
- **Why it's new:** The survey's independent voice benchmarks (openbenchmarks, codebridge) and vendor pages (Retell, Vapi, Bland, Telnyx, ElevenLabs) compete on median time-to-first-audio. None of the surveyed players publishes accuracy by speaker group, a relay-friendly mode, or a release gate on speech equity; that is an inference from the survey, since search was exhausted this session. The taken 'paired tester lab' tests whether M8 answers matched text personas the same way; Equal Ear tests whether M8 hears people the same way, a different layer that no text test reaches. The taken 'language commons' covers languages; this covers accent, dialect, disfluency and disability within any language.
- **Compliance design:** Fair lending: no race, ethnicity, national origin or proxy is collected, inferred or stored for callers. Corpus labels exist only in the offline test set, never in production. Fallbacks are triggered by call behavior and apply to anyone. Disability: accommodations are offered to everyone and given on request (Fair Housing Act reasonable accommodation, ADA-style practice) [COUNSEL]. Health-data laws: the preference is stored as a function ('longer pauses'), never a condition, to stay outside Washington's My Health My Data Act and CCPA sensitive-data categories [COUNSEL]. Biometrics: no voiceprints or speaker embeddings. TCPA and recording: disclosures and consent unchanged; the text mirror needs text consent or a reply in the call's own context. The guard still screens every spoken sentence (cascaded design), so slowing down never weakens the rate rule.
- **Moat:** Consented, redacted fallback events from every tenant improve endpointing and confirmation design for all members, and each model version's published equity report becomes evidence counsel, regulators and sponsors can rely on. A solo MLO renting a generic voice bot can't produce it, and an incumbent selling dial volume has no reason to measure it.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: There is a data-licensing problem. The named corpora are, to my knowledge, released under research or non-commercial terms: CORAAL under its own academic-use terms, and SEP-28k under a CC BY-NC-style license. Running them nightly as a release gate for a commercial product is likely commercial use that the licenses bar. I could not re-check the licenses this round (search budget used up). Corpus dialect labels used as speaker groups are race and ethnicity proxies, which is acceptable offline but must never be joined to production. Redesign: Use only corpora licensed for commercial use, such as CC0 or CC BY sets like Common Voice or LibriSpeech-derived accent sets. Alternatively, buy commercial licenses, or commission consented recordings with commercial-evaluation terms. Record the license of each corpus in the data-rights ledger, and fail CI on any corpus marked non-commercial. / keep 6/10: From knowledge. ASR fairness benchmarking on corpora like CORAAL is well established in research (Koenecke et al., PNAS 2020), but voice-AI vendors (Retell, Vapi, Bland, ElevenLabs) don't publish accuracy by speaker group or gate releases on it. As a deployed release gate in a lending voice agent, it is new. It is a governance strength more than something buyers pay for. Redesign: Run it as part of the existing red-team and model-swap CI. Start with two or three public corpora and a relay-friendly mode, and publish the per-version report on /ai. / keep 5/10: The accommodation-on-request, signal-triggered fallback and read-back parts are cheap and worth building. The nightly equity eval costs a lot for what it buys. The platform rents Google speech-to-text and can't retrain it, so a release gate only matters at vendor or model swaps, which are rare. Corpus licenses may bar commercial use: I recall SEP-28k and CORAAL carry non-commercial or research terms, unverified. The relay, slower-pacing and text-mirror features overlap with Accommodation Memory. Redesign: Move accommodations, relay-friendly mode and fallback into Accommodation Memory. Keep a small equity eval that runs only at STT or voice-model changes, on corpora whose licenses allow commercial testing (counsel to check), not nightly.
- **Sources:** https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1915768117 (Koenecke et al. 2020; DNS-blocked this session, figures recalled), https://openbenchmarks.com/voice-agent-latency/voice-agent-latency-comparison (from survey: latency-only benchmarking), https://www.twilio.com/en-us/changelog/conversationrelay-is-now-available-in-ga (from survey: text before speech), https://oraal.uoregon.edu/coraal (CORAAL corpus; canonical, not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/tests/m8-redteam.spec.ts (existing nightly-suite pattern)


## Instruction Receipts: anything consequential a borrower tells an AI channel becomes a dated receipt, with an honest status and a human deadline (round 3, high impact, ~3 weeks)

**When a borrower tells M8, by chat, text or voice, to lock, float, withdraw, change the loan amount, stop contacting someone, delete their data or that they're going with another lender, M8 says exactly what it can and can't do. It sends a dated receipt, gives the loan officer a deadline, and closes the loop only when a human acts.**

- **Problem:** Borrowers give consequential instructions at 9 PM to whatever is listening. A bot that answers 'Got it!' to 'lock it' creates a lock dispute. 'Withdraw my application' is a regulated event: an express withdrawal changes Reg B notice duties, and the sponsor's HMDA reporting records it as action taken. 'Stop calling my wife' is a revocation for another person, and 'I'm going with another lender' is a request to stop marketing. The survey's Texas AI-voice case (the AI kept pitching after repeated 'no's) shows what happens when an AI treats instructions as conversation. Today nobody can later prove what the borrower said, when, and what the system did about it.
- **How it works:** DETECTION: a closed taxonomy (lock request, float, withdraw, change of scenario, going elsewhere, contact-preference change for self or for another person, data access or deletion request, complaint) detected by a zod-validated classifier with a deterministic keyword backstop, in chat, D4 texts and voice transcripts. Borrower content is data, never instructions to the system. RESPONSE: M8 gives a fixed, guarded acknowledgment, for example: 'I can't lock rates. I've sent your request to [loan officer] at 8:42 PM. It isn't locked until they confirm, and you'll get a text when they do.' No rate unless it comes from a priced result, with APR and the not-a-commitment line. RECEIPT: an append-only record of the verbatim redacted quote, timestamp, channel, instruction type, the human action required, a deadline (the next window in the loan officer's hours, or counsel-set per type) and status. Self-executing types apply at once: a stop on any channel revokes immediately across the network (One Stop), and deletion and access requests open the taken data-request flow with sponsor holds. ROUTING: a pinned Today card with a countdown; an overdue receipt escalates to the team lead or a licensed backup (Coverage Circle). Withdrawal receipts start the taken application clock's Reg B timer and add an action-taken note to the LOS through ARIVE write-back. Lock receipts keep that day's dated snapshot beside the request for any later dispute. BORROWER: a text receipt (as a reply in their own thread, or under service consent) and a page listing every instruction they've given and its status. CLOSE: only a human action plus a confirmation to the borrower closes a receipt.
- **Technologies:** LLM intent classifier with a zod schema and a deterministic keyword backstop (lib/m8 pipeline), Append-only receipts table (extends cc_audit; can feed the taken witnessed ledger), D4 Twilio reply texts and the One Stop opt-out list (cc_optouts), ARIVE Zapier actions (update loan or lead, notes and tasks), Today pinned cards with countdowns (D5)
- **Why it's new:** Chatbots and voice agents in the survey (Total Expert AI Sales Assistant, UWM Mia, Sela, Structurely) capture intents to route leads; none publicly treats a borrower's instruction as a regulated event with an honest-status script, a receipt and a human deadline (an inference; search was exhausted). It differs from the taken 'delegation envelopes' (what an AI may do), 'sunlight receipts' (why a message was sent), 'complaint radar' (complaints only) and 'application clock' (TRID and Reg B timers, which this feeds): this captures what the borrower told the system and proves the follow-through.
- **Compliance design:** The AI never performs a lock, withdrawal, scenario change or pull itself; it only records and routes, and a human acts. The acknowledgment never implies a commitment (Reg Z advertising and lock disputes); all text passes the guard and check:copy. TCPA: revocations, including ones about another person, are honored immediately on every channel, and a revocation the borrower gives for someone else is flagged for that person's record and confirmed with them where consent allows. Receipt texts are replies in the borrower's own thread, or service messages under consent. Reg B: an express withdrawal starts the counsel-confirmed notice logic, recalled as 12 CFR 1002.9(e), not re-read this session. HMDA: the sponsor's action-taken coding stays with the sponsor's system. Privacy: deletion and access requests go to the data-request flow with GLBA and record-keeping holds. Receipts are NPI: encrypted, tenant-isolated, read-audited and kept per the retention vault.
- **Moat:** Receipts and their time-to-close become an operating standard that sponsors and examiners can audit across the network, and the taxonomy grows from counsel review of misses network-wide. A borrower who has seen 'it's not locked until a person confirms' at 9 PM trusts the platform, and that trust follows the MLO to any sponsor.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound. One risk: 'a revocation the borrower gives for someone else is ... confirmed with them where consent allows'. Contacting a third party to confirm a stop request about their own number invites a claim that a revocation was ignored or second-guessed. A countdown and deadline on lock requests can also build reliance and lead to lock disputes if missed. Redesign: When someone revokes for another person's number, suppress that number at once and never reach out to confirm. Re-enable it only if that person contacts the loan officer. Word lock-request deadlines as response targets, not price protection, and have counsel review the copy. / keep 6/10: From knowledge. Chat and voice agents detect intents, and LOS lock-request workflows exist, but I know of none that treats a borrower's instruction to an AI as a regulated event with an honest-status script, a receipt and a human deadline. It is new and directly lowers lock-dispute and revocation risk. Redesign: Build it inside lib/m8 as a closed intent taxonomy plus a receipts table. Make stop and delete self-executing first, then lock and withdraw routing. / keep 8/10: Strong on practicality. Lock and withdraw requests by text after hours are common, and a missed lock request is a real dispute cost. It reuses the existing classifier, guard and Today cards, so a small team can build it in 2 to 3 weeks. One weak spot: writing back to the LOS (ARIVE through Zapier) for withdrawals and action-taken may not be supported, so expect a manual step. Redesign: Ship it with the closed taxonomy, the fixed acknowledgments, the overdue escalation and the borrower receipt page. Treat LOS write-back as a manual task card until a connector supports it.
- **Sources:** https://www.mondaq.com/unitedstates/consumer-law/1821332/ai-robocalls-tcpa-and-texas-converge-in-class-action (from survey: AI kept selling after 'no'), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1002/section-1002.9 (Reg B notifications; DNS-blocked this session, not opened), https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work- (from survey: ARIVE update and adverse actions), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md D4 (cc_optouts, plain-English opt-outs) and D5 (Today), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (One Stop, Coverage Circle)


## Commons Gatekeeper: no data pooling without a registered, privacy-budgeted, antitrust-screened gate (round 3, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**Every feature that pools data across tenants (fraud exchange, fair-lending commons, play results, guard commons, signal weather) runs only through one compute layer. That layer accepts registered query templates only. Each template carries a stated purpose, a legal basis for each data class, a minimum number of contributors, a time lag, a differential-privacy budget, a never-list and a public use register.**

- **Problem:** At scale the platform's biggest asset, pooled data from thousands of MLOs at competing brokers, is also its biggest legal risk. Each 'commons' idea already accepted into the plan pools data separately, so each one is its own antitrust, GLBA and trust exposure, and nothing ties them to one set of rules. Antitrust: DOJ and the FTC withdrew their information-sharing 'safety zones' in 2023, so there is no harbor to point to. DOJ sued Agri Stats (2023) over an information-sharing intermediary and sued RealPage (2024) over algorithmic pricing built on competitors' nonpublic data. The January 2025 DOJ/FTC worker guidelines treat sharing wage and comp information as a possible violation. Privacy: a platform that is each sponsor's GLBA service provider may generally use a sponsor's nonpublic personal information only to perform services for that sponsor (Reg P 1016.11 reuse limits), so pooling NPI across sponsors needs a basis. The plan's 'Lender Pulse' was already rejected on exactly these grounds. Without a gate, the network effect cannot be used lawfully.
- **How it works:** DATA: The gate reads only through a separate Postgres role, scoped column by column. Every column carries a class: (a) GLBA NPI, (b) non-NPI contact data, (c) platform telemetry, or (d) 'never'. The never-list is enforced in the schema and in CI. It covers any price, rate, points, fee, margin, broker comp, lender comp plan, wage or split, and any territory or customer-allocation field. Demographics and proxies are not stored at all, per D2. TRIGGER: A feature that needs cross-tenant data files a 'use manifest'. The manifest states the purpose, data classes, legal basis for each class (NPI only with the borrower's field-level opt-in, otherwise excluded), output granularity, minimum contributors (a conservative internal default of at least 5 sponsors with no one sponsor above 25% of any cell, modeled on the withdrawn safety zones as a heuristic, not a harbor), minimum time lag, per-tenant epsilon per quarter, and a sunset date. Counsel signs it, and it is published on a public use register with a 30-day objection window. Any sponsor tenant may opt out of any use, and opting out never changes its service. SYSTEM: Only signed manifests can run. Each output passes small-cell suppression and differential-privacy noise (OpenDP or Tumult) before anyone sees it. Each tenant's privacy budget is charged, and a use stops when the budget runs out. An antitrust screen rejects any output that describes current or future competitive terms of named or identifiable members. Outputs are KMS-signed and logged, and that log feeds the existing independent-control-attestation idea. MLO: A 'Your data in the commons' panel lists each use their book contributes to, the budget spent, the outputs, and a one-tap opt-out where the sponsor allows it. BORROWER: Their data never leaves the sponsor's tenant unless they opted in to a named use. The sunlight-receipt link (an existing idea) can show which registered uses touched their record. RECRUIT or SPONSOR: The register is the due-diligence answer to 'what do you do with our data?'
- **Technologies:** Postgres column-level grants and RLS with a CI privilege test, OpenDP / Tumult Analytics differential privacy, small-cell suppression, private set intersection for overlap uses, signed query manifests (KMS), append-only output log, public use-register page, ESLint import boundaries
- **Why it's new:** What exists: vendor CRMs pool customer data under broad license grants. Loansifter's license 3.3 takes a perpetual license to customer data, including applicant data used de-identified (survey). Bonzo/MMI and Total Expert monetize enrichment. ICE's ISV terms bar vendors from aggregating data across licensees, which shows how incumbents control pooling contractually rather than technically. I know of no mortgage platform that publishes a per-use register, enforces a never-list in the schema, or charges each tenant a differential-privacy budget. The closest public precedent for DP at scale is the Census Bureau's 2020 disclosure avoidance system, and NIST SP 800-226 (2025) gives evaluation guidance. Verification note: this session's web-search budget was used up and justice.gov was outside the allowed network, so the RealPage, Agri Stats and 2023 withdrawal facts are training knowledge to re-verify. The RealPage settlement terms in particular are not confirmed here.
- **Compliance design:** Antitrust: the never-list means no member ever receives another member's pricing, comp, wages or territory data. Outputs are lagged, aggregated, DP-noised and limited to non-price topics (fraud, consent quality, model fairness, connector health). Outputs never carry a recommendation to act on competitive terms [COUNSEL: Sherman §1 information-exchange analysis for each manifest]. GLBA/Reg P: NPI stays within the sponsor tenant unless the borrower opts in for a named use. The sponsor's service-provider agreement lists the register as the full set of permitted secondary uses [COUNSEL: 1016.11 reuse, 1016.13/.14 exceptions]. Fair lending: demographics and proxies are not stored, and the fair-lending commons runs as a registered use under this gate. No lead is ever sold: the gate has no output type that identifies a person to another tenant. Comp firewall: the reward engine's role cannot read gate outputs. State privacy (CCPA and others): the register doubles as the notice of purposes, and opt-outs are honored per use.
- **Moat:** Each new tenant pushes more registered uses past their minimum-contributor thresholds, so network features unlock as membership grows. A dated register showing years of uses that never touched prices or leads is a trust record that a data-vendor-owned competitor cannot copy without giving up its business model. It is also the legal precondition for every other commons idea, so it compounds with all of them.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: A strong control that reduces risk for the other commons ideas. Two cautions. (1) Data class (b), 'non-NPI contact data', is risky: a borrower's contact details tied to a lending relationship are NPI, because a customer list is NPI under Reg P, so few contact fields are truly non-NPI. (2) Cross-tenant use of NPI by a service provider is limited to the exceptions in 1016.13-.15 even with differential privacy; the fraud-prevention exception covers fraud uses only. Redesign: Treat every borrower-linked contact field as NPI by default. Class (b) applies only to people who never had a customer or consumer relationship, under state-privacy handling. Have each manifest cite the specific Reg P exception or the opt-in it relies on. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted on technology, from knowledge. AWS Clean Rooms (with differential privacy), Snowflake Data Clean Rooms, LiveRamp, and Tumult Analytics and OpenDP already provide registered-query, minimum-threshold, DP-noised cross-party analytics. A public use register with an objection window and a schema-level 'never-list' is new for mortgage, and it is a precondition for every commons idea. Redesign: Buy rather than build: run on a clean-room product with DP, and add the public use register, the counsel-signed manifest and the never-list CI check as the platform's own layer. / keep 5/10: Premature as written. The network has a handful of loan officers under one or two sponsors, so thresholds of 5 or more sponsors with no sponsor above 25% won't be met for years. Differential-privacy budgets, counsel-signed manifests and 30-day public objection windows are real fixed costs that unlock nothing yet. The cheap part is worth doing now because later retrofits are expensive: a column class on every column, a never-list enforced in the schema and in CI, and a separate role for pooled reads. Redesign: Now: column classes, the never-list in CI, and a pooled-read role with a manifest file in the repo. Later, when 3 or more sponsors are live: the public register, DP noise (the OpenDP library) and budgets.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1016/section-1016.11 (Reg P reuse and redisclosure limits; canonical URL, not opened this session), https://www.justice.gov/opa/pr/justice-department-withdraws-outdated-enforcement-policy-statements (DOJ withdrawal of information-sharing safety zones, Feb 2023; training knowledge, host blocked this session), https://www.justice.gov/atr/case/united-states-et-al-v-realpage-inc-et-al (DOJ v. RealPage; training knowledge, host blocked), https://www.justice.gov/opa/pr/justice-department-sues-agri-stats-operating-extensive-information-sharing-scheme-among-meat (training knowledge, host blocked), https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/01/ftc-doj-issue-antitrust-guidelines-business-activities-affecting-workers (training knowledge; exact URL unverified), https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/226/final (NIST differential privacy guidelines; training knowledge), https://opendp.org, https://mortgagetech.ice.com/publicdocs/mortgage/ISV-Partner-Terms-and-Conditions.pdf (survey)


## Licensed Judgment Corpus: the members own what good mortgage AI behavior looks like (round 3, high impact, ~7 weeks)

**Thousands of NMLS-verified MLOs grade and rewrite M8's replies to synthetic borrower turns, and counsel rules on the disagreements. The result is a dated, member-owned gold set that every candidate voice and chat model must pass before any tenant can use it. It is also the training data for a fallback model whose weights the members own.**

- **Problem:** M8 runs on vendor models that change underneath it. Third-party trackers listed gemini-3.1-flash-live-preview with an earliest shutdown of 2026-11-17, and developers report '-latest' aliases dropping Twilio calls (survey). The plan bans borrower data in model training (§2.2), so the industry's usual fix, fine-tuning on real conversations, is closed. The 5.10 training loop improves one owner's voice from that owner's corrections. It doesn't scale into a shared standard, and it can't decide whether a new model is safe for everyone. Vendor benchmarks are generic, and none tests mortgage judgment such as: a rate with no APR, a promise of approval, steering, discussing a loan in an unlicensed state, missing a spoken revocation, or skipping the AI disclosure. The Texas AI-voice suit alleges an agent kept selling after 'no' (survey).
- **How it works:** DATA: A scenario generator builds synthetic borrower turns from templates: product, state, the borrower's stated goal, a twist (a rate question, an 'are you a robot', a hardship, a revocation in casual words, a prompt injection inside an 'uploaded' document), plus the state rule version and guideline citations. It uses no real borrower data and no protected attributes; matched-pair fairness testing stays in the separate paired-tester-lab idea. Any rate in a scenario is a fixture flagged as a priced example. TRIGGER: An MLO opts in to a 10-minute 'judgment round' from Today on a slow day. SYSTEM: It shows two candidate replies or a call snippet. The MLO picks the better one, tags violations from a fixed taxonomy and may write the ideal reply. Only MLOs with an active license in the scenario's state grade that scenario. Items where graders disagree go to a compliance and counsel panel, and its adjudicated answer becomes 'gold' with the citation and rule version attached. USES: (1) Model gate. Every candidate model, voice pipeline or prompt change runs nightly against the gold set plus the existing red-team suite, and no tenant can turn it on until it clears per-category thresholds (zero tolerance on rate without APR and on missed revocation). (2) Co-op model. Preference-tune an open-weight model under a permissive license, using human-written gold replies only, never outputs from a vendor whose terms ban training competing models. The members own the weights, and it serves as a fallback provider and a price check against vendors. (3) The 5.10 per-tenant style layer sits on top, unchanged. MLO: sees their judgments, how many reached gold, and their agreement rate with counsel, as a private skill signal and never shown publicly. Contribution credit flows only through the existing contributor-lane idea, not referenced as a new reward. BORROWER: gets an M8 whose every model swap was approved by licensed professionals and counsel, stated on the AI page.
- **Technologies:** synthetic scenario generator with rule-version stamps, pairwise preference labeling UI (mobile), inter-rater agreement scoring and counsel adjudication queue, nightly eval harness extending tests/m8-redteam*, open-weight model preference tuning (DPO-style), model registry with dated pinned IDs, NMLS license-state check for grader eligibility
- **Why it's new:** What exists: generic speech and LLM benchmarks; vendor-run red teams; enterprise CRMs whose AI behavior is set by the vendor (Total Expert's AI discloses itself 'when required by law or if prompted', per the survey). No mortgage vendor has a labeled corpus from thousands of independent licensed MLOs, because enterprise CRMs are licensed to institutions and the AI-voice newcomers sell to call centers. No one lets the professionals who carry the license decide whether a model may speak to borrowers. Skeptical check: some model vendors' terms bar using their outputs to develop competing models (OpenAI's terms do, and Anthropic's and Google's commercial terms have similar restrictions), so the co-op model trains only on human-written gold replies. Verification note: vendor-terms wording is from training knowledge and was not re-opened this session (web budget exhausted).
- **Compliance design:** No borrower data: the scenarios are synthetic and screened by lib/m8/redact. SAFE Act: grading synthetic text involves no consumer, application or loan terms, so it is not origination activity [COUNSEL: confirm]. Reg Z comp: credit for judgment work comes only from the existing contributor lane, measured by gold items, never by loans [COUNSEL]. Antitrust: the corpus covers compliance and quality behavior only, with no member pricing, fees or comp in any scenario, and the item store is a registered use under the Commons Gatekeeper. AI-rate rule: any spoken or written rate in the corpus is tagged as a fixture, and the gate fails any model that states a rate without the pricing tool result. Fair lending: no protected attributes or proxies in the scenarios; fairness is tested in the separate paired lab. State AI laws: the AI page discloses how models are approved, which supports the Utah, Colorado and California governance evidence. Vendor ToS: a training-provenance flag on every reply blocks vendor-generated text from the training set.
- **Moat:** The corpus grows with every member and every rule change, so the gate gets stricter and broader over time. A competitor can't buy licensed judgment from thousands of independent MLOs across all states. Owning the gold set and a fallback model makes the platform model-agnostic: it can switch to the cheapest vendor that passes, a bargaining position no solo MLO or vendor-owned CRM has.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: Grading synthetic text is not origination. The risks are: (1) Contributor-lane rewards paid to loan officers by a non-sponsor may break state rules that only the sponsoring licensee may compensate an MLO, even for non-origination work. (2) Loan officers employed by sponsors may not own work they produce on company time, so IP assignment needs the employer's consent. (3) Preference tuning on pairs where one side is vendor-generated text is still 'using outputs to develop a competing model' under several vendors' terms, even if only human gold replies are the positive targets. Redesign: Pay contribution credit only as subscription credit to the payer of record, or in counsel-approved units. Get a sponsor consent and IP license for employed loan officers. Train the co-op model only on human-written text: no vendor outputs at all, not even as rejected samples. / keep 5/10: From knowledge. Expert-labeled evaluation and preference data are an established business (Scale AI, Surge, Mercor hire licensed professionals). I know of no mortgage corpus graded by independent licensed MLOs that gates model releases, so it is new in mortgage. It is doubtful busy MLOs will grade without pay, and a co-op fine-tuned model is a large undertaking. Redesign: Start with a small counsel-adjudicated gold set graded by a paid panel, used only as a release gate next to the red team. Defer the co-op model weights. / drop 4/10: It assumes thousands of grading loan officers who don't exist. Loan officers won't spend unpaid time grading synthetic replies, and paying contributor credits for it adds cost and comp-proxy review. Preference-tuning and hosting an open-weight fallback model is beyond a small team and would lag vendor models. The useful kernel, a set of cases adjudicated by counsel that gates model swaps, is an extension of tests/m8-redteam. Redesign: Grow tests/m8-redteam-cases.ts into a gold set of 200 to 500 items that counsel adjudicates, used as a release gate for any model or prompt change. Optionally collect owner and member flags on real (redacted) near-misses. Drop the co-op model.
- **Sources:** https://aiengineerguide.com/tools/model-retirements/gemini-3-1-flash-live-preview/ (survey: model churn), https://www.mondaq.com/unitedstates/consumer-law/1821332/ai-robocalls-tcpa-and-texas-converge-in-class-action (survey: AI kept selling after 'no'), https://www.totalexpert.com/features/ai-sales-assistant (survey: disclosure only when required or prompted), https://openai.com/policies/terms-of-use (output-use restriction; training knowledge, not opened this session), https://www.anthropic.com/legal/commercial-terms (training knowledge, not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md §2.2 (no borrower data in training), §5.10, /home/user/RATEM8/tests/m8-redteam.spec.ts


## Seat Truth: proof that every rewarded seat-month belongs to a real, licensed, independently paid MLO (round 3, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**Before any seat-month earns subscription credits or units, it must pass checks for a verified human, an active NMLS license and sponsor, a payer who isn't the recruiter, and a minimum level of use that has nothing to do with loans. Seats that look like straw seats are held until reviewed. That keeps a subscription-and-tenure ownership plan honest when thousands of MLOs are on it.**

- **Problem:** Rewards tied only to subscription and tenure are the right legal design. At scale, though, they can be gamed in a way production-based plans can't. A recruiter can pay for straw seats to farm platform-months. A sponsor can buy seats for people who never use them. One person can run several identities. A seat can sit idle and still build tenure. Each of these dilutes honest members, invites securities anti-fraud exposure on the unit ledger (Rule 10b-5), and makes the plan look like a recruiting tree, the very pattern the plan's anti-MLM stance rejects. There's also a Reg Z angle: if a sponsor pays for seats in a way that works as disguised loan-officer comp, it needs to be visible. The Comp Firewall (idea 12) proves the engine can't see loans. It doesn't prove the seats are real.
- **How it works:** DATA: Read through the same firewalled reward role, which gains SELECT on four new tables only: identity_checks, license_checks, payer_instruments (a payment-instrument fingerprint, never the card number) and usage_counts (counts of distinct sign-in days and gated actions, with no content and no loan data). TRIGGER: Monthly accrual. SYSTEM: A seat-month counts only if all of these hold. (1) One human, one seat: passkey plus document-and-liveness identity check at enrollment, through a vendor that keeps no biometric template. (2) License: one check per seat per month that the NMLS ID is active with a current sponsor in at least one state, using an SRR-permitted access path or a Consumer Access lookup for the purpose of deciding whether to do business with that subscriber. No bulk monitoring and no scraping. (3) Payer independence: if the recruiter-of-record's payment instrument pays a recruit's seat, those months earn that recruiter nothing. (4) Bona fide use: at least N distinct sign-in days and one gated action in the month, the same floor for everyone, never measured in loans, applications or volume. (5) Graph review: clusters of seats sharing devices, payment instruments or sign-in patterns are held for human review. A held month accrues nothing until it is cleared, and the decision is explained and open to appeal. Each quarter an integrity report (seats checked, held, cleared, voided) is signed next to the Comp Firewall attestation, and the cap-table holder count uses only verified people. MLO: sees 'this month counted' or 'held: we need X', and never a dollar projection. SPONSOR: can buy seats for its own MLOs. Those seats earn credit to the payer of record under the survey's routing design, not units to a third-party recruiter.
- **Technologies:** passkeys (WebAuthn), document plus liveness IDV vendor with no retained template, NMLS SRR-permitted license check, one per seat per month, Stripe payment-method fingerprints, graph clustering over device, payer and session features, firewalled Postgres role with CI privilege test (extends idea 12), KMS-signed quarterly integrity report
- **Why it's new:** What exists: eXp, Real, One Real, reAlpha and NEXA tie equity or revenue share to transactions, so a closed deal is the anti-fraud signal (survey). Loan Factory pays cash or RSUs for referring loan officers. No platform rewards subscription tenure, so none had to solve straw seats, and none proves which seats are real to members, counsel and examiners. The survey's cap-table compliance engine counts holders. This makes sure the counted holders and months are real people. Verification note: the NMLS terms come from the survey's cited PDFs, and the other items are training knowledge, since no new pages could be opened this session.
- **Compliance design:** Reg Z 1026.36(d): the use floor counts sign-ins and gated actions only, never loans, applications or volume. The reward role still can't read loan, pricing or pipeline tables, so no proxy can creep in [COUNSEL]. State rules on who may pay an MLO: rewards stay as subscription credits to the payer of record, or as counsel-approved units [COUNSEL]. Securities: an accurate holder count and anti-fraud controls on unit issuance, with plan documents describing the integrity checks [COUNSEL: Reg D/CF disclosure]. NMLS terms: the use is limited to deciding whether to keep doing business with a subscriber, one lookup a month, no solicitation, minimal retention [COUNSEL: SRR permitted use]. Biometric privacy (BIPA, Texas CUBI, Washington): the IDV vendor gives consent screens and keeps no template. RESPA 8: agents can never hold seats or units, so they're outside the check. ECOA and fair treatment: the same floor for everyone, with an appeal and a written reason. Copy: no earnings claims.
- **Moat:** The plan's credibility depends on unit integrity, and integrity gets more valuable as the member count grows, because each fake seat dilutes more real people. A multi-year, attested record that every rewarded month was a real licensed person is something a fast-follower can't backfill. It also lets the platform say truthfully that its stake can't be farmed, which recruiting-tree models can't say.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: Risks: (1) Liveness and face-match ID checks compute biometric identifiers even when no template is kept, so Illinois's BIPA needs a written release and a retention policy before capture, and Texas's CUBI and Washington's biometric law need notice and consent. (2) A monthly NMLS Consumer Access lookup for every seat, across thousands of seats, is in effect the bulk or automated monitoring those terms forbid. (3) A minimum-usage floor adds a condition beyond 'subscription and tenure only'. It is not loan-based, but it changes the stated plan and must be in the plan documents. Redesign: Use BIPA-compliant written releases and a vendor contract that bans keeping biometrics. Verify licenses through an NMLS-authorized data subscription or a sponsor attestation, not Consumer Access sweeps. Write the use floor into the counsel-approved plan as an eligibility condition. / keep 6/10: From knowledge. Recruiting revenue-share programs (eXp, Real, Loan Factory) use closed transactions as their fraud signal, so none has had to solve straw seats under a subscription-only reward. Identity verification and payment-instrument clustering are standard anti-fraud techniques, but applying them to keep a subscription-and-tenure reward plan honest is new and necessary for idea 12. Redesign: Make it a required control inside the Comp Firewall reward engine rather than its own feature. Use a commercial identity-verification vendor with no stored template, and have counsel confirm NMLS permitted use. / keep 6/10: It's necessary if credits or units are issued, and cheap relative to the dilution risk. Identity verification costs about $1 to $2 per check (Stripe Identity or Persona, from memory), and payer-independence and usage floors are simple queries. At under 200 seats, though, the graph review and the quarterly signed integrity report are overkill, and a human can review new seats. NMLS terms limit how lookups may be used. Redesign: Version 1: identity verification at enrollment, a monthly NMLS check by manual Consumer Access lookup, the payer-independence rule and the use floor, with human review. Automate the graph review after about 500 seats.
