- **Sources:** https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/policies/NMLS%20Document%20Library/Consumer%20Access%20Terms%20of%20Use.pdf (survey), https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/Documents/NMLS%20B2B%20ACCESS%20AGREEMENTv4_20.pdf (survey), https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/17/240.10b-5 (training knowledge, not opened), https://docs.stripe.com/api/payment_methods/object (card fingerprint; training knowledge, not opened), https://www.ilga.gov/legislation/ilcs/ilcs3.asp?ActID=3004 (BIPA; training knowledge), https://nationalmortgageprofessional.com/news/one-real-mortgage-introduces-loan-officer-incentives (survey), https://nationalmortgageprofessional.com/news/nexa-removes-guardrails-its-100-revenue-model (survey), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md idea 12


## Product Gap Ledger: unmet borrower demand turned into new loan products, offered to every lender on equal terms (round 3, medium impact, ~5 weeks)

**When members can't place a borrower, they log why in a structured, de-identified way (product, property type, state, the guideline that blocked it). At scale these become counts, k-anonymous and lagged, published at the same moment to every wholesale lender, the GSEs and housing-finance agencies. Lenders get a reason to build missing products. Members' 'not yet' borrowers get a re-check when a matching product appears.**

- **Problem:** Small brokers see unmet demand first. Typical examples are a non-warrantable condo with one specific defect, ITIN borrowers in a given state, ADU rental income, manufactured homes on leased land, and DPA seconds that won't layer with a given first lien. One broker's anecdotes don't change a lender's product roadmap. Lenders learn about demand only from the loans they already make, and the FHFA Duty to Serve program asks the GSEs to serve manufactured housing, rural and affordable-preservation markets with little granular broker-side evidence. Borrowers who fall in these gaps are told 'no' and lost, or steered into whatever product exists. This differs from the scenario commons (answering a live scenario from guidelines), the not-yet desk (re-screening one MLO's own stalled files) and signal weather (early warnings): this is the network's demand signal aimed at product creation.
- **How it works:** DATA: A structured 'couldn't place' reason the MLO picks when a file or pre-approval ends: product family, occupancy, property type, state, a blocking-rule code with its guideline citation (from the guideline ledger idea), and the stage. No name, address, ZIP, credit score, income figure, rate or price. It never includes demographics or census-tract fields, which aren't stored (D2). TRIGGER: Quarterly, as a registered Commons Gatekeeper use: at least 10 cases from at least 5 sponsors in a cell, at least 90 days of lag, state-level geography only, with DP noise. SYSTEM: Publishes a dated gap report to every lender on the panel, to any wholesale lender that asks, to the GSEs and to state housing-finance agencies, all at once, free, and with no exclusivity. Lenders can post 'we now offer X' back to the ledger, and the platform checks it against their published guidelines before it's listed. The MLO's own matching closed-out files then get a Today card: 'A product now exists for a scenario like [client]'s; re-check with permission'. Any outreach still goes through the send gate, and pricing only through a priced result. MLO: one tap at file close, and they get back new options for their own past 'no's. BORROWER: a real second chance, with no lead created or sold. LENDER: product evidence it can't get from its own pipeline.
- **Technologies:** structured close-out reason codes tied to the guideline ledger, Commons Gatekeeper with k-thresholds and DP noise, dated public report generator, lender 'product available' submission with guideline verification, Today card matching against the MLO's own closed-out files
- **Why it's new:** What exists: lenders see only their own funded and declined files; aggregators sell lead and rate data; HMDA shows originations and denials with about a 15-month lag, with loan-officer and location fields redacted and nothing about loans never applied for or products that don't exist (survey). The GSEs gather Duty to Serve input through comments and pilots. I know of no broker-side, de-identified, guideline-coded record of unplaceable demand, published at once to all lenders, that reconnects each MLO to their own lost borrowers when a product appears. Only a network of independent MLOs across many sponsors and lenders can produce it. Verification note: Duty to Serve and HMDA details are from training knowledge and the survey; no new fetch was possible this session.
- **Compliance design:** No lead is ever sold: outputs are counts. Lenders pay nothing and get nothing identifying, and re-contact happens only inside the MLO's own book under the send gate. RESPA 8: no lender may pay for, sponsor or get early access to the report, and equal simultaneous publication removes any 'thing of value' exchange [COUNSEL]. Reg Z comp: the MLO earns nothing for logging reasons. Antitrust: demand data shared upstream with suppliers. It contains no member prices, fees or comp, and members don't receive each other's cells beyond the public report [COUNSEL]. Fair lending and ECOA: geography stops at the state, there are no demographic or proxy fields, and use is additive only. The ledger may justify new products and education, never narrowing an offer [COUNSEL: Reg B; special purpose credit program rules, including the November 2025 proposal noted in the survey, if a lender builds an SPCP from it]. GLBA: reason codes are not NPI once stripped of identifiers. The record-level code stays in the sponsor tenant, and only gated aggregates leave. AI rule: Today cards never state a rate; pricing comes only from a priced result.
- **Moat:** Every member's close-outs fill more cells past the threshold, and every product a lender builds from the ledger proves its value and pulls in more members and lenders. Over years it becomes the industry's record of unmet mortgage demand, a public good that keeps the members, not a single large lender, at the center of product design.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: The published aggregates are fine. The re-contact loop is the problem. Matching a past declined applicant to a new product using a blocking reason taken from their consumer report (for example a credit-score band) and then marketing to them is a use of consumer-report information for solicitation. Without a firm offer of credit that use may lack a permissible purpose under FCRA 604, and it sits close to the post-HPPA limits on using mortgage-inquiry data. A file the broker 'couldn't place' may also have needed a Reg B adverse-action or incompleteness notice, including under the multiple-creditor rule in 1002.9(g), and the ledger should not stand in for that. Redesign: Re-contact only borrowers who opted in at close-out with 'tell me if a product like this appears', a written consent with its own record. Match on the non-credit scenario fields only. At close-out, also prompt for the required Reg B notice. / keep 5/10: Partly anticipated, from knowledge. Pricing engines (Optimal Blue market analytics) see searches that returned nothing eligible and sell market data built on them, and the GSEs collect Duty to Serve input. A guideline-coded record from brokers, published to every lender at once and tied back to each loan officer's own past declines, looks new. It needs scale before it means anything. Redesign: Capture the structured 'couldn't place' reason at file close from day one, because it is cheap. Delay any publication until Commons Gatekeeper thresholds are met and counsel clears RESPA and antitrust. / drop 4/10: Thresholds of 10 or more cases across 5 or more sponsors per cell won't be reached for years. Lenders build products from their own scenario-desk traffic and investor appetite, not from counts by a small broker network. The loan officer's real benefit, re-checking their own 'not yet' borrowers when a product appears, works for a single tenant without any publishing. Redesign: Keep only the per-loan-officer piece: a structured 'couldn't place' reason at close-out, plus a Today card when a guideline-ledger row matching that reason changes. Revisit the published report at network scale.
- **Sources:** https://www.fhfa.gov/programs/duty-to-serve (training knowledge, not opened this session), https://ffiec.cfpb.gov/documentation/publications/modified-lar/resources/using-mlar-data (survey: redacted HMDA fields), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1002/section-1002.8 (Reg B special purpose credit programs; canonical URL, not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/2025-hmda-data-on-mortgage-lending-now-available/ (survey; HMDA timing), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md §2.2 (fair-lending rule)


## Two-Key Suspension: a due-process charter that keeps the compliance kill switch apart from the member's stake (round 3, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**The platform can freeze a member's sending immediately for a compliance reason. Ending their seat, stopping their vesting or forfeiting their units needs written reasons, a time to fix the problem, and review by a panel of peer MLOs drawn by lot.**

- **Problem:** An MLO who leaves a large company for an MLO-owned platform is betting that the platform can't take the stake away on a whim. Today every software vendor and employer can end the relationship at will. Unvested equity at One Real, reAlpha and NEXA is lost on separation, and the CRM reviews in the survey describe Shape billing after cancellation and MloFlo leads going to other LOs after a cancel. But the platform also has to stop a TCPA or GLBA problem within minutes. Command Center has neither of the two things needed: (1) a way to act fast on compliance that doesn't touch the economics, and (2) a fair process before anything economic happens. Without both, the stake is not credible and recruits won't switch.
- **How it works:** DATA: send-gate decisions and blocks (D3), opt-out and complaint spikes (D4 and Complaint Radar), license-status events (Licensed Hands), billing events, and the counsel-approved plan documents' list of forfeiture events. TRIGGER: any rule that today would disable a user (D1 'disable' signs the account out everywhere). WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES: (1) Key 1, the operational freeze. It is automatic and immediate and covers only what went wrong: one play, one channel, all M8 AI calls, or all outbound. It records a machine-generated reason drawn from the gate telemetry (for example '14 STOPs in 48h on play v3 in FL'). The member can still read their book, reply to inbound messages under the gate, and export (Book Deed). Vesting and platform-month accrual keep running during a freeze. (2) Key 2, an economic action: ending the seat, pausing vesting, or forfeiture or buyback under the plan. This needs a written notice citing the plan clause, an evidence packet, and a cure window where the plan and the law allow one (default 30 days). It then goes to a review panel of 5 members drawn by lot, screened for conflicts (no one from the same sponsor, the same service area, or with a recruiting link to the member), chaired by an independent person (a counsel-picked arbitrator or ombuds). (3) Decisions are published to members anonymized, with the rule applied, so they build up as precedent. (4) The appeal and the clocks run in the app: notice date, cure deadline, panel date, decision. WHAT THE MLO DOES: sees the exact reason for any freeze within seconds, fixes the play or the consent problem, and asks for an unfreeze once a compliance reviewer confirms the fix. For an economic action, they answer in writing and can bring their own counsel. RECRUIT EXPERIENCE: the package page shows the charter, the count of freezes and economic actions last quarter, and their outcomes. BORROWER: is protected from the first bad message, because Key 1 never waits on Key 2.
- **Technologies:** Postgres append-only case ledger with KMS-signed decisions, Send-gate telemetry thresholds (D3/D4) driving scoped freeze flags, Verifiable random draw for panel selection (published seed with commit-reveal), Conflict screen over the sponsor, territory and recruit_links tables, Document assembly for notices and evidence packets (PDF), Role-based access so panelists see a redacted packet with no borrower NPI
- **Why it's new:** No mortgage CRM or AI vendor publishes a suspension or termination process. Their terms allow termination at will, and the survey's review evidence shows the failure modes (Shape billing after cancellation and 'annual contracts' customers never saw; MloFlo leads going to other LOs after a cancel). Real estate and mortgage equity programs (One Real, reAlpha, NEXA, eXp) tie the economics to staying, with no stated process before forfeiture. Precedents exist outside mortgage. The EU Platform-to-Business Regulation (EU) 2019/1150, Art. 4, requires a statement of reasons for suspension and 30 days' notice before termination, and Art. 11 requires internal complaint handling. Seattle's App-Based Worker Deactivation Rights Ordinance (in effect from 2025 as I recall) requires notice and an appeal before deactivation. Cooperative principle 2 (democratic member control) supports peer review. Nobody has split 'stop the harm now' from 'take the stake later' in code. Verification note: this round's web search budget was used up and eur-lex.europa.eu, ecfr.gov and housingwire.com were outside the session's network, so these precedents come from model knowledge and need checking.
- **Compliance design:** Key 1 never waits: the platform's own TCPA, Safeguards and state-law duties are met by the immediate scoped freeze. The panel can never unfreeze sending that counsel or the compliance reviewer says is unlawful; it decides only economic outcomes. The panel doesn't decide licensing or supervision: under the SAFE Act the sponsoring company supervises the MLO, and a sponsor's own action (for example ending sponsorship) is reflected, not reviewed. Forfeiture events in the plan documents are limited to conduct (fraud, a data-charter breach, a proven TCPA violation, a seat unpaid beyond the grace period) and never to loan volume or loan terms, so nothing becomes Reg Z compensation by the back door. Panelists see redacted packets: no borrower NPI, consent proofs shown only as hashes plus the gate decision (GLBA). Their service is unpaid or paid as contributor-lane units for bona fide platform work, never per case outcome. Securities: the forfeiture and buyback terms live in the counsel-approved plan; the charter is incorporated by reference so units aren't illusory [COUNSEL: securities and arbitration enforceability]. Copy: policy language only, no promises about outcomes.
- **Moat:** Every published decision adds to a body of precedent that makes outcomes predictable, and predictability is what lets an MLO put real value on a platform stake. More members make a larger, more conflict-free panel pool. An incumbent owned by a data vendor or an LOS can't credibly copy this, because its terms exist to keep the right to terminate at will.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound, and it reduces risk. Two cautions. Panels of competing MLOs voting to take away a competitor's economic stake carry some group-boycott antitrust risk, which the independent chair and conflict screens mitigate. Paying panelists in units or credits brings back the question of which states let a non-sponsor pay an MLO. Redesign: Make the independent chair's sign-off required for any adverse economic outcome. Write the antitrust protocol into the charter. Panel service is unpaid, or credited only to the payer of record under counsel-approved terms. / keep 6/10: From knowledge, precedents not re-verified. There are precedents outside mortgage: EU P2B Regulation Art. 4 requires reasons and notice, and Seattle's app-based worker deactivation ordinance requires an appeal. I know of no mortgage platform that separates the immediate scoped freeze from economic forfeiture and adds peer review. It is new here and makes the ownership stake credible. Redesign: Write it into the counsel-approved plan documents. Build Key 1 (scoped freeze with a reason) now as part of D3 and D4. Defer the panel machinery until there are enough members to fill it. / keep 6/10: Key 1, a scoped freeze of one play or one channel instead of D1's sign-out-everywhere disable, is cheap and better engineering than what exists now. Key 2 is mostly plan documents and process, low code. Practical limits. A panel of 5 drawn by lot and screened for conflicts needs a member pool much larger than today's. Independent arbitrators or ombuds cost hundreds of dollars an hour. Economic actions will be rare, so the fixed cost of the panel machinery comes before any need. Redesign: Build the scoped freeze now. Write the notice, cure and appeal process into the plan documents, with an independent reviewer that counsel picks. Add the lottery panel once members number over 100.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D1 disable signs out everywhere; D3 gate; D4 opt-outs), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 2.4 and 4.8, https://www.capterra.co.uk/reviews/173673/setshape, https://www.capterra.com/p/212895/Bonzo/, https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2019/1150/oj (Art. 4 and 11; not opened this session), https://www.seattle.gov/laborstandards/ordinances/app-based-worker-ordinances (not opened this session), https://www.ica.coop/en/cooperatives/cooperative-identity, https://mortgageorb.com/one-real-mortgage-launches-compensation-and-stock-awards-programs-for-los


## Open Books Room: the platform's own numbers, shown to the people who own it (round 3, high impact, ~3 weeks)

**Every unit holder and subscribing member sees the same monthly, accountant-checked view of Command Center's own business: where each seat dollar goes, how many members renew, how many months of cash are left, and what was spent on counsel and compliance. It shows platform data only, never anyone's loans, and makes no projections.**

- **Problem:** A stake is worth only as much as the holder can understand it. eXp's agents get a 10-K written for Wall Street, and third-party sources can't agree on its tier rates; one review quotes eXp's own disclosure that the median Tier 1 payout is $0. Real files a 40-F. Private mortgage companies (NEXA, Loan Factory) tell LOs nothing verifiable. An MLO deciding whether to switch has no way to see the business they would part-own. Without honest numbers, recruiting falls back on hype, which check:copy bans and which invites MLM comparisons. A flat, published seat price is only credible if members can see what it pays for.
- **How it works:** DATA: the platform's own books (Stripe billing events, the general ledger, vendor invoices for AI minutes, carrier, 10DLC, LOS-sync, counsel and insurance), seat counts and renewal cohorts from `subscriptions` and `seats`, and the cap table. It never reads loan, pricing, pipeline or borrower tables, and runs under a role with no grants on them, the same firewall pattern as the platform-months engine. TRIGGER: monthly close, plus a quarterly agreed-upon-procedures check by an independent CPA. WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES: (1) A seat-dollar waterfall: for every $1 of seat revenue, how much went to AI and carrier pass-through, infrastructure, compliance and counsel, support, engineering, and margin. (2) Cohort renewal and churn by join quarter, with full denominators. (3) Cash months remaining at current burn. (4) Units outstanding, the dilution history, and every grant class. (5) A data-charter compliance line: zero leads sold, zero aggregate datasets licensed out, and counts of export and delete requests honored on time. (6) A plain risk panel: 'units may be worth nothing; there is no market for them; here is what has to be true for them to be worth anything'. WHAT THE MLO DOES: reads it on their phone the way they read Today, and asks questions in a moderated Q&A whose answers go to all holders at once. RECRUIT: sees a delayed, counsel-cleared version on the package page, so the recruiting pitch is the books, not a promise.
- **Technologies:** Read-only Postgres role over billing, seats, ledger and cap-table tables, with a CI privilege test (reusing the idea-12 pattern), Stripe and accounting-system exports (QuickBooks or Xero API) into a monthly close snapshot, KMS-signed monthly snapshot with a hash chain so past months can't be silently restated, CPA agreed-upon-procedures workpapers attached as PDFs, check:copy extension that bans projections, 'expected value', 'will grow' and earnings phrases on this page, Time-boxed publication windows tied to any Liquidity Ladder transfer window
- **Why it's new:** Public brokerages disclose to all investors in SEC filings that aren't built for LOs; private mortgage companies disclose nothing; co-ops (Lenders One, the CUSOs) report to institutional members, not to individual LOs. I found no mortgage technology vendor that shows its users a per-seat cost breakdown. The survey's pricing complaints (Bonzo's $25/month integration fees, Structurely's credits and onboarding fees, Shape's opaque tiers) show buyers can't see what they pay for. Open-book management exists in general business; applying it to an MLO-owned platform's cap table and seat economics, under the same database firewall that keeps loans out, is new in this market. Verification note: no web checks were possible this round (search budget used up); the eXp and Real facts come from the round's market survey.
- **Compliance design:** Securities: anything given to holders is subject to the antifraud rules (Rule 10b-5) whatever exemption is used. The room runs under counsel's disclosure controls, says the same thing to every holder at the same time, and closes transfer windows around each release so no holder trades on information others don't have [COUNSEL]. Where Reg CF or Reg A applies, the room feeds the required annual report (Form C-AR, or 1-K and 1-SA) rather than replacing it. Reg Z: the page shows platform-level figures only. No member's loans, volume or terms appear, and no unit figure is computed from them, so it can't create a comp proxy. GLBA: no NPI, enforced by role grants. Copy: no earnings claims, no projections, no 'commission'; the recruit version is labelled delayed and counsel-cleared. Antitrust: aggregate seat economics only. It never shows any member's prices to borrowers, comp, or which territories they serve.
- **Moat:** Each month of signed, unrestated history adds credibility that can't be faked later, and a competitor starting now can't backfill a track record. Because the books include the data-charter line, every month without a lead sold becomes visible proof that a large company's owners would never let it publish.
- **Reviews:** keep 6/10: The 'delayed, counsel-cleared version on the package page' shown to recruits, alongside the stake pitch, is offering material for the units. That likely counts as general solicitation, which would end a 506(b) exemption. Under Reg CF, communications outside the funding portal are limited to tombstone-style notices (Rule 204). Showing the books to subscribers who are not holders also extends 10b-5 exposure to non-holders who may later buy. Redesign: Show financials only to holders and to verified offerees inside the counsel-approved offering channel: a 506(c) data room with accredited-investor verification, or the Reg CF portal. The public recruiting page gets the data-charter lines only, with no unit or financial figures. / keep 4/10: From knowledge. Open-book management, co-op member reporting and startup investor updates are long-standing practices, so the reporting is not new. Showing a seat-dollar waterfall to individual loan-officer holders is new in mortgage technology. Securities antifraud exposure from regular disclosures to thousands of holders is serious. Redesign: Make it a quarterly, counsel-reviewed holder report that feeds the Reg CF or Reg A annual filings, with the data-charter line included. Hold the monthly live dashboard until disclosure controls exist. / keep 5/10: Credible for recruiting, but expensive and risky early. A quarterly agreed-upon-procedures check by an independent CPA costs tens of thousands a year. Publishing burn and runway to customer-holders hurts fundraising and helps competitors, and every release adds Rule 10b-5 disclosure-control work and transfer blackouts. It saves the loan officer no time and earns no revenue. Redesign: Start with a counsel-cleared annual report: a seat-dollar waterfall, cohort renewal, the data-charter line and the risk panel. Add quarterly updates and the CPA agreed-upon procedures once revenue covers the cost. Use the report required under Reg CF or Reg A where one applies.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 12 database firewall), https://www.jamilacademy.com/blog/exp-realty-review, https://beangroup.com/agent-guides/exp-revenue-share-explained/, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1495932/000110465926019145/expi-20251231x10k.htm, https://s205.q4cdn.com/544743641/files/doc_news/The-Real-Brokerage-Inc--Announces-Fourth-Quarter-and-Full-Year-2025-Financial-Results-2026.pdf, https://support.getbonzo.com/hc/en-us/articles/21542576428946-Integrations-LOS, https://aitoolsbakery.com/blog/structurely-pricing-2026/, https://www.sec.gov/resources-small-businesses/exempt-offerings/regulation-crowdfunding (Form C-AR; not opened this session)


## Coverage Grants: unserved borrower demand pays existing members' way into new states (round 3, high impact, ~3 weeks)

**When LoanM8 and other front ends keep telling borrowers in a state 'no licensed loan officer here yet', that first-party count opens a published grant round. Members (or incoming recruits) who qualify on objective criteria get subscription credits toward the cost of adding that state license and a sponsor there. A lottery breaks ties, and the 'tell me when' borrowers who opted in hear when someone is licensed.**

- **Problem:** The network grows only as fast as its license coverage, and the plan's recruiting map depended on NMLS and RETR data that their terms forbid using for solicitation or AI. Meanwhile the front end already sees real demand it can't serve: lib/routing.ts tells a borrower in an unlicensed state the honest status and stops. Adding a state costs an MLO pre-licensing education for the state's component, fees, a background check, sponsorship there and weeks of waiting, with no guarantee of business. Large companies send their LOs into new states by assignment; independents rarely go. There is no compliant way to turn unserved demand into new coverage, and no recruiting signal the platform is allowed to use.
- **How it works:** DATA: first-party counts only. Routing results with state 'unlicensed' or 'no nameable MLO' from every front end on the platform, deduplicated by hashed session, plus 'tell me when someone's licensed here' opt-ins (email, or text only with written consent through the D3 gate). There's no tract, ZIP or demographic field, just the state, and optionally a metro chosen by the borrower. TRIGGER: a state crosses a published threshold of unserved requests over 90 days, and at least one sponsoring company on the platform holds, or will obtain, a company license there. WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES: (1) Opens a grant round with published criteria: a member in good standing (no open Two-Key economic action), a clean NMLS record, a seat paid at least N months (or a recruit at signup), and a sponsor licensed in that state that agrees to sponsor them. (2) Picks among qualifiers by verifiable lottery, never by production. (3) Pays the grant as subscription credits on the payer-of-record invoice, released in stages as milestones are met: education done, license issued, 6 paid months after licensing. The credits are never clawed back for low loan count. (4) Moves the state's routing status from 'open' to 'in progress' (Move-In Week's in-transit status), so M8 tells borrowers the honest status. (5) When the license issues, notifies opted-in borrowers once, with nothing of value attached and no lead transferred: they come back through the front door like anyone else. WHAT THE MLO DOES: applies in a minute, follows the licensing checklist in the app, and gets the state in their routing. BORROWER: is told the truth while waiting, then told once when a licensed MLO exists.
- **Technologies:** Routing telemetry from lib/routing.ts and middleware (x-lm8-route) aggregated to state counts, Hashed-session deduplication with no IP retention beyond the existing C4 hashed-IP limits, Grant-round state machine with a commit-reveal lottery, Stripe credit notes on the payer-of-record invoice, Licensing-milestone checklist (Licensed Hands live license clock), D3/D4 send gate for the one-time 'licensed here now' notice
- **Why it's new:** Recruit Radar in the plan mined RETR and NMLS, which their terms bar for solicitation, AI and rules engines (per the survey's reading of RETR ToS 2026-09-17 and NMLS Consumer Access and B2B terms). The survey suggested demand-side recruiting from routing gaps as a concept; this idea turns it into a mechanism with objective selection, staged subscription credits and an honest borrower loop. Large companies assign LOs to states. Real estate revenue-share programs reward recruiting people, not opening coverage. I found no mortgage platform that funds license expansion from first-party unserved demand. Verification note: state licensing costs were not checked this round (search budget used up); take fee figures from the NMLS fee schedule before publishing.
- **Compliance design:** State comp rules: grants are credits on the subscription invoice of whoever pays the seat. If the sponsor pays, the sponsor gets the credit and may pass value on only through its own Reg Z-compliant plan; the platform never pays cash to an MLO [COUNSEL: WA, OR, AZ, CA and TX payee rules]. Reg Z: no grant term depends on loans, volume or terms in the new state, and the 6-month milestone is paid tenure, not production. RESPA §8: the borrower notice carries nothing of value and no referral; it never names a provider in return for anything; borrowers choose. TCPA: the notice goes only to people who asked, by email or by consented text through the D3 gate, once. Fair lending: demand is counted per state (or a metro the borrower picked), never by tract or demographic; the threshold rule is the same for every state, and the fair-lending check runs on the threshold logic. Antitrust: grants never assign exclusive territories outside the existing choice-first territory policy. Copy: no scarcity or urgency ('open area' is policy language); no claims about business volume in the new state. NMLS: the license record is used only to confirm eligibility, which is the permitted use.
- **Moat:** Every new front end and every borrower turned away adds to a demand map only this network has, because it comes from its own routing, not bought data. Each grant adds coverage, which reduces future 'no one here' answers and draws more borrowers to the front ends. That loop compounds and a single-company competitor can't copy it without a multi-sponsor network.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: Risks: (1) Taking 'tell me when someone's licensed here' sign-ins from residents of states where nobody is licensed may count as soliciting or advertising mortgage services in that state, or as lead generation. Some states require a license for that, so the wording must stay away from loans. (2) When the MLO pays for their own seat, a credit to them is value from a non-sponsor, which may break state rules on who may pay an MLO. (3) Notifying the waitlist once a single MLO is licensed directs those borrowers to that one provider. Redesign: Word the waitlist as 'notify me when LoanM8 is available in my state'. Do not discuss loans and do not name an MLO. Get a counsel review for each state. Send grant credits only to the sponsor as payer of record. The notification goes to the front door with choice-first routing and never names the grantee. / keep 5/10: From knowledge; NMLS fees not checked. Large companies assign loan officers to states, and real estate revenue-share programs reward recruiting people. I know of no platform that funds license expansion from its own count of unserved routing requests, using objective criteria and a lottery. It is new. Comp-payee rules in WA, OR, AZ, CA and TX may block value reaching the MLO, and per-state demand is thin early on. Redesign: Log 'no licensed MLO here' routing events and 'tell me when' opt-ins now (cheap). Open grant rounds only after counsel confirms that subscription credits to the payer of record are allowed. / keep 5/10: The demand signal is first-party and free, which is good. But LoanM8's traffic today won't cross meaningful state thresholds soon. The real barrier to a new state is usually the sponsor's company license (bond, net worth, state fees, compliance) rather than the individual's education and fees, so subscription credits for the individual are small next to it. Payee rules on who may pay an MLO still need counsel. Redesign: Now: count unserved demand per state, show members a coverage page, and run the 'tell me when' waitlist with a single honest notice. Add staged grants as subscription credits once a state crosses the threshold and a sponsor commits to that state.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md section 4c (lib/routing.ts, x-lm8-route, choose vs assign states), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (Move-In Week, idea 11 Relay), https://retr.app/terms, https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/policies/NMLS%20Document%20Library/Consumer%20Access%20Terms%20of%20Use.pdf, https://mortgage.nationwidelicensingsystem.org/about/Documents/NMLS%20B2B%20ACCESS%20AGREEMENTv4_20.pdf, https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/


## Exit Clause X-ray: the recruit's current employment contract read on their own device before they move (round 3, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Before a recruit gives notice, they open their current employment agreement, comp plan and offer letter in a browser-only reader. It flags every non-compete, non-solicit, book-ownership, clawback and forfeiture clause, lays each against a counsel-maintained state table, and turns the result into D2 import holds and a list of questions for their attorney. Nothing is uploaded.**

- **Problem:** The biggest fear that keeps an LO at a large company isn't the comp plan; it's being sued on the way out. The repo's research already cites the pattern: Griffin Funding v. former West Capital LOs over diverted leads, Citizens v. SoFi over talent pipelines, Stockton Mortgage over a Gmail account, a former Union Home exec losing on a non-compete, and a CCM non-solicitation enforcement matter. Recruits don't know which of their clauses matter in their state. California voids most non-competes (B&P 16600 and 16600.5). Washington caps them by earnings (RCW 49.62). Oregon requires notice and salary thresholds (ORS 653.295). Customer non-solicits are often enforceable everywhere. A platform that recruits without this exposes the recruit to suits and itself to tortious-interference claims.
- **How it works:** DATA: PDFs or DOCX files the recruit already has, processed entirely in the browser (the same network-blocked pattern as Zero-Upload Book Check). A local clause extractor (deterministic patterns plus a small on-device model through WebGPU or WASM) finds non-compete, non-solicit (customers, employees, referral partners), confidentiality and trade-secret definitions, who owns the database and book, forfeiture-for-competition, clawbacks of draws, sign-on or comp, notice periods and garden leave, choice of law and venue, and arbitration. TRIGGER: the recruit opens the X-ray from the package page or the Honest Twin Trial. WHAT THE SYSTEM DOES: (1) Shows each clause with its page and line, and next to it the counsel-maintained state table row for the recruit's work state and the contract's chosen law (with a last-reviewed date), phrased as 'questions to ask your attorney', never a conclusion. (2) Builds a lawful-exit checklist: don't take company borrower files or LOS exports, since GLBA NPI belongs to the employer or sponsor; give the required notice; don't solicit before leaving; keep personal-sphere contacts separate. (3) Feeds D2 when they later import: the list sources the contract covers are pre-set to 'on hold' and can be released only by the recruit's attestation that their attorney cleared them. Rule-breaking imports (an LOS export tagged with the old company) are refused. (4) Offers a directory of independent employment attorneys in the recruit's state; the platform takes no fees and no referral payment. WHAT THE RECRUIT DOES: reads a plain summary of their own contract in 10 minutes and takes a one-page question list to a lawyer. The platform never sees the document or the summary unless the recruit chooses to share the question list with their attorney.
- **Technologies:** Browser-only document parsing (pdf.js, mammoth) with a Content-Security-Policy that blocks network calls on that route, On-device clause classification (WebGPU or WASM small model) plus deterministic regex patterns, Counsel-maintained, effective-dated state restrictive-covenant table (same versioning as the D3 rules table), D2 import provenance and hold flags (lib/contacts/attestation.ts), Signed local export of the question list (PDF) generated in the browser, Attorney directory with an attestation that no fees flow
- **Why it's new:** Recruiting platforms and large companies give recruits a pitch and leave the exit risk to them. Legal-tech contract reviewers exist for lawyers and enterprises, but none is built for an LO's move, none ties its findings to the import provenance holds that stop a recruit from sending to a list they may not use, and none runs without uploading the contract. It complements the Mobility Protocol (the agreement going forward) and the Lift-Out Guard (team moves) by covering the contract the recruit is leaving. Verification note: the state statutes are cited from model knowledge (CA B&P 16600.5 effective 2024; WA RCW 49.62 thresholds; ORS 653.295; the FTC non-compete rule set aside in Ryan LLC v. FTC, 2024). No web check was possible this round, so counsel must confirm the table before launch, including any 2025–2026 Washington or Oregon amendments.
- **Compliance design:** Unauthorized practice of law: the tool extracts and quotes clauses and shows a counsel-reviewed state table as questions, never advice or an enforceability conclusion, and every screen says 'not legal advice; ask an attorney' [COUNSEL: UPL rules in each state]. Tortious interference: the platform never encourages a breach; the checklist tells the recruit to honor notice and non-solicit terms until their attorney says otherwise, and the attorney-cleared release is logged. GLBA: the checklist forbids taking employer NPI, and D2 refuses LOS-sourced imports from the old company; borrower files stay with the sponsor. Privacy: the document never leaves the device, enforced by CSP and tested in CI with a network-call assertion. RESPA and state comp: the attorney directory carries no payments either way; if counsel approves a flat legal-consult benefit, it is a subscription credit on the payer-of-record invoice and not contingent on joining or on loans [COUNSEL]. Copy: no claims that a clause is unenforceable and no claims about likely outcomes.
- **Moat:** The counsel-maintained clause library and state table get better with every review cycle (built from the platform's own patterns and counsel's work, never from uploaded contracts). Recruits who leave cleanly bring fewer lawsuits, which makes sponsors more willing to take recruits from this network. A large company, which writes these clauses, will never build a tool that helps its own LOs read them.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: The highest risk is unauthorized practice of law: applying a state enforceability table to a specific person's clauses is the core of legal advice, even when phrased as questions. Some states take that view of legal tools sold to consumers. A recruiting platform supplying it also invites tortious-interference claims from the old employer. On-device processing removes the data risk. Redesign: Limit the tool to extracting and quoting clauses, plus general, counsel-written explainers that are not mapped to the user's clauses or state. Leave the per-state analysis to the attorney. Keep the import holds and the no-LOS-export rule. / keep 4/10: Partly refuted, from knowledge. AI contract-review tools are widespread (Spellbook, LegalOn, plus consumer tools), and recruiting firms informally review non-competes. Running entirely on the device and feeding D2 import holds is a new combination. Unauthorized-practice-of-law and tortious-interference risk is high, and a small on-device model's extraction accuracy is doubtful. Redesign: Cut it to a browser-only clause finder that quotes the clauses and produces the attorney question list, with D2 holds set by the recruit's own attestation. Skip state enforceability tables unless counsel maintains them. / keep 6/10: Each recruit uses it once, but it can matter for recruiting conversion, and it feeds the D2 holds. Extracting clauses with an on-device small model is unreliable. Keyword and heading patterns that quote the clause are cheaper and safer. The state table maintained by counsel is the real cost, plus the UPL framing. The statutes (CA B&P 16600.5, RCW 49.62, ORS 653.295, Ryan v. FTC) are from memory and must be confirmed. Redesign: Version 1: a browser-only, pattern-based clause finder, state table rows for the 5 licensed states, an attorney question list, and the D2 import holds. Add the WebGPU model only if the patterns miss too often.
- **Sources:** /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (poaching and non-solicit sources; Zero-Upload Book Check), https://www.housingwire.com/articles/griffin-funding-alleges-west-capital-lending-former-los-diverted-borrower-leads/, https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/citizens-sues-sofi-as-fights-over-talent-pipelines-persist, https://www.housingwire.com/articles/stockton-mortgage-gmail-lawsuit-2/, https://www.mpamag.com/us/news/general/ex-union-home-mortgage-exec-loses-in-court-over-non-compete-clause/528921, https://www.housingwire.com/articles/ccm-enforcement-action-non-solicitation/, https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/who-owns-client-data-only-the-contract-knows, https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/codes_displaySection.xhtml?lawCode=BPC&sectionNum=16600.5 (not opened this session)


## Advice Receipt with a Do-Nothing Baseline and a 12-Month Look-Back (round 3, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Every lifecycle recommendation an MLO or M8 makes (refinance, HELOC, recast, HECM, keep your loan) produces a signed receipt the borrower keeps. It lists every alternative weighed, always including 'do nothing', the priced numbers, the loan officer's pay on each option or a parity attestation, and every conflict. Twelve months later the system privately checks whether the borrower actually came out ahead.**

- **Problem:** Borrowers can't tell whether a loan officer's suggestion served them or the officer. Nothing in mortgage resembles the broker-dealer best-interest record. The anti-steering safe harbor (Reg Z 1026.36(e)) only covers presenting options at origination; it leaves no borrower-facing record and doesn't cover open-end products, recasts or 'stay put'. After closing, every competitor's lifecycle automation pushes toward a transaction. An MLO who wants to be the trusted advisor has no way to prove it, and a sponsor's compliance team has no evidence that lifecycle outreach isn't steering or churning.
- **How it works:** TRIGGER: any workflow that ends in a recommendation or option set: wave ready, idea 5's equity planner, the stay-put planner, the windfall desk, churn guard overrides, or the MLO's own 'send options' button. SYSTEM: it builds the receipt automatically from what was already computed. (a) The borrower's goal in their own words, from M8 or the MLO's note. (b) The options considered, always including 'change nothing', each with numbers only from priced results (rate, APR, points, closing cost, monthly payment, total interest over the borrower's stated horizon, break-even). (c) The safe-harbor triplet where 1026.36(e) applies (lowest rate, lowest points and fees, no risky features). (d) The MLO's pay on each option, as a dollar figure or the sponsor's parity attestation (the comp plan registry already holds the plan). (e) Conflicts: affiliated providers, the product the MLO can't originate and who can. (f) Which assumptions drive the result. It's signed by the tenant key, versioned, and delivered to the borrower's portal as a PDF and a verifiable credential. The borrower can tap 'this doesn't match what I was told', which opens a complaint radar item. LOOK-BACK: at 12 months, with the borrower's opt-in at receipt time, the system re-reads their verified loan facts (idea 4, uploaded statement) and compares the chosen path against the do-nothing path, actual against projected. The result goes to the borrower and to that MLO only, as a learning record, never ranked publicly. The sponsor sees aggregate counts (how many receipts, the share where the chosen option underperformed do-nothing by more than X). MLO: one review screen before sending, with nothing to type twice. BORROWER: a receipt they can show a spouse, CPA or another lender's loan officer (Second Look).
- **Technologies:** Tenant signing keys and W3C VC 2.0 or SD-JWT receipts (the same rail as the signed quote credential idea in the survey), lib/pricing build and canonical input hash so every number traces to a priced run, Comp plan registry for pay-per-option or a parity attestation, Verified loan facts (idea 4) for the 12-month look-back, Complaint radar intake from the receipt's dispute button, Neon append-only receipts table, with witnessed ledger hashing for tamper evidence
- **Why it's new:** Today: 'quote ledger' (taken) logs quotes. 'Sunlight receipts' (taken) explains why a message was sent and which consent was used. 'Churn guard' (taken) blocks refinances with no benefit. None of them records the advice itself: the alternatives weighed (including do nothing), the pay on each option, and a later check on whether the borrower actually benefited. Securities has Reg BI records and insurance has best-interest annuity rules (NAIC model 275 in many states), but mortgage has neither, and no mortgage CRM or LOS I know of produces a borrower-held advice record (from training knowledge; this session couldn't search to confirm). The look-back is new: it measures advice quality from the borrower's own subsequent loan facts.
- **Compliance design:** Reg Z: numbers only from priced results, with APR and the not-a-commitment line on every priced option; 1026.36(e) options where it applies. The receipt states it isn't a commitment to lend or a lock. [COUNSEL: whether a signed receipt could be read as a commitment, and Reg Z advertising triggers in the receipt.] Loan officer pay: the receipt discloses pay or parity, it never makes pay depend on which option the borrower picks, and the look-back result never feeds pay, rewards, ranking or ownership units (comp firewall). Disclosing pay amounts needs sponsor approval [COUNSEL]. Fair lending: look-back aggregates are run through the fair-lending commons to catch any group that systematically gets worse advice, and the results only add review and training. Privacy and GLBA: the look-back needs the borrower's opt-in, receipts sit in the borrower's vault on the retention clock, and the dispute button routes to complaint SLAs. TCPA: the receipt is a transactional document delivered in the portal, not a marketing message. Copy: no 'best-interest' claim the platform can't back. The public phrasing is 'we write down every option we considered, including leaving your loan alone' [COUNSEL on 'fiduciary' wording, which must not be used].
- **Moat:** The records build up: an MLO with 400 receipts and look-backs has documented advice no competitor can produce. Network aggregates (de-identified, opt-in) become the first public evidence of lifecycle advice quality in mortgage, which regulators and sponsors will want. It's credible only on a platform where nobody's pay or stake depends on which option wins, so it's structurally hard for lender-owned or lead-vendor CRMs to copy.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound and protective. Risks: a signed written recommendation may be judged under the fiduciary duty some states impose on brokers (California treats mortgage brokers as fiduciaries), and it raises exposure if a dispute follows. A parity attestation is false whenever lender-paid compensation actually differs by creditor, which Reg Z allows, and that difference is exactly what the anti-steering rule (1026.36(e)) targets. Looking back by group needs demographic data the platform does not store. Redesign: Disclose real compensation by option when it differs, and allow a parity attestation only when the comp plan registry proves it. Run fair-lending look-backs only on the sponsor's HMDA data inside the sponsor's tenant. Have counsel review the receipt wording for commitment risk and for states that impose fiduciary duties. / keep 5/10: Partly anticipated, from knowledge. Net tangible benefit worksheets (state NTB laws, VA and FHA streamline NTB) and Encompass's anti-steering safe-harbor disclosure already record refinance benefit and the options offered. A borrower-held receipt with a 'change nothing' baseline, disclosure of pay or parity, and a 12-month check of the actual outcome goes beyond those and looks new. Sponsors may resist disclosing pay amounts. Redesign: Generate it from the existing anti-steering and NTB data, with 'do nothing' always included. Make pay disclosure parity-attestation only by default. Add the look-back later as opt-in. / keep 6/10: It's cheap when it's generated from results already computed. The do-nothing baseline and the receipt fit the anti-steering posture. Practical problems. Pricing is a daily manual pull for fixed scenarios, so most receipts will lack priced numbers for every option. Per-lender broker compensation differs, and sponsors may resist disclosing pay per option. The 12-month look-back needs a borrower upload of their statement, so few will complete. Redesign: Generate the receipt automatically wherever an option set is shown, with the do-nothing baseline and a parity attestation from the sponsor instead of dollar amounts. Make the look-back opt-in and build it later.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/ (Reg Z 1026.36(d) and (e) anti-steering safe harbor; not reopened this session), https://www.sec.gov/rules-regulations/2019/06/regulation-best-interest (Reg BI, as the analog; not reopened), https://content.naic.org/sites/default/files/model-law-275.pdf (NAIC suitability in annuity transactions model, best-interest analog; unverified), Segment survey: open-end HELOCs fall outside 1026.36(d), so a parity attestation is needed (homeowner lifecycle survey), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 2.4 (comp firewall)


## Servicer Handoff Watch (round 3, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**When a past client's loan is transferred to a new servicer, the system catches it from the goodbye or hello letter the borrower forwards, or from the borrower's own MERS ServicerID lookup. It checks the new servicer against known-good contact routes, starts the 60-day RESPA grace clock, and walks the borrower through autopay, escrow and the insurance mortgagee clause. It also warns against transfer scams, and it never pitches a refinance.**

- **Problem:** Servicing transfers are when borrowers are most confused and most exposed. Autopay breaks, the insurer still pays the old servicer, escrow balances show up late, and fraudsters send fake 'your loan has been sold, pay here' letters. It's also when the new servicer's retention desk starts calling with its own refinance offers. The originating MLO, the person the borrower actually trusts, usually never learns it happened. RESPA gives borrowers protections (notice timing, a 60-day window in which a payment sent to the old servicer can't be treated as late) that few borrowers know about.
- **How it works:** DATA: (1) Letters the borrower forwards to their M8 address or uploads (the idea 4 upload path), which M8 parses for the old and new servicer, the effective date, the new loan number and the payment address. (2) A one-tap guided MERS ServicerID lookup the borrower runs themselves (MERS's free public servicer lookup), never scraped by the platform. (3) Counterparty atlas (taken) known-good phone numbers and payment addresses for each servicer. (4) A per-wholesaler note, private to each MLO, of broker non-solicitation terms in their own broker agreement. TRIGGERS: a forwarded letter; a borrower question; a Today 'Year one' check at 90 days after closing that asks 'did you get a transfer letter?' (early transfers are common). SYSTEM: it verifies that the payment address and phone in the letter match the atlas anchor for that servicer, or else flags 'don't pay yet, call the number on your old statement'. It starts the clock (transfer date + 60 days) and checks that both notices arrived in the required windows. It generates the borrower's checklist: re-enroll autopay, tell the insurer the new mortgagee clause, confirm escrow transferred, watch the first statement. If a late fee appears within the 60-day window, it drafts the borrower's notice of error (Reg X 1024.35) for them to send. MLO: a Today card 'Servicer changed for [client]: service call suggested', with a service-only script, and a quarterly view of how many past clients changed servicers (with the counterparty atlas). The MLO's private note on the wholesaler's non-solicitation terms reminds them what their own agreement says. BORROWER: a 'your loan moved' page with the clock, the verified contacts and the checklist.
- **Technologies:** M8 inbound email parsing (Resend inbound or a similar route) and document extraction, Borrower-run MERS ServicerID lookup link, Counterparty atlas anchors for servicer contacts and payment addresses, QStash timers for the 60-day window and first-statement check, Notice-of-error letter drafting (same rail as the PMI Exit Concierge), Send gate for any outbound service message
- **Why it's new:** Today: servicers send the notices and then retain or recapture the borrower; the survey notes servicer recapture is lender-centric and broker MLOs have no visibility. 'Hold for you' (taken) waits in a servicer's phone queue; 'counterparty atlas' (taken) keeps the verified routes this idea reuses; 'year one map' (taken) schedules the purchase year; 'refund radar' (taken) chases money owed. None of them detects the transfer, checks the letter against known-good anchors, runs the RESPA 60-day clock and turns it into a service touch from the originating MLO. The survey's 'Servicer Watch' opening sketched transfer tracking; this version adds fraud verification against the atlas, the statutory clock and the error-notice workflow. Verification note: the RESPA section 6 rules (12 USC 2605; 12 CFR 1024.33: notices generally at least 15 days before and within 15 days after the transfer; the 60-day no-late-fee period) are high confidence from regulation text, but ecfr.gov was blocked this session, so re-check exact wording.
- **Compliance design:** RESPA and Reg X: the borrower sends their own notices of error. The MLO isn't the servicer and never claims to act for the borrower without a signed third-party authorization the borrower gives the servicer [COUNSEL]. Non-solicitation: the MLO's own broker agreement terms are surfaced to that MLO only, never shared across companies (avoiding the antitrust problem that sank 'Lender Pulse'). TCPA: the transfer touch is service-only, a refinance mention turns it into marketing, which is a separate play under the send gate, and it's suppressed by churn guard. GLBA: forwarded letters are nonpublic personal information in the borrower's vault, read-audited. Fraud: 'don't pay' warnings are only ever 'verify with the number on your previous statement'. The system never gives a payment address itself unless it matches the atlas anchor. HPPA: no credit data; the signal is a borrower-forwarded document. Fair lending: every client with a transfer gets the same treatment.
- **Moat:** The atlas and the transfer-pattern data get better with every member's clients: which servicers send which letter formats, which payment addresses are genuine, and how long escrow transfers take. A network of MLOs sees more transfers than any single broker, and because the platform sells no leads and owns no servicer, borrowers have a reason to forward their letters to it.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound. Wholesale broker agreements often bar the broker from soliciting refinances after a transfer, and a 'service call' can turn into one. The text assumes the private note handles this, but the gate should enforce it. Redesign: Mark the transfer touch as service-only, with a hard block on refinance content for the period in the wholesaler's early-payoff and non-solicit clause, entered as a dated rule for each wholesaler. / keep 5/10: From knowledge. Servicers send the RESPA notices and recapture the borrower, and I know of no broker-side tool that detects a transfer from the borrower's forwarded letter, checks it against verified anchors, and runs the 60-day clock. It is new as a combination and cheap given the counterparty atlas. It depends on borrowers forwarding their letters. Redesign: Build it as a small vault workflow: parse the letter, check it against the atlas, then show the checklist and the 60-day clock. Use the same upload path as Verified Loan Facts. / keep 6/10: Wholesale-brokered loans often transfer servicing early, payment-redirection fraud is real, and the RESPA 60-day window (12 CFR 1024.33) is a clear service touch. Weaknesses. Borrowers rarely forward letters unprompted. The MERS ServicerID lookup is borrower-run, and the atlas of known-good anchors needs upkeep. The loan officer often already knows the investor and the servicing-released status from the file, which is a better trigger. Redesign: Trigger from the file's servicing-released or investor data plus the 90-day Year One prompt. Offer forwarding and MERS lookup as fallbacks. Version 1: a check of the payment address against the atlas, the 60-day clock and the borrower checklist. Add error-notice drafting later.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/2605 (RESPA section 6, servicing transfers; not reopened this session), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1024/subpart-C/section-1024.33 (Reg X 1024.33; host blocked this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1024/35/ (notice of error; not reopened), https://www.mers-servicerid.org/sis/ (MERS ServicerID public lookup; unverified), Segment survey opening 'Servicer Watch' and players Rocket/Mr. Cooper recapture (homeowner lifecycle survey)


## Supervision Desk: risk-sampled sponsor review of every MLO and AI conversation, with a Reg B discouragement sentinel (round 3, high impact, ~6 weeks)

**The sponsoring brokerage's compliance officer gets a daily queue of conversations picked by risk-based sampling from texts, M8 chats, M8 calls and logged MLO calls. Each one is pre-flagged for discouragement, steering, promises and rate talk, and every reviewer decision is kept as the brokerage's supervision evidence.**

- **Problem:** Sponsors answer to state examiners for what their MLOs say, but they almost never review one-to-one communications. They review ads, if anything. Reg B 12 CFR 1002.4(b) bars oral or written statements that would discourage a reasonable person, on a prohibited basis, from applying. That reaches pre-application talk: the Seventh Circuit's 2024 Townstone decision held that ECOA covers prospective applicants. Discouragement lives in exactly the texts and calls nobody samples ('that area is tough', 'with your accent', 'at your age, a 30-year?'). Every AI-drafted message a loan officer approves becomes that loan officer's own statement. Small brokers have no tool that turns 'we supervise our originators' into evidence. Securities firms learned the cost of unsupervised and off-channel messaging the hard way, through SEC and CFTC recordkeeping fines.
- **How it works:** Data: the D4 conversation store, M8 chat transcripts (already redacted by lib/m8/redact), M8 call transcripts, the loan officer's call notes, and an 'on-channel coverage' signal. That signal counts borrowers who said 'I texted your cell' or whose file went quiet on the business line while the LOS showed activity. Trigger: a nightly sampler picks conversations with risk-weighted stratified sampling. Weights come from the sentinel's flags, new MLOs (first 90 days), new plays, M8 guard near-misses, complaint-radar hits, and a fixed random floor (for example 2% per MLO per week) so review never depends only on what the classifier caught. The sentinel is a rule lexicon plus a classifier. It flags discouragement phrases tied to protected bases (national origin, age, familial status, marital status, receipt of public assistance, disability), steering ('you'd do better with product X' without priced options), promises ('you'll be approved', 'locked in'), rate talk outside a priced result, and fee waivers. What the system does: shows each item in a review card with the flag, the exact sentence, the rule cited and a three-way decision (no issue, coach, escalate). Escalation opens a remediation task. The sponsor can also push a corrected follow-up to the borrower, only through the D3 send gate. What the compliance officer and MLO see: the reviewer sees a 15-minute daily queue. The MLO sees only coaching notes on their own messages, never a score that affects pay (Reg Z 1026.36(d) and the comp firewall). What the borrower experiences: nothing directly. If a discouraging statement was made, they get a corrected, neutral message inviting them to apply, so the remedy reaches the person harmed. Outputs: a supervision log kept on the strictest retention period (Reg B 1002.12(b) 25 months; Reg Z 1026.25 3 years for LO comp records; state books-and-records rules from the D3-style table) and a quarterly 'supervision coverage' report: sampled per MLO, reviewed, findings, time to remediate.
- **Technologies:** Stratified risk-based sampling with a random floor, Rule lexicon plus fine-tuned text classifier over redacted transcripts, Existing lib/m8/guard.ts and redact pipeline, Speech-to-text transcripts from M8 voice (C15/C16), Neon append-only review log, Role-based access (sponsor compliance role), D3 send gate for corrective messages
- **Why it's new:** Mortgage CRMs (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert, Jungo) sell compliance as consent checkboxes and ad approval. None that I found publicly advertises sampled supervisory review of one-to-one human and AI conversations with a discouragement classifier (inference from vendor marketing; not re-verified this session because web search and fetch were blocked). Securities firms have this through archiving-and-review vendors built for FINRA Rule 3110 and SEC recordkeeping (Smarsh and Global Relay are the category), but those vendors aren't mortgage-aware: they know nothing about Reg B prohibited bases, licensed states, priced-result rate rules or the send gate. It differs from items already taken. 'Ad desk' pre-clears marketing. 'Paired tester lab' tests M8 itself with matched testers. 'Complaint radar' finds complaints. 'Exam room' gives examiners scoped access. This is the sponsor's own ongoing supervision of human originators plus AI, in every channel, before anyone complains.
- **Compliance design:** Reg B 1002.4(b) discouragement and ECOA prospective-applicant reach (Townstone, 7th Cir. 2024; the CFPB's later move to vacate its own settlement doesn't change the statute or private and state enforcement) [COUNSEL]. Fair lending: the sentinel flags statements; it never infers anyone's protected characteristic and never feeds any ranking model. Flags are about the speaker's words, not the borrower. GLBA and the Safeguards Rule: reviewers see redacted transcripts under role-based, need-to-know access, and every read goes to the audit log. Reg Z 1026.36(d): review findings never adjust compensation inside the platform. The comp firewall's database role can't read the review tables. TCPA: corrective follow-ups go through the D3 gate like any message, and a stop still wins. Wiretap and recording: review covers only calls already recorded with the all-party notice from D3, never an unrecorded call. State law: retention period follows the strictest applicable rule [COUNSEL]. Employment: coaching notes are supervision, not an automated employment decision. No ranking of MLOs for discipline is produced automatically. No lead is sold, and no review data leaves the sponsor's tenant.
- **Moat:** The sentinel's rule set and classifier improve with every reviewer decision across every sponsor. Labels are shared as de-identified rule refinements and counsel-approved examples, never transcripts. A brokerage that has two years of supervision logs on the platform has an exam history it can't take to a competitor without losing continuity, and examiners who see the same report format from many brokers learn to trust it.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: Strong. Employee-monitoring notice laws (New York Civil Rights Law 52-c, Connecticut, Delaware) need written notice to monitored loan officers. A corrective message to a borrower should be drafted with counsel, because it admits a problem. Redesign: Add a monitoring acknowledgment at onboarding for every state. Route corrective messages through counsel-approved templates. / drop 5/10: Largely refuted, from knowledge. Communications surveillance with lexicons and sampled review queues is a mature category: Smarsh and Global Relay, which have mortgage customers, and speech-analytics QA such as CallMiner, Verint, Observe.AI and Level AI, which lenders use with compliance and fair-lending phrase detection. A Reg B lexicon and coverage of M8 conversations are small increments. Sponsors still need it, but it isn't new. Redesign: Build a thin version on D4 and M8 transcripts: a random floor plus guard near-miss sampling and a three-way decision, and export to the sponsor's archiving vendor where one exists. Don't present the classifier as new. / keep 6/10: It's something sponsors can pay for, and supervising AI and human messages is a real duty. A small broker's compliance officer, often part-time or outsourced, may not do 15 minutes a day, and early message volume is low. A trained classifier for discouragement isn't needed at first; a lexicon plus random sampling covers it. Townstone and the CFPB's later moves weren't re-verified. Redesign: Version 1: a random floor plus lexicon flags, a weekly queue sized to the sponsor's chosen review rate, a three-way decision, and the retention log. Sell it as a sponsor add-on. Add the classifier when there are enough labeled reviews.
- **Sources:** https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/4/ (Reg B 1002.4(b) discouragement; not opened this session), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/12/ (Reg B record retention, 25 months; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/25/ (Reg Z record retention; not opened), https://media.ca7.uscourts.gov/ (CFPB v. Townstone Financial, 7th Cir. 2024, prospective applicants; from training knowledge, not opened), https://www.sec.gov/newsroom/press-releases (SEC off-channel communications recordkeeping actions 2021-2024; from training knowledge, not opened), https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/3110 (FINRA 3110 supervision model; not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D4 conversation store; C4 redaction and guard), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 2


## Living Policy Manual: every sentence of the brokerage's written compliance program compiled to the control that enforces it (round 3, high impact, ~7 weeks)

**The brokerage's written programs (Safeguards WISP, AML program, Red Flags program, TCPA and telemarketing policy, fair lending, vendor management, record retention) are generated from the platform's real settings. Each policy sentence links to the control that enforces it and the evidence that it ran, a drift alarm fires when a setting contradicts the written policy, and the annual reports regulators expect are drafted from live evidence.**

- **Problem:** The most common exam finding in small-company compliance is not a missing policy. It's 'the policy says one thing and practice does another', or 'the policy is stale'. Small brokers buy policy templates, file them and never connect them to how their CRM, texting and AI actually behave. The documents they must keep are written: the FTC Safeguards Rule requires a written information security program, access limited to need-to-know (16 CFR 314.4(c)(1)), and a Qualified Individual who reports in writing to the board at least annually (314.4(i)). Nonbank lenders and originators need an AML program with a compliance officer, training and independent testing (31 CFR 1029.210), and the Red Flags Rule needs a written identity-theft program (16 CFR 681). Once a brokerage adds AI voice and automated plays, its written TCPA policy goes stale the day a play changes.
- **How it works:** Data: the tenant's actual configuration. That includes D3 state rules and caps in force, the plays enabled and their auto-send status, voice on or off, retention settings, the role and access grants from D1, the vendor list (Twilio, Resend, Neon, the voice and model providers, LOS connectors), MFA enforcement, the audit log, and the training completions recorded in the platform. Trigger: any setting change, plus a nightly comparison. What the system does: (1) Policy compilation. A counsel-approved template library is written as structured clauses. Each clause carries a 'control binding': a machine-checkable assertion such as 'marketing texts are capped at 1 per day' bound to rules.ts, 'only owner and team lead can export contacts' bound to the role table, or 'AI calls open with the AI disclosure' bound to the voice script and the post-call check. The brokerage's manual is rendered from the clauses that match its configuration, so the document describes what the system really does. (2) Drift alarms. If an admin changes a setting so a bound clause becomes false (turns on auto-send for a play the policy says requires approval, adds a user without MFA, extends retention), the change is either blocked until the policy is amended by someone with policy authority, or allowed with a logged exception and an expiry date. (3) Annual cycle. It drafts the Safeguards Qualified Individual report (risk assessment changes, control results, incidents, vendor oversight), the AML independent-test evidence pack (it doesn't perform the test; it gives the tester sampled evidence), the Red Flags annual review and a training roster. What the compliance officer sees: a manual where every paragraph shows a green or red 'enforced by' chip that links to the control and its last evidence. What the MLO sees: one-page 'what our policy means for you' cards and the training they owe. What the borrower sees: nothing, except that the public privacy and texting terms are generated from the same clauses, so they can't drift either.
- **Technologies:** Policy-as-code clause library (structured YAML clauses with assertions), Configuration snapshot and diff engine over the tenant's settings, Pre-change policy check (block or exception with expiry), Document rendering to PDF and DOCX with per-clause evidence links, Neon audit log and the existing role model, Counsel-signed template versions with effective dates
- **Why it's new:** Policy-template vendors and compliance consultancies sell documents. GRC tools (Vanta, Drata) bind SOC 2 controls to cloud evidence but know nothing about TCPA caps, Reg B, AML for loan companies or Red Flags. Mortgage CRMs don't generate the brokerage's written programs at all (inference from vendor marketing, not re-verified this session because web access was blocked). The taken 'law as tests' idea turns rules into replayable test cases for the platform's own decisions. 'Independent control attestation' is a CPA report on the platform's controls. 'Shared security desk' runs Safeguards operations as a network. The survey's 'AI governance binder' documents models. This is different: it is the brokerage's own written program, generated from and continuously reconciled with its configuration, with change-time blocking when a setting would make the written policy false.
- **Compliance design:** The templates are counsel-approved and versioned. The platform never presents itself as giving legal advice to the brokerage, and every manual says the brokerage adopts it and stays responsible for it [COUNSEL]. Safeguards Rule: supports 314.4(c)(1) need-to-know access, 314.4(f) service-provider oversight and 314.4(i) annual reporting, and the drafts are marked for the Qualified Individual's review and signature. AML (31 CFR 1029.210): the platform doesn't make SAR decisions and doesn't perform the independent test; it packages evidence for the person who does. TCPA and state mini-TCPAs: the written telemarketing policy is rendered from the D3 rules actually enforced, so the policy can never promise less than the gate does. GLBA Reg P: the privacy notice clauses are bound to actual sharing settings, so 'we don't share for third-party marketing' (no lead is ever sold) is checked, not just claimed. Comp: policy clauses about LO compensation link to the comp plan registry but the engine never reads loan data.
- **Moat:** Every brokerage that adopts a clause improves the library: exam findings members report (anonymized) become new clauses and new bindings. A manual tied to two years of evidence is hard to replace with a template. Examiners who see the same 'enforced by' format across many brokers start asking non-members why they can't show it.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Templates approved by counsel and adopted by the brokerage avoid practising law. The risk is that auto-generated public privacy and texting terms change whenever settings change, which could alter the Reg P notice without the required revised-notice process. Redesign: Treat changes that affect the privacy notice as needing a revised Reg P notice (1016.8) and the sponsor's sign-off before publishing. / drop 4/10: Partly refuted, from knowledge. Vanta and Drata generate policies from templates and continuously test controls with drift alerts. Ncontracts sells policy management to mortgage companies. Binding policy text to TCPA and D3 settings and generating the privacy notice from actual sharing settings are mortgage-specific increments. Redesign: Keep one high-value piece: render the public texting terms, the privacy notice and the telemarketing policy from the live D3 and sharing configuration, so they can't drift. Leave the full written program to existing GRC tools. / keep 5/10: Drift alarms on settings the platform controls are cheap and valuable. A brokerage's WISP, AML and Red Flags programs cover systems the platform can't see (LOS, email, laptops), though, so 'enforced by' chips would cover a fraction and could mislead. A counsel-approved template library for all of them is a large fixed legal cost. Redesign: Start with what the platform fully controls: the telemarketing and TCPA policy, the texting terms and the privacy-notice sharing statements, rendered from the D3 config, with drift blocking. Add WISP and AML sections later, clearly marked 'platform controls only'.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (FTC Safeguards Rule 314.4(c)(1), (f), (i); host blocked this session, cited from regulation text known well), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-31/subtitle-B/chapter-X/part-1029 (AML program for loan or finance companies, 1029.210; not opened), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-F/part-681 (Red Flags Rule; not opened), https://www.consumerfinance.gov/compliance/supervision-examinations/ (CFPB Supervision and Examination Manual, compliance management system module; not opened), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1016 (Reg P privacy notices; not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D1 roles and audit log; D3 rules table)


## Tripwire Records: decoy contacts seeded into every export, vendor feed and tenant to prove no lead is ever sold and catch any leak (round 3, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Every export, connector and vendor gets its own unique decoy records, using phone numbers, emails and mailing addresses the platform controls. If a decoy is ever called, texted, emailed, mailed or offered back in a purchased list, the platform knows a leak happened, exactly which export or vendor it came from, and when.**

- **Problem:** 'No lead is ever sold' is the platform's central promise, but today it is only a promise. A brokerage can't prove to a borrower, a regulator or a recruit that its data never left through a careless export, a compromised assistant account, a vendor or a departing loan officer. Borrowers who get spam after applying blame the last company they talked to. Under the Safeguards Rule a brokerage must detect unauthorized access and, for 500 or more consumers, notify the FTC within 30 days of discovery. Most small brokers would never discover a quiet exfiltration at all. The taken 'list fingerprint' idea detects a bought list when it comes in. Nothing detects data going out.
- **How it works:** Data: a pool of decoy identities owned by the platform. These are Twilio numbers that only ever receive and record, catch-all mailboxes on a platform-owned domain, and mail drops (a PO box or virtual mailbox), each combined with a synthetic name and plausible but non-existent loan details. Trigger: whenever the platform produces data that leaves its control (a CSV export, a Book Deed export, a connector sync to an LOS or Zapier, a vendor feed such as a DNC-scrub upload, a sponsor-handoff copy, a model or voice vendor's context), it inserts one to three decoys unique to that event. The mapping from decoy to event is stored in a sealed table only the security role can read. What the system does: the decoy numbers and inboxes are monitored. Any inbound contact (a call, a text, an email, a piece of mail, a reverse-lookup hit) is matched to its seeding event and opens a security incident. The incident starts the Safeguards incident-response steps, identifies the export, the user who ran it and the vendor involved, and starts the breach-assessment clock. Decoys are excluded from Today, from the send gate, from every metric, from the fair-lending and ranking models, and from any count shown to anyone. They are tagged at the schema level and filtered by a database view so no feature can see them by accident.
