A decoy inside a list someone later tries to import into the network (caught by list fingerprint) proves that list was built from a network export. What the MLO sees: a line on each export receipt, 'this export carries tripwires', which also deters casual misuse. What the brokerage sees: a quarterly 'no-sale proof' report: number of seeded events, decoys contacted (ideally zero), and any incidents with root cause. What the borrower sees: a public statement, backed by the quarterly report, that the network seeds and monitors its data and how many leaks it found.
- **Technologies:** Platform-owned receive-only Twilio numbers with call and SMS logging, Catch-all mailboxes on a platform domain, plus a virtual mailbox for postal mail, Sealed seeding map (KMS-encrypted, security-role only), Schema-level decoy flag with filtered database views, Incident workflow tied to the Safeguards response plan, Integration with the taken list-fingerprint check on import
- **Why it's new:** Seed names are an old, standard control in the direct-mail list-rental industry: list owners plant names to catch unauthorized reuse. Security teams use honeytokens (canary tokens) to detect breaches. I found no mortgage CRM or LOS that seeds exports and vendor feeds per event to attribute leaks of borrower data, or that publishes a 'no-sale proof' (inference from vendor marketing; not re-verified because web search was exhausted this session). It differs from the taken ideas. 'List fingerprint' is privacy-preserving overlap detection on lists coming in. 'Witnessed ledger' makes consent records tamper-evident. 'Retention clock and crypto shred' destroys data on time. 'Shared security desk' runs Safeguards operations. Tripwires are an active detection control on data going out, with per-event attribution.
- **Compliance design:** No real person is ever a decoy. Every decoy contact point is owned by the platform, so no consumer is contacted, tracked or deceived. Decoys are excluded from HMDA-style analysis, fair-lending tests, send-gate counts and any metric or report shown to MLOs or examiners, except the security report, so the data stays accurate for regulators. Decoy records don't masquerade as borrowers to any lender for underwriting: they are never placed in a loan file sent to a lender for a decision, only in CRM-level exports and marketing-type feeds. LOS syncs carry decoys only where the connector partner agrees in writing [COUNSEL]. GLBA and the Safeguards Rule: supports 314.4(c)(8) monitoring and logging of authorized users' activity and the 314.4(h) incident response plan; a tripwire hit starts the breach assessment, including the 30-day FTC notice for 500 or more consumers if applicable [COUNSEL]. Vendor contracts: the data processing addendum tells vendors that feeds carry tracking records, which avoids any claim of deception and strengthens no-resale terms. TCPA: decoy numbers never send outbound messages. Inbound calls to them are recorded with notice where required by all-party consent law (D3's table).
- **Moat:** The more exports, vendors and tenants there are, the more decoys are in circulation and the more likely any leak is caught and attributed, so detection improves with network size. Over time the quarterly reports become a public track record no competitor can produce retroactively. A data vendor that owns its CRM (the MMI and Bonzo pattern) can't credibly offer this, because its business includes reusing data.
- **Reviews:** keep 7/10: Seeding the loan officer's own Book Deed export, which they lawfully take when they leave, means the loan officer's legitimate later use at a new CRM or sponsor shows up as a 'leak'. That chills the promised portability and amounts to monitoring a departed member. Decoys in sponsor or LOS copies can also corrupt records the sponsor must keep accurate, such as HMDA data and books and records. Redesign: Put no tripwires in exports to the person who owns the data, or flag only contact from a third-party reseller, never the loan officer's own use. Put decoys in LOS or sponsor copies only with written agreement, tagged in the data, and outside any reportable dataset. / keep 6/10: From knowledge. The technique is old: list-rental seed names, and honeytokens such as Thinkst Canary (canary.tools). I know of no mortgage CRM or LOS that seeds each export and vendor feed with its own decoys to attribute leaks and publish a no-sale proof. Applying it here is materially better because it gives evidence for 'no lead is ever sold'. It is cheap to build. Redesign: Start with decoys on CSV and Book Deed exports and vendor feeds, using platform-owned Twilio numbers and mailboxes, filtered by a database view. Keep them out of LOS syncs unless the partner agrees in writing. / keep 6/10: Cheap for email: a catch-all domain gives unlimited unique addresses. Phone numbers cost about $1 a month each (from memory), so unique decoys per event don't scale and need a pool. A few PO boxes cost little. Putting decoys into LOS syncs would pollute partner systems and needs partners' written agreement, so leave it out. Detection is rare, but the deterrent and the 'no-sale proof' are good recruiting and trust signals. Redesign: Version 1: unique email decoys in every CSV export, Book Deed and vendor feed, with a small rotating pool of receive-only numbers attributed by combination. No decoys in LOS syncs. Publish a quarterly count.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314 (Safeguards Rule monitoring 314.4(c)(8), incident response 314.4(h), FTC notice for 500 or more consumers; not opened this session), https://canarytokens.org (honeytoken practice; not opened), https://thedma.org (direct-marketing list rental seed-name practice; general industry knowledge, not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (no lead is ever sold; Twilio, Resend, Neon switches), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (Book Deed export)


## Reviewer Proof: hidden check drafts in the one-tap approval queue that show whether human review is real (round 3, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Now and then the approval queue includes a draft with a known, planted problem, such as a rate without its APR, a promise, an out-of-state property or a message to someone who said stop. These are never sent. Whether the MLO catches them builds a measured review record, and a play qualifies for auto-send only when that record is strong.**

- **Problem:** The plan's safety rests on 'every AI draft goes to the loan officer for one-tap approval until counsel approves auto-send'. One-tap approval decays into rubber-stamping: people approve what they're shown, especially on a phone between showings. If a regulator, a court or a plaintiff's lawyer asks 'what did your human review actually catch?', today nobody can answer. Rubber-stamped AI output is still the loan officer's and the sponsor's statement under UDAAP and the Reg Z advertising rules. State AI laws (Colorado's AI Act, California's ADMT rules) lean on meaningful human review. And the taken 'delegation envelopes' idea widens an AI's authority 'only with evidence', but nothing measures the human's side of that evidence.
- **How it works:** Data: the approval queue (M8 drafts of texts and emails, call follow-ups), the gate's decision for each draft, and the review interactions (time on card, scroll to the end, edits, approve or reject, reason). Trigger: at a low, unannounced rate (for example 1 in 40 drafts, never more than one a day per MLO), the queue includes a check draft built from a library of planted faults. Examples: a rate without APR or without the not-a-commitment line, 'you're approved', a property in an unlicensed state, a marketing message during quiet hours, a message to a contact whose stop is on file, or a discouraging phrase. The check draft looks like the real drafts around it but is addressed to a decoy record (see Tripwire Records) and is physically unable to send: the D3 gate refuses it on a hard flag no matter what the MLO taps. What the system does: if the MLO rejects or edits out the fault, it logs a catch and shows a short 'nice catch' note with the rule. If they approve it, the platform blocks it, shows a brief explanation of what was planted and why it matters, and logs a miss. It also computes, per MLO and per play, a review record: catch rate with confidence interval, median time on card and edit rate. Auto-send eligibility for a play (already gated by counsel approval) also requires a minimum demonstrated catch rate over a minimum sample, and auto-send is withdrawn if the record falls. For the sponsor: a review-quality report per MLO for the supervision file. For examiners: aggregate evidence that human review is tested. What the MLO experiences: an occasional extra card and a learning moment, never a penalty to pay. What the borrower experiences: nothing. No check draft ever reaches a real person.
- **Technologies:** Fault-injection library of planted drafts versioned with the guard rules, Hard-flag refusal in lib/send-gate/gate.ts for check drafts, Decoy recipients from Tripwire Records, Review telemetry (dwell, edits, decisions) in the audit log, Binomial confidence intervals for catch rates, Auto-send eligibility rule tied to the delegation and play settings
- **Why it's new:** Attention checks and seeded errors are a known way to measure human review quality: in survey research, in content moderation QA, and in airport screening's threat image projection (TIP), where X-ray screeners are shown fictional threats to measure vigilance. Mortgage CRMs and AI-voice vendors offer 'human in the loop' approval but, as far as public marketing shows, no measurement of whether the human actually reviews (inference; not re-verified because web search was exhausted this session). It differs from the taken ideas. 'Delegation envelopes' limits what each AI agent may do. 'Law as tests' replays rule cases against the platform's decisions. 'Paired tester lab' tests M8 for fair-lending parity. 'Guard commons' shares guard improvements. This measures the human reviewer and makes that measurement a condition of automation.
- **Compliance design:** No check draft can be delivered: the gate refuses it on a hard flag, it is addressed only to platform-owned decoys, and a test asserts that no check draft ever reaches the Twilio or Resend send path. Reg Z 1026.36(d) and the comp firewall: review records never affect pay or ownership units, and the reward engine's role can't read them. Supervision: records go to the sponsor's supervision file as evidence of training and monitoring, not as automated discipline. Any employment action stays a human decision, which avoids automated-employment-decision issues [COUNSEL]. Transparency to MLOs: the platform's terms tell every MLO that the queue contains occasional test drafts, so it is disclosed, not deceptive. The rate is low enough not to burden them. TCPA and the AI rules: strengthens 2.5 of the plan (one-tap approval) by making it measurable. Auto-send still also needs counsel approval of the play.
- **Moat:** The fault library grows from real near-misses across the network: every guard near-miss and every supervision finding becomes a new planted fault (de-identified). Catch-rate baselines across many MLOs make each individual's record meaningful. A brokerage switching away loses its measured review history and its auto-send eligibility evidence.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: Low risk. Planted drafts are disclosed in the terms and physically cannot be sent. Make sure review records are not used, inside or outside the platform, as an automated discipline score that steers pay. Redesign: Keep the hard send block and the CI test. Show sponsors records only as supervision evidence. / keep 5/10: From knowledge. Known analogs are airport threat image projection, gold-standard tasks in content-moderation QA, and attention checks in surveys. I know of no mortgage CRM or voice vendor that measures whether the human in the loop actually reviews. It is new here and strengthens plan 2.5. MLOs may find it irritating or patronizing even though it is disclosed. Redesign: Run it at a very low rate (no more than 1 in 50), disclosed in the terms, and use it only as a condition for turning on auto-send, never in supervision scoring by default. The test that no check draft can reach the send path is mandatory. / keep 6/10: It's cheap to build: fault templates, a hard gate flag and a no-send test. It turns 'human in the loop' into evidence. It only pays off if auto-send for a play is a real goal, and loan officers may find it annoying. The terms of service disclosure handles the risk of deception. Redesign: Ship it with the gate's hard refusal and the CI test as the first step. Use a low rate (1 in 50, at most 1 a day). Tie it to auto-send eligibility only after counsel approves the play.
- **Sources:** https://www.tsa.gov (threat image projection in X-ray screening; general knowledge, not opened this session), https://leg.colorado.gov/bills/sb24-205 (Colorado AI Act, human review; not opened), https://cppa.ca.gov/regulations/ (California ADMT regulations; not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md section 2.5 (one-tap approval until counsel-approved auto-send), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D3 send gate, D4 sending path)


## Accommodation Memory: a borrower's disability and communication accommodations honored by every channel and every AI, and walled off from every ranking (round 3, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**When a borrower asks for an accommodation (no voice calls because they're deaf or hard of hearing, calls through a relay service, larger print or screen-reader-friendly documents, a trusted person on the line, slower pacing, written summaries after every call), it is recorded once in the borrower's words. Every channel, every play, M8 voice and every loan officer in the network honors it. A database firewall guarantees it can never influence who gets contacted or ranked.**

- **Problem:** Disability is a protected class under the Fair Housing Act, which covers residential real estate-related transactions including making loans (42 U.S.C. 3604(f), 3605). AI channels make accommodation failures automatic. M8 voice calling a deaf borrower, a relay call dropped as a robocall, a PDF a screen reader can't read, or a fast AI voice for someone with a cognitive disability each turn a preference into a barrier. Website accessibility lawsuits under ADA Title III run in the thousands each year and keep growing. CRMs store 'notes' that nobody's automation reads. The opposite risk is just as real: once disability information is in a CRM, any scoring model that can see it, or a proxy for it, becomes a fair-lending problem.
- **How it works:** Data: an 'accommodations' record per borrower, captured only when the borrower states it. It can come through the consent passport, M8 chat ('Do you have any preferences for how we communicate, for example text only, relay calls, large print, or someone joining you?'), a loan officer recording it with the borrower's words, or a relay call. It uses a controlled vocabulary of actions (no voice; prefer text or email; accept relay or TRS calls; documents in accessible format; trusted contact may join with the borrower's authorization; slower speech rate; written recap after calls; extra time on deadlines where the law allows) plus an optional free-text note. Trigger: every send, call and document generation. What the system does: (1) The D3 gate reads accommodations as hard channel rules, for example 'no voice' blocks M8 calls and dialer tasks and Today shows 'text instead'. (2) M8 voice adapts: slower TTS rate, a written recap texted afterward with consent, and recognition of relay-operator calls so they're never treated as spam or hung up on. (3) Documents and links sent from the platform are produced to WCAG 2.2 AA, with tagged PDFs and plain-language summaries, and an automated accessibility check runs in CI on every borrower-facing template and page. (4) Firewall: accommodations live in a separate table that the Today ranker, play targeting, scoring models, fair-lending datasets and the reward engine can't read (Postgres role grants plus a CI privilege test, the same pattern as the comp firewall). A schema lint forbids derived features from it. The send gate is the only reader, and it can only restrict or reshape a channel, never decide whether to contact. (5) Portability: with the borrower's authorization, the accommodation travels with them under homecoming routing and mobility, so the borrower never has to re-explain. What the MLO sees: an icon on the contact and a plain instruction ('Text only; send a written recap after calls'), not a diagnosis. What the borrower experiences: being asked once, respectfully, and never having to repeat themselves.
- **Technologies:** Separate accommodations table with Postgres role isolation and CI privilege test, D3 send-gate channel rules, Speech-rate control and post-call recap in the Google TTS voice path (C15), Relay and TRS call recognition in the Twilio inbound flow, Tagged-PDF generation and axe-core or Playwright accessibility checks in CI, Consent passport capture UI
- **Why it's new:** Mortgage CRMs keep free-text notes and opt-out flags. I found no CRM or AI-voice vendor that turns a stated accommodation into an enforced channel rule across every automation, or that isolates disability data from scoring by design (inference from vendor marketing; not re-verified because web search was exhausted this session). Accessibility scanners check websites, not borrower-specific communication paths. It differs from the taken ideas. 'Language commons' is multilingual M8. 'Protective pause' is an exploitation and coercion circuit breaker. 'Fair lending commons' and 'paired tester lab' test models for disparate impact. 'Sunlight receipts' explains why a message was sent. This is an accommodation record the borrower states, enforced everywhere and firewalled from every decision.
- **Compliance design:** Fair Housing Act: disability is protected (42 U.S.C. 3604(f), 3605), so the record is used only to accommodate, never to select, deprioritize or price. The firewall makes that technical, and the fair-lending check confirms no model input correlates with it [COUNSEL: reasonable-accommodation duties in lending]. ECOA and Reg B: the platform never asks about disability as a condition of service, never infers it, and the question is framed as communication preferences offered to everyone. Privacy: it is sensitive data under several state privacy laws and may be health data under Washington's My Health My Data Act, so it is captured only by the borrower's own statement, has its own consent and is deletable on request; GLBA NPI controls also apply [COUNSEL]. TCPA: relay calls are handled per FCC relay rules, and accommodations never widen consent; they only narrow or reshape contact. ADA Title III and WCAG 2.2 AA for borrower-facing surfaces [COUNSEL: Title III coverage of the platform versus the brokerage]. AI disclosure still precedes every AI interaction, in an accessible form (spoken and texted).
- **Moat:** Accommodation handling improves with network use: relay-call patterns, document templates and the M8 voice settings that work get refined across tenants without sharing anyone's data. A borrower's stated preference following them across the network is something only a network with shared routing can do. Brokerages get a defensible disability-compliance posture they can show examiners and fair-housing testers.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Sound. The optional free-text note will collect diagnoses ('I'm deaf'). That is health data under Washington's My Health My Data Act, with its separate consent and private right of action, and sensitive data under the CCPA. Redesign: Drop the free-text field, or have the guard strip condition words so only functional preferences are stored. / keep 5/10: From knowledge. CRMs store communication preferences, and accommodation records exist in health and government CRMs. Enforcing a stated accommodation as a hard D3 channel rule, firewalled from ranking, is a sound design but only modestly new. It overlaps the accommodation-on-request part of Equal Ear. Redesign: Merge with Equal Ear's accommodation mode into one preference table read only by the send gate and the voice runtime, using a functional vocabulary. Add a WCAG check on borrower templates in CI. / keep 7/10: Low cost (a table, gate rules, role grants plus a CI privilege test) and it reduces fair-housing and ADA risk. Frequency is low, but it's needed and makes a good showing to examiners. The WCAG 2.2 AA template checks are a modest addition to CI. The relay-call handling needs care with voice vendors. Redesign: Merge in Equal Ear's relay-friendly mode, signal-triggered fallback and read-back. Ship the functional vocabulary, gate enforcement and firewall first, and add portability later.
- **Sources:** https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/3605 (Fair Housing Act, residential real estate-related transactions; not opened this session), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/3604 (3604(f) handicap; not opened), https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/ (WCAG 2.2, W3C Recommendation 2023; not opened), https://www.fcc.gov/consumers/guides/telecommunications-relay-service-trs (relay services; not opened), https://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=19.373 (Washington My Health My Data Act; not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D3 gate; C15 voice), /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (comp firewall role-isolation pattern, idea 12)


## Possession-Proven Consent: every written consent bound to proof that the person holding that phone gave it, the exact words they saw, and the seller they named (round 3, high impact, ~4 weeks)

**A consent counts only when the platform can show three things: the device on that number confirmed it (a texted YES reply, or carrier number verification where available), the exact hashed disclosure text and seller names displayed at that moment, and which entities and channels it covers. The send gate then only allows senders inside that scope.**

- **Problem:** The weakest link in TCPA defense is not whether a form had a checkbox. It's whether the person who owns the phone actually gave consent. Typed-in numbers, a friend's number, lead-generator forms and reassigned numbers are what the 2026 wave of mortgage TCPA suits is made of. Even after the one-to-one rule was vacated, consent still has to run to the seller calling. In a multi-sponsor network that raises a hard question: does consent given to 'Loan Officer X at Sponsor A' cover the same loan officer after a move to Sponsor B, or an M8 call placed for a team? D3 already treats a texted yes as written consent, but it stores neither the exact text shown nor a scope that a sender can be checked against.
- **How it works:** Data: at capture (consent passport, M8 chat, a web form, an open-house QR code), the platform records a SHA-256 hash and archived copy of the exact disclosure text and its version, the named sellers (the loan officer, the sponsor and the platform as service provider), the channels and purposes, the timestamp, the user agent, and the possession proof. Possession proof, in order of strength: (a) a carrier number-verification check, where the borrower's mobile network supports it, run while they are on mobile data (for example through the GSMA Open Gateway Number Verification API or Twilio Verify's Silent Network Auth); (b) a texted confirmation: the borrower replies YES to a confirmation text naming the seller and purpose (double opt-in); (c) a one-time code typed back. A web-form consent without (a), (b) or (c) is stored as 'unconfirmed' and the gate treats it as relationship-only. Trigger: every send. What the system does: the D3 gate adds a scope check. The sender's entity (sponsor and loan officer) must be among the named sellers, the channel and purpose must match, and the number must have no Reassigned Numbers Database hit dated after the possession proof. If the loan officer moves sponsors (the mobility protocol), consents that named the old sponsor are flagged 'scope changed'. M8 then asks, by an allowed channel, for a fresh confirmation naming the new sponsor; it never assumes. What the MLO sees: a consent badge with strength (carrier-verified, text-confirmed, code-confirmed, unconfirmed) and the exact wording the borrower saw, one tap away. What the borrower experiences: one extra tap or reply at signup ('Reply YES to let Loan Officer X at Sponsor A text you about your mortgage; reply STOP anytime') and the certainty that nobody else can sign them up.
- **Technologies:** Carrier number verification (GSMA Open Gateway Number Verification / Twilio Verify Silent Network Auth) where supported, Double opt-in by SMS through the D4 Twilio path, Content-addressed archive of disclosure text versions (SHA-256), Seller-scope model in the consent store, FCC Reassigned Numbers Database query (via a Somos-authorized path), D3 gate scope check
- **Why it's new:** Consent-certificate vendors (ActiveProspect TrustedForm, Verisk Jornaya) prove that a form was submitted, using session replay that now draws CIPA wiretap claims. Neither proves that the number's holder submitted it, and the certificate belongs to the lead seller or buyer. Mortgage CRMs record opt-in flags. The consent passport (plan 5.3) lets borrowers see and change their consent. The survey's 'consent receipts as credentials' makes a portable receipt. None binds consent to possession proof plus an exact seller scope that the send gate enforces at each send and that reacts to a sponsor change. This was not re-verified this session: web search was exhausted and fetches to vendor hosts were blocked, so carrier API availability per carrier is a MED-confidence recollection to confirm.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA prior express written consent (47 CFR 64.1200(f)(9)) needs a signed written agreement naming the seller. An E-SIGN-compliant texted YES works, and the exact disclosure text is preserved. Marketing consent is never a condition of service. Consent runs only to the named sellers, which fits the post-vacatur rule that consent still runs to the seller, without relying on the vacated one-to-one rule. Revocation: any reasonable means still wins immediately on every channel (D4). Possession proof never makes a stop harder. FCC Order 26-67's exclusive-method option isn't used. Reassigned numbers: the 64.1200(m) safe harbor requires a database check. The gate makes a reassignment after the proof date void the consent. Privacy: carrier number verification returns a match or no-match only, with no location or subscriber data stored. It is used only with the borrower's action on their own device, and it is a GLBA service provider under the Safeguards vendor list [COUNSEL: carrier API terms and state privacy law]. Mobility: never transfer consent to a new sponsor by assumption. RESPA and lead rules: the platform never resells or shares consents. Scope can't be widened to add a third-party seller, so 'no lead is ever sold' is structural.
- **Moat:** Every consent captured this way becomes a stronger asset the longer the network runs, and possession-proven, scope-bound records are exactly what a member defense or TCPA insurance program (taken: evidence priced coverage) can price on. Competitors can copy the double opt-in, but their incumbent books are full of unconfirmed form consents they can't retroactively prove. A network that starts clean stays ahead.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: Strong, and it lowers risk. After a sponsor change, the 'fresh confirmation naming the new sponsor' is itself a solicitation for the new entity. Its channel must have its own lawful basis, and it may hit the old employer's non-solicit. Redesign: Ask for re-confirmation for a new sponsor only by email or through the Lawful Lanes basis, never by text under the old consent, and only within the recruit's attested non-solicit status. / drop 5/10: Largely refuted, from knowledge. Double opt-in SMS confirmation is standard and is required by many carriers and platforms. Carrier possession checks ship as Twilio Verify Silent Network Auth and GSMA Open Gateway Number Verification. Consent certificates that record the exact disclosure and named seller are what TrustedForm and Jornaya moved to around the one-to-one rule. Checking seller scope at the gate and on a sponsor change is a useful increment. Redesign: Fold it into the consent passport and D3: require a texted YES or a code for marketing consent, store the hashed disclosure and named sellers, and re-confirm consent on a sponsor change. Don't present it as a standalone product. / keep 8/10: Double opt-in by a texted YES is cheap (one SMS), strong evidence, and fits D4 and the consent passport. Hashing the disclosure version is trivial. Flagging consent when the loan officer changes sponsor is a real portability fix. The carrier number-verification APIs (Twilio Silent Network Auth, GSMA Open Gateway through US carriers) have uncertain US coverage and pricing as of 2026-11 and weren't verified, so treat them as optional. Redesign: Version 1: texted-YES or one-time-code confirmation, exact disclosure hashing, the named-seller scope check in D3, and the flag on sponsor change. Add carrier verification later where coverage is confirmed.
- **Sources:** https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-64/subpart-L/section-64.1200 (PEWC 64.1200(f)(9); reassigned-number safe harbor 64.1200(m); host blocked this session), https://www.kelleydrye.com/viewpoints/blogs/ad-law-access/eleventh-circuit-vacates-tcpa-11-consent-rule (one-to-one rule vacated; repo-sourced), https://www.gsma.com/solutions-and-impact/gsma-open-gateway/ (Number Verification API; not opened, MED confidence on US carrier support), https://www.twilio.com/docs/verify/api/silent-network-auth (Twilio Verify SNA; not opened), https://www.nationalmortgagenews.com/news/mortgage-tcpa-lawsuits-surge-with-11-new-cases (2026 mortgage TCPA suits; from survey), https://www.manatt.com/insights/newsletters/client-alert/fcc-replaces-revoke-all-rule-and-permits-callers-to-designate-an-exclusive-revocation-method (FCC 26-67; from survey), /home/user/RATEM8/PATCHLOG.md (D2 consent log; D3 texted yes as written; D4 opt-out list)


## Leak Scan: continuous crawl of each member brokerage's website and application flow for trackers that send borrower data to ad tech, checked against its own privacy notice (round 3, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**A headless browser visits every member brokerage's public site, rate pages and application flow on a schedule, with a synthetic borrower. It records every third-party request, pixel, session-replay script and chat widget that receives page content or form fields, then compares what it sees with the brokerage's Reg P privacy notice and the platform's no-sale promise, and opens a fix ticket for each mismatch.**

- **Problem:** A brokerage can run Command Center perfectly and still leak borrower data through its own website. Ad pixels, session-replay scripts and third-party chat widgets on application or prequalification pages can send names, emails, loan amounts or page URLs with query strings to ad platforms. Tax-preparation firms were caught sending tax data to Meta's pixel (The Markup, 2022; a congressional report, 2023), and pixel and session-replay suits under CIPA and other wiretap laws are now a litigation industry. For a mortgage broker that is a GLBA problem (nonpublic personal information disclosed to a non-affiliated third party outside what the privacy notice says) and a direct contradiction of 'no lead is ever sold'. Small brokers have no idea what their website builders and marketing agencies installed.
- **How it works:** Data: each member brokerage's list of its own domains and application entry points, entered at onboarding (the brokerage authorizes the scan in writing). Trigger: a weekly crawl, plus a crawl whenever the site's script inventory changes, detected by a lightweight daily fetch. What the system does: Playwright drives a synthetic borrower through the public pages and as far into the prequalification or application flow as the brokerage authorizes, typing synthetic values only (the Tripwire decoys, so any later contact to them is also detected). It records every network request with its destination, the cookies set, and whether any typed value, hashed value (SHA-256 of the email is a common pixel pattern) or sensitive URL parameter appears in a third-party payload. It classifies destinations (advertising, analytics, session replay, chat, CDN) and compares them with the brokerage's Reg P privacy notice categories and opt-out statements, its cookie banner behaviour, and the platform's own rule that borrower-facing Command Center pages use first-party analytics only. Findings are scored: critical (form values or NPI to ad tech), high (session replay on application pages), medium (ad pixels on rate pages with sensitive URLs), low (an undisclosed analytics vendor). What the brokerage sees: a short report with screenshots of the request, plain-English fixes ('remove the session replay script from /apply', 'strip the loan amount from your URL'), a re-scan button, and a dated clean bill when fixed. What the borrower sees: a public 'scanned for trackers' line on the brokerage's privacy page, linked to the last clean date.
- **Technologies:** Playwright headless crawling with network interception, Synthetic borrower identities (Tripwire decoys), Payload matching for raw, URL-encoded and SHA-256-hashed form values, Tracker classification lists (open-source tracker databases), Reg P privacy-notice clause parsing (from the Living Policy Manual clauses), Scheduled jobs and a findings dashboard per tenant
- **Why it's new:** Generic privacy scanners exist: The Markup's Blacklight for anyone, and consent-management and data-mapping vendors for enterprises. None is mortgage-specific: none walks a broker's application flow, checks payloads for loan data, or compares the findings with the brokerage's Reg P notice and a no-sale promise. Mortgage CRMs don't look at the broker's own website at all (inference from vendor marketing; not re-verified because web search was exhausted this session). It differs from the taken ideas. 'Shared security desk' runs Safeguards operations and fraud-domain blocking. 'Sunlight receipts' explains messages. 'List fingerprint' detects bought lists. The survey's 'CIPA-safe AI listener architecture' covers the platform's own voice vendors, not members' websites.
- **Compliance design:** Only domains the brokerage owns and authorizes in writing are scanned. The crawler identifies itself, respects authorization scope, and never submits an application or triggers a real credit pull. It uses synthetic data only, never real borrower data. GLBA and Reg P: findings map to the privacy-notice categories in 12 CFR 1016; the platform flags and explains but the brokerage decides and fixes [COUNSEL]. CIPA and other wiretap laws: the report frames session replay and third-party chat on application pages as risk, without giving the brokerage a legal conclusion. The platform's own pages follow the same rule (Vercel Analytics only, no tracking cookies; CLAUDE.md). No lead is ever sold: the crawler's synthetic data doubles as tripwires. RESPA and ads: scanning is software, not a thing of value tied to referrals, and it's priced in the flat subscription. Results are never used to rank or advertise one brokerage over another.
- **Moat:** The tracker classification and payload patterns improve with every member site scanned. Fixes found on one site (a website builder's default chat widget, a marketing agency's pixel template) are pushed as warnings to every member who uses the same vendor. Over time the network becomes the place where a broker's web stack is known-clean, which is something a lender-owned or data-vendor-owned CRM has no reason to build.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Application flows are often hosted by POS vendors (Floify, Blend, ARIVE) whose terms bar bots. The brokerage cannot authorize automated access to a vendor's domain, which risks the CFAA and a breach of terms. Synthetic prequal entries can also create lead or application records in the LOS, distorting HMDA data and the books. Redesign: Scan vendor-hosted pages only with the vendor's written permission, otherwise only the brokerage's own pages. Stop before any submission that creates a record, or use a vendor sandbox. / drop 4/10: Refuted, from knowledge. ObservePoint already runs scheduled crawls that fill in forms to audit tags. Feroot, Reflectiz and Source Defense, and healthcare pixel scanners that grew after the HHS tracking guidance, already scan form flows for data leaking to third parties. The Markup's Blacklight is free. Mapping findings to Reg P notice categories is a thin mortgage layer. Redesign: Partner with or resell an existing tag-audit scanner and add a Reg P category mapping. Only build an in-house crawl if members ask for it. / keep 6/10: Feasible and cheap with Playwright, which is already used for QA. Pixel and session-replay suits are a live risk for lenders. It's a monthly report rather than a weekly tool, and many broker sites run on vendor templates the broker can't change much. The fixes depend on the website vendor, and walking the application flow needs written authorization each time. Redesign: Run it monthly on public pages and the authorized entry points, using synthetic tripwire data. Report vendor-level findings so one fix can be shared across members on the same website vendor. Consider reusing open-source tracker-detection code.
- **Sources:** https://themarkup.org/pixel-hunt/2022/11/22/tax-filing-websites-have-been-sending-users-financial-information-to-facebook (tax sites and Meta pixel; from training knowledge, not opened this session), https://themarkup.org/blacklight (Blacklight tracker scanner; not opened), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-X/part-1016 (Reg P privacy notices; host blocked), https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/codes_displaySection.xhtml?lawCode=PEN&sectionNum=631 (CIPA 631; not opened), /home/user/RATEM8/CLAUDE.md (Vercel Analytics only, no third-party trackers), /home/user/RATEM8/scripts/qa/ (existing Playwright QA scripts)


## Day Shape: the day built from the loan officer's calendar and each person's legal call window (round 3, high impact, ~5 weeks)

**Each morning Today lays out a time-blocked day. Call blocks go where the most of today's people are inside their own legal window. Calls are hand-dialed one tap at a time with the gate re-checked at each dial, notes are dictated after each call, and a shield holds non-urgent texts during focus time.**

- **Problem:** The plan's Today screen says who to call, but not when. A loan officer covering WA, OR, AZ, CA and TX deals with two to three time zones. Arizona doesn't observe daylight saving time, and Texas spans Central and Mountain. Every recipient also has a different quiet-hours window in the D3 table. So the loan officer guesses, calls people outside their window (D3 blocks those calls), or lets the day get eaten by inbound texts while files wait. Hiya's 2026 survey says 86% of people won't answer an unknown number (vendor survey), so when and how the loan officer calls matters more than how many calls go out. No mortgage CRM builds the loan officer's day from their real calendar and each person's legal window.
- **How it works:** Data: (1) The loan officer's calendar, read-only free/busy only, through Google Calendar freeBusy or Microsoft Graph getSchedule. No event titles are read. (2) D5 Today cards. (3) The D3 rules table: each recipient's zone, quiet hours, caps and Sunday rules. (4) LOS deadlines from the connector: lock expiry, appraisal, clear to close, closing. (5) Each contact's own reply history: the hours when they have actually answered texts or calls before. This is behavior, not a demographic.

Trigger: 6 AM local, and again whenever the calendar changes.

What the system does: it builds a proposed day. Call blocks are placed where the largest number of today's cards fall inside their legal window at the same time. Focus blocks cover files with deadlines in the next 48 hours. Showings and meetings already on the calendar are left alone. It also says what it couldn't fit: 'Two Texas past clients only fit after 5 PM Central. Tomorrow 9 AM?'

Power Hour mode: one card at a time. The gate re-runs decide() at the moment of the tap, so a stop that arrived a minute ago blocks the call. Each call starts only when the loan officer taps it: a tel: link, or a Twilio call that rings the loan officer's phone first and then the contact. There is no list dialing, no predictive or sequential dialing and no number generation. The call is not recorded by default, which avoids all-party-consent and CIPA exposure. After hang-up there is a 20-second dictation in the loan officer's own voice through the existing /api/m8-listen route. It becomes a structured note, any tasks, and a drafted follow-up, which goes to one-tap approval and then through the D4 send path. Where the LOS supports it, a note is written back (ARIVE Zapier 'update lead' or Encompass notes, later).

Interruption Shield: during focus blocks, an inbound text from a contact gets one AI-disclosed acknowledgment, for example: 'This is M8, an AI assistant for [first name]. They're with a client and will reply by 2:30. If this is about a closing or a wire, reply URGENT.' It breaks through at once when the file has a deadline within 48 hours, or the text mentions a wire, closing, appraisal or lock. Wire mentions always route to the loan officer and never get an AI answer.

What the loan officer experiences: one plan, Start Power Hour, and the day stops being reactive.

What the borrower experiences: calls at hours they actually answer, inside the law, and a fast, honest 'got it' when they text.
- **Technologies:** Google Calendar API freeBusy and Microsoft Graph getSchedule (free/busy scopes only), lib/send-gate decide() re-run at the moment of each dial, D3 per-state zone and quiet-hours table, Twilio Voice click-to-call bridge (loan officer leg first), Existing /api/m8-listen Speech-to-Text for post-call dictation, Server-side model with a zod schema for note, task and draft extraction, under lib/m8 redaction and guard, LOS connector (ARIVE Zapier actions; Encompass later) for deadlines and note write-back, QStash for re-planning on calendar change, D5 Today ranking as the input queue
- **Why it's new:** Mortgage CRMs such as Bonzo and Shape advertise power dialers that work through lists, which is volume-first. Calendars sit in separate apps. The AI-voice vendors (Flair's West Capital deployment: 318,000+ dials, about 3.6% answered) optimize dial volume. No product found in the market survey schedules a loan officer's own hand-dialed calls by each recipient's statutory window, the loan officer's real free/busy time, and the recipient's own observed answer hours, with the consent gate re-checked at each tap. That claim is an inference from public marketing in the market survey; this session could not run new searches because the shared search budget was used up. It is also distinct from the plan's voice briefing and stand-up (5.7), which read the screen aloud but don't schedule, and from the taken 'hours back ledger', which measures time saved rather than shaping the day.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: every call starts with a human tap on one specific contact. Nothing dials from a list or generates numbers, which keeps the design outside the autodialer definition in Facebook v. Duguid (2021). Florida's 2023 FTSA amendment narrowed its definition to automated selection and dialing, and one tap per call is designed to avoid that; counsel must confirm for each mini-TCPA. Quiet hours and caps are enforced per recipient zone through D3. Counsel should also weigh the stricter 9 AM–8 PM window, because Connecticut is 9 AM–8 PM.

Recording: off by default. If a loan officer turns it on, D3's all-party-state recording notice plays before anything of substance.

Shield auto-replies: one acknowledgment answering a text the person sent, never marketing. It discloses M8 is an AI (California B&P 17941, Utah) and quotes no rates or terms. A stop still revokes everything at once.

GLBA: calendar access is free/busy only, dictation audio is deleted after transcription, and every read is audited.

Fair lending: ordering uses legal windows, deadlines and the person's own reply hours. It never uses race, ethnicity, tract, language or age, and the fair lending check runs on the scheduler like any other ranking.

RESPA: none.

Licensing: the D3 licensed-state check is re-run per call.
- **Moat:** The schedule improves the longer the loan officer uses it, as reply-hour history builds per contact. Across the network, de-identified answer-hour patterns by state and time of day (no contact identities, no cross-tenant contact data) give a new member a useful plan on day one. Because every call passes the same gate, the network also produces the per-call evidence trail that the litigation-packet and witnessed-ledger ideas need.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: One tap per call keeps it outside Duguid and the amended FTSA. The Interruption Shield's AI auto-reply is a responsive single message, which is fine, but it must not fire on a message that is itself a revocation, and it must not go to a number with an active stop. Redesign: Run every shield reply through the D3 gate and the revocation classifier before sending. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted, from knowledge. AI calendar planners (Motion, Reclaim) build time-blocked days, and compliance dialers (Gryphon, Convoso) enforce each recipient's calling window by time zone. Bonzo and Shape have power dialers. Combining free/busy time, each person's legal window, re-running the gate at every one-tap dial, and dictation after each call is moderately new for a solo loan officer. Redesign: Ship Power Hour (one card at a time, gate re-checked at the tap) first, because it is the compliance core. Add calendar-aware blocks and the Interruption Shield later. / keep 8/10: Daily use, real time saved, cheap data: freeBusy, the D3 rules and Today cards. One-tap dialing keeps it outside autodialer definitions. Reply-hour history will be sparse at first. The interruption-shield auto-reply is an AI text that needs the disclosure and must stay one per thread. Twilio's ring-the-loan-officer-first bridging adds per-minute cost, so tel: links are the default. Redesign: Build it into D5 Today as a 'Plan my day' and Power Hour mode, defaulting to tel: links, recording off. Add the shield and dictation after the core is used.
- **Sources:** https://www.hiya.com/newsroom/press-releases/state-of-the-call-2026-ai-deepfake-voice-calls-hit-1-in-4-americans-as-consumers-say-scammers-are-beating-mobile-network-operators-2-to-1, https://www.housingwire.com/articles/west-capital-flair-ai-voice/, https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/20pdf/19-511_p86b.pdf, https://www.manatt.com/insights/newsletters/tcpa-connect/florida-amends-ftsa, https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-64/subpart-L/section-64.1200, https://developers.google.com/workspace/calendar/api/v3/reference/freebusy/query, https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/graph/api/calendar-getschedule, https://www.goodwinlaw.com/en/insights/publications/2025/02/alerts-practices-dpc-ftec-ai-voice-products-subject-to-california-invasion-of-privacy-claims


## Throughput Governor: marketing volume sized to what the loan officer can actually close on time (round 3, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**When the pipeline is heading past the loan officer's own proven on-time pace, Command Center automatically slows the plays that create new demand. Service and inbound work stays fully on. It offers coverage instead of letting files go late.**

- **Problem:** In a rate dip, a CRM plus AI dialing creates more applications than one loan officer and one processor can close on time. The result is missed locks, blown contract dates, extension fees and angry agents. That costs more hours than it saves, and it causes the 'promised a closing date' complaints the guard is built to prevent. Every CRM and AI-voice vendor surveyed sells more volume. None connects marketing automation to the loan officer's measured capacity.
- **How it works:** Data: the milestone guardian's learned durations for this loan officer (application to clear to close, by loan type), the active pipeline by stage from the LOS connector, lock expirations, the assigned processor or assistant from D1 team records, and the play schedule from 4.7.

What the system computes, weekly and on every new application: an 'on-time pace', the number of new files per week this loan officer has historically taken to closing without a lock extension or a missed contract date. It projects the next 30 days of load against that pace.

Above 100% of pace: plays that create demand (refinance marketing, M8 marketing calls, rate-watch alerts set to marketing) are slowed, never cancelled. People are deferred in the order they were enrolled in the play, not by score. Service plays (milestone status, PMI, escrow, the borrower's own requested capsules) and every inbound channel stay fully on. The speed-to-lead promise on inbound forms changes to what the loan officer can really keep ('a loan officer will call you today by 6 PM' instead of '5 minutes').

What the loan officer sees: one card. For example: 'You're at 118% of your on-time pace. 3 marketing plays slowed; 14 people moved to next week. Options: bring in a same-sponsor backup loan officer for live transfers, send two files to the shared licensed-processor pool, or override (logged).'

What the borrower and agent experience: fewer files that go late, and honest response-time promises.
- **Technologies:** Milestone guardian pace model per loan officer (Postgres window functions over LOS milestone events), LOS connector events (ARIVE Zapier triggers, Floify Custom CRM webhooks, Encompass webhooks later), Play scheduler with a deferral queue (QStash), D3 send gate (the governor only ever removes or defers sends, never adds), Today card plus team-lead view, Fair lending commons test harness on deferral outcomes
- **Why it's new:** The surveyed CRMs and voice vendors (Bonzo, Shape, Total Expert's AI Sales Assistant, Flair, Sela) measure success in dials, leads and appointments, and none publishes a capacity control. Lenders run capacity planning in LOS operations reports, separate from marketing. None of the taken ideas couples demand-creating automation to the loan officer's own measured closing throughput. 'Deal clock' and 'contingency clock' track individual files, and 'wave ready' stages refinance files, but neither throttles marketing. This is an inference from the market survey; it wasn't re-searched this session because the shared search budget was used up.
- **Compliance design:** TCPA: the governor only reduces marketing sends, so exposure goes down. Every send still passes the D3 gate.

Reg Z 1026.36(d): the governor reads no pay data and changes no loan term. It sits outside the comp firewall's tables.

Fair lending and ECOA: deferral order is enrollment time within a play, never a score, geography, tract or any proxy. A quarterly test checks that deferral rates are equal across tracts, with tract used only for testing and never as an input. It runs in the fair lending commons. Inbound applicants are never deprioritized, so nobody is discouraged from applying.

The 'speed to lead' text is changed through copy rules, which forbid timing promises the loan officer can't keep. The guard still blocks closing-date promises.

RESPA: no effect on agents. The same response-time promise goes to every agent.

GLBA: only the loan officer's own tenant data is used. Pace priors for new members are de-identified aggregates with no lender names, which avoids the antitrust problem that sank 'Lender Pulse'.
- **Moat:** Each loan officer's pace model sharpens with every closed file. Network-level, de-identified pace priors by loan type and state (no lender identity, no competitor data) let a new member start governed from week one. It also gives the platform a credible public line no volume vendor can say: 'we never sell you more than you can close'.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: It only reduces outreach. Inbound applicants are never deprioritized, so there is no discouragement risk. No compliance defect found. / keep 6/10: From knowledge. Contact-center dialers pace calls to agent availability, which is an analog. I know of no mortgage CRM or voice vendor that throttles demand-creating plays to the loan officer's measured on-time closing pace. It is new and fits the no-volume-pressure stance. Redesign: Begin with a simple pace estimate from LOS dates and a Today card that slows plays. Run the deferral-equity test in the fair-lending commons before automatic throttling. / keep 5/10: With a few closings a month per loan officer, a learned 'on-time pace' is statistically noisy for a long time. Pipeline-by-stage and lock data from ARIVE through Zapier is uncertain. Loan officers want more volume and will override. The value shows up mainly for teams. Redesign: Let the loan officer or team lead set a capacity number (files per week). When the pipeline passes it, demand-creating plays slow automatically, with an honest speed-to-lead promise. Learn the pace from data only after about 30 closed files.
- **Sources:** https://www.housingwire.com/articles/west-capital-flair-ai-voice/, https://forkast.news/sela-raises-21m-for-voice-ai-agents-that-originate-1b-in-mortgage-loans-monthly/, https://www.totalexpert.com/features/ai-sales-assistant, https://help.floify.com/en/articles/16300950-how-to-create-a-custom-integration, https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/36/, https://www.wsgr.com/en/insights/doj-and-ftc-withdraw-antitrust-guidelines-for-competitor-collaborations.html, /home/user/RATEM8/LoanM8_MLO_Command_Center_Build_Prompt.md (4.6, 4.7)


## Letter Truth: every outstanding pre-approval letter re-checked each night against today's priced rate, taxes and insurance (round 3, medium impact, ~3 weeks)

**Each night Command Center re-runs the payment and DTI math behind every live pre-approval letter at today's priced example. It flags any letter that now overstates the buyer's power before an offer goes in that can't close. The Agent Line's verification answers always reflect the loan officer's current letter.**

- **Problem:** A letter written when rates were a quarter point lower still says $520k three weeks later. The buyer writes an offer, the file goes to underwriting at today's rate, the debt-to-income ratio breaks, and the deal dies or needs an emergency restructure. That costs the loan officer a weekend and the agent relationship. Letter tools (LenderLogix QuickQual, Maxwell FlexLetters, Pre Approve Me) let agents and loan officers generate letters within a maximum the loan officer set. Nothing the market survey found re-checks outstanding letters when pricing moves.
- **How it works:** Data:
- The pre-approval record from the LOS or POS connector: program, the maximum price and loan amount, the assumed rate, taxes and insurance used, the DTI limits the loan officer recorded from AUS findings, and the expiry.
- Today's priced example for that program, from the licensed PPE adapter or the manual ARIVE pull (lib/pricing, dated).
- County property-tax rates from assessor open data, and the HOA from the listing once the letter is tied to an address.
- The insurance figure the borrower provided.

Trigger: nightly, and again whenever a new priced snapshot lands.

What the system does:
- Re-runs the loan officer's own original math at today's priced rate, using lib/pricing amortization and APR.
- If the letter's maximum is now higher than the same assumptions support, it creates a Today card. For example: 'Kim's $520k letter now supports about $497k at today's priced example (dated 11-18, not a quote).'
- The loan officer chooses one: reissue at the supported amount, price Kim's actual scenario, or leave it with a logged reason.
- A letter past its stated expiry is marked expired automatically.

What the agent experiences: when a listing agent verifies the letter on the Agent Line (idea 8), M8 answers only 'valid, reissued on [date] for up to $X' or 'expired'. It never gives the reason, the rate or any income detail.

What the borrower experiences: a message the loan officer approves first, sent through the gate. It explains that rates moved and what that means, with a priced example that carries its APR and the not-a-commitment line, and an offer to talk.
- **Technologies:** LOS or POS connector for pre-approval records (ARIVE Zapier lead and loan triggers), lib/pricing build, APR and amortization against dated snapshots, Licensed PPE adapter (Optimal Blue Loansifter Broker Product and Pricing API or Lender Price) or the manual ARIVE pull, County assessor open data or a property-tax vendor for tax rates, Agent Line verification tools (verify_letter) reading the current letter state, M8 guard (rate only with APR and the not-a-commitment line), QStash nightly job, D3 send gate for the borrower message
- **Why it's new:** QuickQual, FlexLetters and Pre Approve Me produce letters within a range the loan officer approved. Verification still means calling the loan officer, and none advertises re-validating issued letters against moving prices. This comes from earlier repo research; it couldn't be re-searched today because the shared search budget was used up. Among the taken ideas, the Agent Line verifies that a letter is real, and 'pricing coverage planner with float watch' watches floating locked loans. Neither re-checks pre-approved buyers who haven't applied on a property yet. Letter Truth closes that gap and makes the Agent Line's answers current.
- **Compliance design:** Rates: the borrower sees a rate only from a priced result, with its APR and the not-a-commitment line (M8 guard).

The AI never changes a letter. The loan officer decides, and the decision is logged.

ECOA and Reg B [COUNSEL]: reissuing a letter at a lower amount may be a counteroffer or adverse action where the request counts as an application or a preapproval-program request (12 CFR 1002.9; HMDA 12 CFR 1003.2(b)(2)). In a brokered file, the multiple-party notice rule 1002.9(g) decides who sends notice. The card says 'talk to counsel's notice script', and the system attaches the sponsor's approved notice workflow.

Fair lending: every live letter is re-checked the same way, with no selection by person or area.

HPPA and FCRA: no new credit pull. It uses only figures already in the file, and a re-pull would need the borrower's authorization and the credit cost router.

GLBA: agents learn only status, date and amount, under the borrower's logged, revocable sharing authorization from idea 8.

TCPA: the borrower message is a service message about their own pre-approval, sent with consent or by email, and approved by the loan officer.

RESPA: the same verification answers go to every agent.
- **Moat:** Once listing agents learn that a LoanM8-network letter's verification code is current every night, those letters carry more weight in multiple-offer situations for every member loan officer. The trust builds across the network and no single competitor can copy it without the verify line plus nightly pricing. Every overstated letter caught also feeds the pace model with fewer dead deals.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Reissuing a letter at a lower amount can be a counteroffer or adverse action under Reg B and HMDA, which is already flagged. Telling a listing agent the buyer's approved amount discloses the buyer's NPI to the seller's side and needs the buyer's specific direction. The message to the borrower that 'rates moved' encourages action and may be treated as marketing. Redesign: By default the Agent Line confirms only valid or expired, and gives the amount only when the borrower opts in for each letter. Treat the borrower message as marketing unless counsel classifies it as informational. / keep 6/10: From knowledge and earlier repo research. QuickQual, FlexLetters and Pre Approve Me generate letters within limits the loan officer set and don't re-check issued letters against moving prices. Nightly re-checking with an Agent Line answer that is always current looks new. Reissuing at a lower amount may trigger Reg B adverse-action or counteroffer notices, and daily prices depend on the manual pull or L4b. Redesign: Build it after pricing (L4b) exists. Flag affected letters to the loan officer only, with the sponsor's counsel-approved notice workflow attached. Never adjust a letter automatically. / keep 6/10: The problem is real: letters overstate buying power after rates rise. But the platform only knows a letter's inputs (max price, assumed rate, DTI, taxes and insurance) if it issued the letter or the loan officer recorded them, since LOS and POS letter tools don't export them through Zapier. Pricing is a daily manual pull for fixed scenarios, so 'today's priced rate for that program' is example pricing, and nightly checks are pointless when the price changes once a day. The Reg B counteroffer and adverse-action question adds friction on every reissue. Redesign: Cover only letters issued or registered through the Agent Line, with their inputs captured at issue. Re-check whenever a new priced snapshot lands, not nightly, and show the loan officer a card with counsel's notice workflow.
- **Sources:** https://info.lenderlogix.com/quickqual-instant-pre-approval-letters-lenderlogix, https://himaxwell.com/features/flexletters, https://highnote.io/mortgage-pre-approval-letter-agents-guide/, https://digitalmarketplace.optimalblue.com/product-overview, https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/9/, https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1003/2/, https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work-, /home/user/RATEM8/docs/work/command-center-ideas-2026-11-18.md (idea 8, Agent Line)


## Connector Co-op: one integration layer the members own, fixed once for everyone (round 3, high impact, ~8 weeks)

**A shared, member-governed integration layer covers ARIVE, Floify, Encompass, nCino, Blend and the pricing engines. Every incoming event is verified, stored for replay and reconciled nightly. A field change at one vendor is caught by contract tests and fixed once for every tenant. Partner agreements are negotiated as one customer group, never by scraping.**

- **Problem:** Every broker CRM rides the same fragile rails. ARIVE offers only per-loan-officer Zapier with Primary-LO scope. Floify's Custom CRM webhooks need Support approval. Encompass webhooks aren't guaranteed real-time, and ICE auto-deletes subscriptions to endpoints that fail. Blend events are granted case by case. When a vendor changes a field or a subscription silently dies, each loan officer finds out from a borrower asking why they got no update, and spends hours re-keying. Supergood reports that ARIVE's agreement bars vendors from its APIs, which is vendor-reported and unverified, so access is a legal question as well as a technical one. A solo loan officer or a small broker has no leverage to fix any of this.
- **How it works:** Data: a canonical loan and milestone model using MISMO 3.6 names, fed by:
- ARIVE Zapier triggers, with each loan officer's own Pro credentials
- Floify Custom CRM Integration webhooks
- Encompass webhooks, only after ICE partner approval
- nCino webhooks
- Blend event subscriptions
- pricing APIs: Loansifter Broker Product and Pricing, and Lender Price

Ingest:
- Each vendor's signature is verified: HMAC-SHA256 for nCino, the Encompass signature, Blend Basic auth plus heartbeats, and a per-tenant secret URL with rate limiting for unsigned Zapier hooks.
- Every event is stored raw and append-only, so it can be replayed.
- The system re-reads the vendor on a schedule, where the vendor allows it, to catch missed events. Where it doesn't, the loan officer uploads a weekly export that is diffed against the stored events.
- A subscription-health monitor alerts before ICE's auto-delete thresholds are hit.
- One rule from the survey: no event by itself sends a marketing message or changes a pay figure. Events only create Today cards or update status.
- Floify's own borrower texts are detected, so borrowers never get two status texts.

Fix once: recorded fixtures from member sandboxes, using synthetic loans only, run as contract tests every night against each adapter. When ARIVE renames a Zapier field, the test fails, one fix ships, and every tenant is repaired.

Governance: members vote on connector priorities. A public 'Integration Truth' page shows, for each vendor, what Command Center reads and writes, the agreement status, and the data map that goes in the sponsor's FTC Safeguards packet. The co-op negotiates partner terms as one paying customer group of vendors' own users.

What the loan officer sees: a green 'syncs healthy' line on Today, or 'ARIVE missed 2 events overnight, recovered from replay'.

What the borrower experiences: status that's never stale and never duplicated.
- **Technologies:** ARIVE Zapier app (per-loan-officer Pro credentials), Floify Custom CRM Integration webhooks, ICE Encompass Developer Connect webhooks (under ISV terms, later), nCino Mortgage webhooks (HMAC-SHA256), Blend event subscriptions (Basic auth, heartbeats), Optimal Blue Loansifter Broker Product and Pricing API and Lender Price JWT API, MISMO 3.6 canonical names, Append-only raw event store in Neon with row-level security, plus replay workers (QStash), Contract tests with synthetic-loan fixtures in CI, Public integration and data-map page, plus an FTC Safeguards packet generator
- **Why it's new:** This combines four of the market survey's openings into one member-owned operating model: event safety, collective bargaining for API access, the Safeguards packet and the ARIVE-first connector. Incumbents each maintain their own Zapier mappings (Bonzo charges $100 setup plus $25 a month to host its ARIVE integration). Surefire is tied to Encompass. Supergood sells unofficial API layers that work by automating logged-in sessions. No vendor shares integration fixes across competing customers, publishes what each LOS allows, or negotiates as its users. Its owners are vendors, not users. This doesn't repeat the taken 'one entry' (write-once intake), 'lender mail room' or 'lender switch kit': it is the shared plumbing under them.
- **Compliance design:** Vendor terms: no screen-scraping or unofficial APIs. Supergood-style session automation is banned because ICE's Partner Connect terms bar bypassing the platform and circumventing tokens, and Loansifter's license 3.2.1 restricts copying. Encompass connects only after ICE's written approval, with the revenue share budgeted and counsel reviewing the ban on data replication. ARIVE runs on each loan officer's own credentials while a written partner agreement is sought before scaling.

GLBA and the FTC Safeguards Rule (16 CFR 314): per-connector data maps, minimum-necessary fields, encryption, tenant isolation, an audited read log, and the 30-day FTC notice runbook for incidents affecting 500 or more consumers. These go in each sponsor's vendor-oversight file.

TCPA and Reg Z: no LOS event can trigger a marketing send or a pay-related change by itself. Every send passes D3.

Antitrust [COUNSEL]: joint purchasing of software access by customers is generally permissible but needs counsel. The co-op never shares lender pricing, turn times or competitive performance across members, which keeps it clear of the rejected 'Lender Pulse'.

Ownership rewards are unaffected: subscription and tenure only.
- **Moat:** Each new member adds a sandbox fixture, a vote and negotiating weight. Each vendor change that breaks one tenant hardens all of them. Over time the co-op holds the most complete map of what every LOS and POS really allows, plus signed partner agreements, which no single CRM vendor can match without being owned by the users.
- **Reviews:** keep 8/10: Joint negotiation by customers is generally lawful, but it needs counsel. Raw append-only event storage may conflict with ICE's ban on data replication and with Loansifter's copying limits. Redesign: Set retention for each vendor from the terms table, and get written vendor approval before storing raw events. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted, from knowledge. Unified-API vendors (Merge.dev, Paragon, Codat) already maintain integrations once for all customers, with signature checks, replay and health monitoring. Mortgage CRMs each keep their own Zapier mappings (Bonzo charges for hosting its ARIVE integration). What's new is user ownership, publishing what each LOS allows, and negotiating vendor terms as one customer group. The engineering itself is standard. Redesign: Absorb Connector Canary Mesh as its monitoring layer. Present it as the platform's integration architecture plus a public 'Integration Truth' page and collective vendor negotiation, not as a new technology. / keep 7/10: Necessary plumbing. Signed ingest, raw event storage, replay and contract tests pay for themselves on the first silent breakage. Collective bargaining has no leverage at a few dozen seats. ICE partner approval and revenue share are a cost and a delay. ARIVE runs on each loan officer's own credentials until a written agreement exists. Bonzo's ARIVE hosting fee is a vendor-pricing claim from the survey. Redesign: Absorb Connector Canary Mesh (schema fingerprints, quarantine of unverified versions). Build ARIVE and Floify first, with replay and contract tests. Defer the Encompass adapter and any 'negotiate as a group' work until there are paying seats.
- **Sources:** https://support.arive.com/support/solutions/articles/61000312645-how-does-zapier-integration-work-, https://www.arive.com/integration-partners, https://supergood.ai/api-report-card/arive, https://help.floify.com/en/articles/16300950-how-to-create-a-custom-integration, https://help.floify.com/en/articles/15141079-encompass-third-party-recipient-sync, https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/reference/webhook, https://developer.icemortgagetechnology.com/developer-connect/changelog/222-major-release, https://mortgagetech.ice.com/publicdocs/mortgage/ISV-Partner-Terms-and-Conditions.pdf


## Own-AI Bridge: the loan officer's own assistant works their book through a gated, sponsor-approved MCP server (round 3, medium impact, ~4 weeks)

**Loan officers who already live in Claude or ChatGPT can ask 'who closes this week and what's missing?' or 'draft Dana's status text' there. Command Center answers through its own MCP server with minimum-necessary data and audited reads. Every write lands as a draft in the Today approval queue and goes out only through the send gate.**

- **Problem:** Loan officers increasingly do their thinking in a general AI assistant. Today they copy and paste borrower data into it, which means borrower NPI going into an unvetted processor with no audit, or they keep switching screens. nCino is the only LOS or POS with first-party MCP servers, and its LO server can write loans and run DU and LPA. That's a dangerous capability to hand an AI without a human tap and the borrower's authorization. ARIVE, Encompass, LendingPad, Byte, Calyx and MeridianLink have none. Neither the copy-paste habit nor a raw LOS MCP connection is safe, and both cost time.
- **How it works:** Data: the loan officer's own tenant only, meaning contacts, Today cards, pipeline status, consent state and drafts. Responses return minimum-necessary projections: first name, milestone, and the missing-item list. SSNs, DOBs, account numbers, income figures and credit data are never returned.

Server: a stateless MCP server following the 2026-07-28 revision noted in AGENTS.md, plus an OAuth flow.

Allowed clients: the sponsor keeps an allow-list of AI clients whose enterprise terms provide zero retention and no training. A loan officer can connect only an approved client.

Tools:
- Read-only: today_summary, pipeline_status, missing_items, contact_timeline_summary, consent_status.
- Draft: draft_text, draft_email, add_note_draft, schedule_followup_draft. Every draft lands in the Today approval queue.
- Never offered: send, call, run_aus, pull_credit, lock, or price outside get_pricing. A rate appears only inside a get_pricing result carrying its APR and the not-a-commitment line. Text from the outside AI is checked by the M8 guard before it can become a draft.

Tokens: scoped per session, expire after 8 hours, and can be revoked from /mlo/team. Every tool call is logged in the GLBA read audit that the sponsor can see.

As a client: Command Center can also call nCino's LO MCP server with only its read tools on its allow-list, so loan officers at nCino lenders get status without write risk.

What the loan officer experiences: they stay in their assistant, and the work still lands in one approval queue.

What the borrower experiences: nothing different, except that every message still passes the same gate and guard.
- **Technologies:** Model Context Protocol server (stateless 2026-07-28 revision; @modelcontextprotocol/server and Vercel mcp-handler per AGENTS.md), OAuth 2.1 with per-session scoped, expiring tokens, Sponsor AI-client allow-list tied to vendor zero-retention terms, Minimum-necessary response projections, with lib/m8 redaction, M8 output guard applied to inbound drafts, Today approval queue and D3 send gate, GLBA read-audit log (cc_audit), nCino Mortgage LO MCP server as a read-only client
- **Why it's new:** nCino publishes LO and Admin MCP servers (beta) with write and AUS tools. ICE says customers can deploy their own agents into Encompass via MCP. Model Match and ATTOM offer MCP data servers for their own data. No CRM was found that exposes a loan officer's whole book to the loan officer's own assistant with three things together: sponsor-controlled client approval, minimum-necessary projections, and writes that are drafts-only and must pass a consent gate. The taken 'delegation envelopes' sets limits on what each AI agent may do. This is the actual bridge and data contract for external assistants, governed by the sponsor. The repo's AGENTS.md already plans an MCP surface pending attorney review. Market status was not re-searched this session because the search budget was used up.
- **Compliance design:** GLBA and the FTC Safeguards Rule: the external AI vendor becomes a service provider to the sponsor. So the sponsor must approve the vendor (allow-list), contract terms must give zero retention and no training, responses are minimum-necessary, and every read is audited and visible to the sponsor. Counsel confirms whether the loan officer's personal AI subscription can ever qualify; the default is the sponsor's enterprise account only.

TCPA: no send or call tools exist. Drafts go through D3 and the loan officer's one-tap approval.

AI behavior: no rate outside get_pricing, and the guard runs on all externally drafted text. Documents and contact text are data, never instructions: tool outputs are labelled data, and the red-team suite gains MCP prompt-injection cases.

Credit, AUS and lock actions are never exposed, consistent with HPPA and FCRA permissible-purpose limits and with the borrower's authorization.

Fair lending: the server ranks nothing new. It serves the existing Today ranking, which is already tested.

State AI laws: borrower-facing messages still carry M8's AI disclosure where the content is AI-drafted, per counsel.

ATTORNEY_REVIEW_LIST already requires review before any MCP surface ships.
- **Moat:** The more loan officers work through their own assistants, the more Command Center becomes the trusted system of record and the safety layer under every AI, rather than one more screen competing for attention. Because the platform is owned by its members, the allow-list, tool schemas and audit format can be published openly as a standard that other brokers adopt. An enterprise CRM tied to one LOS vendor has little incentive to open the same way.
- **Reviews:** keep 9/10: It meets Safeguards service-provider oversight through the allow-list and the enterprise zero-retention terms. Minimal projections can still leak NPI through free-text timeline summaries. Redesign: Run the redaction pass on every tool output, and test it in the MCP red-team cases. / keep 5/10: Partly refuted, from knowledge. General CRMs already ship MCP servers and assistant connectors: HubSpot's MCP server and Claude and ChatGPT connectors, and Salesforce's MCP support. nCino and ICE offer MCP access for lender staff. The difference here is a mortgage tool set limited to drafts, with sponsor allow-lists, minimum-necessary projections and the D3 gate. That is a materially safer combination but not a new category. Redesign: Share one MCP codebase with Pocket Loan Officer. Expose read and draft tools only, and gate the server behind ATTORNEY_REVIEW_LIST and sponsor enterprise-client approval. Add MCP prompt-injection cases to the red team. / keep 5/10: The design (read-only projections, drafts only, the gate) is sound and the build is moderate. The usable base is small. Approved clients need enterprise terms with zero retention and no training, and small brokers rarely buy enterprise AI seats, while consumer plans fall short of that bar. Attorney review is required before any MCP surface ships. It saves time only for loan officers who already work inside an assistant. Redesign: Build it after the core is in place: start with the read-only tools plus draft_text into the approval queue, for sponsors who have an approved enterprise client. Publish the tool schemas. Pocket Loan Officer stays deferred.
- **Sources:** https://developer.ncinomortgage.com/mortgage/docs/lo-mcp-server, https://developer.ncinomortgage.com/mortgage, https://ir.theice.com/press/news-details/2026/ICE-Unveils-AI-Voice-and-Chat-Agents-for-Mortgage-Servicing-at-ICE-Experience-2026/default.aspx, https://modelmatch.com/model-match-mcp, https://www.attomdata.com/solutions/delivery/property-data-api/pricing/, https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-314, /home/user/RATEM8/AGENTS.md (section 4, MCP revisions and SDK), /home/user/RATEM8/ATTORNEY_REVIEW_LIST.md (MCP review requirement)
